# Mapping vendor features onto Runvil's universal inference API: the Gateway handles most of them and one opaque field handles the rest

- Report type: analytical, with recommendations
- Written for: the owner of the Runvil project, and whoever implements the work
- Subject: how each model vendor's features can be supported through Runvil's universal inference API. OpenRouter, a commercial service that puts one API in front of many vendors' models, was the first reference consulted for each feature.
- Feeds: two work items in the Runvil class's lesson plan of 2026-11-17. Item 2, "Vendor chat completions", adds support for vendors' own protocols behind the Gateway. Item 3 is a standalone version of the gateway client.
- Sources: every source was read on 2026-11-22
- Not done: nothing was run against a live model

## Executive summary

**What this report examined.** Runvil runs prompts, which are Markdown files with Lua code in them. The Engine executes a prompt, the gateway client ("the client") sends each model request, and the Gateway is the server that forwards the request to a model vendor or to a model on the local machine. Runvil plans one universal inference API between the client and the Gateway: chat completions, OpenAI's original format for chat models, plus a few optional extra fields. The Gateway would translate each request into the vendor's own protocol. The goal is that code outside the Gateway knows as little as possible about which vendor's model is answering, and that a prompt never has to know. The planned work is a tool loop (the model asks for a tool, gets the result, and continues) with reasoning, the text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer, on Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google models, on other vendors whose API copies chat completions, and on local servers.

The report takes 105 features, each one thing that a vendor's API does in its own way, and asks of each: which parts of Runvil would need vendor-specific code to support it? Examples are each vendor's rules for tool calls, each vendor's way of caching the start of a request, and each vendor's handling of reasoning. Anthropic calls reasoning thinking and signs it: it attaches a string that it checks when the reasoning comes back in a later request. For each feature the report first looked at OpenRouter, a commercial service that does the job Runvil's Gateway is meant to do: it puts one API, chat completions with fields of its own added, in front of many vendors' models.

**The answer.** For every feature that the planned work needs, 75 of the 105, all the vendor-specific code can be in the Gateway. Nothing outside it is written for one vendor: the changes there are the opaque field, an optional extra field on an assistant message that the client and the Engine would store and send back without reading, and a few settings or fields that are the same for every vendor. No feature requires a prompt to know its vendor. Runvil neither uses nor needs the other 30 features. Nothing proposed here is built yet.

- **65 features, 52 of them among the 75, need only the Gateway**: it translates, so the vendor gets what the request asked for, in its own format. This covers reasoning settings, caching, the format for describing tools, the reason a reply ended, errors, and token counts. Nine of the 65 also need a new setting or field, such as a value for how much reasoning a model should do.
- **25 features, 17 of them among the 75, need the opaque field.** Some vendors send data in a reply that they expect back, unchanged, in later requests, such as Anthropic's signed thinking blocks and OpenAI's encrypted reasoning, which only OpenAI can read. Anthropic answers HTTP 400 when a block comes back changed, moved, or with one missing in between, Google does the same when a tool call's signature is missing, and Anthropic and OpenAI drop, without an error, reasoning that another account produced. (A block is one element of a reply: a piece of text, a piece of reasoning, or a tool call.) The Gateway would put such data in the opaque field, where prompts never see it. The client and the Engine are changed for it once.
- **10 features, 6 of them among the 75, need a little compatibility code.** Here the Gateway changes what a request asks for (it removes a temperature that a model rejects, for example) or repairs what one server gets wrong. A prompt author could notice most of these changes.
- **5 features would need a separate vendor path**, on which the client or the Engine speaks one vendor's own format. With four, the vendor keeps data or a running job on its own servers (files and conversations stored at the vendor, background jobs, and batch jobs), and the fifth is requests over a WebSocket connection. Runvil uses none of the five.

Confidence: medium - the vendors document their rules, and open-source projects whose code was read already convert requests in these ways, but the opaque field exists only as a design.

**What OpenRouter's design shows.**

- **Its rule for reasoning data is the one Runvil wants, and its format loses information.** OpenRouter returns reasoning data in `reasoning_details`, a list of entries on the assistant message, and the caller has to send the list back whole, unchanged, and in order. But its chat completions reply keeps `reasoning_details`, the text, and the tool calls in three separate fields, so nothing records where each piece of reasoning was among the text and the tool calls, and Anthropic's and Google's checks of returned data depend on that. The five open-source gateways examined that also split a reply by kind of block lose the order too. OpenRouter's own client library needed nine fixes in 2026 to its handling of this data.
- **It hides the vendor for settings, and it does not hide where data that has to go back came from.** A caller sets one reasoning-effort value, one marker for the part of a request to cache, and one format for tool definitions, and reads one of five values for why a reply ended and one of 27 error types. But Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google accept signed or encrypted reasoning only in the customer account that produced it. For a caller of OpenRouter that means the provider that served the request. A provider is a company that serves a model, one model can have several (Claude is served by Anthropic itself and by cloud platforms such as Amazon Bedrock), and data produced through one provider is not accepted through another. The signatures and encrypted values that OpenRouter returns end with an undocumented suffix that names the model version and the provider. When a request sends reasoning blocks back, OpenRouter routes it only to a provider that can read them, and answers HTTP 400 when the caller's own settings rule out every such provider. Its staff advise callers to name the provider themselves and forbid any other.
- **It has three routes, and ten vendor features are missing from its chat completions route.** The other two (a route is a URL path) are a Messages route in Anthropic's format and a Responses route in the format of OpenAI's newer API. Five of the ten work only on the Messages route, three only on the Responses route, and two on both. So a caller that wants a vendor's own summary of an over-long conversation, citations on supplied documents, OpenAI's `phase` labels (commentary or final answer), or tools whose results may come in a later request has to pick a route by the model's vendor. One coding agent, pi, already sends all its Claude requests to the Messages route to keep signed thinking. Runvil's Gateway would handle all ten on its one route, with the opaque field and, for a few that Runvil does not use, a new vendor-neutral field.

**What should change in Runvil's plan.**

1. **The opaque field should record which endpoint (one account at one provider) produced its contents, be used for vendors with OpenAI-style APIs too, hold other data that has to come back, and be sent once at the end of a reply.** In the design of Runvil's 2026-11-17 report on Anthropic's Messages API, the field holds a reply's whole list of blocks, with a label for the protocol and the model. The label should also record the endpoint, one backend in the Gateway's configuration, because the vendors accept such data only in the account that produced it. Of the vendors with OpenAI-style APIs, DeepSeek, Moonshot, Alibaba, Zhipu, and MiniMax return reasoning as plain text and expect it back, Runvil's client never sends it back, and DeepSeek answers HTTP 400 to a request with tools that lacks it, so a tool loop fails there today. Other data that has to come back is, for example, the `phase` label on each assistant message in a reply of OpenAI's Responses API. And the Gateway should send the field complete, on the last chunk of a streamed reply, so that the client has no fragments to join.
2. **The Gateway needs a translator for OpenAI's Responses API early, second after Anthropic's.** From GPT-5.4 on, OpenAI's own chat completions API supports tool calling only while reasoning is off, and on five GPT-6 models not at all. Today the Gateway forwards chat completions requests to OpenAI unchanged, so a tool loop cannot run on those five and runs on the others only without reasoning. Confidence: high - OpenAI's migration pages state the limit, and they were fetched twice.
3. **Today's Gateway has four defects that should be fixed before any vendor translator is built.** It loses a streamed reply's token counts when they arrive in a last chunk of their own, as in OpenAI's format. It replaces an upstream's error text with a fixed phrase, so when a vendor rejects a request as too long for the model, the Engine is not told and does not start compaction (shortening the conversation), though its own estimate before a request is sent still can. It skips an error that arrives in mid-stream, so a failed reply can look complete. And it waits on a silent stream without a deadline.
4. **The Gateway needs a table of what each model accepts, and Runvil needs one rule for a request that asks for something the model cannot do.** Models differ in which settings they accept, so the Gateway has to look that up for each request before it forwards it. Today it publishes such data in its model list and does not use it. The suggested rule: the Engine refuses the run at its first step, as it does today, when the model fails a requirement that the prompt declared in its header (a minimum context size, or thinking on or off). Any other setting that a model rejects or ignores, such as a temperature, the Gateway would drop or replace and report in a reply field, `gateway_warning`.
5. **The opaque field should not copy OpenRouter's `reasoning_details`, in name or in format, and Runvil can use OpenAI's own names for three fields.** The three names, all in OpenAI's chat completions format, are `reasoning_effort`, `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens` for a count of the tokens written to the vendor's cache, and, if Runvil adopts citations, `annotations` with `url_citation` entries.

The rest of the plan should stay: one route for model requests, the Anthropic translator first, and prompts that name a model role, such as `strong`, and never a vendor. Settings that OpenRouter's callers write as one vendor spells them (the header values that switch on Anthropic's beta features, Google's content-filter thresholds for Gemini, and `provider.options`, OpenRouter's object for one provider's own settings) would never be in a prompt: the Gateway would send Anthropic's values itself, and its operator would write the others once in its configuration.

**How solid the evidence is.** Nothing was run against a model. Every statement comes from documentation, a published API specification, source code, or traffic that open-source projects recorded in their tests, all read on 2026-11-22. OpenRouter's own published material says what OpenRouter does for 85 of the 105 features. For 7 it is inferred, and for 13 its material is silent, so the account is of what an open-source gateway or library does (6) or of what the vendor documents (7). That the handling proposed for Runvil works is shown for 25 features (those 6 and 19 others) by an open-source project that already handles the feature that way. For the other 80 it is shown by the vendor's documented rule and, where OpenRouter's material covers the feature, by what OpenRouter does with that rule. A second, independent reading gave 128 verdicts on the claims the conclusions depend on most: 97 held as recorded, 22 held with a correction, 4 did not hold, 3 were conflicts between two sources of one publisher, and 2 said more than their source supports. The corrections are applied, and none of the four failed claims is made here.

**What the project owner needs to decide.** There are eleven decisions, with the recommended answer in parentheses where there is one. Six come from the changes: the endpoint in the opaque field's label (yes), the field's use for vendors with OpenAI-style APIs (yes), a second translator for OpenAI's Responses API or for Google's Gemini API (OpenAI's), the rule in change 4 (yes), a name of Runvil's own for the field or OpenRouter's (Runvil's own), and which of change 5's names to use now (its first two). The other five:

- Whether a planned standalone version of the client, which could talk to OpenRouter without the Gateway, has to send OpenRouter's reasoning data back, which takes client code written for OpenRouter alone (no, though a model reached that way loses its signed or encrypted reasoning).
- Whether the Gateway should also accept requests in Anthropic's Messages format on a second route (no).
- Whether the Gateway or the Engine sends the follow-up request when a vendor stops a reply partway, as Gemini does at its output limit (the Gateway, so that the client sees one complete reply).
- Whether to move the 26 evidence files behind this report out of their temporary folder (yes).
- Whether a prompt should still be able to read which provider serves its model, which today is the one way a prompt can depend on its provider (no recommendation: no feature needs it, and what existing prompts use it for was not examined).

## Terms used in this report

Each term has the meaning given here throughout the report. The summary and each numbered section explain a term briefly at first use. The notes in section 3 do not, so this list is the place to look one up.

**Vendors, providers, and APIs**

- **Vendor**: a company that makes models and defines its own API for them. The vendors in this report are Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, xAI, DeepSeek, Mistral, Moonshot (Kimi models), Alibaba (Qwen models), Zhipu (GLM models), MiniMax, and Meta.
- **Provider**: a company that serves requests for a model. One model can have several: Claude, for example, is served by Anthropic itself and by cloud platforms such as Amazon Bedrock, Google Vertex AI, and Microsoft Azure.
- **Local server**: a program that runs a model on one's own machine and serves an HTTP API for it: llama.cpp, vLLM, or Ollama.
- **Open-weight model**: a model whose weights are published, so anyone can run it.
- **Caller**: the program that sends requests to an API.
- **Route**: one URL path of an HTTP API, such as `/chat/completions`.
- **Chat completions**: OpenAI's original request and reply format for chat models. Most vendors and local servers accept some version of it.
- **Vendor protocol**: a vendor's own request and reply format, such as Anthropic's Messages API, OpenAI's Responses API, or Google's Gemini API. In Google's newer Interactions API, Google's servers store the conversation.
- **Vendors with OpenAI-style APIs**: vendors whose own API copies the chat completions format: DeepSeek, Moonshot, Alibaba, Zhipu, MiniMax, Mistral, and xAI.
- **Gateway** written in lower case: any server that takes a caller's requests and translates them for several vendors, such as OpenRouter (commercial) or LiteLLM and Bifrost (open source). The capitalized Gateway is Runvil's.
- **Upstream**: the server that a gateway forwards a request to.
- **Translator**: the code in a gateway that converts a request into one vendor's protocol and the vendor's reply back.
- **OpenRouter**: a commercial gateway that puts one API in front of the models of many vendors. It has three routes for model requests: a chat completions route (a superset of that format: it adds fields of its own), a Messages route in Anthropic's format, and a Responses route in the format of OpenAI's newer Responses API.
- **OpenRouter's model catalog**: its public list of models and their properties.
- **OpenRouter's API specification**: the machine-readable description of its API that OpenRouter publishes.
- **Model id**: the name a caller sends to pick a model. OpenRouter's look like `deepseek/deepseek-v3.2`.
- **Model library**: a library that gives a program one message type that works across several vendors, such as the Vercel AI SDK, Pydantic AI, or rig.
- **Feature**, or **vendor feature**: one of the 105 things this report examines: a field, a rule, a kind of data, or a whole capability that differs from one vendor, provider, or local server to the next, or that OpenRouter adds.

**Requests, replies, and the data in them**

- **Reasoning**, also called **thinking**: text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer. Anthropic and Google call it thinking, and OpenAI calls it reasoning. A **reasoning model** is one that can write reasoning before it answers.
- **Block**: one element of the content of a message in a vendor's format: a piece of text, a piece of reasoning, or a tool call. Anthropic calls these content blocks, OpenAI's Responses API calls them output items, and Google calls them parts.
- **Signature**: a string a vendor attaches to a block of a reply, most often a reasoning block. When the block is sent back, the vendor checks the signature to confirm that the block is its own, unchanged output. Google calls its version a thought signature.
- **Encrypted value**: a string that only the vendor can read, such as OpenAI's encrypted reasoning.
- **Turn**: for Anthropic and Google, everything from one user message to the next, which can hold several model replies and tool results.
- **Tool**: a function the model can ask the caller to run. A tool definition is the JSON that describes it, and a tool call is the model's request to run it.
- **Tool loop**: the cycle in which the model asks for a tool call, the program runs the tool and sends the result back, and the model continues. A **round** is one model request and its reply inside the loop.
- **Vendor-run tool**: a tool that the vendor, and not the caller, runs on its own servers during a request, such as web search or code execution.
- **MCP**: Model Context Protocol, a standard way for a separate server to offer tools to a model.
- **Harness**: a program that runs a tool loop against a model for a user, such as the coding agents pi and opencode.
- **Chat template**: the text template shipped with an open-weight model. It turns a message list into the model's own input format, and tools or reasoning work on a local server only where the template handles them.
- **Structured output**: asking the model for a reply in JSON that matches a schema the caller supplies.
- **Prefix**: the beginning part of a request that stays the same from one request to the next: the system text, the tool definitions, and the earlier messages.
- **Prompt caching**: a vendor stores the processed form of a request's prefix, so that a later request that starts the same way is cheaper and faster.
- **Cache marker**: a `cache_control` field placed on one block of a request. It tells the vendor to cache the request up to and including that block.
- **Reasoning-effort setting**: a request setting that tells a model how much reasoning to do.
- **Finish reason**: the field in a reply that says why the model stopped. Anthropic's name for its version is stop reason.
- **`tool_choice`**: the request setting that says whether the model may or must call a tool. A forced tool choice makes the model call a tool, or one named tool.
- **Prefill**: ending a request with a partial assistant message that the model is supposed to continue.
- **Usage**: the token counts that a reply reports: prompt tokens, output tokens, cached tokens, and reasoning tokens.
- **Streamed reply**: a reply that arrives as a series of chunks while the model writes it (server-sent events, SSE).
- **Sticky routing**: OpenRouter's documented practice of sending a conversation's later requests to the provider that served its earlier ones, unless that provider is unavailable.
- **Pinning a provider**: a caller tells OpenRouter which provider to use and forbids any other.
- **`reasoning_details`**: OpenRouter's field for reasoning data that goes back to the vendor: a list of entries on the assistant message, mostly reasoning entries plus a few records of OpenRouter's own. It has no place for a vendor's text and tool-call blocks or for the order of a reply's blocks.

**Runvil and its parts**

- **Prompt**: a Markdown file with Lua code blocks that asks language models for replies. Vendors also use the word for the input of a model request, as in "prompt caching" and "prompt tokens".
- **Runvil**: the system that runs prompts and sends their model requests, through its client and its Gateway, to the models.
- **Gateway**: Runvil's server. It accepts model requests and forwards each one to a vendor or another provider, or to a model running on the operator's own machine.
- **Operator**: the person who installs and configures a Gateway.
- **Gateway client**, or **the client**: Runvil's HTTP client library. It sends each model request to the Gateway in chat completions format and always asks for a streamed reply.
- **Engine**: the part of Runvil that executes a prompt. It holds the conversation as a list of message records.
- **Lua code**: the code inside a prompt. It starts each model request and can read each message record through five fixed fields: `role`, `content`, `tool_calls`, `tool_call_id`, `meta`.
- **Projection**: the Engine step that turns the stored message records into the messages of a request.
- **Compaction**: shortening a conversation that no longer fits the model's context window, usually by replacing older messages with a summary. A compactor is the Lua function that does it in Runvil.
- **Precheck**: the Engine's estimate, before a request is sent, of whether it fits the model's context window.
- **Model role**: one of five names (`default`, `strong`, `small`, `fast`, `creative`) that a prompt uses in place of a model's name.
- **Host**: the program that embeds the Engine and decides which model fills each model role. Workshop, Runvil's desktop app, is a Host.
- **Binding**: the Engine's record of which model fills a role and which request settings go with it.
- **Requirement check**: in its front matter (the YAML header of the prompt file) a prompt can require two things of the model that fills a role: a minimum context size (`min_context`) and thinking on or off (`thinking`). The Engine checks this before any Lua runs and ends the run with `requirements_unmet` if the model falls short.
- **Pack**: Runvil's unit for installing tools into a run. Runvil's own tools, such as code execution and web access, are Packs and run inside the Host.
- **Gateway configuration**: one file, `gateway.toml`. A **model entry** in it gives the model id that callers send, the model's name at the upstream, its endpoints, and its settings.
- **Endpoint**: in statements about Runvil, one backend in the Gateway's configuration (an endpoint entry): one base URL with its API key, so one account at one provider. In statements about OpenRouter, one provider's offering of one model.
- **Model list**: what the Gateway publishes about each model, at `GET /v1/models`.
- **Model sheet**: a file that the Runvil project publishes every week, listing each cloud provider's models with their settings. The Gateway fills model entries and its model list from it.
- **Per-model table**: data the Gateway would look up while it handles a request: which settings and values the model accepts. It is a proposal: today the Gateway publishes such data in its model list and does not use it for requests.
- **Tool dialect**: the Gateway's existing conversion for a model that writes tool calls as text in a format of its own: it describes the tools to the model in a system message and turns the model's text into ordinary tool calls.
- **`gateway_warning`**: a field the Gateway adds to a reply. Today it has one use, a tool call the Gateway could not parse, and the client does not pass it on. This report proposes it for every setting the Gateway drops or replaces.
- **Universal inference API**, or **universal API**: Runvil's planned API between its client and the Gateway: OpenAI's chat completions format plus a few optional extra fields.
- **Vendor-neutral setting**: a setting or field that means the same thing for every vendor and names none of them.
- **Opaque field**: an optional extra field on an assistant message in the universal API, designed and not yet built. The Gateway fills it with data that the vendor sent and expects back unchanged, and can keep data of its own in it. The client and the Engine store it and send it back in every later request of the conversation without reading it, and prompts never see it. In the 2026-11-17 design it holds the reply's whole list of vendor blocks, in the vendor's order, and has the placeholder name `provider_content`.
- **Label on the opaque field**: values the Gateway stores inside the opaque field, beside the vendor's blocks, that say what produced them: in the 2026-11-17 design, the protocol and the model. The Gateway sends the blocks back only when the label matches where the next request is going.
- **Lesson plan items 2 and 3**: two work items in the 2026-11-17 lesson plan of the Runvil class, which the project owner teaches: vendors' own protocols behind the Gateway (item 2), and a standalone client that can talk to other servers (item 3).
- **The 2026-11-17 report**: an earlier Runvil report, "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build". It designed the opaque field ("the 2026-11-17 design").

**Evidence**

- **Row**: one of the 105 features, by its number in the tables of section 3. Each has a row in a summary table and a note under the table, and "(row 28)" points to both.
- **Evidence item**: a numbered entry in one of the 26 evidence files, cited by a code such as `R36`. The letters of the code name the file, and section 12 lists the files.
- **Recorded traffic**: HTTP requests to OpenRouter, with its replies, that open-source projects saved in their test suites.

**The six options for supporting a vendor feature, from best to worst** (section 1 defines them in full). The order is the project owner's stated preference, quoted there in Table 1.

- **Gateway only**: the Gateway translates the request and the reply, and no code outside the Gateway knows the vendor. The client, the Engine, and prompts are unchanged, or change only in a way that is the same for every vendor: a new vendor-neutral setting, or reading one more standard field.
- **Opaque field**, as an option: the feature works only if the client and the Engine store the opaque field and send it back. They would be changed for that once, for Anthropic's signed thinking blocks (row 1).
- **Compatibility code**: code in the Gateway that changes what a request asks for, in a way a prompt author could notice (a setting is dropped, a forced tool call is relaxed, a schema keyword is removed), or that repairs what one server gets wrong in the format it claims to follow. Changing only the layout of a request or a reply is translation, and stays "Gateway only".
- **Vendor branch in shared code**: the client or the Engine needs a branch for one vendor inside the code that all vendors' requests pass through. No feature needs it.
- **Separate vendor path**: the feature can be reached only through a route or request format that is specific to one vendor, which the client or the Engine would have to speak, around the universal API. A new route on the Gateway that is the same for every vendor is not one: it counts as a new vendor-neutral setting, and the feature is then "Gateway only".
- **Prompt must know the vendor**: the author of a prompt would have to know which vendor's model runs it. Runvil's design goal rules this out, and no feature needs it.

**Phrases that can follow an option in the feature tables**

- **With no new client or Engine work**: follows "Opaque field" and is about the opaque field alone. The feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once, for row 1. Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor.
- **If adopted**: marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work does not need. The planned work is a tool loop with reasoning on Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google models, on vendors with OpenAI-style APIs, and on local servers. A feature without the mark is part of that work, or is a vendor behavior that the work runs into.

## 1. How to read this report: each feature is judged by which parts of Runvil would have to contain vendor-specific code

Runvil runs prompts written as Markdown files with Lua code blocks. Four pieces of code take part in a model request:

- **The prompt's Lua code** starts each model request.
- **The Engine** runs the prompt and holds the conversation as a list of message records.
- **The gateway client** (called "the client" from here on) is the HTTP library that sends each request.
- **The Gateway** is the server that receives the request and forwards it to a vendor (a company that makes models, such as Anthropic or OpenAI), to another provider (a company that serves a vendor's model), or to a model on the local machine.

Today the client and the Gateway exchange every request in chat completions, OpenAI's original request and reply format for chat models. The Gateway forwards it as it is, apart from the model's name, with one exception: for a model that writes its tool calls as text, it converts them into ordinary tool calls (the tool dialect) and adds a `gateway_warning` field to the reply when it cannot parse one. Runvil's plan is a universal inference API between the client and the Gateway: chat completions plus a small number of optional extra fields. The Gateway would translate each request into the vendor's own protocol, such as Anthropic's Messages API, OpenAI's Responses API, or Google's Gemini API, and the reply back. No such translator is built yet.

**The question asked about every feature.** Anthropic signs the reasoning its models write, OpenAI encrypts it, and Google signs tool calls. Each vendor has its own way to cache the start of a request, describe tools, and report errors. For each such feature, the question is: to support it, which parts of Runvil would have to contain vendor-specific code? Vendor-specific code is code written for one vendor, provider, model, or local server, which would not be needed if every model spoke the same protocol.

**Why OpenRouter is the first place the report looks.** OpenRouter is a commercial service that puts one API in front of the models of many vendors. Its chat completions route (a route is a URL path) accepts everything that format accepts and adds fields of its own. Runvil's universal API sets out to be the same, and OpenRouter has paying customers who report what breaks. So for each feature the report first looks at what OpenRouter does, and on which of its routes.

- Where OpenRouter offers one field that works for every vendor, Runvil's Gateway can probably translate in the same way.
- Where OpenRouter tells its callers that a result depends on the vendor, the model, or the provider, the rule behind it is the vendor's or the provider's own, and Runvil's Gateway will run into the same rule: a model that refuses a request to turn reasoning off, for example. Section 5 goes through such situations and says what the Gateway would have to do in each.

OpenRouter's server code is closed source, so where its published material is silent, the report looks at how open-source gateways and model libraries handle the same feature, and at the vendors' own documentation.

**The six options.** Runvil could support a vendor feature in six ways. Table 1 lists them from best to worst. The names are this report's, and the order is the project owner's stated preference, quoted in the last column.

*Table 1. The six ways Runvil could accommodate a vendor feature, from best to worst.*

| Option | What it means | The project owner's words |
|---|---|---|
| **Gateway only** | The Gateway translates the request and the reply, and puts each vendor's finish reasons, errors, and token counts into one common form. No code outside the Gateway knows the vendor. The client, the Engine, and the prompt's Lua code are unchanged, or gain only a setting or field that means the same for every vendor. | "Ideally we handle it all in the Gateway" |
| **Opaque field** | The feature works only if the client and the Engine store the opaque field, described under this table, and send it back. | "adding opaque fields to messages which the engine and the Lua never see, is perfectly fine" |
| **Compatibility code** | Translating is not enough: one vendor, model, or local server would reject or mishandle the request, or its reply departs from the format it claims to follow. A little code in the Gateway changes what the request asks for (it drops a setting, relaxes a forced tool call, or removes a schema keyword) or repairs the fault (it rewrites an id). Changing only the layout of a request or a reply is translation. | "if we need a little compatibility code to massage things that come from the engine to fit a specific vendor, that is ok too (but note it in the report)" |
| **Vendor branch in shared code** | The client or the Engine needs a branch that runs for one vendor only, inside the code that every vendor's requests pass through. | "A small vendor patch-up in the universal path is unfortunate but could be ok as long as the vendor is big (Anthropic is big)" |
| **Separate vendor path** | The client or the Engine would have to speak one vendor's own format, on a route or in a request format that goes around the universal API. A new route on the Gateway that is the same for every vendor is not one. | "Less desirable would be having to have a vendor-specific path that bypasses all of the universal path" |
| **Prompt must know the vendor** | The author of a prompt would have to know which vendor's model runs it. Runvil rules this out. | "a complete non-starter would be anything that forces the Lua prompt to care about the vendor" |

The project owner's summary of the goal: "we want to try to make it so the engine doesn't have to care. but if it has to care, then only care a little bit."

**The opaque field.** Some vendors send data in a reply that they expect back, unchanged, in later requests of the same conversation: Anthropic's signed thinking blocks and OpenAI's encrypted reasoning, for example. (A block is one element of a vendor's reply: a piece of text, a piece of reasoning, or a tool call.) The opaque field is Runvil's planned place for such data: an optional extra field on an assistant message in the universal API. The Gateway would fill it. The client and the Engine would store it and send it back in every later request without reading what is in it, which is how this report reads "never see" for the Engine, and the prompt's Lua code could not reach it. An earlier Runvil report, "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build" (2026-11-17), designed the field and gave it the placeholder name `provider_content`. In that design the field holds the reply's whole list of vendor blocks, in the vendor's order, plus a label that says which protocol and which model produced them. This report keeps the design and proposes four additions (section 8): the label also names the endpoint (one account at one provider), the field is also used for plain-text reasoning that some vendors expect back, it also holds data that is not a block, such as the `phase` label on each assistant message of an OpenAI reply, and the Gateway sends it to the client whole, on the last chunk of a streamed reply.

**How a feature is assigned to an option.** Supporting one feature can take several pieces of work, for example a translator in the Gateway plus the opaque field in the client and the Engine. The feature is listed under the worst option that any of those pieces needs. The first test is where vendor-specific code would have to be: in the Gateway alone, also in the client or the Engine, or also in a prompt. Two boundaries need a further rule.

- **Gateway only, or opaque field?** The code that stores the opaque field and sends it back names no vendor. But the field holds one vendor's data, which no code outside the Gateway reads, and the feature fails without it, so a feature that depends on it is counted apart. The field would be built once, for Anthropic's signed thinking blocks (row 1 of the feature tables). Every other feature that uses it reuses that code and is marked "Opaque field, with no new client or Engine work". The mark is about the field only: such a feature can still need a new vendor-neutral setting. A vendor-neutral setting or field is different from the opaque field: it holds a value that means the same for every vendor, such as a reasoning-effort setting (how much reasoning a model should do), so it does not change a feature's option. It changes the client, the Engine, and sometimes what a prompt can set, and each feature's note says whether it needs one.
- **Gateway only, or compatibility code?** Compatibility code changes what the client asked for or what the vendor answered, and a prompt author could notice most of these changes. The project owner's words in Table 1 ask that such code be noted, so section 2 lists the ten features that need it (Table 3). The same words rank "Opaque field" above "Compatibility code", though both keep vendor-specific code in the Gateway: opaque fields are "perfectly fine", and compatibility code is "ok too".

**Features Runvil neither uses nor needs are marked "if adopted".** Examples are code execution on the vendor's servers, batch jobs, and conversations stored by the vendor, and the tables say what Runvil would need if it chose to use such a feature. A feature without the mark is part of the planned work or is a vendor behavior that the work runs into. The planned work is a tool loop, in which the model asks for a tool, gets its result, and continues, with reasoning on Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google models, on vendors with OpenAI-style APIs, and on local servers.

**The feature tables.** Section 3 holds the 105 features in ten summary tables, Tables 5 to 14. A summary table has six columns: the row number, the feature, the vendors that have it, what OpenRouter does with it, what Runvil needs, and the confidence in that answer. Under each table, a note for each row gives the details. The rows are numbered 1 to 105 across the tables, and elsewhere in the report "(row 28)" points to that feature's row and note.

**Where each answer comes from.** Each note has a line with this name, which names the sources for the whole note. It starts with the main source, one of the five kinds below, and then names any open-source project whose code handles the feature in the way proposed for Runvil.

- **Stated by OpenRouter**: what OpenRouter does with the feature is in its documentation, API specification, model catalog, changelog, or the source code of its official client libraries.
- **Inferred about OpenRouter**: not stated by OpenRouter, and worked out from what the note names: other programs' code that special-cases OpenRouter, recorded traffic, an issue report, or a field missing from the API specification.
- **Taken from another open-source project**: OpenRouter's material is silent, so the note says how a named open-source project handles the same feature. That shows the feature can be handled and what it takes, and not what OpenRouter does.
- **Stated by the vendor**: in the vendor's own documentation, read on 2026-11-22, where OpenRouter's material is silent.
- **From earlier Runvil research**: from an earlier Runvil research report, named and dated, or from Runvil's code.

**Why another project's code counts as evidence for what Runvil needs.** What Runvil needs for a feature depends on the vendor's own rule and on whether a gateway can translate it, and not on how OpenRouter works inside. So an open-source project that already translates a feature in the way proposed is evidence that Runvil's Gateway can do the same. Such code also shows where a format fails: the five open-source gateways examined that split a reply into separate fields by kind of block (agentgateway, Bifrost, Envoy AI Gateway, Portkey's AI Gateway, and LiteLLM) all lose the order of the blocks, which Anthropic and Google check when the blocks come back. That is why the opaque field keeps the vendor's list whole and in order. In both cases the code was read and not run.

**Evidence items.** Codes such as `R36` or `AN12`, in the notes and in square brackets at the ends of sentences, are evidence items: numbered entries in the evidence files, each with its exact quote, its source (a URL, or a repository, commit, and line), and the date it was read. The letters name the file, and section 12 lists the files.

**Confidence.** Every "What Runvil needs" answer has a confidence of high, medium, or low. The report sets no thresholds for these, so the reason given with each is what to judge it by, and it says so when the answer depends on another project's code or on an inference.

## 2. Of 105 features, 65 need only the Gateway, 25 need the opaque field, 10 need compatibility code, and 5 need a separate vendor path

Section 3 lists 105 features, each one thing that a vendor's API, a local server, or OpenRouter itself does in its own way. For each one, the question is which parts of Runvil would have to contain vendor-specific code to support it. The answer is one of six options, which section 1 defines and orders from best to worst (Table 1).

Table 2 gives the count for each option: what each feature would need once Runvil supports it in the way this report proposes. The five under "Separate vendor path" are the exception: they are counted as the vendors offer them today, and not by the ways around that Table 4 names for four of them. The translators, the opaque field, and the per-model table (a table of what each model accepts) that the proposal needs are not built yet: today the Gateway forwards chat completions requests as they are. Table 2 separates the 75 features that are part of the work Runvil has planned, or that this work runs into, from the 30 that Runvil neither uses nor needs. The planned work is a tool loop with reasoning on Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google Gemini models, on vendors whose own API copies the chat completions format (such as DeepSeek and Mistral), and on local servers.

*Table 2. How many of the 105 features need each option.*

| Option | All features | Features needed for the work Runvil has planned | Features Runvil does not use (marked "if adopted" in section 3) | What these features are |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gateway only | 65 | 52 | 13 | Translation that the Gateway can do alone: reasoning settings, prompt caching (except Anthropic's cache diagnostics, row 40, which need the opaque field), the format for describing tools, finish reasons, errors, token counts, streaming, model lists, and the choice among a model's providers. |
| Opaque field | 25 | 17 | 8 | Signed, redacted, and encrypted reasoning, Google's thought signatures, the order of blocks inside a reply, and plain-text reasoning that a vendor wants back. Among the features Runvil does not use, also the blocks produced by tools the vendor runs. |
| Compatibility code | 10 | 6 | 4 | Small changes and repairs, every one of them in the Gateway (Table 3). |
| Vendor branch in shared code | 0 | 0 | 0 | No feature needs it, provided the planned standalone client does not have to send reasoning data back to OpenRouter (below). |
| Separate vendor path | 5 | 0 | 5 | Files, conversations, and jobs kept at the vendor, and requests over a WebSocket connection (Table 4). |
| Prompt must know the vendor | 0 | 0 | 0 | No feature needs it. |
| Total | 105 | 75 | 30 | |

Source: counted from Tables 5 to 14 in section 3.

**New vendor-neutral settings.** A vendor-neutral setting is a setting or field that means the same thing for every vendor and names none of them. A feature that needs a new one is still counted under its option, because the code it adds to the client and the Engine knows no vendor. Sixteen features need a new one, and for six more a new one is optional: the feature works without it, and Runvil could add it to give prompts control.

- Nine of the sixteen are under "Gateway only": a reasoning-effort value (row 21), a count of tokens written to the cache (row 38), a record that the tool set changed (row 41), a tool result that arrives later (row 53), a schema for the reply (row 55), citations on a reply (row 64), a file part in a message (row 69), audio and video parts (row 70), and a route that counts a request's tokens (row 79).
- Six are under "Opaque field": a flag that defers a tool's definition (row 52), a request for vendor-run code execution (row 65), images or audio in a reply (row 71), an id for a tool that one vendor has (row 73), a request for the vendor's summary (row 77), and an id for one of Anthropic's Skills (row 82).
- One is under "Separate vendor path": a files route on the Gateway with its file ids (row 68). It is the only separate-path feature whose note counts the way around as a new setting.
- Only the settings of rows 21 and 38 are part of the planned work. Feature 70 covers image, audio, and video input, and it is among the 75 for images alone, which the Engine's messages hold today. The other thirteen settings would be needed only if Runvil adopted a feature it does not use.
- The six optional ones are a record that the reasoning effort changed (row 27), a conversation id (row 36), a request's cost (row 39), a way for a prompt to force a tool call (row 43), images in a tool result (row 54), and more requirements for a prompt to declare (row 94). The client sets `tool_choice`, the request field that says whether a model may or must call a tool, only to `auto` today.

The thinking switch is not among them: a request has one today, and only the field it is sent in would change (row 22).

**How sure the answers are.** The answer has high confidence for 55 features, medium for 43, and low for 7. The report sets no thresholds for the three, and each feature's note gives its reason. The usual reason for a high is that the vendor documents its rule and OpenRouter or several open-source projects already translate it. The usual reasons for a medium or a low are that one project or one page is the only source, that no project examined shows the proposed handling working, that part of the handling is not designed, or that the vendor's rule is only days old, as OpenAI's rules for GPT-6.2 of 2026-11-17 are. The seven low ones (rows 40, 51, 53, 65, 71, 73, and 82) are all features Runvil does not use.

### All ten pieces of compatibility code can live in the Gateway

The project owner accepts a little compatibility code if the report notes every case, and Table 3 notes the ten. The client, the Engine, and the prompt's Lua code stay as they are, with one exception that is the same for every vendor: the client would have to pass on `gateway_warning`, in which the Gateway reports a change (rows 28 and 44), and it drops that field today.

*Table 3. The ten features that need compatibility code: what the code does, where it goes, and what a prompt author could notice.*

How to read Table 3: none of this code is written, so "Size" is an estimate. A translator is the Gateway code that would convert requests to one vendor's protocol and the replies back. The `openai` upstream code forwards chat completions requests to a server with an OpenAI-style API, and the local upstream code to a model on the local machine.

| Row | What the code does | Vendors | Where in the Gateway | Size | What a prompt author could notice |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 12 | Gemini expects Google's signature on the first tool call of each model reply, and tool calls from another vendor's model have none. The code puts Google's documented placeholder value there. | Google | The Gemini translator | About twenty lines | Google says the placeholder will lower the model's performance. |
| 28 | Removes a sampling setting that the model rejects, ignores, or holds at a fixed value: `temperature`, `top_p`, and `top_k`, and on xAI's reasoning models stop sequences and the presence and frequency penalties. | Anthropic, Google, Moonshot, OpenAI, DeepSeek, and xAI (stop sequences and penalties only) | Request handling, with the per-model table | One table lookup | A sampling setting in a prompt, such as a temperature, has no effect. |
| 46 | In a request, rewrites each tool-call id into the format the target vendor accepts, on the call and on its result. In a reply, replaces an id that is empty or repeated. | Anthropic, Mistral, Amazon Bedrock (a provider), Moonshot (the chat templates of its Kimi models), Google | Each translator, and the `openai` upstream code | One function per id rule | Almost nothing: a prompt reads the Gateway's id where a reply's id was empty or repeated. |
| 48 | Removes the parts of a tool's JSON schema that the target vendor rejects, and sends any header the vendor requires for `strict`, the flag that asks the vendor to guarantee the model's arguments match the schema. | Google, Anthropic, OpenAI | Each translator | Small. Gemini's newer schema fields take plain JSON Schema with a few keywords changed. | A schema keyword is dropped. |
| 101 | One small adapter for each server whose API looks like OpenAI's and differs in a detail: reply content that arrives as a list of pieces (Mistral), an error code inside the body of a reply (MiniMax), extra finish reasons (DeepSeek), and reasoning data in fragments, which the adapter joins (OpenRouter). For xAI the evidence shows nothing to repair. | Mistral, MiniMax, xAI, DeepSeek, and OpenRouter when the Gateway forwards to it | The `openai` upstream code, chosen by endpoint | A few lines each, more for OpenRouter | Nothing. |
| 104 | Before forwarding to llama.cpp, handles three cases: a `tool_choice` that names one tool, which the server silently treats as `auto`; tools sent to a model whose chat template cannot show them, which the server is reported to ignore; and a grammar sent with tools, which the server rejects. The Gateway would translate or reject the first and hold back the tools or the grammar. | llama.cpp (a local server) | The local upstream code | A few checks | Tools or a grammar are held back, or a forced choice of one tool is refused. What the caller is told is not designed. |
| 30 (if adopted) | When a request ends with a partial assistant message for the model to continue (a prefill), adds the vendor's flag where the vendor needs one, and refuses the request for a model that rejects a prefill. | Anthropic, DeepSeek, Moonshot | Request handling | A few lines | A prefill is refused on the models that reject one, such as Claude 4.6 and later. |
| 44 (if adopted) | When a request forces the model to call a tool and the model refuses to be forced (DeepSeek and Qwen only while reasoning is on), lets the model choose, adds an instruction to call the tool, and reports the change. The alternative is to refuse the request. | Anthropic, DeepSeek, Alibaba (Qwen), Zhipu (GLM), Meta | Request handling, with the per-model table | One table lookup | "Must call a tool" turns into "may call a tool". |
| 51 (if adopted) | OpenAI has tools whose input is free text and not JSON. The code hands such a tool call to the client as an ordinary function call with one string argument. | OpenAI | The Responses translator | Small | Nothing. |
| 56 (if adopted) | Where one provider's offering of a model cannot enforce a schema, gets schema-shaped JSON anyway by forcing the model to call a tool whose arguments have that schema. | Anthropic (Claude where Amazon Bedrock serves it), and some providers of other vendors' models | Each translator | Small | A weaker guarantee that the JSON matches the schema. |

### No feature needs a vendor branch in shared code or a prompt that knows its vendor, and the five that need a separate vendor path are features Runvil does not use

**Vendor branch in shared code: no feature needs one once it is supported as proposed. The client has one today, and a standalone client could need one.**

- **The client sends the thinking switch in a field that only local servers understand.** The switch, which says whether a model should reason before it answers, goes out as `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, which local servers such as llama.cpp and vLLM read, in the code that every vendor's requests pass through. The proposal removes that branch: the client would send one vendor-neutral on/off field, whose name is not chosen yet, and the Gateway would convert it for each vendor (row 22). The Gateway's design-decision page (`decisions/gateway.md` in the `runvil-design` repository) plans less: the Gateway would translate today's field for a provider that ignores it, and the client would keep the branch. Table 2's count of none assumes the proposal.
- **The standalone client would need vendor-specific code if it had to send reasoning data back to OpenRouter directly.** A standalone version of the client that can talk to servers other than Runvil's Gateway is planned (item 3 of the 2026-11-17 lesson plan). OpenRouter, and Vercel AI Gateway, a similar commercial service, send their reasoning data in fragments during a streamed reply and leave it to the caller to join them (row 18). Code for that in the client would be a vendor branch. One way to avoid it is to reach OpenRouter through Runvil's Gateway and let the Gateway join the fragments (part of row 101's compatibility code). The other, which section 8 recommends (decision 5), is that such a client sends back only fields it does not have to read, at the price that a model reached through OpenRouter loses its signed or encrypted reasoning.

**Separate vendor path: five features, and Runvil uses none of them.** With four, the vendor keeps data or a running job on its own servers, and a request in the universal API, which holds the whole conversation, has no way to refer to it. The fifth is a second kind of connection. Table 4 gives the way around, where there is one. For three it is a route on the Gateway that is the same for every vendor. Such a route is not a separate vendor path, so the feature would then be "Gateway only".

*Table 4. The five features that would need a separate vendor path, and how Runvil could avoid one.*

| Row | Feature | Why a separate path is needed | How Runvil could avoid it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 68 | Vendor file stores and file search | The file and its id exist in one vendor account, and the file is uploaded through a route that only that vendor has. | The Gateway could offer a files route that is the same for every vendor and translate its file ids into each vendor's ids, at the price of storing those ids. |
| 74 | Conversations stored by the vendor | The vendor stores the conversation's history, and the caller sends only an id. | Runvil does not need it. Sending the whole conversation each time, with the vendor's reasoning data returned in the opaque field, gives the model the same reasoning as a stored conversation. The open-source gateway LiteLLM imitates the feature for vendors that lack it, and needs a database to do so. |
| 75 | Background jobs with polling, canceling, and webhooks | The job keeps running after the request ends, and the caller needs a second route to fetch the result. | The Gateway could offer a jobs route that is the same for every vendor, if Runvil ever wants this. |
| 76 | Batch jobs | The caller submits many requests together through a separate batch route, and the vendor answers them later. | The Gateway could offer a batch route that is the same for every vendor, if Runvil ever wants this. |
| 83 | WebSocket transport | OpenAI exchanges requests and replies over one connection that stays open. The universal API is one HTTP request with one streamed reply, so the client would also need an open connection to the Gateway. | No design exists. |

**Prompt must know the vendor: no feature needs it. These come closest.**

- **Tools that only one vendor offers (row 73, an "Opaque field" feature that Runvil does not use).** Examples are Google's Maps grounding, xAI's search of posts on X, and Moonshot's formula tools for Kimi. A prompt would ask for a capability by a vendor-neutral id, and the Gateway would use the vendor's tool where the model has one and answer "unavailable" where it does not. None of that is designed, no project examined has a vendor-neutral form of Maps grounding, and Runvil can decline to offer these tools.
- **Settings that OpenRouter accepts only in a vendor's own words (rows 26, 72, 96, and 97, all "Gateway only").** They are the value `reasoning.mode: "pro"` that selects OpenAI's pro reasoning mode, Google's safety thresholds for Gemini, the values of Anthropic's beta header, and `provider.options`, OpenRouter's object for parameters that only one vendor has. In OpenRouter the calling program writes these values. In Runvil a prompt decides what a request asks for and must not contain them, so the Gateway would add Anthropic's header values itself, and the operator, the person who configures the Gateway, would write the others once in the configuration entry for the model. A prompt names only a model role, such as `strong`, and the role is mapped to that entry.
- **Requests that name no vendor but that some models refuse (rows 22 and 28, and rows 30 and 44, which Runvil does not use).** Turning thinking off, setting a temperature, a prefill, and forcing a tool call each fail or are ignored on some models, so a prompt's author can see that the result differs from model to model, without the prompt naming a vendor. Thinking off is a requirement in the prompt's front matter, the header of the prompt file, and on a model that always thinks the Engine ends the run at its first step. On a model that can switch, the Gateway would send the vendor's own off setting. Claude Sonnet 5.5 counts as able to switch, though it rejects the off value `disabled`: the nearest value it has is `between_tools` (no thinking before the reply, and short notes between tool calls), which the Gateway would send, reporting the replacement in `gateway_warning`, a reply field. Nothing that a prompt can read changes, so this is translation and row 22 stays "Gateway only". For rows 28, 30, and 44 (Table 3) the Gateway changes what was asked: it would drop a temperature that a model rejects and let the model choose where it refuses a forced tool call, reporting both in `gateway_warning`, and it would refuse a prefill that a model rejects.
- **A prompt can already find out its provider today, and through it usually its vendor.** The model handle, the Lua object a prompt gets for a model, has a read-only `provider` field that holds a provider id (source: `decisions/models-and-conversations.md` in `runvil-harness`). A prompt could test it and behave differently for each provider. None of the 105 features requires a prompt to read that field, and whether to keep it is decision 8 in section 8.

## 3. The feature tables: 105 vendor features, what OpenRouter does with each, and what Runvil needs

This section lists the 105 features. Most are things that vendors' APIs do in their own way, a few belong to local servers, and three are about OpenRouter and providers (rows 20, 39, and 93). They are in ten groups, each with a subsection and a table: reasoning data that a vendor expects back (3.1, rows 1 to 20), reasoning settings and sampling (3.2, rows 21 to 30), prompt caching (3.3, rows 31 to 41), tools (3.4, rows 42 to 54), structured output and message layout (3.5, rows 55 to 60), vendor-run tools, citations, files, and media (3.6, rows 61 to 73), conversations, jobs, and context handling on the vendor's side (3.7, rows 74 to 83), finish reasons, errors, usage, and streaming (3.8, rows 84 to 93), model discovery, routing, and pass-throughs for vendor settings (3.9, rows 94 to 100), and vendors with OpenAI-style APIs and local servers (3.10, rows 101 to 105).

**The parts of Runvil that the tables name.** The Gateway is Runvil's server, the client is the HTTP library that sends it each model request, the Engine runs a prompt (whose code is Lua code), and the Host is the program that embeds the Engine and picks the models. The universal API is the planned format between the client and the Gateway: OpenAI's chat completions format plus a few optional extra fields. The opaque field is one of them, for data that a vendor expects back unchanged. Neither is built: today the Gateway forwards a request as it is, apart from one conversion for models that write tool calls as text (the tool dialect), with a `gateway_warning` field when it fails. The notes use the names in "Terms used in this report" without explaining them again.

**How each subsection is laid out.** An introduction says what the group is and what its special terms mean. Then comes a summary table with one row for each feature, and under it a note for each row, headed "Row" with the row's number and the feature's name.

**The columns of a summary table.** There are six.

- **Row**: the feature's number, 1 to 105 across the ten tables and the same everywhere in this report.
- **Feature**: a short name and one sentence on what the feature is.
- **Vendors**: the vendors that have it. "All" means every vendor, and a few cells name a provider, a local server, or OpenRouter.
- **What OpenRouter does with it**: what a caller of OpenRouter sends or receives for the feature, and on which of its three routes (URL paths): chat completions, Messages (Anthropic's format), or Responses (the format of OpenAI's newer Responses API). OpenRouter is a commercial service that does the job Runvil's Gateway is meant to do, so its handling of each feature is the first comparison. Runvil's requests do not go through it.
- **What Runvil needs**: starts with an option in bold and then says what the Gateway, the client, or the Engine would have to do. Section 1 defines the six options, and four occur, here from best to worst. "Gateway only": the Gateway translates, and no code outside it knows the vendor. "Opaque field": the client and the Engine also have to store the opaque field and send it back. "Compatibility code": code in the Gateway changes what a request asks for, or repairs what one vendor, model, or local server gets wrong. "Separate vendor path": the client or the Engine would have to speak one vendor's own format, around the universal API. Two phrases can follow the option:
  - "With no new client or Engine work", after "Opaque field", is about the opaque field alone: the row reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once, for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor.
  - "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work does not need: a tool loop with reasoning on Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google models, on vendors with OpenAI-style APIs, and on local servers. A row without the mark is part of that work or is a vendor behavior that it runs into.
- **Confidence**: High, Medium, or Low: how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The note gives the reason.

**The lines of a note.** Each note has the same nine labeled lines: Vendors, What the vendor does, What OpenRouter does with it, What a caller loses through OpenRouter, What Runvil needs, New vendor-neutral setting needed, Where the answer comes from, Confidence, and Evidence items. The setting line says whether the universal API needs a new setting or field that means the same for every vendor: Yes, No, or Optional, which means that the feature works without one and Runvil could add one to give prompts control. "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note. It starts with the main source, one of five kinds (section 1): Stated by OpenRouter, Inferred about OpenRouter, Taken from another open-source project (where OpenRouter's material is silent), Stated by the vendor, or From earlier Runvil research. It then names any project whose code handles the feature in the way proposed for Runvil.

**To find the rows for one vendor:**

- **xAI:** rows 6, 22, 28, 34, 36, 61, 73, 74, 101

### 3.1 Reasoning data that a vendor expects back: every vendor's version fits the one opaque field

This group is about reasoning data that a vendor sends in a reply and expects back in later requests of the same conversation. The data comes signed or encrypted, and then has to go back unchanged, or as plain-text reasoning: readable text in a message field of the vendor's own, such as `reasoning_content`. Runvil plans to put all of it into the one opaque field. OpenRouter returns reasoning in two fields of the assistant message. In `reasoning_details`, a reasoning entry holds readable text, a summary, or an encrypted value, and a `format` value that names the vendor format it came in, such as `anthropic-claude-v1`. `reasoning` holds the reasoning text as one plain string.

A reply in a vendor's own protocol is an ordered list of blocks, and Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google depend on that order when the reply is sent back. OpenRouter's chat completions reply has separate fields for reasoning, text, and tool calls, so it does not show the order, and whether that ever breaks a request is not known (row 3). The opaque field holds the reply's whole list of blocks in the vendor's order, so the Gateway sends them back in that order.

Terms used in this subsection: A tool call is a tool use in Anthropic's API and a function call in OpenAI's and Google's. Google calls one model reply inside a tool loop a step. The verification round was a second, independent round of evidence gathering that re-read the most important claims from their live sources.

**How to read Table 5.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 5. Reasoning data that a vendor expects back: 20 features.*

|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Signed thinking blocks that must be sent back exactly.** Claude's reasoning arrives in blocks that Anthropic signs, and Anthropic rejects a request that sends them back changed, reordered, or with one missing from the middle. | Anthropic | It returns each thinking block, with its signature, as one entry in `reasoning_details` and tells the caller to send the list back unchanged. | **Opaque field.** The Gateway needs an Anthropic translator that puts the reply's blocks into the opaque field, and the client and the Engine have to store the field and send it back without reading it. | High |
| 2 | **Thinking blocks returned only in encrypted form (redacted thinking).** When Anthropic redacts part of Claude's reasoning for safety, it returns a block that holds only an encrypted value, and the caller has to send that block back unchanged. | Anthropic | It returns the block as an encrypted entry in `reasoning_details`. **Notes on Table 5, row by row.** One note for each row, in row order.

#### Row 1. Signed thinking blocks that must be sent back exactly

- **Vendors:** Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** Claude's reasoning arrives in `thinking` blocks, each with a `signature`. Blocks that are sent back have to be complete and in their original order, including blocks whose text is empty: Anthropic answers with HTTP 400 when a block was edited or moved, or when one is missing between two that are sent. Anthropic accepts a request in which the thinking of earlier turns is left out entirely, and Claude then answers without that reasoning. It requires the thinking blocks of the current tool loop back, and what its API answers when those are missing was not found.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the reply has `message.reasoning_details[]`, with one entry of type `reasoning.text` for each thinking block. Each entry has `text`, `signature`, and `format: "anthropic-claude-v1"`. OpenRouter's rule for sending it back is: "the entire sequence of consecutive reasoning blocks must match the outputs generated by the model".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The reply does not show where each block was among the text and tool calls (row 3). Also, when a caller sends back only `reasoning`, without `reasoning_details`, OpenRouter builds unsigned thinking from it, which Anthropic rejects.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field.** The Gateway needs an Anthropic translator that returns the reply's block list in the opaque field and sends it back to Anthropic in later requests. The universal API gains the field. The client has to copy it from the final chunk of the streamed reply and send it back with the assistant message. The Engine has to store it on the assistant record where Lua cannot see it, have the projection write it onto that message in later requests, and remove it after a compaction (row 4).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for what it returns. Runvil's answer follows the model library rig and the harnesses Hermes Agent and Agno, which store the whole block list and send it back unchanged. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-11-17 report, which designed the field.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic documents the rule and those three projects meet it. No request was run through a Gateway built this way.
- **Evidence items:** R36, R52, AN7, AN8, AN9, H50, R73, AN6

#### Row 2. Thinking blocks returned only in encrypted form (redacted thinking)

- **Vendors:** Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** A `redacted_thinking` block holds reasoning that Anthropic redacted for safety, as an encrypted value in its `data` field. It goes back under the same rule as a signed thinking block (row 1).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the block arrives as one `reasoning_details` entry of type `reasoning.encrypted`, with the encrypted value in its `data` field.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A type of its own: Anthropic's block shares the `reasoning.encrypted` entry type with OpenAI's and Gemini's encrypted values, so only the entry's `format` value says whose it is.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway has to keep the `redacted_thinking` block in the reply's block list inside the opaque field and send it back with the other blocks.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: its documentation names Anthropic's redacted thinking as one source of `reasoning.encrypted` entries.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic and OpenRouter both document the block, and the Gateway would handle it exactly as a signed thinking block (row 1).
- **Evidence items:** R37, AN2, AN8, R74

#### Row 3. The order of reasoning, text, and tool calls inside one reply

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (interleaved thinking, its mode in which thinking blocks appear between the tool calls of one reply), OpenAI, Google
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic rejects a thinking block that was moved (row 1), and its prefix check (row 4) counts "changing text before a thinking block in its own message" as a change. OpenAI's documentation says to keep a reply's output items "in their original order", and from GPT-6.2 on OpenAI drops reasoning when content before it was inserted or edited (row 9). Google wants each signature back on the part it came with (row 10).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The reply's fields `reasoning_details`, `content`, and `tool_calls` are separate, with no field in OpenRouter's API specification for the order between them. Its documentation does not say in what order it sends them to the vendor.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Whether the missing order breaks anything is not known: no failure through OpenRouter is on record, and every recorded Claude reply has one thinking block ahead of its text and tool calls, which went back successfully. Open-source gateways with the same three fields do lose the order. agentgateway and Bifrost rebuild a reply as reasoning first, then text, then tool calls. Envoy AI Gateway (now named Agent Router) keeps only the first text block and the last thinking block. Portkey's AI Gateway moves tool uses to the end. LiteLLM also rebuilds the order, and in real use its rebuild produced Anthropic's "cannot be modified" 400 error.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. Apart from the field, the client has to return the text that arrives with tool calls and the Engine has to store it, a change that is the same for every vendor. Today the client drops that text, so the Engine's record has empty content, and the Gateway builds a message from its text and tool calls when it cannot send the field back (rows 5 and 19). The Gateway has to return the reply's block list in the vendor's order inside the opaque field and send it back as it is.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** What OpenRouter does is inferred about OpenRouter, from its API specification and from recorded traffic. Runvil's answer is taken from other open-source projects: the five gateways above lose the order, and the model library rig keeps it by storing the vendor's raw blocks. LLM Gateway, a commercial service like OpenRouter, keeps it by adding a `block_index` value to each reasoning entry.
- **Confidence:** High - every gateway examined that splits a reply into fields by type and records no position loses the order, which the verification round confirmed, and a Gateway that stores the vendor's list whole, as rig does, has no order to rebuild.
- **Evidence items:** AN13, J9, J30, J74, J86, J100, K6, K7, K57, Q31, VF16, H50, WT13, OA26, OA27, GG4, L33, L20

#### Row 4. Thinking blocks that are valid only while the earlier conversation is unchanged (prefix-bound thinking)

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (seven Claude models: Fable 5.1 and 5.2, Mythos 5.1 and 5.2, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, and Haiku 5.5)
- **What the vendor does:** A thinking block is valid only while the prefix before it (`system`, `tools`, and every earlier message) is unchanged since Claude produced it. JSON formatting and key order do not count as a change. When the check fails, Fable 5.2, Mythos 5.2, and Mythos 5.1 answer with HTTP 400, and Anthropic lists a 400 as possible on the other four. When the request sets `thinking.block_binding.prefix_mismatch_behavior: "drop_block"`, the API drops the block and the request succeeds.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route OpenRouter passes the upstream's HTTP 400 through and "does not drop thinking blocks for you". The `drop_block` setting exists only on its Messages route. So does Anthropic's `input_transformations` report, a list in the reply of what Anthropic changed in the request, such as a thinking block it dropped.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A caller on the chat completions route cannot send the setting and does not get the report.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field.** The Gateway has to send `drop_block` to Anthropic. Its translator also has to build the same request from the same conversation every time: Anthropic warns gateways not to move a system message and not to reorder, merge, or split blocks. By the Engine's decision page, a prompt can already add records only at the end, except inside a compactor. After each successful compaction the Engine has to remove the opaque field from the records that remain, because their thinking blocks no longer match what comes before them. The 2026-11-17 design has that removal among the field's rules (row 1). With `drop_block` Anthropic would drop those blocks itself, but the Gateway cannot send the setting in every request: Anthropic answers a request that sets it while thinking is off with HTTP 400.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its own routes, in its guides for these Claude models and its API specification. For Runvil's answer: the harnesses Goose, Zed, pi, and opencode and the model library rig send `drop_block`. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-11-17 report, for those five projects and the rule for compaction.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Anthropic documents the rule and five projects send `drop_block`, but no request was run. The Gateway writes JSON object keys in sorted order, and the check covers everything before a block, so it passes only if Anthropic ignores key order, as its documentation says it does.
- **Evidence items:** R62, AN3, AN10, AN12, AN4, AN6

#### Row 5. Reasoning data that works only in the account or at the endpoint that produced it

- **Vendors:** Anthropic: thinking from the Claude models Sonnet 5.5, Haiku 5.5, Fable 5.2, and Mythos 5.2 works only in the organization (the customer's account at the vendor) that produced it. OpenAI: reasoning is tied to the organization and the model family. Google: Vertex AI scopes a signature to "the same conversation history, model, and Google Cloud project".
- **What the vendor does:** A signed or encrypted reasoning value is readable only where it was made. When it is sent anywhere else unchanged, Anthropic and OpenAI drop it without an error. Gemini rejects the request that continues a tool call: OpenRouter's staff confirmed an HTTP 400 when such a request moves between Google AI Studio, where Google serves Gemini directly, and Vertex AI.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** When a request sends reasoning blocks back, OpenRouter routes it only to a provider that can read them.
