When the caller's request rules out every such provider, it answers with HTTP 400. Separately, the signatures and encrypted values in OpenRouter's examples and in recorded traffic end with a base64 suffix. Decoded, it is an `endpoint_slug` that names the dated version of the model and the provider that produced the value.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Provider fallback for conversations that send reasoning back. Also, the value the caller stores is OpenRouter's wrapper around the vendor's value, not the vendor's own bytes.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway has to write the endpoint that produced the blocks into the label on the opaque field, one value more than the 2026-11-17 design stores. When the label does not match where a request is going, the Gateway has to rebuild the message from its text and tool calls (row 3), which leaves the reasoning out. With Anthropic that is known to work between turns and is untested in the middle of a tool loop (row 1). Gemini then needs the placeholder signature of row 12.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the routing rule. Inferred about OpenRouter for the suffix: it was decoded from 22 values in documentation examples, and from recorded traffic. It arrived vendor by vendor: the earliest recorded OpenAI value with it is dated 2026-08-04 on the Responses route and 2026-09-22 on the chat completions route, and the earliest Claude signature 2026-09-29, on the chat completions route. All 34 recorded requests that sent such a value back were answered by the OpenRouter endpoint that the suffix names. Runvil's label follows the model library rig, which labels each reply with the protocol, the provider, and the model. The gateway Bifrost instead strips the reasoning and retries once when the vendor refuses it. The model library Pydantic AI sends ids of data stored at the vendor back only to an endpoint that shares that data.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the vendors differ on what "account" means (an organization, or a Google Cloud project on Vertex AI). Two of the Gateway's endpoints in one account have different labels, so the Gateway can leave out reasoning that the vendor would have read.
- **Evidence items:** R63, AN5, AN11, R41, OA15, OA27, G37, GG22, H27, H51, J39, V21, VF1, VV7, VV18, WT2 to WT7, F45, VT8

#### Row 6. Encrypted reasoning items that exist only in a vendor's Responses API

- **Vendors:** OpenAI, and Azure OpenAI (OpenAI's models served by Microsoft Azure). Also xAI and Meta, which have Responses APIs of their own: xAI's chat completions route has "no field for the ciphertext" (the encrypted reasoning), and Meta's documentation says its chat completions route does not keep reasoning from one request to the next.
- **What the vendor does:** In OpenAI's Responses API, reasoning arrives as reasoning items, each with an `encrypted_content` value. The items arrive in order, next to the reply's function calls. Only the vendor's Responses route has them.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the reasoning arrives as `reasoning_details` entries of type `reasoning.summary` and `reasoning.encrypted`. An encrypted entry has `data` (the encrypted value), an `id` like `rs_...`, and `format: "openai-responses-v1"`, or `xai-responses-v1` or `meta-responses-v1`. Its documentation says: "OpenRouter automatically requests encrypted reasoning content". It rejects a request in which the same entry appears twice.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The reply does not show where one item ends and the next begins, or each item's position among the reply's other output items when there are several.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway needs a translator for the Responses API that returns the reply's item list, in order, in the opaque field and sends it back in later requests. Whether the same translator can serve xAI's and Meta's Responses APIs was not examined.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the layout of the entries. Inferred about OpenRouter that it calls the vendor's Responses API when it forwards the request. Three open-source gateways do the translation Runvil needs, serving a chat completions caller from the Responses API: LiteLLM with its chat-to-Responses bridge, Bifrost, and new-api. The model libraries rig and the Vercel AI SDK keep the items.
- **Confidence:** Medium - this is a second translator, after the one for Anthropic (row 1), and Runvil has not designed it.
- **Evidence items:** R35, R58, OA13, OA25, OA9, OV14, OV65, K16, K17, J35, H19, H54, J95

#### Row 7. Tool calls together with reasoning on OpenAI's newest models

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-5.4 and later, and the GPT-6 family)
- **What the vendor does:** From GPT-5.4 on, OpenAI's Chat Completions API "does not support tool calling with `reasoning_effort` values other than `none`", where `none` turns reasoning off. On five GPT-6 models (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6.1 Terra, GPT-6.2 Astra, GPT-6.2 Sol) "tool calling requires Responses" (the Responses API). Four of the five also reject the effort value `none`. GPT-6.1 Terra accepts `none` and still needs the Responses API for tools. OpenAI states this as a limitation and gives no HTTP status or error text for a request that breaks it.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's model catalog lists `tools` and the chat completions route for these models, so it must convert a chat completions request with tools into a Responses API request.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller cannot tell which of OpenAI's APIs ran the request.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway needs the translator for OpenAI's Responses API (row 6) before a tool loop with reasoning can run on these models, and before any tool loop can run on the five GPT-6 models. Sending the reasoning back between rounds is a separate feature (row 6). Today the client sends tools and no effort value and the Gateway forwards the request unchanged, so the model uses its default: `none` on GPT-5.4, where tools still work, and `medium` from GPT-5.5 on, where OpenAI says chat completions does not support the request. This follows from OpenAI's per-model defaults, and no such request was run.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** The rule is stated by the vendor, and was fetched again in the verification round. What OpenRouter does is inferred about OpenRouter, from its model catalog.
- **Confidence:** High - it is OpenAI's own rule, a second fetch confirmed it, and OpenAI itself says to "Use Responses for reasoning with tools".
- **Evidence items:** OA35, OA36, OA37, OA9, VV1, L4, L13

#### Row 8. Commentary and final-answer labels on several messages in one reply (`phase`)

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-5.3 Codex, and GPT-5.4 and later)
- **What the vendor does:** In OpenAI's Responses API a reply can hold several assistant message items. Each has a `phase` value: `commentary` for an intermediate remark, or `final_answer`. The caller has to send each `phase` back with its message. Without it the model can take a commentary message for the final answer and stop early.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter supports `phase` on its Responses route only. Its documentation says: "The Chat Completions format cannot represent multiple output items with distinct phases in a single response".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** On the chat completions route, the labels and the split of the reply into separate messages. OpenRouter does not say what happens to the commentary text.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway's translator for the Responses API (row 6) has to keep the reply's item list, with each item's `phase`, in the opaque field. It has to put the final answer's text into the ordinary message text that the client and prompts read. The commentary messages stay only in the item list.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its own routes, in its GPT-5.4 guide and its API specification. For Runvil's answer: the gateway LiteLLM stores a map of the items' order, with each message's `phase`, in a field named `openai_responses_replay`. The model library Vercel AI SDK stores a `phase` on each text part of a message.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the only evidence that the `phase` values can be kept this way is those two open-source projects.
- **Evidence items:** OA40, OA41, K17, H20

#### Row 9. Reasoning that the vendor drops after the conversation's history was edited

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-6.2 and later), Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI drops reasoning that the caller sends back after a switch to another model family and, from GPT-6.2 on, after an edit to earlier history or a change of `safety_identifier`, the request field that identifies the end user to OpenAI. It lists each drop in the `input_transformations` array of its reply. Anthropic reports the thinking blocks it dropped in an array of the same name (row 4).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter returns the report only in the results of its Responses route and its Messages route.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A caller on the chat completions route is not told that reasoning was dropped.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to make an end-user identifier and send the vendor the same one in every request of a conversation. What it is made from is not designed (row 98), and a value that is fixed for a caller needs no record of conversations. Today Runvil sends none. The Gateway should also write the vendor's report to its log, and would not pass it to the client. The Engine already avoids edits to earlier history: by its decision page it adds records only at the end, except in a compaction.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its routes, in its API specification, and stated by the vendor for the drops and the report, in OpenAI's and Anthropic's documentation.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the OpenAI rule is five days old: its changelog dates it 2026-11-17, and the sources were read on 2026-11-22.
- **Evidence items:** OA27, OA21, AN4, AN12, L3, L4

#### Row 10. Google's signatures on tool calls (thought signatures)

- **Vendors:** Google (Gemini 3 and 4)
- **What the vendor does:** A Gemini tool call is a `functionCall` part, with its thought signature in the part's `thoughtSignature` field. The first `functionCall` part of each step in the current turn has to go back with its `thoughtSignature`. Otherwise Google answers with HTTP 400.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the signature arrives as a `reasoning_details` entry of type `reasoning.encrypted` with `format: "google-gemini-v1"`. The tool call object in `tool_calls` stays plain.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The reply does not show which part the signature belongs to, and OpenRouter does not document how it puts each signature back on its part.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway needs a Gemini translator that keeps the reply's ordered list of parts, signatures included, in the opaque field and sends that list back in later requests.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for what it returns, in its API specification and in the source of its official client library. For Runvil's answer: open-source projects get the signature back onto its part in six ways. Two keep it with the part: data attached to each part (the model library Vercel AI SDK and the harness opencode) and a block beside the part that holds only the signature (the model library TensorZero). The gateways agentgateway, Bifrost, and LiteLLM hide it inside the tool-call id. A caller sends tool-call ids back anyway, so that way needs no opaque field, but it makes ids of up to 4,423 characters, which break clients that normalize ids. Three more ways are in use.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Runvil has not designed this translator, and with six ways in use there is no settled practice to copy.
- **Evidence items:** GG1 to GG5, GG17, GG19, K10, K12, K61, J10, J31, H14, GX29

#### Row 11. Thought signatures on parts that are not function calls

- **Vendors:** Google
- **What the vendor does:** These are signatures on a text part, the final part of a reply, or an image part. Google accepts a request that leaves them out, but says the model's later answers can get worse without them. One case is strict: when a request combines Google's built-in tools (its vendor-run tools) with the caller's own functions, every part of the reply is signed and all of them have to be sent back.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route OpenRouter returns these signatures in `reasoning_details` too (row 10). When its official client library sends a conversation back, it removes Gemini's readable reasoning text from the array, because Google answers "Corrupted thought signature" when that text comes back changed, and it keeps the encrypted entries, which hold the signatures.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Possibly the part that each signature belongs to: the array does not record it, and OpenRouter does not document how it puts each signature back on its part.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway's translator for Gemini (row 10) has to put the reply's ordered list of parts, signatures included, into the opaque field and send that list back to Google in later requests.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in the source code of its official client library, for what it sends back. Stated by the vendor, in Google's documentation, for the rule.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Google documents the rule, but most of the open-source gateways examined, LiteLLM and Envoy AI Gateway among them, send back only the first signature of a reply.
- **Evidence items:** GG7, GG13, GG20, J31, K12, GG17, GG18, GG19, J89

#### Row 12. A placeholder signature for tool calls that did not come from Gemini

- **Vendors:** Google
- **What the vendor does:** A function call that another model made has no thought signature, which Gemini expects (row 10). For such calls Google documents a fixed value, `skip_thought_signature_validator`, sent in the place of the signature, as a last resort that "will negatively impact model performance".
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's documentation does not mention the placeholder value.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown. Either OpenRouter inserts the placeholder itself or it lets Google's HTTP 400 error reach the caller.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** The Gateway's translator for Gemini (row 10) has to put the placeholder on the first function call of each step, in about twenty lines of code, when the assistant message has no opaque field or the label on the field does not match where the request is going.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from other open-source projects, for the fix, because OpenRouter's material is silent. Five projects insert a placeholder signature on such calls: the gateways LiteLLM, Envoy AI Gateway, and new-api, the model library Vercel AI SDK, and Gemini CLI, Google's own harness. new-api sends a second value that Google documents, `context_engineering_is_the_way_to_go`. The value is stated by the vendor, in Google's documentation.
- **Confidence:** High - Google documents the value, and five projects do the same thing.
- **Evidence items:** GG8, GG24, GG28, GG30, GG31, K13, H15, J89, J96

#### Row 13. Answers longer than one request allows (Gemini 4 long decode)

- **Vendors:** Google (Gemini 4)
- **What the vendor does:** Google calls producing a longer answer over several requests long decode, and each request in the series a hop. When the output reaches the per-request cap, the reply ends with `finishReason: CONTINUATION` and a `continuationToken`. The next request repeats the same contents and adds the token.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the reply ends with `finish_reason: "length"` and `native_finish_reason: "CONTINUATION"`, the vendor's own stop value. The token arrives in `reasoning_details` as an entry of type `reasoning.encrypted` with `id: "gemini-continuation"`. The caller runs the loop itself: it appends the stopped reply to the conversation as the last assistant message and sends the request again. OpenRouter removes that message before it forwards the request to Google, and sends the request only to the provider that issued the token.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller has to store a token that OpenRouter says "can reach several MB" and send it back on each hop, and it gets the usage of each hop separately.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway should run the hops inside one request from the client and return one reply. It has to add up the usage of the hops, and the Engine's precheck needs the first hop's prompt-token count in place of the sum, which counts the input once for each hop. LiteLLM and the Vercel AI SDK report the sum in the usual fields and that count in an extra field. Whether Runvil does the same is not decided, and an extra field would be the same for every vendor. This applies only when a prompt sets `max_tokens` above the per-request cap of 65,536 tokens. A reply that stops because it used up its own `max_tokens` budget stays an ordinary stop with the finish reason `length`.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, for the loop it leaves to the caller. Google's own client libraries for Python and JavaScript, LiteLLM, and the Vercel AI SDK run the hops inside the library and hand the application one reply, which shows the loop can be hidden from the application.
- **Confidence:** Medium - those four libraries run the hops this way, but what each hop costs, and how a long request behaves if the client receives nothing meanwhile, were not tested.
- **Evidence items:** GG41 to GG50, O59, GX46 to GX56, CT7, CT9, CT16, CT26, GG23, L29

#### Row 14. Plain-text reasoning that must be sent back

- **Vendors:** DeepSeek (when `tools` is present), Moonshot Kimi K3, Alibaba Qwen (`preserve_thinking`), Zhipu GLM (`clear_thinking: false`), MiniMax
- **What the vendor does:** These vendors with OpenAI-style APIs return plain-text reasoning in `reasoning_content` and expect that field on the assistant message in later requests. DeepSeek requires it when the request has `tools` and answers a tool conversation that lacks it with HTTP 400. Alibaba's Qwen reads it when its `preserve_thinking` setting is on, and Zhipu's GLM when the request sets `clear_thinking: false`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller may send the reasoning back in `reasoning`, in `reasoning_details`, or in `reasoning_content`, which OpenRouter calls "an alias". In its replies OpenRouter uses `reasoning` and `reasoning_details` entries of type `reasoning.text` whose `format` value is `unknown`. The key `reasoning_content` is in none of 911 recorded replies, these vendors' models among them. OpenRouter's documentation does not say that OpenRouter supplies the field for a caller that left it out, and DeepSeek's HTTP 400 has reached callers through OpenRouter.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller is not told the rule: OpenRouter's documentation does not mention it.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. Today the client and the Engine send no reasoning back. The Gateway has to put the reply's reasoning into the opaque field and write it back into the vendor's own field in later requests. That puts the opaque field to use on endpoints whose protocol is `openai`, which the Gateway reaches in plain chat completions format. Where it has no reasoning to write (no opaque field, or a label that does not match) and the vendor rejects a request without the field, it has to send the field with a single space, as LiteLLM does for DeepSeek and Moonshot. The space fills a required field and changes nothing that the request asks for, so it is translation and not compatibility code.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter, from recorded traffic and from issue reports. For the Gateway's handling: pi and opencode send the reasoning back, and LiteLLM calls its single space the smallest value DeepSeek accepts.
- **Confidence:** High - DeepSeek documents the rule, so a tool loop that Runvil forwards to DeepSeek fails today. This was deduced from the code, not run.
- **Evidence items:** OV22, OV23, OV28, OV40, OV48, OV54, R53, R59, K22, WT20, VV19, OV29, L29, L116

#### Row 15. Mistral replies whose content is a list of reasoning and text chunks

- **Vendors:** Mistral (its own chat route, `/v1/chat/completions`)
- **What the vendor does:** On Mistral's own chat route, when reasoning is on, `content` is a list of chunks and not a string: `ThinkChunk` entries hold the model's reasoning and `TextChunk` entries hold the answer. A `ThinkChunk` can have an optional `signature`. In a streamed reply `delta.content` is such a list while the model reasons and a plain string once the answer starts. Mistral's documentation says to send the whole assistant message back in later requests, `ThinkChunk` entries included, because dropping them "degrades model performance".
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter returns the reasoning in `reasoning` and in `reasoning_details`, and `content` stays a string. Its list of allowed `format` values names other vendors only, so an entry from Mistral would get the value `unknown`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Possibly the signature: OpenRouter does not document whether the signature on a chunk survives.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, plus a **Gateway only** change to the form of the reply. The Gateway has to give the client plain answer text in `content` and the reasoning in a reasoning field that the client reads today (row 17). That changes only the layout of the reply, so it is translation and not compatibility code. It also has to put the original chunks into the opaque field and send them back to Mistral in later requests. Today a reply whose `content` is an array fails in the client with the error `MalformedResponse`.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor, for the chunk list and the request to send it back. Inferred about OpenRouter, from the list of `format` values in its API specification.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Mistral documents the chunk list for its own chat route only, and its OpenAI-compatible route, `/openai/v1/chat/completions`, is in public preview and can change.
- **Evidence items:** OV31, OV35, L106

#### Row 16. A reasoning summary together with encrypted reasoning

- **Vendors:** Alibaba (`qwen4-max`)
- **What the vendor does:** When a request sets `thinking_display` to "summarized", `qwen4-max` returns a readable summary in place of its full reasoning, plus the reasoning itself as an encrypted value in a field named `encrypted_content`. Alibaba's documentation says the encrypted value "is for restoring historical thinking" and that the client "can only pass ... back as-is".
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Not documented. If OpenRouter returned the encrypted value in `reasoning_details`, the entry's `format` value would be `unknown`, because its list of `format` values has no value for Alibaba.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown: nothing shows whether the encrypted value passes through OpenRouter intact.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The Gateway has to put what Alibaba returned into the opaque field and send it back to Alibaba unchanged in later requests.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor, in Alibaba's documentation, for what the model returns.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the only source is one page of Alibaba's documentation.
- **Evidence items:** OV49

#### Row 17. Plain-text reasoning that local servers return under three field names

- **Vendors:** llama.cpp (field `reasoning_content`), vLLM (field `reasoning`, renamed from `reasoning_content`), Ollama (field `thinking`), and providers that serve open-weight models
- **What the vendor does:** Each server returns the reasoning text in its own field. The text can also arrive inside the reply text, wrapped in `<think>` tags, and today the Gateway passes such a reply through unchanged. Some models want the reasoning sent back between tool calls, depending on the model's chat template: the templates for gpt-oss (OpenAI's open-weight model), Kimi, Qwen3, and GLM expect it back.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter does not serve local servers. Its upstreams return reasoning in a `reasoning` field, in a `reasoning_content` field, or inside the text, and it converts them all to `message.reasoning` plus entries of type `reasoning.text` in `reasoning_details`. On input it also accepts `reasoning_content` (row 14).
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Only the name of the field that the upstream server used.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, to send the reasoning back. Reading the reasoning in a reply is **Gateway only**. The Gateway has to put the reply's reasoning into the opaque field and write it into later requests under the field name that the server's reply used (as in row 14). For reading, today the Gateway forwards the field under the server's name and the client reads all three names. The Gateway would convert the name to one of the three, so the client needs no change. That can wait until a third-party client reads the Gateway's replies.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, for the form it returns. Taken from other open-source projects, for the field names: the source code of llama.cpp, vLLM, and Ollama. From earlier Runvil research, a reading of the Gateway's and the client's code, for today's behavior.
- **Confidence:** High - the field names and Runvil's present behavior were read from source code.
- **Evidence items:** OV67, OV73, OV77, R44, L25, L24, L26

#### Row 18. How reasoning data that a vendor expects back arrives in a streamed reply

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** For example, Anthropic sends `thinking_delta` events and then one `signature_delta`, and Google sends the signature last, sometimes on a part that is otherwise empty.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter streams fragments of `reasoning_details` in `delta.reasoning_details`, and the client has to join them. Its documentation gives the rule for the chat completions route in four bullets and no code: "The complete reasoning sequence is built by concatenating all chunks in order", without saying whether `index` or `id` tells which fragments belong to one entry. OpenRouter's official client library joins consecutive fragments of the same type and starts a new entry when the type changes, and it says the `index` field "cannot be used to reassemble the array". The model libraries rig and LangChain join fragments that have the same type and `index`. Of the fifteen special cases that OpenRouter's client library has for reasoning data, nine are bug fixes dated 2026. One known way of losing reasoning data, for entries that arrive after the answer text has started, was still unfixed on 2026-11-22.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A signature can arrive after the answer text has started, so the caller has to keep collecting until the stream ends.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, by design choice. Unlike OpenRouter, the Gateway should assemble the vendor's list of blocks itself and send it once, whole, in the opaque field on the final chunk of the streamed reply. The client then copies that one value and needs no per-vendor code for joining fragments.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation and in the source code of its official client library, for how fragments are joined. Inferred about OpenRouter, from that library's history of fixes, that joining in the client fails often.
- **Confidence:** High - joining the fragments in the client is where OpenRouter's own client library kept breaking, and a Gateway that sends the list whole leaves the client nothing to join.
- **Evidence items:** R45, R46, R47, F1, F2, F3, F59, F60, K72, VF6, VT9, F67

#### Row 19. Switching model or provider in the middle of a conversation

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** Given reasoning data that it did not produce, Anthropic drops blocks from another model or account without an error, OpenAI removes reasoning items that came from another model family or organization, and Gemini rejects a request that lacks its signature on a function call (row 10).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter does not document the general case. The two cases it documents are in rows 5 and 13.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, plus two pieces of compatibility code: Gemini's placeholder signature (row 12) and tool-call ids rewritten into the format the target vendor accepts (row 46). Apart from the opaque field, the client has to return the text that arrives with tool calls and the Engine has to store it (row 3), a change that is the same for every vendor. When the label on the opaque field does not match where a request is going, the Gateway should rebuild the message from that text and the tool calls, without the reasoning. Anthropic accepts that between turns, and it is untested in the middle of a tool loop, where Anthropic's documentation requires the blocks back (row 1). pi and Goose drop the readable reasoning text in the same way, and rig and opencode pass it on as plain reasoning text. The comparison has to be made in the Gateway, because Workshop can pick a different model for each round while the Engine's binding keeps the model from the start of the run.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from other open-source projects, for the switch, because OpenRouter documents no general rule. pi drops another model's reasoning: "Thinking from another model is dropped". Goose replays only reasoning that the current model produced, and retries once without reasoning when the vendor rejects it. rig keeps a vendor's own items only on an exact match of protocol, provider, and model. For any other reply it clears the vendor's items and passes the reasoning on ("reasoning becomes plain text, or stays reasoning when the request asks for it and the model reads it"), dropping only redacted or empty reasoning. opencode, when the model differs, sends readable reasoning on as `{ type: "reasoning", text: item.text }` without its vendor metadata, and drops it only when it has no text. Pydantic AI turns it into ordinary text wrapped in thinking tags, and an issue report describes Claude then printing its reasoning to users for more than 90 turns. The issue names two triggers, an unsigned part and a part from another provider, and does not say which one caused the 90 turns.
- **Confidence:** Medium - every project examined withholds one vendor's signed or encrypted data from another model, but they split on whether to drop the readable reasoning text or pass it on as plain text, and the one that wraps it in tags has a reported failure.
- **Evidence items:** R83, H51, H61, H65, H69, H72, G27, VT12, AN5, OA27, GG4, R64, AN6, R73

#### Row 20. OpenRouter's own data inside `reasoning_details`

- **Vendors:** OpenRouter (not a vendor)
- **What the vendor does:** No vendor is involved: OpenRouter itself produces and reads this data.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter also puts data for three features of its own into `reasoning_details`, in entries of type `reasoning.encrypted` or `reasoning.server_tool_call`: its record of which deferred tools a conversation has loaded (a deferred tool is one whose definition is held back from the model until a search finds it), its records of calls to tools that OpenRouter runs on its own servers, and "the model's internal video navigation steps", which it returns "as encrypted reasoning rather than as tool calls". If the caller drops such entries, OpenRouter answers with HTTP 400 and a code such as `deferred_tools_state_missing`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A client that filters out reasoning entries breaks tool search, OpenRouter's feature for finding deferred tools, because its record is among those entries.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The opaque field can also hold data of the Gateway's own. The client would send it back with each request, so the Gateway would stay stateless and store nothing about a conversation between requests. The planned work has no such data and needs no code for this. The first use would be the Gateway's record of loaded tools, if Runvil adopts tool search (row 52).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation, for what it stores.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter, a gateway too, documents keeping its own data this way, with named error codes for a request that lacks it.
- **Evidence items:** R42, P50, P51

### 3.2 Reasoning settings and sampling: one effort setting works for every vendor, but some models refuse "thinking off" or a temperature

This group of features is about the request settings that control a model's reasoning: how much of it to do, whether to do it at all, a token budget for it (a cap on how many tokens the model may spend on reasoning), and how much of it comes back in the reply. It also covers a required output limit, prefill, and the sampling settings `temperature`, `top_p`, and `top_k`, which control how randomly the model picks each next word (rows 21 to 30).

OpenRouter shows that one vendor-neutral effort setting can be converted into every vendor's own reasoning control, provided the gateway knows what each model accepts. In Runvil such model facts come from the model sheet, which gives `effort_levels` and `max_output` and feeds the Gateway's model list today. The per-model table is the same kind of data, which the Gateway would consult while it handles a request. A request that names no vendor can still reach a model that cannot do what it asks: a Runvil prompt can ask for thinking off on a model that always reasons (row 22), or set a temperature on a model that rejects or ignores it (row 28).

Terms used in this subsection: `models.use` is the call in a prompt's Lua code that selects a model role and sets request options such as a temperature. Adaptive thinking is Anthropic's mode in which the model decides for itself how much to think.

**How to read Table 6.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 6. Reasoning settings and sampling: 10 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 21 | **Reasoning-effort setting.** Vendors use different field names and value lists for this setting. | Every vendor that has reasoning models | It accepts one effort setting with seven values, lists each model's values in its model catalog, and maps a value the model lacks to the nearest one it has, by tables it does not publish. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert one vendor-neutral effort value into each vendor's own setting, and the client, the Engine, and prompts each gain that one setting. | High |
| 22 | **Switching reasoning on or off, and models that cannot switch it off.** Some models always reason, so a request to switch reasoning off is rejected, or the model has no "off" setting at all. | Anthropic, Google, xAI, OpenAI, Zhipu, Moonshot | Its model catalog marks the models that always reason. A request to switch reasoning off on one of them gets an HTTP 400 error from OpenRouter itself. On a model id that always points at a model's newest version, OpenRouter uses the lowest effort instead. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to translate a prompt's thinking on/off requirement into each vendor's own setting, and its model list has to say which models always reason, so that the Engine refuses a prompt that requires thinking off on one of them. | High |
| 23 | **Reasoning token budget.** Older models limit reasoning with a number of tokens in place of an effort value. | Anthropic, Google, Alibaba | It accepts a token budget as an alternative to the effort setting and rejects a request that sets both. For a model that no longer takes a budget, it accepts the budget and does not forward it. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to work out a token budget from the effort value for a model that takes a budget and no effort value. | High |
| 24 | **Thinking modes that differ from one Claude model to the next.** The request field that selects how Claude thinks accepts different values on different Claude models, and one value exists on Claude Sonnet 5.5 only. | Anthropic | It picks the thinking mode for each model from the caller's vendor-neutral reasoning settings. That one value can be requested only on OpenRouter's Messages route. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to pick the thinking mode for each Claude model from the per-model table, which has to record the modes that model accepts. | High |
| 25 | **Reasoning summaries, and the settings that show or hide them.** On request each vendor returns a summary of the model's reasoning, never the raw reasoning, and each has its own setting for asking. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google | It asks Anthropic for summarized thinking by default. It gives callers one setting for the length of the summary and one that hides the reasoning. One value of Anthropic's setting can be requested only on OpenRouter's Messages route. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to ask each vendor for reasoning summaries by default, so the Host has reasoning text to show. | High |
| 26 | **OpenAI's pro reasoning mode and reasoning context setting.** Two settings that only OpenAI has: one asks for deeper reasoning, and the other says which earlier reasoning the model may use. | OpenAI | It accepts both under OpenAI's own names among its otherwise vendor-neutral reasoning settings. It sends a request for pro mode to a separate pro listing of the model, and removes the pro setting for providers that do not support it. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to take both settings from a model entry that the operator configures, so that prompts never send them. | Medium |
| 27 | **Changing the reasoning effort in the middle of a conversation.** Two vendors let a request change the effort without losing the prompt cache, by writing the change into the conversation history. | OpenAI, Anthropic | It invented a form for its chat completions route: a system message with no content that holds the new effort. It converts between that form and each vendor's form, and answers HTTP 400 for a model without the feature. | **Gateway only**, plus an optional new vendor-neutral setting. Without the feature Runvil needs no code, and each changed effort causes one prompt-cache miss. With it, the Engine would add a vendor-neutral "settings changed" record. | Medium |
| 28 | **Sampling settings that a model rejects, ignores, or holds at a fixed value.** Many newer models reject or ignore a temperature and the other sampling settings in a request. | Anthropic, Google, Moonshot, OpenAI, DeepSeek, and xAI (stop sequences and penalties only) | Its model catalog no longer lists the sampling settings for many Anthropic and OpenAI models. Its documentation says a request that sets them is silently ignored on Claude Opus 4.7 and rejected on Claude Haiku 5.5. | **Compatibility code.** The Gateway has to remove the sampling settings from a request when the per-model table says the model rejects or ignores them, and report each removal in `gateway_warning`. | High |
| 29 | **An output limit that the vendor requires.** Anthropic rejects a request that does not say the most tokens the reply may have. | Anthropic | When the caller sends no output limit, OpenRouter adds a default of 65,536 tokens before it forwards the request to Anthropic, as an example in its documentation shows. | **Gateway only.** When a request has no output limit and the vendor requires one, the Gateway has to send the model's own maximum output size. | High |
| 30 | **Assistant prefill.** Some models reject a prefill, and others accept one only with a vendor's own flag. | Anthropic, DeepSeek, Moonshot | It forwards the request unchanged and returns the provider's HTTP 400 error when the model rejects prefill. Its documentation is silent on the flags that DeepSeek and Moonshot require. | **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would add the vendor's flag where one exists and refuse the request early for a model that rejects prefill. | Medium |

**Notes on Table 6, row by row.** In each note, "Where the answer comes from" gives the source for what OpenRouter and the vendors do.

#### Row 21. Reasoning-effort setting

- **Vendors:** Every vendor that has reasoning models.
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI's field is `reasoning.effort` in its Responses API and `reasoning_effort` in chat completions, a name that DeepSeek and Moonshot's Kimi models also use. Anthropic's field is `output_config.effort`, and Google's is `thinkingLevel`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends one setting, `reasoning: {effort}`, with one of seven values: `max`, `xhigh`, `high`, `medium`, `low`, `minimal`, `none`. OpenRouter's model catalog lists each model's values in `supported_efforts`. A value other than `none` that the model lacks is mapped to "the nearest supported level", which the documentation shows only by examples such as `xhigh` to `high`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A full table of what each value turns into on each model, which OpenRouter does not publish. One value means different things. On Claude models before 4.6 OpenRouter turns it into a token budget, a share of the request's output limit. On Claude 4.6 and later it sets `output_config.effort`, which governs the answer as well as the reasoning. On Gemini 3 and later it selects a thinking level.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert the effort value into the vendor's own setting, using the per-model table (the model list's `effort_levels` are unused today). In the universal API the setting should be OpenAI's chat completions field `reasoning_effort`. The client has to send it, the Engine needs one more option on the binding, and Lua one more option on `models.use`.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes. An effort value, which Runvil lacks today. Its list of values, and what the Gateway does with a value a model lacks, are not decided.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, except for the full tables. LiteLLM, Vercel AI Gateway, and Requesty publish theirs, which shows that the conversion can be built, not what OpenRouter's own tables hold.
- **Confidence:** High - eleven other projects, among them gateways, model libraries, and harnesses, have this same vendor-neutral setting.
- **Evidence items:** R1, R2, R4, R13, R16, R20, R27, M15, K25 to K28, H13, H33, H55, H58, H63, H67, Q40

#### Row 22. Switching reasoning on or off, and models that cannot switch it off

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Fable, and Mythos), Google (Gemini 4), xAI (Grok 4.5 and later), OpenAI (GPT-6 Astra), Zhipu (GLM 5.3), Moonshot (Kimi K3).
- **What the vendor does:** These models cannot switch reasoning off: the vendor rejects a request to switch it off, or the model's only reasoning settings are effort values that all leave reasoning on. Claude Sonnet 5.5 also rejects the off value, but accepts a mode that is nearly off, `between_tools` (row 24).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's model catalog marks such a model with `reasoning.mandatory: true`. On 2026-11-22 the flag was on 151 of the 354 catalog entries with reasoning settings. A request to stop reasoning on one of these models, with `reasoning: {"enabled": false}` or the effort `none`, gets HTTP 400 from OpenRouter itself, with the message `Reasoning is mandatory for this endpoint and cannot be disabled.`, before the request goes to any provider. On a `~latest` alias such as `~anthropic/claude-opus-latest`, OpenRouter changes the request to the lowest effort the model supports.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A switch for reasoning off that works the same on every model: the same request succeeds on one, fails on another, and is quietly changed on an alias.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** A prompt asks for thinking on or off only through the `thinking` requirement in its front matter, and the model list gives each model one of three modes. On an `always` model `thinking: false` fails the requirement check, on a `never` model `thinking: true` fails it, and the Engine ends the run with `requirements_unmet`. On a `switchable` model both pass, and every request has the switch, today as `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, which only llama.cpp and vLLM understand (section 7). The client should send it under a standard name, the same change for every vendor. The Gateway has to translate it into the vendor's own form, for example `thinking.type` for Claude and DeepSeek, `enable_thinking` for Alibaba's Qwen, `reasoning_effort: "none"` for OpenAI's models that accept it, and template arguments (values for the model's chat template) for a local server. The model list has to say `thinking: always` for the models above, except Claude Sonnet 5.5: that model has to be `switchable`, and for "off" the Gateway sends it `between_tools` and reports the replacement in `gateway_warning` (section 8).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. The setting exists already, as a prompt's `thinking` requirement and the switch in each request. Only the request field's name changes, to a standard name that is not decided yet.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, and by the vendors for Grok, GLM 5.3, Kimi K3, and the off switches.
- **Confidence:** High - the three modes and the requirement check exist today, so the refusal needs no new Engine code.
- **Evidence items:** R14, R15, R22, R23, R26, R27, OV15, OV21, OV39, OV46, OV54, AN20, L30

#### Row 23. Reasoning token budget

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Claude 4.6 and older), Google (Gemini 2.5), Alibaba (some Qwen models).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic's budget field is `thinking.budget_tokens`, Google's is `thinkingBudget`, and Qwen's is `thinking_budget`. Claude 4.6 takes either a budget, which Anthropic marks as deprecated, or adaptive thinking with an effort value.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends the budget as `reasoning.max_tokens`. OpenRouter rejects a request that sets it together with `effort`. For the models that no longer take a budget, which are the Claude models that support only adaptive thinking (4.7 and later) and Gemini 4, it accepts the budget and sends none, without an error: "Budgets are not forwarded". For a model that takes only a budget, it turns an effort value into one: 10% (`minimal`) to 95% (`max`) of the request's output limit.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Any notice that a budget was dropped.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** For a model that takes a budget and no effort value, the Gateway has to work the budget out from the vendor-neutral effort value (row 21), as OpenRouter does.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. Anthropic and Alibaba document their own budget fields.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter documents the formula it uses for this conversion, so the Gateway can do the same.
- **Evidence items:** R3, R13, R17, AN20, OV47

#### Row 24. Thinking modes that differ from one Claude model to the next

- **Vendors:** Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** A request to Claude selects the thinking mode in the field `thinking.type`. `adaptive` works on Claude 4.6 and later. `enabled` with a token budget works on Claude 4.6 and older and gets HTTP 400 on 4.7 and later. `disabled` gets HTTP 400 on Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, and the Fable and Mythos models, and works on the others. `between_tools` works on Sonnet 5.5 only: the model does no reasoning before it replies, but in a tool loop it still writes short notes between tool calls, and those come back as thinking blocks. `between_tools`, and `disabled` on Haiku 5.5 and Opus 5, work only with an effort of `high` or below. A request with thinking off still has an effort, because Anthropic's effort also governs the answer.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter chooses the `thinking.type` value for each model itself, from the vendor-neutral reasoning settings the caller sent. It accepts `between_tools` only on its Messages route.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** On the chat completions route, the middle setting `between_tools`.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to choose the `thinking.type` value for each Claude model from the per-model table, which has to record the values the model accepts. With thinking on it sends `adaptive` where the model accepts it, Claude 4.6 included, and `enabled` with a budget (row 23) on older models. With thinking off it sends `disabled`, or on Sonnet 5.5 `between_tools`, the nearest value that model has, which is translation. What it sends when thinking is off and the effort is above `high` is not decided.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter and by Anthropic. From earlier Runvil research: Table A5 in Appendix A of the 2026-11-17 report has the same list.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic documents which `thinking` values each model accepts, so the per-model table can be complete.
- **Evidence items:** R20 to R23, AN20 to AN26

#### Row 25. Reasoning summaries, and the settings that show or hide them

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`thinking.display`), OpenAI (`reasoning.summary`), Google (`includeThoughts`)
- **What the vendor does:** Each vendor returns a summary only on request, and never the raw reasoning.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter sends `display: "summarized"` to Anthropic by default. OpenRouter's own `reasoning.summary`, named like OpenAI's, sets how long the summary is, and `reasoning.exclude` hides the reasoning from the reply.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The `updates` value of Anthropic's `thinking.display`, which OpenRouter accepts only on its Messages route. With `updates` the thinking text comes back empty, and only the notes a model writes between tool calls (row 24) come back as readable text.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to ask each vendor for reasoning summaries by default and return each summary as reasoning text in `reasoning_content`, `reasoning`, or `thinking`, the three reply fields the client reads today, so the Host has reasoning text to show.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter asks Anthropic for summaries by default, and all three vendor settings are documented, so the Gateway can send them.
- **Evidence items:** R5, R6, R24, AN18, AN19, GG35, L25

#### Row 26. OpenAI's pro reasoning mode and reasoning context setting

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-5.6 and later)
- **What the vendor does:** `reasoning.mode: "pro"` asks the model for deeper reasoning than the default mode, `standard`. `reasoning.context` controls which reasoning from earlier turns of the conversation the model may use: all of it (`all_turns`) or none (`current_turn`).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter accepts both settings under the same names inside its `reasoning` object, whose other settings are vendor-neutral. It reroutes a request with `pro` to a separate listing of the model in its catalog, one whose id matches `*-pro`. For providers that do not support `reasoning.mode`, it removes the setting before it forwards the request.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A `reasoning` object that names no vendor: the caller has to write two values into it that only OpenAI understands.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** Both have to be settings that the operator puts on a model entry in the Gateway's configuration and that a prompt never sends. For pro mode, the Host fills a model role with the entry that has it. A model entry without `reasoning.context` leaves the choice to the model, which on the GPT-5.6 family is `all_turns`.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. OpenAI documents both settings itself.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the settings are documented, but leaving them to the operator is a design choice that no evidence item tests.
- **Evidence items:** R7, R8, OA28, OA30

#### Row 27. Changing the reasoning effort in the middle of a conversation

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-6, with a `configuration_update` item), Anthropic (with `output_config` on a system message that has no content)
- **What the vendor does:** The change is written into the conversation history at the point where it takes effect, and every later request has to keep it at that position. OpenRouter's documentation says this changes the effort "without invalidating the prompt cache", which implies that changing a request's effort setting does invalidate it. No source read says why, since the effort is not part of the prefix.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter invented a form for its chat completions route: a system message with no content and a field `configuration_update: {reasoning: {effort}}`. It converts between this form and the vendors' forms across its three routes. For a model that does not support the feature, it answers with HTTP 400.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** OpenAI's item can change two things: the reasoning effort and, in a part named `text`, the response format. OpenRouter's form has only the first.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, plus an optional new vendor-neutral setting. Runvil needs no code to work without this feature: a changed effort then causes one cache miss, a request that the vendor processes in full. With the feature, the Engine would add a "settings changed" record to its message list, and the Gateway would translate it into each vendor's form. That record is the optional vendor-neutral setting: it names no vendor and is the same for every vendor. It is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional. The "settings changed" record in the Engine's message list, needed only if Runvil wants the feature.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. OpenAI and Anthropic document their own forms.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the three forms are documented, but nobody has measured how much the feature saves by keeping the cache.
- **Evidence items:** R30, OA44, OA45, AN27, C56

#### Row 28. Sampling settings that a model rejects, ignores, or holds at a fixed value

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Claude 4.7 and later, and every model under API version `2026-10-21`), Google (Gemini 3.6 and later, which includes Gemini 4), Moonshot (Kimi K3), OpenAI (its reasoning models), DeepSeek (while thinking is on), and xAI (its reasoning models, for `stop` and the presence and frequency penalties only).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic rejects `temperature`, `top_p`, and `top_k`: Claude 4.7 and later accept only the default values, and under that API version every model answers HTTP 400, even to a default value. Google ignores them. Kimi K3 holds `temperature` at `1.0` and `top_p` at `0.95`: "passing any other value returns an error". DeepSeek ignores a temperature: setting one "will not trigger an error but will also have no effect". OpenAI's guide for GPT-6 says to remove `temperature` and `top_p` when the reasoning effort is not `none`, and does not say what happens to a request that keeps them. xAI's rule is about three other request settings: "`presencePenalty`, `frequencyPenalty`, and `stop` cannot be used with reasoning models. Requests that include them return an error." No xAI rule against the three sampling settings was found.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's model catalog has a list per model named `supported_parameters`. For many Anthropic and OpenAI models that list no longer has the sampling settings: 10 of 16 Anthropic models list none of the three, and 43 of 62 OpenAI models list neither `temperature` nor `top_p` (counted without aliases and `:batch` copies). Even for those models the outcome differs: OpenRouter's documentation says the settings are "silently ignored" on Claude Opus 4.7 and that Claude Haiku 5.5 "rejects" them. Since Anthropic itself rejects them on Opus 4.7, OpenRouter must remove them there first, which its documentation does not say. Every Google, Moonshot, DeepSeek, and xAI model still lists `temperature`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Any sign that a value had no effect: where the settings are ignored, the request succeeds and the caller is not told.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** Today the client sends a temperature as the prompt set it and the Gateway forwards it, so a vendor's refusal fails that round. The Gateway has to remove the sampling fields when the per-model table says the model rejects or ignores them: one lookup per request, which for OpenAI's GPT-6 also needs the request's effort value. A prompt author can notice it: `models.use('strong', {temperature = 0})` changes nothing on these models. The Gateway also has to report the removal in `gateway_warning` (section 8), which the client would then have to pass on. For xAI's reasoning models the table would list `stop` and the two penalties, which Runvil's client never sends (section 7).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter and by the vendors.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic, Google, Moonshot, DeepSeek, and xAI document which settings their models reject or ignore, and OpenAI's guide says to remove them, so the per-model table can record each rule.
- **Evidence items:** AN65, AN66, M35, GG92, OA29, OV15, OV21, OV39, W167, L53

#### Row 29. An output limit that the vendor requires

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`max_tokens` is required)
- **What the vendor does:** A request to Anthropic without `max_tokens` fails.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** When the caller sends no limit, OpenRouter adds `max_tokens: 65536`. The number is from an example in its documentation, not from recorded traffic: the example prints a request without a limit and the body OpenRouter sent to Anthropic for it, which the option `debug: {"echo_upstream_body": true}` returns.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A caller that sends no limit, meaning "no cap", gets a cap anyway.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** When a request has no output limit and the vendor requires one, the Gateway has to send the model's `max_output` value, which the model list already publishes.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. Anthropic's API reference lists `max_tokens` as required.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic documents the requirement, and OpenRouter also fills in a value where the caller sent none.
- **Evidence items:** O67, AN90

#### Row 30. Assistant prefill

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (rejected on Claude 4.6 and later), DeepSeek (`prefix: true` on a beta URL), Moonshot (`partial: true`)
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic rejects a prefill on Claude 4.6 and later. DeepSeek accepts one only when the last message has `prefix: true` and the request goes to a separate beta URL. Moonshot accepts one only when the last message has `partial: true`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter forwards the request as it is. When the provider rejects it, the documentation says "OpenRouter returns the provider's 400". It is silent on DeepSeek's and Moonshot's flags.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing is translated, and whether a prefill works on DeepSeek's or Moonshot's models through OpenRouter is not documented.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code**, if adopted. When a request ends with an assistant message, which in the Engine is a trailing assistant record, the Gateway would add the vendor's flag where one exists, and for DeepSeek also use the beta URL. For a model that rejects a prefill, such as Claude 4.6 and later, it would refuse the request before it reaches the vendor. It would forward the request unchanged for every other model. Runvil's tool loop never ends a request with an assistant message, so the planned work does not need this. What happens when a prompt's Lua code appends an assistant record last was not examined.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. Anthropic, DeepSeek, and Moonshot document their own rules.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the vendors document their rules, but whether Runvil should offer a feature that some models reject outright is undecided.
- **Evidence items:** AN69, OV26, OV43, T41, T65

### 3.3 Prompt caching: the Gateway can do the vendor-specific work itself, and one diagnostic feature needs the opaque field

Vendors differ in whether they cache a request's prefix by themselves or only when the request has a cache marker, in how long a cache entry lives, in how a conversation's requests reach the machine that holds its cache, and in how a reply counts cached tokens. The prefix is the start of a request that repeats from one request to the next, and a cache marker is a `cache_control` field on one block that tells the vendor to cache the request up to and including that block.

The Gateway can do all the vendor-specific work: place the cache markers, apply the cache lifetime that the operator chose, send a key that keeps a conversation on one machine, and report the token counts. The client and the Engine gain only things that are the same for every vendor: a count of tokens written to the cache (row 38), an optional conversation id (row 36), and, if adopted, a cost field (row 39) and a record that the tool set changed (row 41). The one feature found to need the opaque field is Anthropic's cache diagnostics (row 40), which reports why a request missed the cache. Rows 37, 39, 40, and 41 are marked "if adopted" because Runvil's planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need them. Nothing proposed here is built: today neither the client nor the Gateway sends a cache marker, so caching works only where the vendor caches by itself (row 34) [L101].

OpenRouter shows that a gateway can translate a cache marker: it accepts `cache_control` whichever vendor serves the request and converts it into OpenAI's own marker where needed (row 35). It does not translate the lifetime (row 33), it uses only the last marker for Google's Gemini (row 37), and it leaves the choice of places, which differ by vendor, to the caller (row 31).

Terms used in this subsection: Vendors' documentation also calls a cache marker a cache breakpoint, as in OpenAI's field `prompt_cache_breakpoint`. A request writes to the cache when the vendor stores its prefix and reads from it when the vendor reuses a stored prefix. A cache lifetime, which vendors call a TTL (time to live), is how long the vendor keeps a cache entry. Implicit caching is Google's name for caching that the vendor does by itself. Z.ai is the platform of Zhipu, the maker of the GLM models.

**How to read Table 7.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 7. Prompt caching: 11 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 31 | **Cache markers on blocks the caller chooses.** The caller puts a cache marker on up to four blocks of a request. | Anthropic, Alibaba | Its chat completions route accepts the marker on the text blocks of a message and on tool definitions. The caller still chooses the places. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway should decide where markers go, starting with Anthropic's automatic caching field (row 32) where the provider accepts it. | High |
| 32 | **Automatic caching through one top-level request field.** One field on the whole request tells Anthropic to place the cache marker itself and to move it forward as the conversation grows. | Anthropic | It accepts the field on all three of its routes. For Amazon Bedrock, which rejects the field, it rewrites it into a marker at the end of the request. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway should add the field itself, or a marker on the last block for Claude on Amazon Bedrock. | High |
| 33 | **How long a cache entry lives.** Each vendor has its own set of allowed cache lifetimes and its own price for writing to the cache at each lifetime. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Moonshot | It accepts a lifetime on a cache marker and drops it when it converts the marker into another vendor's form. For Google's Gemini it sets a fixed lifetime itself. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to take the lifetime from a setting that the operator makes in its configuration and send it in the vendor's own field. | Medium |
| 34 | **Caching that the vendor does by itself.** The vendor caches a repeated prefix without any field in the request. | OpenAI, DeepSeek, xAI, Groq, Moonshot, Z.ai, Google | Nothing for the caching itself. It keeps the conversation on one provider (row 36). | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send the unchanged part of a conversation as exactly the same bytes on every request. | High |
| 35 | **OpenAI's own cache markers and cache options.** OpenAI's newer models have their own field for marking a block and a separate set of caching options for the whole request. | OpenAI | It uses OpenAI's field names. It converts a marker on a block to and from Anthropic's form, depending on which model serves the request, and sends the whole-request options to OpenAI only. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to set the lifetime option where the operator chose a lifetime. Whether it should also mark blocks is not designed, and OpenAI caches without markers. | High |
| 36 | **A key that keeps a conversation on the machine that holds its cache.** The caller sends the same key with every request of a conversation, and the vendor routes those requests to the machine that has the conversation's cache. | OpenAI, xAI, Z.ai, and any model with several providers | It uses sticky routing, keyed by a hash of the opening messages or by a session id from the caller. Its documentation does not say whether the caller's key is passed on to OpenAI or xAI. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to compute a key from the conversation's opening messages, or use a conversation id if the client sends one, and send it in the vendor's own field or header. | Medium |
| 37 | **Cache objects that the caller creates and refers to by name.** With Google, a separate API call stores content in a cache object, and later requests point at that object. | Google | It hides the cache object behind the same cache marker that a caller uses for Anthropic, and it creates and reuses the object itself. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would create the cache object and find it again by listing the vendor's caches, with nothing kept between requests. | Medium |
| 38 | **Counts of tokens read from and written to the cache.** Every vendor reports how many prompt tokens came from the cache or went into it, but the vendors build these counts in different ways. | All | It reports the whole input as one total, with one count of tokens read from the cache and one of tokens written to it. For Google's Gemini the two counts can cover the same tokens. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report the whole input as the total, with cache reads and cache writes as separate parts, and the client and the Engine have to store one new count of cache writes. | High |
| 39 | **The cost of a request, reported next to its token counts.** OpenRouter adds what a request cost to the reply, where vendors report token counts only. | None - OpenRouter's own addition | It adds the amount charged, a breakdown of that amount, and a flag that says whether the caller's own vendor key was used. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would work out the cost from the token counts and each model's prices and add it to the reply. | Medium |
| 40 | **Diagnostics that say why a request missed the cache.** The caller tells Anthropic which reply came before, and Anthropic answers with the reason the new request could not use the cache. | Anthropic | Its chat completions and Messages request schemas have no diagnostics field, so it appears not to offer the feature. | **Opaque field**, if adopted. The Gateway would store each reply's message id in the opaque field and send it to Anthropic with the next request. | Low |
| 41 | **Changing the tools in mid-conversation without losing the cache.** Anthropic lets a caller add or remove tools partway through a conversation, through blocks in a system message, in a way that leaves the cached prefix valid. | Anthropic | It offers the feature on its Messages route only, because its chat completions format cannot express the blocks that the feature uses. | **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral record that says the tool set changed, if adopted. The Gateway would translate the record into Anthropic's blocks. | Medium |

**Notes on Table 7, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 31. Cache markers on blocks the caller chooses

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (also Claude on Amazon Bedrock, Google Vertex AI, and Microsoft Azure) and Alibaba's Qwen.
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic and Alibaba allow `cache_control` on up to four blocks and look for an existing cache entry at most 20 blocks back from each marker. A prefix under a minimum goes uncached (512 to 4,096 tokens by model at Anthropic, 1,024 at Alibaba).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its chat completions route accepts `cache_control` on the text blocks of a message and on tool definitions.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller still has to choose the places and keep to four markers. Harnesses choose by the vendor name at the start of OpenRouter's model id: opencode marks the last tool definition, the system text, and the end of the conversation for `anthropic/`, only the system text and the end of the conversation for `qwen/` or `alibaba/qwen`, and nothing for other models.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway should decide where markers go, and the client still sends none. For Claude the first step needs no marker: Anthropic's automatic caching field (row 32). Markers on the tool definitions and the system text should follow only if measurements of Runvil's own conversations show requests that miss the cache (how many is not set). Two cases cannot use the automatic field: Claude on Amazon Bedrock, where the Gateway has to mark the last block itself (row 32), and Alibaba's Qwen, which OpenRouter's documentation says "requires explicit cache breakpoints", so the Gateway has to place markers from the start, as opencode does.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: where it accepts the marker. Taken from another open-source project: the gateway agentgateway adds markers itself, by policy settings such as `cache_system` and `cache_tools`, when its caller sent none. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-11-17 report recommended this order of work, having found that the projects it read converge on the three places opencode marks for Claude.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic and OpenRouter both document the marker and its limits, and agentgateway already places markers itself.
- **Evidence items:** C6, C7, AN30, AN32, F19, J15, C13, C70, W35

#### Row 32. Automatic caching through one top-level request field

- **Vendors:** Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** One top-level field, `"cache_control": { "type": "ephemeral" }`, turns on automatic caching. Anthropic then puts the cache marker on the last block that can be cached and moves it forward as the conversation grows. Every platform that serves Claude accepts the field except Amazon Bedrock, which answers HTTP 400.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It accepts the top-level `cache_control` on all three of its routes and, for Bedrock, rewrites it into a cache marker at the end of the request.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing, as far as OpenRouter's documentation says.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway's Anthropic translator should add the field to each request for a Claude model. For Claude on Amazon Bedrock it has to put a cache marker on the last block instead.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the field and its rewrite for Bedrock. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-11-17 report recommended that the Gateway's translator add the field itself.
- **Confidence:** High - it is one field, Anthropic documents which platforms accept it, and OpenRouter already passes it on and rewrites it for Bedrock.
- **Evidence items:** C6, C23, AN31, AN36

#### Row 33. How long a cache entry lives

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`5m` or `1h`), OpenAI (`30m` or `12h`), Moonshot (`5m` or `1h`), and Google's cache objects (row 37; any `ttl`). The first value is the default.
- **What the vendor does:** Each vendor charges more for a cache write with a longer lifetime: at Anthropic 1.25 times the input price for 5 minutes and 2 times for 1 hour.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** A caller sets a lifetime inside a cache marker: `cache_control.ttl` in Anthropic's form or `prompt_cache_breakpoint.ttl` in OpenAI's form (row 35). OpenRouter's documentation says "TTLs are not translated": it drops a `cache_control` lifetime on the way to OpenAI and a `prompt_cache_breakpoint` lifetime on the way to Anthropic or Google. For Gemini it fixes the lifetime at 5 minutes.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A lifetime that holds for every vendor.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The lifetime has to be a setting that the operator makes in the Gateway's configuration, on a model entry or an endpoint entry (its form is not designed). The Gateway has to write it into each request in the vendor's own field: for Claude the `ttl` of `cache_control`, on a block or at the top level (row 32), for OpenAI, and for Moonshot in its own API, `prompt_cache_options.ttl`, and for a Google cache object (row 37) the object's `ttl`. With no lifetime in the request, the vendor's default applies.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the two fields and the dropped lifetimes. The operator setting follows from Runvil's design goal: a lifetime is a vendor's own value, and a prompt that set it would have to know its vendor.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the lifetimes and their prices are documented, but nobody has measured which lifetime costs less on Runvil's conversations, so it is open whether one fixed setting per model is enough.
- **Evidence items:** C19, C22, C64, C65, C62, C69

#### Row 34. Caching that the vendor does by itself

- **Vendors:** OpenAI, DeepSeek, xAI, Groq (a provider that serves other vendors' models), Moonshot, Z.ai, and Google's Gemini models through implicit caching.
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor caches a repeated prefix by itself.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Nothing for the caching itself. Sticky routing keeps the conversation on one provider (row 36).
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing, unless the caller turns sticky routing off (row 36).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send the unchanged part of a conversation as exactly the same bytes on every request, because any difference in the prefix is a cache miss. Two rules in today's code work toward that. Under the Engine's append-only rule Lua code can only add records at the end of a conversation, and earlier records change only when a compactor rewrites them. The Gateway passes the client's messages on to the upstream as they are. The new duty comes with the vendor translators: each has to write the same messages the same way every time.
