- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: these vendors need no field in the request. From earlier Runvil research: both rules were read in Runvil's code.
- **Confidence:** High - the vendors do the caching themselves, and Runvil's code already has both rules, though nothing was run to confirm that the bytes match.
- **Evidence items:** C1, C2, C10, C12, L11, L97

#### Row 35. OpenAI's own cache markers and cache options

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-5.6 and later)
- **What the vendor does:** `prompt_cache_breakpoint` goes on a text block, and `prompt_cache_options` at the top level of the request. Two of its settings are `mode`, which when set to `explicit` makes OpenAI cache only at marked blocks, and `ttl`, which a marker can override with a `ttl` of its own.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It accepts both fields under the same names. It converts a marker on a block to and from `cache_control`, according to the model that serves the request. It does not convert `prompt_cache_options`, which in its words "stays OpenAI-only".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A lifetime set on a marker that OpenRouter converts (row 33). Also two parts of OpenAI's API that OpenRouter's API specification leaves out: `prewarm`, a setting in `prompt_cache_options` that prepares the cache without generating output, and OpenAI's own cache diagnostics, of which only the name of a reply field, `prompt_cache_diagnostics`, was found.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** OpenAI caches without any marker (row 34), so the Gateway can send a request with neither field. It has to set `prompt_cache_options.ttl` when the operator chose a lifetime (row 33). Whether it should also mark blocks, and whether Runvil would use `prewarm` or OpenAI's cache diagnostics, is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: both fields, the conversion, and the settings it leaves out.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenAI and OpenRouter both document the two fields, and OpenAI caches even when the Gateway sends neither.
- **Evidence items:** C3, C4, C19, C21, OA64, OA61

#### Row 36. A key that keeps a conversation on the machine that holds its cache

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (request field `prompt_cache_key`), xAI (HTTP header `x-grok-conv-id`), Z.ai, and any model with several providers.
- **What the vendor does:** A stable key steers a conversation's requests to the machine that holds its cache. A cache also belongs to one provider, so a conversation on a model with several providers has to stay with that provider.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It uses sticky routing and recognizes a conversation by the account, the model, and a hash of the first system message and the first message after it. A `session_id` field, an `x-session-id` header, or `prompt_cache_key` from the caller replaces the hash. Its documentation does not say whether the caller's key is passed on to OpenAI or xAI. For Z.ai it says it sends "a session affinity key with each request", made from the caller's account and, if sent, its `session_id`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Sticky routing is off when the caller sets `provider.order`, the request setting that lists which providers to try and in what order. Two pages of OpenRouter's documentation give two durations for sticky routing, 60 minutes and 10 minutes.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to compute a key from a hash of the conversation's opening messages and send it in the vendor's own field or header (which one Z.ai takes was not found). The hash changes when a conversation's opening messages change, and a conversation id sent by the client would not. Staying with one provider needs no work today, because the Gateway sends every request for a model to the first endpoint in the model entry's list.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional: a conversation id that the client sends, the same for every vendor. OpenAI's `prompt_cache_key` is a candidate name.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: sticky routing and the keys it accepts. Taken from another open-source project: pi sends its own session id to OpenRouter in the `x-session-id` header. From earlier Runvil research: the first-endpoint rule was read in the Gateway's code.
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenAI and xAI document where the key goes, so the Gateway can send one. How much a key improves cache use has not been measured, so it is open whether the hashed key is enough.
- **Evidence items:** C25 to C29, C16, OV18, OV58, F36, C12, L117

#### Row 37. Cache objects that the caller creates and refers to by name

- **Vendors:** Google (Gemini 2.5 and 3; unavailable on Gemini 4 and on the Interactions API)
- **What the vendor does:** The cache object is a `cachedContents` resource.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It hides the cache object behind the same `cache_control` marker that a caller uses for Anthropic. It creates the object and reuses it on later requests, uses only the last marker in a request, and fixes the lifetime at 5 minutes.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Direct control of the cache object: its name, its lifetime, updating it, and deleting it.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would create the cache object and then either find it again by listing the vendor's caches or keep its name from one request to the next. LiteLLM does the first, which needs nothing kept between requests: it stores a hash of the marked prefix as the cache object's `displayName` and later lists the cache objects to find it. Keeping the name would take the opaque field, as the id in row 40 does. Which way the Gateway would use, and which part of a request it would cache, is not decided. Runvil may not need the feature, because Google's implicit caching (row 34) already caches a repeated prefix.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: it creates and reuses the object, but not how it finds it again. Taken from another open-source project: LiteLLM.
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenRouter and LiteLLM show that a gateway can run the cache objects for its caller, but Google's newest models do not have them, so it is open whether Runvil should build this at all.
- **Evidence items:** C11, C15, GG52 to GG57, K32

#### Row 38. Counts of tokens read from and written to the cache

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic reports cache reads and cache writes in counters of their own, and its `input_tokens` leaves both out. OpenAI and DeepSeek report inclusive counts: their total of prompt tokens includes the cached tokens. OpenAI's Responses API also splits the writes by lifetime.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** In its reply's usage, `prompt_tokens` is the whole input. Inside it, `prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens` counts the tokens read from the cache and `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens` the tokens written to it.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The split of cache writes by lifetime. Also, the two counts cannot be subtracted from the total in the same way for every vendor: in recorded traffic they are separate parts of the input for Anthropic and for OpenAI, but count the same tokens on the Gemini request that creates a cache object (row 37).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway's Anthropic translator has to add the three counters `input_tokens`, `cache_read_input_tokens`, and `cache_creation_input_tokens` to get the whole input. The Gateway has to report cache reads and cache writes as separate parts of that total, so that no token is counted in both (what it should report for that Gemini request is not decided). The client and the Engine have to add one count of cache writes, the same for every vendor, to their `Usage` record, which today counts cache reads and not cache writes.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a count of the tokens written to the cache. Its field name, `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens`, is now OpenAI's own field name as well.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the field names. Inferred about OpenRouter, from recorded traffic: how the two counts relate for each vendor. Taken from other open-source projects: LiteLLM and agentgateway both add Anthropic's three counters together.
- **Confidence:** High - recorded traffic confirms this layout: with reads and writes as separate parts of the input, token counts and prices give exactly the cost that was charged, in nine of nine replies from Claude models and four of four from OpenAI models.
- **Evidence items:** C34, C41, C42, C73, C74, Q30, WT23 to WT25, L101

#### Row 39. The cost of a request, reported next to its token counts

- **Vendors:** No vendor. It is OpenRouter's own addition.
- **What the vendor does:** Vendors report token counts only.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It adds three fields to its reply's usage: `usage.cost`, the amount charged for the request, `usage.cost_details`, a breakdown of that amount, and `usage.is_byok`, which says whether the request used the caller's own vendor key (BYOK, bring your own key).
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would work out the cost from the token counts and each model's prices and add it to the reply's usage. The model sheet has prices for prompt tokens and output tokens only, so prices for cache reads and cache writes would have to be added to it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional: the cost itself, as a field in a reply's usage that is the same for every vendor.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the three cost fields. From earlier Runvil research: the model sheet's prices were read in Runvil's code.
- **Confidence:** Medium - working out a cost needs no vendor-specific code outside the Gateway, but the unit is open: six other gateways copied the field and do not agree on it (US dollars at one, points at another).
- **Evidence items:** C43, Q44, L70

#### Row 40. Diagnostics that say why a request missed the cache

- **Vendors:** Anthropic
- **What the vendor does:** The caller sends `diagnostics.previous_message_id` in the request, set to the id of the previous reply in the conversation. Anthropic's reply then has `cache_miss_reason`, which says why the new request could not use the cache.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The request schemas of its chat completions route and its Messages route, the two that were checked, have no diagnostics field.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The whole feature.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, if adopted. The Gateway would store each reply's message id in the opaque field, beside the vendor's blocks, and send it to Anthropic with the next request as `diagnostics.previous_message_id`. Unlike a cache object (row 37), the id cannot be found again from the request, so it has to travel with the conversation.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No, unless the reason is to reach the caller, in which case the reply would need a field for it. Where the Gateway would report the reason is not designed.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor: the feature, in Anthropic's documentation. Inferred about OpenRouter: the two request schemas have no such field, so a caller cannot send it.
- **Confidence:** Low - none of the projects read for this report sends this field through a gateway, so nothing shows that storing the id and sending it back works.
- **Evidence items:** AN33, AN34

#### Row 41. Changing the tools in mid-conversation without losing the cache

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (the content blocks `tool_addition` and `tool_removal`, and the message field `clear_at`)
- **What the vendor does:** Changing a request's tool list changes the prefix, so the cache is normally lost. The two blocks avoid that: the caller puts one in the content of a system message partway through a conversation, and it adds or removes tools without changing the request's `tools` list. `clear_at` is a field on such a system message, with the values `next_user_message` and `never`. Anthropic's documentation calls a message that has it turn-scoped, and the evidence has no description of what the field does, only that it too keeps the cached prefix.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It offers the two blocks and `clear_at` on its Messages route only. For the blocks its documentation gives the reason: "the Chat Completions API has no representation for these blocks".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The whole feature, for a caller that uses the chat completions route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral record that says the tool set changed, if adopted. Today the need does not arise: the Engine fixes a conversation's tool list at the first round of its tool loop, every later round sends the same tools, and Lua code that offers a tool after that round gets an error. If Runvil adopted the feature, the Engine would have to accept such a later offer and add a "tools changed" record to the conversation, and the client would send it with the other messages, each in the same way for every vendor. The Gateway would translate the record into Anthropic's two blocks. The record's format is not designed. `clear_at` has no use in Runvil today, because the Engine accepts system records only at the start of a conversation.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes, if the feature is adopted: the record that says the tool set changed. Without it the feature cannot be used through the universal API.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the feature is on its Messages route only. From earlier Runvil research: the Engine's two rules were read in Runvil's code.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the record would be designed from Anthropic's blocks alone, so it is open whether it would fit another vendor's way of changing tools.
- **Evidence items:** AN64, AN79, AN80, C56, T42, L98, L99

### 3.4 Tools: definitions translate cleanly, while ids, schemas, and forced tool choice need small fixes

Most of the work on tools is translation: tool definitions, the words of `tool_choice`, the switch for several calls at once, the place of tool results, and tool dialects. Four features need compatibility code in the Gateway, because a plain translation would be rejected or has no form to translate into. A vendor can reject a tool-call id, the string that names one call and that its result quotes, when another vendor wrote it (row 46). A vendor can reject part of the JSON Schema that describes a tool's arguments (row 48). Some models refuse a forced tool choice (row 44), and chat completions has no form for OpenAI's plain-text tools (row 51). Runvil uses neither of these last two today. Tool search would need the opaque field (row 52).

OpenRouter documents no fix of its own for ids, schemas, or a forced tool choice. A vendor's error comes back to the caller: OpenRouter's documentation says so for a forced tool choice [T10], and callers reported it for ids until 2026-03-30 [T64, VT15]. It publishes per-endpoint data for the forced choice only: since 2026-08-28 each endpoint has a `supports_tool_choice` object, four true-or-false flags that say which values of `tool_choice` passed OpenRouter's own tests [T8]. Its API specification puts no format rule on tool-call ids [T15]. It lists a parameter, `strict_tool_schemas`, whose meaning was not established and which none of the 476 models in its catalog declares [T6].

**How to read Table 8.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 8. Tools: 13 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 42 | **The format for describing a tool.** Vendors lay out the JSON of a tool definition in different ways. | All | It takes definitions in OpenAI's layout and converts them for providers with another layout. A tool's name, description, and argument schema arrive whole. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway's translators have to convert each tool definition into the vendor's layout. | High |
| 43 | **The words that say whether the model may or must call a tool.** Vendors use different words for the same choices: the model may call a tool, must not, must call one, or must call one named tool. | OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Mistral | Its chat completions route accepts OpenAI's four words, and per-endpoint flags say which of them work. No route offers a Google-only mode that validates calls, and only the Responses route accepts OpenAI's list of allowed tools. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to translate the caller's word into the vendor's word, and a way for a prompt to force a tool call would be an optional new vendor-neutral setting. | High |
| 44 | **Models that refuse to be forced to call a tool.** Six Claude models, DeepSeek and Qwen with reasoning on, GLM, and Meta's own API do not accept a forced tool choice. | Anthropic, DeepSeek, Alibaba (Qwen), Zhipu (GLM), Meta | It forwards the request, and the vendor's error comes back. Its per-endpoint flags mark a forced choice as unsupported on the three Claude models whose flags were read, and as supported on two of three Meta models, against Meta's documentation. | **Compatibility code**, if adopted. For a model that refuses, the Gateway would let the model choose, add an instruction to call the tool, and report the change, or else reject the request before sending it. | Medium |
| 45 | **The switch for several tool calls in one reply.** A request can say whether the model may ask for several tool calls at once, and Anthropic's switch has the opposite meaning from OpenAI's. | OpenAI, Mistral, Anthropic, some open-weight models | Its chat completions route accepts OpenAI's switch. How it converts the switch for Anthropic is not documented, and its model catalog lists the switch as supported on 18 of 476 models. | **Gateway only.** The planned tool loop sends no switch and needs no code for it, and in a request that has one the Gateway has to flip its value for Anthropic. | High |
| 46 | **Tool-call id formats that differ by vendor.** An id that one vendor wrote can break the format rule of the vendor that the next request goes to. | Anthropic, Mistral, Amazon Bedrock, Moonshot (Kimi), Google | It hands the caller each id as the vendor wrote it. Whether it rewrites ids when a conversation moves to another vendor is not documented, and callers have reported Mistral's rejection of another vendor's ids arriving through OpenRouter. | **Compatibility code.** The Gateway has to rewrite each id in a request to fit the vendor it is going to, the same way on the call and on its result, and replace an empty or repeated id in a reply. | High |
| 47 | **The required place and order of tool results.** Anthropic and Google each prescribe where the results of a reply's tool calls go in the next request, and reject a request that breaks the rule. | Anthropic, Google | It takes each result as a standard OpenAI-style tool message. How it regroups the results for each vendor is not documented. The text of each result arrives whole. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to regroup the results for each vendor, and the Engine already sends one result message per call today, right after the assistant message. | High |
| 48 | **Tool schemas that a vendor rejects, and the strict flag.** Vendors reject some JSON Schema features in a tool's schema, most of all when the strict flag on the tool asks the vendor to guarantee that the model's arguments match the schema. | Google, Anthropic, OpenAI | It documents no cleaning of schemas. For Anthropic it removes the strict flag unless the caller sends a special Anthropic header, and the caller is not told. | **Compatibility code.** The Gateway has to remove from each schema what the receiving vendor rejects, and send any needed header for the caller. | Medium |
| 49 | **Streamed tool-call arguments, and tool calls cut off by the output limit.** Vendors stream a tool call's arguments in different ways, and a reply that reaches its output limit can stop in the middle of a tool call. | All | It streams tool calls in OpenAI's format and switches on Anthropic's option that streams arguments unchecked. In recorded traffic, a cut-off call from an OpenAI model is reported as an ordinary tool call, and only the vendor's own finish reason shows the cut. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to stream tool calls in OpenAI's format and report a cut-off call with the finish reason for the output limit, which already fails the round in the client today. | High |
| 50 | **Tool calling for models without built-in tool support.** The chat template of some open-weight models has no place for tool definitions, so the model cannot be offered tools in the usual way. | Providers that serve open-weight models | It may pass tool definitions on as written, convert them to the provider's format, or turn them into text for the model. The last is stated in one code comment, and another page says such tools are ignored. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has one tool dialect today, and Runvil's rule is to write another when a configured model needs one. | Medium |
| 51 | **Tools whose input is plain text instead of JSON.** OpenAI lets a tool take free text, optionally limited by a grammar, where an ordinary tool takes a JSON object. | OpenAI | It offers these tools only on its Responses route. Its chat completions route accepts only ordinary function tools and OpenRouter's own server-run tools. | **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would offer the tool to the model in OpenAI's plain-text form and hand each call to the client as an ordinary tool call with one text argument. | Low |
| 52 | **Tool search and deferred tool loading.** A request can hold back most of its tool definitions, and the model searches for a tool and gets its definition only when it needs it. | Anthropic, OpenAI | It passes a per-tool deferral flag through where the provider defers tools itself, and elsewhere withholds the tool until the conversation shows that it was found. A separate opt-in protocol has OpenRouter run the search and keep its record of loaded tools in an encrypted entry of each reply. | **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. The Gateway would use the vendor's feature where it exists and otherwise run the search itself, with its record of loaded tools in the opaque field, and the Engine would mark the tools to defer. | Medium |
| 53 | **Asynchronous tool calls.** The model's reply does not end at the tool call: it can go on to an answer, and the conversation can continue before the tool's result is delivered. | OpenAI | It supports this only on its Responses route. On its chat completions route it removes the tool's asynchronous flag, and the call runs like any other tool call. | **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral kind of tool result, if adopted. The Engine's tool loop would record a pending tool result, the same for every vendor, and the Gateway would turn it into OpenAI's own form or into a placeholder result. | Low |
| 54 | **Images in tool results.** A tool can return images as well as text for the model to look at. | Anthropic, Google, OpenAI | Its specification allows images in a tool result, and its Responses documentation says that they are forwarded only to models that take images. It does not publish what a text-only model receives. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. A tool result would hold images in the same way for every vendor, and the Gateway would decide for each model whether to forward them. | Medium |

**Notes on Table 8, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 42. The format for describing a tool

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI puts each definition under `tools[].function`. Anthropic uses `tools[]` and puts the argument schema in a field named `input_schema`. Google's Gemini uses a list of function declarations.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller writes definitions in OpenAI's layout. OpenRouter passes them on as written to providers that use OpenAI's interface and transforms them for the others.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing of a tool's name, description, or argument schema where the model supports tools. It does remove the strict flag for Anthropic models (row 48) and the asynchronous flag on its chat completions route (row 53).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** Each vendor translator in the Gateway has to rewrite the tool definitions into its vendor's layout.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter documents this conversion and every translator examined performs it, so Runvil's Gateway can too.
- **Evidence items:** T1, T2

#### Row 43. The words that say whether the model may or must call a tool

- **Vendors:** OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Mistral
- **What the vendor does:** In the order may, must not, must, and must call one named tool, OpenAI's `tool_choice` takes `auto`, `none`, `required`, and a named function. Anthropic writes `auto`, `none`, `any`, and `tool`. Google's Gemini writes `AUTO`, `NONE`, and `ANY`, and names a tool by limiting `ANY` to one name in `allowedFunctionNames`. Mistral writes `auto`, `none`, and `any`, and its documentation as read gives no form for a named tool. Gemini also has `VALIDATED`, a mode in which the model decides whether to call a function and each call is checked against the function's schema.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its chat completions route accepts the four OpenAI words, and its per-endpoint `supports_tool_choice` flags say which of them work.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Gemini's `VALIDATED` mode, which none of its three routes accepts, and, on the chat completions route, OpenAI's `allowed_tools`, which limits the model to a named subset of the request's tools. The Responses route accepts it.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to translate the caller's word into the vendor's word. The client sends only `auto` today.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional: the translation needs none. A forced tool choice would be one, if Runvil decides to offer it to prompts.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its routes and flags, and by the vendors for their words.
- **Confidence:** High - the translation is a fixed table of words that each vendor documents. Only Mistral's form for one named tool is missing from it.
- **Evidence items:** T7, T8, T54, GG83

#### Row 44. Models that refuse to be forced to call a tool

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Claude Fable 5.2, Mythos 5.2, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Fable 5.1, and Mythos 5.1), DeepSeek and Alibaba's Qwen (both with reasoning on), Zhipu's GLM, Meta
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic answers a forced tool choice on these six models with an HTTP 400: `tool_choice: type "tool" and "any" are not supported for this model.` DeepSeek answers with a 400 when reasoning is on. Alibaba's documentation says that Qwen with reasoning on supports neither `required` nor a named tool. Zhipu's and Meta's say that they accept `auto` only, and Meta's adds that every other value returns an HTTP 400.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its documentation says that it forwards the request and that "the provider's 400" comes back. Its `supports_tool_choice` flags show `required: false, function: false` on every endpoint of Claude Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5, and Sonnet 5.5 (the other three Claude models were not read) and on 12 of the 13 endpoints of `deepseek/deepseek-v4-pro`. For Meta the flags disagree with Meta's documentation: on two of the three Meta models read, they show all four values as supported. Nothing read settles whether OpenRouter rewrites the value for those two or the flags are wrong.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing compared with calling the vendor: OpenRouter adds no vendor-neutral way to say "the model must call a tool" that works on every model.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would look the model up in its per-model table, and for DeepSeek and Qwen also check whether reasoning is on. For a model that refuses, it would change the setting to `auto`, add an instruction that tells the model to call the tool, and report the change in `gateway_warning`. The alternative is to reject the request before sending it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No, beyond the forced tool choice itself (row 43).
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter and by the vendors. LiteLLM and the open-source gateway agentgateway translate a forced choice into the vendor's word and send it on: neither changes it to `auto` for a model that refuses.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the vendors document the refusals, but the fix has not been seen working in any gateway.
- **Evidence items:** T9, T10, T48, T55, T60, OV24, OV50, OV56, OV64, VV22

#### Row 45. The switch for several tool calls in one reply

- **Vendors:** OpenAI, Mistral, Anthropic, and open-weight models whose chat template allows only one tool call per reply, such as Meta's Llama 3.x
- **What the vendor does:** With OpenAI and Mistral, `parallel_tool_calls` set to true allows several calls. With Anthropic, `disable_parallel_tool_use` set to true forbids them.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its chat completions route accepts `parallel_tool_calls`. Its documentation does not say how it converts that switch into Anthropic's. Its model catalog lists `parallel_tool_calls` as supported on 18 of 476 models.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The catalog flag is no guide to which models honor the switch: the 18 include none from OpenAI, Anthropic, or Mistral.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The planned tool loop meets this behavior and needs no code for it: the client does not send the switch, each model follows its vendor's default, and the Engine already runs the calls of one reply as one batch. In a request that does have the switch, the Gateway's Anthropic translator has to flip the value: `parallel_tool_calls` set to false goes to Anthropic as `disable_parallel_tool_use` set to true, and the reverse. For a model that allows only one call per reply, the Gateway's model list has a flag (`parallel_tool_calls`), and what the Gateway should do with it was not examined.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its own switch. The conversion for Anthropic is taken from other open-source projects: LiteLLM and agentgateway both flip the value.
- **Confidence:** High - two translators flip the value the same way, so the conversion is known to work.
- **Evidence items:** T13, T14, T60, L48

#### Row 46. Tool-call id formats that differ by vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, Mistral, Amazon Bedrock, Moonshot (the chat templates of its Kimi K2 models), Google
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic requires an id to match `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+$`. Mistral's validator library, `mistral-common`, requires exactly nine alphanumerics on tokenizer versions 3 to 11 and any non-empty id on newer ones, and Mistral answers a wrong id with an HTTP 400 and its error code 3280. Amazon Bedrock allows 64 characters at most. Moonshot's guide for the open-weight Kimi K2 model gives ids the form `functions.name:idx`. Google's Gemini could send a call with no id before Gemini 3, and since then the result has to quote the call's id.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller receives each id as the vendor wrote it. Recorded traffic shows ids that start with `toolu_`, `toolu_bdrk_`, `call_`, and `chatcmpl-tool-`, nine-character ids from Mistral, and `functions.name:0`. It also shows one vendor's ids accepted by another with an HTTP 200, and no case of another vendor's id sent to Mistral. OpenRouter does not document whether it rewrites ids for the next vendor. Reports of Mistral's error arriving through OpenRouter end on 2026-03-30, the day before a commenter who said he works at OpenRouter promised a fix that nothing since confirms.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing in replies. In requests, a caller cannot count on another vendor's id being accepted.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** In a request, the Gateway has to rewrite each id to fit the vendor the request is going to, the same way on the tool call and on the result that quotes it. A hash of the original id does this without storing anything. In a reply, before the client sees it, the Gateway has to replace an id that is empty or repeats an earlier one, because some providers send such ids and the Engine requires ids to be unique across the whole conversation. Each request holds the earlier ids, so this needs no store either. The code is small, one function for each vendor's id rule. Not designed: what happens when two ids hash to the same value (opencode hashes again), and how a replaced id stays matched to the vendor's own block in the opaque field.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter, from recorded traffic and third-party reports. Four open-source projects rewrite ids in requests: Bifrost, opencode, pi, and the harness Roo Code.
- **Confidence:** High - four projects rewrite ids the way the Gateway would, and each vendor's rule is known.
- **Evidence items:** T15, T16, T18, T59, T62, T64, OV30, OV37, OV38, OV41, GG85, J34, L46, WT27, WT28, VT15, VV20

#### Row 47. The required place and order of tool results

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, Google
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic requires all the results for one reply's tool calls to be in the user message that follows that reply, ahead of anything else in that message. Google requires all of a reply's tool calls to come first, followed by all of their results, in the order of the calls according to its Vertex AI documentation. Each rejects a request that breaks its rule with an HTTP 400.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends each result as a standard OpenAI-style message with the role `tool`. OpenRouter does not document how it regroups those messages for each vendor.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing of a text result (row 54 covers images).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to regroup the tool results into the layout that the vendor requires. The Engine already produces one `tool` message for each call today, directly after the assistant record that made the calls.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the `tool` message format. The vendors' rules are from earlier Runvil research (the 2026-09-20 report "Per-model tool differences: what varies, how much it matters, and what Runvil must accommodate").
- **Confidence:** High - both vendors document their rules, so the layout the Gateway has to produce is known.
- **Evidence items:** T19, T49, GG9, GG85

#### Row 48. Tool schemas that a vendor rejects, and the strict flag

- **Vendors:** Google, Anthropic, OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** Google's older `parameters` field accepts only a subset of the schema format of OpenAPI. Anthropic requires the root of the schema to be an object. With `strict` on, Anthropic rejects, among others, `oneOf`, `not`, and limits such as `minimum` and `maxLength`, and OpenAI requires `additionalProperties: false` and every property marked required.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Nothing in its documentation says that it changes a schema. For Anthropic models it removes `strict` from the tool definitions unless the caller sends the header `x-anthropic-beta: structured-outputs-2025-11-13`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A request that asked for strict checking runs without it, and the caller is not told.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** The Gateway has to remove from each schema what the receiving vendor rejects, and itself send any header the vendor needs. The same cleaning applies to the schema of a structured reply (row 55). For Gemini this is small and getting smaller. The older field needs references inlined (each reference to a shared definition replaced with the definition itself) and keywords such as `additionalProperties` dropped. The newer fields `parametersJsonSchema` and `responseJsonSchema` take plain JSON Schema, and the projects that use them change little more than `const`, which they write as a one-value `enum`. Not designed: whether the Gateway removes a keyword that strict mode rejects or drops `strict` instead, and how it tells the caller (`gateway_warning` is the field proposed for dropped settings).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. Seven open-source projects show the Gemini part. Four of them clean schemas for the older field: agentgateway, LiteLLM, the gateway Portkey's AI Gateway, and the model library LangChain. Three send plain JSON Schema: Vercel AI SDK, Bifrost, and the harness Gemini CLI.
- **Confidence:** Medium - seven projects show the Gemini part, but what each vendor rejects keeps changing, so the Gateway's rules need upkeep.
- **Evidence items:** T20, T21, T22, T51, T58, T62, GG87, GX36 to GX45

#### Row 49. Streamed tool-call arguments, and tool calls cut off by the output limit

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** In a streamed reply, OpenAI sends a tool call's argument JSON in fragments, and Gemini sends each call whole. Anthropic has an eager streaming option that sends argument text as the model produces it, unchecked, so the caller can receive invalid JSON. A reply cut off in the middle of a tool call ends with the vendor's own finish reason for the output limit.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route, tool calls arrive in OpenAI's streaming format, as entries in `delta.tool_calls[]` of each chunk. OpenRouter sets Anthropic's `eager_input_streaming: true` on every streamed request that has tools. Recorded traffic shows a cut-off tool call reported as `finish_reason: "tool_calls"`, the value for an ordinary tool call, beside `native_finish_reason: "length"` (or `"max_output_tokens"`, depending on the OpenAI protocol that OpenRouter used). `native_finish_reason` is the field in which OpenRouter passes on the vendor's own value. The 24 replies that rig recorded for this case on 2026-09-23 are all from OpenAI models: the 16 with a low or middle output limit show this, and the other 8 hold complete calls.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The cut does not show in `finish_reason`.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send tool calls to the client in OpenAI's streaming format and report the finish reason `length` for a cut-off call. The finish reason is in the last chunk, so the Gateway can still set it after the arguments have gone out. The client already fails the round today when a reply's tool calls end with `length`. The Gateway should also check that a finished call's arguments are valid JSON. What it does when they are not is not designed: today the Engine rejects such a call.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the streaming format and the eager streaming setting. How a cut-off call is reported is inferred about OpenRouter from rig's recorded traffic.
- **Confidence:** High - the fix is one value that the client already handles. The recordings are from OpenAI models only, and the answer does not depend on them: the Gateway would turn each vendor's own value for the output limit into `length`.
- **Evidence items:** T24 to T28, O78, AN78, F51, WT29, VF4, L44, L45

#### Row 50. Tool calling for models without built-in tool support

- **Vendors:** Providers that serve open-weight models
- **What the vendor does:** The model's chat template has no place for tool definitions.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its one `tools` field can get three treatments: tool definitions are passed to the provider as written, or transformed into the provider's own format, or turned into a YAML template, text that describes the tools to the model. The third is stated in a single code comment in its documentation. It conflicts with another page, which says that when none of a model's providers supports a setting, "the parameter is ignored".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Knowledge of which treatment a request got: the reply does not say.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has one tool dialect today, `gemma3_tool_code`, and Runvil's rule is to write another when a configured model needs one. A dialect is translation: it changes only how the tools and the calls are written.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. The dialect and the rule are from earlier Runvil research, which read the `runvil-gateway` code and Runvil's design document for the Gateway on 2026-11-22.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the Gateway's own dialect shows that the conversion works for one model, and each further model needs its own. OpenRouter's one code comment adds little support.
- **Evidence items:** T1, T5, L108

#### Row 51. Tools whose input is plain text instead of JSON

- **Vendors:** OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI has custom tools, also called freeform tools, whose input is plain text. A custom tool can come with a grammar that the text has to follow, written in the `lark` grammar syntax or as a `regex`. `apply_patch`, a tool of OpenAI's Codex harness whose input is the text of a patch, is one.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It offers custom tools only on its Responses route. On its chat completions route the accepted tool types are `function` and OpenRouter's own server tools.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The whole feature, on the chat completions route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would offer the tool to an OpenAI model as a custom tool and hand each call to the client as an ordinary function call with one string argument that holds the text. It is compatibility code because the Gateway changes the call instead of translating it. Without that, the universal API, the client, and the Engine would all need a second format for a tool call, which would be a new vendor-neutral field.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No: to the client and the Engine the tool stays an ordinary function with one string argument. How the Gateway would know which tools to offer as custom tools, and with what grammar, is not designed.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for where custom tools are offered.
- **Confidence:** Low - the conversion is an inference: no project examined does it.
- **Evidence items:** OA54, T37

#### Row 52. Tool search and deferred tool loading

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** Deferred definitions stay out of the prefix until the model's search finds them. Anthropic marks a tool with `defer_loading`. Its search tool has a regex variant and a BM25 variant, which ranks tools by keyword relevance, and a search returns `tool_reference` blocks that name the tools found. OpenAI has a `tool_search` tool and namespaces (named groups of tools). Each vendor's blocks that record a search have to be sent back unchanged in later requests.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It accepts a `defer_loading` flag on each tool and passes it through where the endpoint defers natively, such as Claude models at Anthropic and Vertex. Elsewhere it withholds a flagged tool until the caller sends it again without the flag or, on its Responses and Messages routes, a search names it: OpenRouter's own regex search there matches the model's regular expression against the deferred tools. `deferred_tools` is a separate request field, and the only search on the chat completions route. With it OpenRouter runs the search on any route and keeps its own record of loaded tools as an encrypted entry in each reply (its documentation says "inside the encrypted reasoning it returns"), which the caller has to send back unchanged.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Where the endpoint does not defer natively, a `tool_reference` block in a tool result on the Messages route "reaches the provider as text". A request that names the BM25 variant gets an HTTP 400: OpenRouter's search tool has only the regex variant.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. The Gateway would use the vendor's own feature where there is one (its search blocks come back through the opaque field). Elsewhere it would do what OpenRouter does: hold the deferred definitions back, offer the model a search tool, answer the search itself, and add the definitions found, with its record of loaded tools in the opaque field. The Engine would mark the tools to defer with a vendor-neutral flag. Not designed: how the Gateway would answer a search in the middle of a request, and what happens to its record when a conversation is compacted or moves to another model.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a deferral flag on a tool definition.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. The vendors' own rules are from earlier Runvil research: the 2026-10-14 report "What to steal: how popular harnesses handle MCP servers and dynamic tools, and how models are trained for them".
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenRouter's `deferred_tools` is the same design, so a gateway can do it. But OpenRouter's record breaks when earlier messages are edited, trimmed, or compacted, and Runvil compacts conversations.
- **Evidence items:** P46 to P53, AN62, AN63

#### Row 53. Asynchronous tool calls

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (GPT-6 models)
- **What the vendor does:** Normally a reply ends at a tool call, and the next request has to hold the result. With `async: true` on a tool definition, the reply can hold the call and an answer, and later requests may add messages before one of them delivers the result. OpenAI marks each such call in its reply with `async: true` and expects the mark back when the call is sent again.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It supports asynchronous tools only on its Responses route. On the chat completions route "the flag is removed before the request is routed and the call behaves like any other tool call".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The whole feature, on the chat completions route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral kind of tool result, if adopted. The Engine's tool loop would get a pending tool result, a marker for a result that will arrive in a later request, which is the same for every vendor. The Gateway would turn it into OpenAI's own form on GPT-6 models and into a placeholder result on every other model. Not designed: how a tool is marked as asynchronous, and where the Gateway keeps OpenAI's per-call mark between requests, for which the opaque field is one candidate.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a pending tool result, which the universal API has to be able to hold.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. OpenRouter's own agent SDK, its official client library for agents, does asynchronous tools in the client for any model by giving the model placeholder results. Vercel AI SDK and Bifrost keep OpenAI's mark with each call and send it back.
- **Confidence:** Low - no project examined does this for a caller that speaks chat completions, as Runvil's Gateway would.
- **Evidence items:** OA42, OA43, CT39 to CT41

#### Row 54. Images in tool results

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, Google, OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** Each accepts images in a tool result.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its API specification lets a `tool` message on the chat completions route hold content parts (text, images, and so on). Its documentation of the Responses route says that the non-text parts of a tool result are "only forwarded to supported multimodal models".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Knowledge of what a text-only model receives in place of the images.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. A record in the Engine can already hold text parts and image-URL parts on any role, and whether Runvil's own tools return images today was not checked. A tool result that holds images would be the same for every vendor. The Gateway would forward the images to models that take them. What it should send a text-only model in their place is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional: a tool result that can hold images, needed only if Runvil adopts the feature. It takes no new field, because a `tool` message can already hold content parts in OpenRouter's specification.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** Medium - forwarding images to a model that takes them is plain translation, but no project examined shows what to send a text-only model.
- **Evidence items:** T43, L102

### 3.5 Structured output and message layout: the Gateway can translate both

Rows 55 and 56 are about a JSON reply, with or without a schema that the reply must match. The schema is written in JSON Schema, the standard language for describing what a piece of JSON must look like. A prompt would supply it through one new vendor-neutral setting, called a response schema.

Message layout means where a request holds its system text, which roles its messages have, and how they are ordered (rows 58 to 60). System text is the standing instruction a caller gives a model ahead of the conversation. In chat completions it is a message with the role `system`.

The Gateway can do all the vendor-specific work for these and for refusals (row 57), by translation alone except in row 56. None of it is built yet.

**How to read Table 9.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 9. Structured output and message layout: 6 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 55 | **Asking for JSON output, with or without a schema.** A caller asks for valid JSON, or for JSON that matches a schema, and each vendor has its own request field for this. | OpenAI, Anthropic, Google | It accepts OpenAI's form of the request for every vendor, and it publishes for each endpoint whether the endpoint supports a schema request. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would translate one vendor-neutral response schema into each vendor's field. Schema keywords that a vendor rejects would be removed by the compatibility code that cleans tool schemas (row 48). | High |
| 56 | **A schema request to an endpoint that cannot enforce one.** The same model can enforce a schema at one provider and not at another. | Anthropic (Claude served by Amazon Bedrock), others | Its documentation gives two answers: one page says such a request fails, another says the schema setting is ignored. | **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would get the JSON through a forced tool call, which does not guarantee a match with the schema, or it would check the reply and report a mismatch. | Medium |
| 57 | **Refusals.** The model declines to answer, and the vendor reports this inside a successful reply and not as an error. | OpenAI, Anthropic | On its chat completions route it returns the refusal text in a refusal field and sets the finish reason to content filter. Anthropic's refusal category is dropped there. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert each vendor's refusal into the standard refusal field and the content-filter finish reason, and the client has to read that field, which is the same change for every vendor. | High |
| 58 | **Where system text goes.** Each vendor expects system text in a different place in the request. | Anthropic, Google, OpenAI | The caller sends a system message, and for Anthropic OpenRouter moves it into Anthropic's top-level system field. What it sends to OpenAI is undocumented. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to move system text into each vendor's own field, except that for OpenAI's Responses API it should send the text as a first message with the role developer, which keeps it eligible for the prompt cache. | Medium |
| 59 | **System messages in the middle of a conversation.** A caller places a system message between two messages of a conversation instead of at the start. | Anthropic, others | It states no rule. One sentence in its documentation reads as if, for Claude models, the text of such a message is moved to Anthropic's top-level system field. | **Gateway only.** No code is needed today, because the Engine allows system records only at the start of its message list. If that changed, the Gateway would have to leave such a message in place for the models that accept one. | Medium |
| 60 | **Message cleanup rules.** Each vendor has its own rules for a valid list of messages, and a gateway has to tidy a request to fit them. | Anthropic, Google | It merges messages and renames roles inside its own translator, so the caller does not have to. It does not publish its rules. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to drop empty text blocks for Anthropic and rename roles for Google, and it should merge same-role messages for Google. | High |

**Notes on Table 9, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 55. Asking for JSON output, with or without a schema

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (`response_format`), Anthropic (`output_config.format`), Google (`responseJsonSchema`)
- **What the vendor does:** A request without a schema, called JSON mode, asks only for valid JSON. Each vendor has its own field and accepts a different subset of JSON Schema.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter accepts OpenAI's field, `response_format`, for every vendor. For each endpoint that supports a schema request, it lists `structured_outputs` among the endpoint's parameters. The listing does not say how strictly the schema is enforced. That, OpenRouter says, "varies by provider": some providers guarantee a match and others "treat it as a strong hint".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The guarantee that the reply matches the schema.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. Today the client builds its request body from nine keys (`model`, `messages`, `stream`, `stream_options`, `tools`, `tool_choice`, `temperature`, `max_tokens`, `chat_template_kwargs`), none of which asks for JSON or a schema, and a search of the client's and the Engine's code found no `response_format`. If Runvil adopted the feature, a prompt's Lua code would set the response schema, the Engine and the client would pass it on, and the Gateway would translate it into the vendor's field. The compatibility code that cleans tool schemas (row 48) would also remove the schema keywords a vendor rejects, so a prompt author writes plain JSON Schema and not one vendor's subset.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a response schema. Its form is not designed.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, whose documentation describes `response_format`, `structured_outputs`, and the uneven enforcement.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter performs this translation, and each vendor documents its own field. It is less certain that a reply then matches its schema.
- **Evidence items:** T30 to T34, AN76, GG90, L49, T22

#### Row 56. A schema request to an endpoint that cannot enforce one

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Claude models served by Amazon Bedrock), and some endpoints of other vendors' models
- **What the vendor does:** Whether a schema is enforced depends on the endpoint, not on the vendor or the model. OpenRouter lists `structured_outputs` at 3 of the 5 endpoints of Anthropic's Claude Sonnet 5.5, with Amazon Bedrock among the two without it, and at 38 of the 46 endpoints of Zhipu's GLM 5.3. An endpoint without it only writes free text, and nothing there enforces a schema.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its documentation gives two answers: one page says the "request will fail", and another says the "parameter is ignored". Its response healing, an optional add-on, repairs broken JSON syntax in a reply on non-streaming requests only, so a caller that always streams, as Runvil's client does, gets no repair.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A consistent answer on whether its request will fail.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code**, if adopted. The Gateway would know such an endpoint from the model sheet's `structured_outputs` flag, which it does not read today. It could turn the response schema into a tool definition, force the model to call that tool, and return the call's arguments as the reply, as Bifrost does for Anthropic's models on Google Vertex AI. Nothing guarantees that the arguments match the schema: OpenRouter counts tool arguments that fail their schema when it scores providers, and no source gives their rate. Or the Gateway could pass the schema to the model in a system message, as Bifrost does for DeepSeek, check the reply against the schema, and report a mismatch. How it would report one on a streamed reply, the only kind Runvil's client gets, is not designed: an error and `gateway_warning` are the candidates. Either way the prompt gets a weaker guarantee than it asked for.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. It uses the response schema of row 55.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from another open-source project, Bifrost, because OpenRouter's documentation contradicts itself. The check-and-report way is from a Runvil survey of 2026-10-28.
- **Confidence:** Medium - only Bifrost's code shows the forced tool call, and nothing was run.
- **Evidence items:** T34, T35, J-table 2, T33, T23, J46, W65, L49

#### Row 57. Refusals

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (`refusal`), Anthropic (`stop_reason: "refusal"` with `stop_details`)
- **What the vendor does:** The reply has HTTP status 200. OpenAI puts the refusal text in a `refusal` field. Anthropic sets `stop_reason: "refusal"` and adds a `stop_details` object with a category, such as `cyber`, and an explanation.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the reply has `message.refusal` together with `finish_reason: "content_filter"`. A policy block, where the provider reports the refusal as an error and not as a reply, comes back as HTTP 403 with `error_type: "refusal"`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** On the chat completions route, Anthropic's category. OpenRouter's Messages route keeps it.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert each vendor's refusal into the standard `refusal` field and set the finish reason to `content_filter`. For Anthropic the text can be the explanation in `stop_details`, as in LiteLLM. The client keeps no `refusal` field today, so a refusal with no other text reaches the Engine as an empty reply. The client has to read the field, which is the same change for every vendor. Two points are not designed, and neither puts vendor code outside the Gateway: whether Runvil keeps the category, for which chat completions has no field, and how the refusal text reaches a prompt, since none of the five fields Lua reads holds it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. `refusal` is already a field of the chat completions format.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, whose documentation gives this conversion and the HTTP 403 error. The client's part is from Runvil's code.
- **Confidence:** High - the vendor-specific part is one conversion, which OpenRouter performs, and it documents refusals on all three of its routes.
- **Evidence items:** T36, O17, AN72, AN73, AN75, L36, L37

#### Row 58. Where system text goes

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (top-level `system`), Google (`systemInstruction`), OpenAI (top-level `instructions`, with a new rule from GPT-6.2 on)
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI's `instructions` is a field of its Responses API. From GPT-6.2 on, OpenAI appends top-level `instructions` after `input`, the list that holds the conversation, and bills them uncached: at the normal input price, even when the rest of the request was served from the prompt cache.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends a `system` message. For Anthropic, OpenRouter moves it out of the message list into Anthropic's top-level `system` blocks. Its documentation prints one example: a request, and the body OpenRouter sent to Anthropic for it, as echoed by the `debug: {"echo_upstream_body": true}` option. For OpenAI it documents nothing.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Knowing in advance whether, for OpenAI, the text goes into `instructions`, where GPT-6.2 and later models bill it uncached. The echo option shows it for one request at a time, on streamed requests only, and OpenRouter says not to use it in production.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to move system text into `system` for Anthropic and into `systemInstruction` for Google. For OpenAI's Responses API it should leave `instructions` empty and send the text as the first message in `input`, with the role `developer`, which is where OpenAI says to put instructions so that they stay eligible for the prompt cache.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for Anthropic, in the printed example. Stated by the vendor for Google's field, OpenAI's GPT-6.2 rule, and OpenAI's advice to use a `developer` message.
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenAI's rule was five days old when it was read on 2026-11-22 and may still change.
- **Evidence items:** O67, OA62, T38, GG88, T39

#### Row 59. System messages in the middle of a conversation

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (placement rules on 11 models), others
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic accepts such a message, with the role `system` inside the message list, on 11 Claude models and not on Claude Sonnet 5. The message cannot be first, must directly follow a user message or an assistant message that ends in a vendor-run tool's result, must come before an assistant message or be last, and cannot be placed between a tool call and its result. Any other position gets an HTTP 400 error. DeepSeek's chat API also accepts such messages.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its one statement is about its own extension to chat completions for changing the reasoning effort partway through a conversation: a system message with no text and a `configuration_update` field (row 27). On Claude models, it says, that field "stays inline in `messages` while ordinary system text is still sent as the top-level `system` prompt", so a system message with both text and the field is split in two. That reads as if the text is moved to the top.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown. OpenRouter states no rule for such a message, for Claude or for any other vendor's models.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** This row is counted with the planned work because it needs no code: the Engine rejects any edit that puts a system record after another record, so a system message can only lead the list today and the Gateway never receives one in the middle of a conversation. If Runvil ever allowed one, the Gateway would have to leave it in place for the models that accept it, because Anthropic says that moving it into `system` "makes the thinking already in the conversation invalid".
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter, from that one sentence. The vendors' rules are from their own documentation, and the Engine's from Runvil's code and design decisions.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Runvil's code shows that it never sends such a message, but only Anthropic's documentation says what the Gateway would have to do with one.
- **Evidence items:** AN79, AN80, T39, AN6, L23, T55, R30

#### Row 60. Message cleanup rules

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (rejects empty text blocks, merges same-role messages itself), Google (roles `user` and `model`)
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic answers a request that has an empty or whitespace-only text block with HTTP 400. Google accepts two roles only. Its own harness, Gemini CLI, makes the two alternate before each request, and whether Google's API requires that was not checked.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter merges messages and renames roles inside its own translator, so the caller does not have to. It does not publish its rules.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Advance knowledge of how its messages will be combined, and nothing else.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to drop empty and whitespace-only text blocks before it sends a request to Anthropic, because the Engine appends an assistant record with empty text for each round in which the model asks for tool calls. For Google it has to rename roles (`assistant` to `model`, and a tool result inside a `user` message), and it should merge consecutive messages from the same role, as Gemini CLI does. All three change the layout of a request and not what it asks for, so they are translation and not compatibility code.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, whose documentation says that it combines and formats messages for each provider. Anthropic's rules are quoted from its documentation in the 2026-11-17 report, which also records the Engine's empty assistant records. Google's roles are from its documentation.
- **Confidence:** High - Anthropic's rules and Google's two roles are documented.
- **Evidence items:** T38 to T40, GG88, GG31

### 3.6 Vendor-run tools, citations, files, and media: mostly features Runvil has not adopted

This group has thirteen features: vendor-run tools (vendors call them server tools), the citations and stored files that go with them, and media such as PDFs, images, audio, and video. An ordinary tool is one that the caller defines and the caller's program runs.

Nine of the thirteen are marked "if adopted". Six of the nine concern tools that a vendor runs and what those tools return (rows 61 to 65 and 73). Runvil searches the web and runs code with ordinary tools of its own, so a vendor-run tool is a choice for Runvil and not a need. The other three are files and media that Runvil's requests and replies have no place for today (rows 68, 69, and 71). Two features need no vendor-specific code, because Runvil's own tools meet the tool loop's need: tools whose schema a vendor fixes (row 66) and MCP run by the vendor (row 67). Two are part of the planned work: image input (row 70), since the Engine's messages can contain images today, and Google's refusal of a request's input (row 72), which any request to a Gemini model can meet. The new vendor-neutral settings and fields that the notes name are not designed.

Terms used in this subsection: a content part is chat completions' name for one block of a message's content. An annotation is an entry on a reply that ties a span of the reply's text to a source. Display duties are rules in a vendor's terms of service about what an application (in Runvil, a Host such as Workshop) must show its user.

**How to read Table 10.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 10. Vendor-run tools, citations, files, and media: 13 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 61 | **Web search run by the vendor.** Each of these vendors has a web search tool of its own that the model can use while it answers. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, xAI | It offers one vendor-neutral search tool on every model. By default the tool uses the vendor's own search where the provider offers it, and the search service Exa elsewhere. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. A prompt would keep offering Runvil's own search tool, and that tool's code or the Gateway would answer its calls from the vendor's search. | Medium |
| 62 | **Blocks from vendor-run tools that must be sent back unchanged.** A reply in which the vendor ran a tool contains blocks that record the call and its result, and the vendor expects them back unchanged in the next request. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google | It shows a tool call in a different form on each of its three routes. On its chat completions route a record of the call, counts of tool calls in the usage, and citation annotations replace the vendor's own blocks. | **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, if adopted. The Gateway would store the vendor's tool blocks in the opaque field and send them back on the next request. | Medium |
| 63 | **A turn that the vendor paused.** When Anthropic's loop of vendor-run tool calls reaches its limit, Anthropic ends the reply before the turn is finished, and the caller has to send that unfinished reply back so the model can continue. | Anthropic | The pause is in the list of stop reasons on its Messages route. What a caller on its chat completions route sees is not documented. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would itself send the unfinished reply back to Anthropic, inside the same client request, so the client sees one reply. | Medium |
| 64 | **Citations.** When a model's answer draws on web pages or on documents, each vendor returns the sources in a format of its own. | OpenAI, Anthropic, Google | On its chat completions route it returns web citations as a list of annotations on the reply, in OpenAI's format. Citations on documents that the caller supplied are available on its Messages route only. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would convert each vendor's web citations to OpenAI's annotation format, and the client, the Engine, and Lua would get a vendor-neutral way to read annotations on a reply. | Medium |
| 65 | **Code execution and containers run by the vendor.** The vendor runs the model's code in a container on its own servers, and some vendors return a container id that the caller sends back to keep the container's state. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google | It does not wrap the vendors' code execution. It offers sandbox tools of its own, which are not available on its chat completions route, and its Responses route accepts OpenAI's own tool. | **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. Runvil runs code with its own tools today, and if it used the vendor's version the Gateway would store the container id and the result blocks in the opaque field. | Low |
| 66 | **Computer use and other caller-run tools whose schema the vendor fixes.** The caller's program runs the tool, such as one through which the model operates a computer, but the vendor decides the tool's name and the form of its arguments. | Anthropic, OpenAI | Its Messages route accepts Anthropic's bash and text editor tools and rejects Anthropic's computer tools. Its Responses route accepts OpenAI's computer tool. | **Gateway only.** No vendor-specific code is needed: a tool that Runvil defines itself, with its own schema, is an ordinary tool, which the Gateway translates like any other, and works on every vendor. | Medium |
| 67 | **Remote MCP run by the vendor.** The vendor, not the caller's program, connects to the MCP server, from its own servers. | Anthropic, OpenAI | Only its Responses route has MCP tools that OpenRouter or the provider runs. Its guide tells callers to turn MCP tools into ordinary tools and run them themselves. | **Gateway only.** No vendor-specific code is needed: Runvil's MCP Pack already connects to MCP servers from inside the Host, and the Gateway sees only ordinary tools. | High |
| 68 | **Vendor file stores and file search.** The caller uploads a file to one account at one vendor and refers to it by an id in later requests, and the vendor can search the stored files for the model. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google | It has a Files API of its own with its own file ids. It can also forward an upload to OpenAI's or Anthropic's store, using the caller's own key for that vendor. | **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. To use a vendor's store directly, Runvil would upload to that vendor's own route and write its file id into a message. A files route in the Gateway that is the same for every vendor would make the feature Gateway only. | Medium |
| 69 | **PDF and document input.** The caller sends a PDF or another document in a message, and some models read the file directly while others need its text extracted first. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google | Its chat completions route accepts a file as one part of a message's content. It hands the file to models that read files and converts it to text for the others. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would pass the file through or convert it to text, and the Engine would get a new vendor-neutral content part for files. | Medium |
| 70 | **Image, audio, and video input.** The caller sends media to the model as a URL or as data placed inside the request, and each provider sets its own limits on what it accepts. | Every vendor with models that accept images. Google for video. | It has one content part type each for images, audio, and video. It sends a video URL only to providers that accept one. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to replace a URL with the data itself where a vendor needs that. The Engine has image parts today and would need new vendor-neutral part types for audio and video. | Medium |
| 71 | **Image and audio output from a chat model.** Some chat models return images or audio inside their reply, next to the text. | Google, OpenAI | It uses fields of its own that OpenAI's message format lacks: one to ask for media, one for images in the reply, and one for streamed audio. Google signs each image, and OpenRouter has not published how it returns those signatures. | **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, plus a new vendor-neutral field, if adopted. The Gateway would store Google's signatures on images in the opaque field. | Low |
| 72 | **Safety settings and blocked requests.** Google lets a request set content-filter thresholds in Google's own categories, and it reports a request whose input it refuses as a successful reply that contains no answer. | Google | Its chat completions route has no field for the safety settings. It turns a blocked request into an error with HTTP status 403. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway should take default safety settings from a model entry, where the operator sets them, and it has to turn a blocked request into an ordinary reply whose finish reason says the content was filtered. | Medium |

The opaque field would hold the signed parts of Google's tools. | Low |

**Notes on Table 10, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 61. Web search run by the vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, xAI
- **What the vendor does:** Each vendor has a vendor-run web search tool of its own.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It offers one vendor-neutral tool, `{"type": "openrouter:web_search"}`, on every model. The tool's `parameters.engine` setting takes `auto`, `native`, or the name of a search engine. `auto`, the default, uses the vendor's own tool where OpenRouter's record of the endpoint lists it in `native_tools`, and Exa elsewhere.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** With the `native` engine, which is the vendor's own search, most of the tool's vendor-neutral settings are ignored. A model can also have the vendor's search on only some of its provider endpoints, so the provider decides which search runs.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. A prompt would offer Runvil's own search tool as it does today. Today that tool searches through the Gateway's route `/v1/tools/web_search`. The vendor's search would supply the results in one of two ways. Either the tool's handler, the code that runs the tool, sends a second model request, the Gateway switches the vendor's search on in it, and the handler returns that reply's text as the tool result. The conversation then holds an ordinary tool call and a text result.
