Or the Gateway runs the tool loop itself: it answers each search call inside the client's request and returns one finished reply, so the conversation holds only that reply. Not designed: what tells the Gateway to use the vendor's search in the first way, and where it would get the vendor's results in the second. The vendor's blocks never enter the conversation (row 62).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No for a prompt, which offers the same tool either way (in rows 65 and 73 the vendor's tool would itself run in the conversation). The handler's second request, in the first way, might need a new field to ask for search.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its search tool. Gemini CLI, Google's open-source harness, runs Google Search the first way, as an ordinary tool that makes a second model request. OpenRouter, LiteLLM, and Bifrost run tool loops inside the gateway, the second way.
- **Confidence:** Medium - projects show both ways, the first with a vendor's search and the second with tools that the gateway runs itself.
- **Evidence items:** P10 to P14, AN37 to AN41, GG68, GX13 to GX22, CT35

#### Row 62. Blocks from vendor-run tools that must be sent back unchanged

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`server_tool_use` blocks, and `web_search_tool_result` blocks with `encrypted_content`), OpenAI (`web_search_call` and other call items), Google (`toolCall` and `toolResponse` parts, every one signed)
- **What the vendor does:** A reply in which the vendor ran a tool contains blocks that record the call and its result, and the vendor expects them back unchanged in the next request. Anthropic answers 400 when a result block's `encrypted_content` is missing or changed, and Google errors when a signed part is missing. Anthropic documents no rule for a call and its result that are both left out. The model would then lack what the tool returned.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It shows a tool call in a different form on each of its three routes. On its chat completions route the form is an entry of type `reasoning.server_tool_call` in `reasoning_details`, a record that the caller sends back, which its API specification describes and its guides do not. On its Responses route the form is output items with a named type and readable fields. On its Messages route the form is Anthropic's own blocks.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The vendor's own blocks, on the chat completions route: the caller gets the record, counts of tool calls in the usage, and annotations.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, if adopted. Once the opaque field exists, the Gateway would store the vendor's tool blocks in it and send them back on the next request.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its API specification, for its three forms. opencode, the model library TensorZero, and rig send the vendor's raw blocks back as they received them, and LiteLLM keeps them in an extra message field, `provider_specific_fields`, that the caller sends back.
- **Confidence:** Medium - four open-source projects send the vendor's blocks back, but only one of them is a gateway, and OpenRouter's own chat completions route does not return the blocks.
- **Evidence items:** P25 to P36, GG13, H8, H45, H66, K45, CT28 to CT33, WT31 to WT33

#### Row 63. A turn that the vendor paused

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (the stop reason `pause_turn`)
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic runs a Claude model's loop of vendor-run tool calls and results. When the loop reaches its limit, 10 iterations per request by default, Anthropic ends the reply with the stop reason `pause_turn` before the turn is finished. To continue, the caller sends the unfinished reply back unchanged, with the same tools, in a new request.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** `pause_turn` is in the list of stop-reason values of its Messages route. Neither its documentation nor its API specification says what a caller on its chat completions route sees when Anthropic pauses.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown on the chat completions route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. Anthropic pauses only when a vendor-run tool is offered to the model. The Gateway would then itself send the unfinished reply back to Anthropic, inside the same client request, so the client sees one reply.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor for the pause and how to continue, and stated by OpenRouter for the list of stop-reason values only. Pydantic AI sends the unfinished reply back by itself, up to 10 times. No gateway examined does that for Anthropic's pause: LiteLLM and Bifrost pass it on to the caller, as does the model library LangChain. In the tool loops that OpenRouter, LiteLLM, and Bifrost run themselves, the caller never sees a pause.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the answer follows Anthropic's documented way to continue, but only a library was seen doing it. Passing the pause on has no agreed form: six projects mark it in five ways, from the finish reason `length` to the raw value `pause_turn`.
- **Evidence items:** P32, P34, P85, VV8, CT1, CT8, CT12, CT14, CT15, CT18, CT19, CT20, CT27

#### Row 64. Citations

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (`url_citation` annotations), Anthropic (citation objects, and citations on supplied documents), Google (grounding metadata, with display duties)
- **What the vendor does:** Each vendor returns the sources of an answer in a format of its own. Google's grounding metadata is an object that lists the search queries, the web sources, and the parts of the text that each source supports.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route, citations are entries in `message.annotations[]`, each with a nested `url_citation` object. Citations on supplied documents are available on its Messages route only.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Its documentation says nothing about the vendors' display duties or about Google's search suggestions snippet, a piece of HTML that Google's terms require showing "to the end user who submitted the prompt".
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would convert each vendor's web citations to `annotations` entries with `url_citation`, a format in OpenAI's own chat completions reference. The client, the Engine, and Lua would get a way to read annotations, the same for every vendor. Not designed: how a citation on a supplied document, which has no URL, would be returned, and how Google's snippet would reach the application, whose duty it is to show it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: annotations on a reply.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the annotation format: it converts every vendor's web citations to it. Stated by the vendor for Google's terms. The point about the snippet is taken from nine open-source projects, in which it reaches the application only inside a raw Gemini object, which is Google's grounding data passed through unconverted.
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenRouter shows the conversion of web citations working, and the display duties remain the application's.
- **Evidence items:** P38 to P45, Q47, GX13, GX23

#### Row 65. Code execution and containers run by the vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, OpenAI, Google
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor runs the model's code on its own servers in a container, an isolated environment for running code that is also called a sandbox. Anthropic and OpenAI return a container id, and the caller sends it back in later requests to keep the container's state.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It does not wrap the vendors' code execution. It offers sandbox tools of its own, `openrouter:shell` and `openrouter:bash`, which are not available on its chat completions route. Its Responses route accepts OpenAI's own `code_interpreter` tool type.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The vendor's feature, on the chat completions route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. If Runvil used the vendor's version, the Gateway would store the container id and the result blocks in the opaque field and send them back on the next request.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a vendor-neutral way for a prompt to ask for vendor-run code execution.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its sandbox tools. Stated by the vendor for what has to go back: Anthropic starts a new container unless a request passes the earlier container's id back, and Google requires its signed code-execution parts back when code execution runs beside ordinary tools. Three model libraries keep those parts and send them back.
- **Confidence:** Low - the four gateways examined that return a chat completions reply turn Google's code-execution parts into text or skip them, and Runvil has no plan to adopt the feature.
- **Evidence items:** P19, P22, P23, AN48, GX25 to GX28

#### Row 66. Computer use and other caller-run tools whose schema the vendor fixes

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (bash, text editor, memory, and computer tools), OpenAI (a computer tool)
- **What the vendor does:** The caller's program runs these tools, and the vendor fixes the schema of each.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its Messages route accepts Anthropic's bash and text editor tool types. Anthropic's computer tools are "rejected at validation". Its Responses route accepts OpenAI's computer tool type, `computer_use_preview`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Anthropic's computer use.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** No vendor-specific code is needed. The tool loop needs such tools, and Runvil's own shell and file tools do the job of Anthropic's bash and text editor tools. A memory or computer tool would be one more ordinary tool with a schema of Runvil's own. The Gateway translates an ordinary tool the same way for every vendor.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the tool types its routes accept and reject. What Runvil's tools do is from Runvil's design documents.
- **Confidence:** Medium - Runvil chose its own tool schemas over the vendors' fixed ones, and what a model loses by that was not examined.
- **Evidence items:** P19, P21, AN42, AN43

#### Row 67. Remote MCP run by the vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`mcp_servers`, `mcp_toolset`), OpenAI (the `mcp` tool, with approval items)
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor connects to the MCP server itself, from its own servers. OpenAI's approval items ask the caller to approve a tool call before it runs.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Only its Responses route has it, as the tool types `mcp` (OpenAI's own) and `openrouter:mcp`. `openrouter:mcp` was added to OpenRouter's API specification on 2026-11-05. Its guide tells callers to turn MCP tools into ordinary tools themselves, so the caller's program connects to the MCP server and runs the tools.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Anthropic's MCP connector, which is its version of the feature.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** No vendor-specific code is needed. The tool loop uses MCP through Runvil's MCP Pack, which already connects to MCP servers from inside the Host and lets the model list and call their tools through two ordinary tools, on every vendor.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, whose guide recommends the arrangement Runvil has. What Runvil's MCP Pack does is from Runvil's design documents.
- **Confidence:** High - Runvil already runs MCP in the Host.
- **Evidence items:** P80 to P82, AN47

#### Row 68. Vendor file stores and file search

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (Files API), OpenAI (files, vector stores, and the `file_search` tool), Google (File API URIs)
- **What the vendor does:** The caller uploads a file once and refers to it by an id in later requests. The store belongs to one vendor account. OpenAI's vector stores are collections of uploaded files that its `file_search` tool searches. Google's File API gives each upload a URI that the request then refers to.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It has its own Files API, whose file ids start with `or_file_`. Adding `?provider=openai` or `?provider=anthropic` to a Files API request forwards it to that vendor's store, with the caller's own key for that vendor, which OpenRouter calls BYOK (bring your own key). `file_search` "requires BYOK" too.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A vendor's file id works only with that vendor.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. To use a vendor's store directly, some part of Runvil would upload to a route in the vendor's own format and write the file's id, which works at that vendor only, into message content. A prompt could not do that without knowing the vendor, and which part would is not designed. A files route in the Gateway that is the same for every vendor is not a separate vendor path: with it the feature would be Gateway only, the better option. The Gateway would translate its own file ids into each vendor's and so has to store that mapping between requests.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes, for the Gateway route: the route itself and its file ids.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for its Files API and the `provider` parameter. That API shows that a gateway can have a files route and file ids of its own, but it does not translate those ids into a vendor's.
- **Confidence:** Medium - direct use needs the separate path because of how the vendors' stores work, and the Gateway route means the Gateway stores data between requests.
- **Evidence items:** P67 to P70, AN56, AN57, OA51, GG73

#### Row 69. PDF and document input

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google, whose models read the file themselves. Other models need the document's text.
- **What the vendor does:** The model reads a document that the request holds as a `file` part or a document part.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route the document goes in a `file` content part. Its `file-parser` plugin, a step that OpenRouter runs once per request, handles the file in one of three ways: `native` hands the file to a model that reads files itself, and `mistral-ocr` and `cloudflare-ai` turn the PDF into text first.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would pass the file unchanged to a model that reads files itself and parse it into text for the others. The Engine would get a `file` content part beside the `text` and `image_url` parts it has today, the same for every vendor.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a file part.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, which does this for every model.
- **Confidence:** Medium - no parser is chosen for the Gateway, and the quality of the parsed text is untested.
- **Evidence items:** P63 to P66

#### Row 70. Image, audio, and video input

- **Vendors:** Every vendor with models that accept images. Google for video.
- **What the vendor does:** The caller sends media as a URL or as inline data (the file's bytes placed inside the request as base64 text). Each provider sets its own limits. For video, YouTube links work on Google AI Studio only, and Google Vertex AI takes inline data only. Those are the two Google services that serve Gemini models.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It has three content part types for media: `image_url`, `input_audio`, and `video_url`. It sends a video URL only to providers that support video URLs.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown. OpenRouter does not say whether it downloads a URL and sends the data inline when a provider takes inline data only.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to turn a URL into inline data where the vendor needs it. Audio and video, which Runvil does not send today, would need new content part types in the Engine, the same for every vendor.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes, for audio and video: new content part types.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the part types and for where it sends video URLs. The URL conversion is taken from another open-source project: LiteLLM forces base64 for Anthropic's models on Amazon Bedrock and Google Vertex AI, which refuse image URLs.
- **Confidence:** Medium - LiteLLM shows the conversion, but most providers' limits, which say when to convert, are unpublished.
- **Evidence items:** P71 to P75, P86, GG74, GG76

#### Row 71. Image and audio output from a chat model

- **Vendors:** Google, OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** A chat model returns image parts or audio parts inside its reply, next to the text. Google's image models put a signature on every image part.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** It uses three fields: `modalities` in the request asks for image or audio output, `message.images[]` in the reply holds the images, and `delta.audio` in the chunks of a streamed reply holds the audio. All three are extension fields outside OpenAI's message format.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Unknown. OpenRouter has not published how it returns the signatures on images.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work, plus a new vendor-neutral field, if adopted. Apart from the opaque field, the client, the Engine, and Lua would have to accept media in a reply, the same for every vendor: today Runvil's client fails on a reply whose `content` is not a string. The Gateway would store Google's signatures on images in the opaque field and send them back.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: media in a reply.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the three fields. Stated by the vendor for the signatures: Google says the next response may fail if they are not sent back.
- **Confidence:** Low - how a gateway returns the signatures is unpublished, and few models have the feature: 13 of the 476 entries in OpenRouter's model catalog list image output, and 4 list audio output.
- **Evidence items:** P76, P77, P79, GG14, GG96

#### Row 72. Safety settings and blocked requests

- **Vendors:** Google
- **What the vendor does:** A request can include `safetySettings`, a list of content-filter settings in Google's own categories and thresholds. When Google refuses a request's input, it answers with HTTP 200, no candidates, and the reason in `promptFeedback.blockReason`. A candidate is Google's name for what chat completions calls a choice: one generated reply.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its chat completions route has no field for the safety settings. It has `provider.options`, a field for a provider's own options, and does not say whether that field forwards Google's safety settings. It returns a blocked request as an error with `error_type: "content_policy_violation"` and HTTP status 403.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Possibly control over the safety settings.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway should take default safety settings from a model entry, where the operator sets them, and send them with each request to that model. Where Google sends no candidates, the Gateway has to build a choice itself with `finish_reason: "content_filter"`, so that the client gets an ordinary reply. A prompt that set Google's thresholds itself would have to know the vendor.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter, from its API specification, that a caller cannot count on sending safety settings. Of ten open-source projects examined, the gateways new-api and one-api send a safety default with every request to Gemini models, set by the operator and not by the caller, and LiteLLM, agentgateway (an open-source gateway), Bifrost, and Pydantic AI turn a blocked request into a `content_filter` choice. No project examined offers a vendor-neutral setting.
- **Confidence:** Medium - projects show both the operator's default and the converted reply, and all ten use Google's own setting names and values.
- **Evidence items:** GG59, GG60, GG64, GG65, GX1 to GX12

#### Row 73. Tools that exist at one vendor only

- **Vendors:** Google (URL context, Maps grounding, and code execution beside functions), xAI (X search), Moonshot (formula tools), Alibaba (`enable_search`), Zhipu (the `web_search` tool type)
- **What the vendor does:** Each is a tool, or a form of a tool, that only this vendor has. Google's URL context reads the pages at the URLs named in a request. Its Maps grounding bases an answer on Google Maps data and comes with a display duty to show the Maps sources. Code execution beside functions is Google's code execution (row 65) in the same request as the caller's ordinary tools. Google signs the parts that its built-in tools return in such a request and requires them back. xAI's X search searches posts on X. Moonshot's formula tools are the official tools of its Kimi models: the model calls them as ordinary tools, and the caller runs each call through Moonshot's separate Formula API. Alibaba's `enable_search`, a request switch for its Qwen models, and Zhipu's `web_search` tool type are vendor-run web search that OpenRouter's search tool does not use.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** X search is a vendor-specific object inside its vendor-neutral search tool. The other tools are undocumented or cannot be reached through OpenRouter.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** All of these tools except X search, as far as its documentation and API specification show.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. A prompt would ask for a capability by a vendor-neutral id, and the Gateway would use the vendor's own tool where the vendor has one and answer "unavailable" elsewhere. In Pydantic AI, for example, a program asks for `WebFetchTool`, and on a Gemini model the library switches on Google's URL context. For a tool that only one vendor has, such as Maps grounding, the id works on that vendor's models and the Gateway answers "unavailable" on all others. The prompt still names no vendor. A prompt that named the vendor's tool would have to know the vendor, and no other feature comes closer to that. Runvil can also simply decline to offer these tools. The opaque field is needed only for the signed parts of Google's tools.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: the vendor-neutral id by which a prompt asks for a capability.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for X search, and inferred about OpenRouter for the other tools, which are missing from its documentation and its API specification. Stated by the vendor for Google's signed parts. Pydantic AI has vendor-neutral tool classes for web search, web fetch, and code execution that map to each vendor's own tool. No project examined has a vendor-neutral form of Maps grounding.
- **Confidence:** Low - a vendor-neutral id is shown only for tools that several vendors have, and Runvil has no plan to adopt these tools.
- **Evidence items:** P12, P15, GG69, GG70, OV17, OV42, OV52, OV56, GX13 to GX28

### 3.7 Conversations, jobs, and context handling on the vendor's side: a tool loop with reasoning works without any of them

Most model requests are stateless: each one contains the whole conversation, and the vendor needs nothing from earlier requests to answer it. The ten features here go beyond that. With four, the vendor keeps data or a job on its own servers: a stored conversation (row 74), a background job (row 75), a batch job (row 76), and code that it runs for the model (row 82). With three, the vendor shortens the model's input itself, which in Runvil is the job of a compactor that the prompt chooses: it summarizes older messages (row 77), clears earlier tool use or thinking (row 78), or drops old items (row 81, with predicted outputs). The other three are a route that counts tokens (row 79), service tiers (row 80), and a WebSocket transport (row 83).

A tool loop with reasoning works without any of them: Runvil sends the whole conversation in every request, and the planned opaque field returns the vendor's reasoning data with it. Service tiers (row 80) are the one feature without the "if adopted" mark: they are an operator's setting that the planned work can use to fill Runvil's `fast` model role.

Stored conversations, background jobs, batch jobs, and the WebSocket transport need a separate vendor path as the vendors offer them (so do stored files, row 68), and of the four OpenRouter documents only batch jobs. The other six need the Gateway alone or the opaque field, and compaction, token counting, and Skills with tool-calling code would also need a new vendor-neutral setting or route.

Terms used in this subsection: a beta feature is one that a vendor has released for testing and that a caller turns on with a request header. OpenRouter's own trimming, an option it calls `context-compression`, shortens a request that is too long for the model by removing or shortening messages in the middle (its one method, `middle-out`).

**How to read Table 11.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 11. Conversations, jobs, and context handling on the vendor's side: 10 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 74 | **Conversations stored by the vendor.** The vendor keeps the conversation on its own servers, and the caller sends an id in place of the earlier messages. | OpenAI, xAI, Google | It rejects such a request with an HTTP 400 error, and it does not offer Google's Interactions API. | **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. A request that names a stored conversation is in one vendor's own format and works only there, and Runvil sends the whole conversation instead. | High |
| 75 | **Background jobs with polling, canceling, and webhooks.** The caller starts a long request as a job, gets a job id, and later asks for the result, cancels the job, or is notified when it finishes. | OpenAI | Its API specification lists the on-off field for this mode and has no route for fetching or canceling a job. | **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. OpenAI's job routes are its own, and a job route on the Gateway that returns a job id would avoid them. | High |
| 76 | **Batch jobs.** The caller submits many requests together, and the vendor answers them later at half the normal price. | Anthropic, OpenAI | It has a batch route of its own, with batch versions of models, and tells callers to remove Anthropic's beta features from a batch. | **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. Each vendor's batch route is its own, and a batch route on the Gateway would avoid them, but a batch does not suit the Engine, which waits for each reply. | High |
| 77 | **Compaction done by the vendor.** The vendor summarizes the older part of a long conversation and returns the summary in a block that the caller has to send back unchanged. | Anthropic, OpenAI | It offers this on its Messages and Responses routes only, without Anthropic's on-demand form. Its chat completions route has only its own `context-compression` trimming. | **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. A compactor would ask for the vendor's summary through the new setting, and the Gateway would return the vendor's block in the opaque field. | Medium |
| 78 | **Context editing done by the vendor.** Anthropic clears earlier tool use or earlier thinking out of a request on its own servers before the model reads it, while the caller goes on sending the full history. | Anthropic | Its API specification has the field on the Messages request only. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. The operator would turn it on with a setting on a model entry, and the Gateway would add Anthropic's field to each request for that model. | Medium |
| 79 | **Token counting.** A route that estimates how many input tokens a request contains. | Anthropic | Its API specification lists 123 routes, and none of them counts tokens. | **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral route, if adopted. The Gateway would offer one token-counting route for every vendor, and without it the Engine estimates the count itself. | Medium |
| 80 | **Service tiers and fast mode.** A vendor sells the same model at several tiers that trade price against speed, and the request picks one. | OpenAI, Anthropic | It accepts the same tier values for every vendor that sells tiers and lists each tier as a separate endpoint of the model. The caller keeps the whole feature. | **Gateway only.** To offer a model's fast tier, the Gateway has to let a model entry select a tier and send the vendor's tier field for it. The Host can then assign that entry to the `fast` role. | High |
| 81 | **Predicted outputs and automatic truncation.** Two OpenAI features: the caller supplies text it expects in the reply so that the reply comes faster, and OpenAI drops old items from a conversation that is too long. | OpenAI | It accepts predicted outputs on its chat completions route and truncation on its Responses route, as OpenAI does. | **Gateway only**, if adopted. Each is one request field, and Runvil should decline both: few models support predicted outputs, and truncation lets OpenAI, not the prompt's compactor, shorten the conversation. | Medium |
| 82 | **Skills, and code the model writes to call tools.** The vendor runs code on its own servers, a stored Skill in a container at Anthropic or a program the model writes at OpenAI, and gives the caller an id or a fingerprint to send back. | Anthropic, OpenAI | Its API specification gives a request no way to ask for either feature. | **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. The Gateway would store the id or the fingerprint in the opaque field, and a prompt would ask for either feature through the new setting. | Low |
| 83 | **WebSocket transport.** OpenAI can exchange requests and replies over one WebSocket connection that stays open, in place of separate HTTP requests. | OpenAI | It does not offer this transport for model requests. | **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. The client would need an open connection to the Gateway as well, which the universal API does not have. | High |

**Notes on Table 11, row by row.** Each note covers one row.

#### Row 74. Conversations stored by the vendor

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (`previous_response_id`, `store`, and its Conversations API), xAI, Google (its Interactions API)
- **What the vendor does:** In OpenAI's Responses API, `store` asks OpenAI to keep a reply, `previous_response_id` names the stored reply that a new request continues, and the Conversations API keeps a whole conversation as a lasting object with its own id. xAI's Responses API has this mode too, and so does Google's Interactions API, which Google now recommends over its older Gemini API (`generateContent`).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's documentation says: "Requests that set `store: true` or a non-null `previous_response_id` are rejected with a `400` error". All three of its routes for model requests are stateless, and it does not offer Google's Interactions API.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The whole mode.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. A request that names a stored conversation by id is in one vendor's format, and only that vendor can answer it, so the client would have to speak that format beside the universal API. Serving such requests for every vendor would take a database of conversations in the Gateway and routes for reading them back. Runvil needs none of this: the Engine's whole requests, with the opaque field once it is built, give the model the same reasoning as a stored conversation. The run log, which records each request the Engine issues and its answer, would also lack the messages of a request that named a stored conversation.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. Six open-source gateways leave the feature out for a vendor that lacks it (agentgateway, Bifrost, claude-code-router, Portkey's AI Gateway, Envoy AI Gateway, and new-api), and Bifrost and agentgateway forward the id to the vendor or drop it. A seventh, LiteLLM, emulates it by rebuilding the history from its database of logged requests. Google's Interactions API is reached separately from the Gemini API: in LiteLLM on a route of its own, in the Vercel AI SDK through a second model object, and in Google's client libraries as `client.interactions`.
- **Confidence:** High - such a request works at one vendor only, the one emulation needs a database, and OpenRouter declines the feature.
- **Evidence items:** OA1, OA2, OA24, GG79, J-table 2, CT44 to CT46, GX64 to GX66, OV13, L100

#### Row 75. Background jobs with polling, canceling, and webhooks

- **Vendors:** OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** The caller fetches the result through a retrieve route. Until the job finishes it can poll for the job's status, cancel the job, or have OpenAI send a webhook to a URL it controls.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's API specification lists the request field `background` on the Responses route and gives only its type, a boolean. Its two routes for Responses requests both take a new request, and neither fetches or cancels a job.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The job mode, as far as the specification shows.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. OpenAI serves a job's result on routes of its own, which is the separate vendor path. To avoid it, the Gateway would return a job id and offer a second route for fetching the result, the same for every vendor, beside the one route of the universal API.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter for the field and the routes. That a job cannot be used is inferred about OpenRouter, whose documentation does not say what its server does with a request that sets the field.
- **Confidence:** High - fetching a result later takes a second route whatever the design, and OpenRouter's API specification has none.
- **Evidence items:** OA57, OA58, OA2, S2

#### Row 76. Batch jobs

- **Vendors:** Anthropic, OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor processes the batch asynchronously, at half price.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter has a batch route of its own, `/batches`, typically billed at half price too. Its model catalog has `:batch` variants of models: model ids that end in `:batch` and work only on that route.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Anthropic's beta features inside a batch: OpenRouter tells callers to remove them, while Anthropic's batch API accepts most.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. Each vendor takes batches on a route of its own, which is the separate vendor path. A batch route on the Gateway that is the same for every vendor, as OpenRouter's is, would avoid it. Either way a batch does not suit the Engine, which runs every round live: it sends the request and waits for the reply before it continues.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** High - both vendors and OpenRouter serve batches on a separate route, so Runvil would need one too.
- **Evidence items:** AN58, AN59, OA59, OA60

#### Row 77. Compaction done by the vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (the request strategy `compact_20260112`, `compaction` blocks in the reply, and the stop reason `compaction`), OpenAI (compaction items)
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor writes the summary and returns it in a block (Anthropic) or an encrypted item (OpenAI) that the caller has to send back in later requests. Each vendor has two forms. In the threshold form the caller turns the feature on, at Anthropic with the strategy `compact_20260112`, and the vendor compacts by itself once the conversation passes a token threshold: the reply then contains a `compaction` block and can end with the stop reason `compaction`. In the on-demand form the caller asks in a separate request, at Anthropic under the beta header `compact-2026-09-04`, and gets back one signed block with the summary as readable text.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter offers it on its Messages and Responses routes only. Its chat completions route has only its own `context-compression` trimming.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The feature, on the chat completions route, and Anthropic's on-demand form on every route: it is outside the request schema of OpenRouter's Messages route.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. The Engine's rule is that the prefix changes only at an explicit compaction, when a compactor runs. The on-demand form fits that rule as one more compactor: it would ask for the summary through the new setting, and the Gateway would return the vendor's block in the opaque field of an assistant message that replaces the summarized records. That takes new vendor-neutral code in the client and the Engine, because Lua never sees the opaque field, and none of it is designed. Only Anthropic's block has readable text for that message. With OpenAI's item, the summarized part would be lost in a later request to another model, because the opaque field does not go to it. The threshold form goes against the rule: the vendor, and not a compactor, decides when to compact.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes: a way for a compactor to ask for the vendor's summary.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, for the routes. How to hold the block is taken from another open-source project, LiteLLM, which keeps Anthropic's compaction blocks in an extra message field, `provider_specific_fields.compaction_blocks`, and builds the same kind of block itself for other vendors.
- **Confidence:** Medium - LiteLLM shows that an extra message field can hold the block, but the compactor that would use it is untried.
- **Evidence items:** AN50, AN52, OA46, OA47, K6, CT4, CT10

#### Row 78. Context editing done by the vendor

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (the strategies `clear_tool_uses_20250919` and `clear_thinking_20251015`)
- **What the vendor does:** Each strategy is an entry in the request field `context_management.edits`: one clears earlier tool use, the other earlier thinking.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** In OpenRouter's API specification only the Messages request has the field.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The feature, on every route but Messages.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The operator would turn either strategy on with a setting on a model entry, and the Gateway would add it to each request for that model. The Engine's requests and records stay whole, but the model then reads less than the Engine sent, and Anthropic's strategy, not the prompt's compactor, picks what is cleared. The threshold form of compaction (row 77) and truncation (row 81) have the same effect.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the Gateway's part is a single request field at one vendor, but how it would work beside a prompt's compactor was not examined.
- **Evidence items:** AN51, AN52

#### Row 79. Token counting

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`count_tokens`)
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic's route returns an estimate. Whether Google offers the same was not checked.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** None of the 123 routes in OpenRouter's API specification counts tokens, and its documentation never mentions `count_tokens`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The route. A caller estimates the count itself or reads the token counts a reply reports afterward.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, plus a new vendor-neutral route, if adopted. The Gateway would offer one token-counting route, which the Engine's precheck would call. The route is the same for every vendor, so it counts as a new vendor-neutral setting and not as a separate vendor path. Where the count would come from for a vendor without such a route is not designed. Runvil works without it today: the precheck estimates the count by its own rule and, once a round has reported usage, starts from the reported numbers.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes, if the feature is adopted: a vendor-neutral route on the Gateway that counts the tokens of a request.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter, from the route missing in its specification.
- **Confidence:** Medium - OpenRouter has no token-counting route to copy, and that absence is the only evidence, so the Gateway's route would be Runvil's own design.
- **Evidence items:** AN86

#### Row 80. Service tiers and fast mode

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (`service_tier`), Anthropic (`speed: "fast"`)
- **What the vendor does:** The request field is `service_tier` at OpenAI, and `speed: "fast"` asks for Anthropic's fast mode.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter accepts the same `service_tier` values for every vendor that sells tiers and lists each tier as a separate endpoint of the model. For the fast tier it documents three spellings as "fully interchangeable on all APIs": `speed: "fast"`, `service_tier: "fast"`, and `service_tier: "priority"`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller keeps the whole feature.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** Any model can fill Runvil's `fast` model role, and a fast tier of the same model is one more choice that these vendors sell. To offer it, the Gateway has to let a model entry select a tier and send the vendor's field for it, and the Host can then assign the entry to the `fast` role. This is new code: today the Gateway forwards each request as the client wrote it, and the client sends no tier.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter. From earlier Runvil research (Runvil's own code) for what the Gateway and the client do today.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter documents one set of tier values for every vendor that sells tiers, so Runvil's Gateway can translate a model entry's setting the same way.
- **Evidence items:** OA66, OA67, AN84, AN85, L4, L13

#### Row 81. Predicted outputs and automatic truncation

- **Vendors:** OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** The text the caller expects most of the reply to contain goes in the request field `prediction`, which a few older models support. `truncation: "auto"` makes OpenAI drop items from the start of a conversation that is too long for the model's context window.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter accepts `prediction` on its chat completions route and `truncation` on its Responses route, the routes they have at OpenAI. It also has its own `context-compression` trimming.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The caller keeps both.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, if adopted. The Gateway would set `truncation` on a request it translates into the Responses API, and would pass on `prediction`. Runvil should decline both: few models support predicted outputs, and truncation replaces OpenAI's rejection of a request that is too long, which like the precheck makes the Engine call the prompt's compactor, with OpenAI's own shortening.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. Chat completions format already has `prediction`, so the universal API needs no new field. How a prompt would supply the predicted text is not designed.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** Medium - the Gateway's part is two documented request fields, and the prompt's part for predicted outputs is open.
- **Evidence items:** OA47, OA48, OA68, OA69, O60

#### Row 82. Skills, and code the model writes to call tools

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`container.skills`), OpenAI (programmatic tool calling: `program` items with a fingerprint "that must be round-tripped")
- **What the vendor does:** A Skill is code kept at Anthropic under an id. A request names the Skills it wants in `container.skills`, for use with Anthropic's code execution tool, and the caller sends that tool's container id back in later requests. With OpenAI's programmatic tool calling, the model writes a program, OpenAI runs it, and the program calls the caller's tools. The reply contains `program` items with the code and a fingerprint, which the caller has to send back unchanged.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** In OpenRouter's API specification, `container.skills` appears only in the reply schema of the Messages route, and programmatic tool calling is outside the tool types that the Responses route accepts.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Both features.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, plus a new vendor-neutral setting, if adopted. The Gateway would store the container id or the fingerprint in the opaque field, and the client and the Engine would send on the new setting.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Yes. A prompt may not use Anthropic's Skill id, so it would ask by a vendor-neutral id that the Gateway maps to the vendor's own, as proposed for tools that one vendor has (row 73).
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor for the two features. Inferred about OpenRouter, from its API specification, that a caller cannot ask for either.
- **Confidence:** Low - the vendors document that an id or a fingerprint has to come back, which the opaque field covers. The setting is not designed, because Runvil has no plan to adopt either feature.
- **Evidence items:** AN88, OA55

#### Row 83. WebSocket transport

- **Vendors:** OpenAI
- **What the vendor does:** In this mode of OpenAI's Responses API, requests and replies travel as events on the open connection, and the caller can send further events while a reply is under way, such as `response.steer`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter does not offer it for model requests. Its setup guide for OpenAI's harness Codex says of this transport's setting: "Leave `false` for OpenRouter".
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The transport.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Separate vendor path**, if adopted. For a prompt to use the mode, events would have to pass both ways between the client and the Gateway as well as between the Gateway and OpenAI, and the universal API is one HTTP request with one streamed reply. No design for such a connection exists.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter.
- **Confidence:** High - a WebSocket is a different transport from HTTP.
- **Evidence items:** OA6, OA72

### 3.8 Finish reasons, errors, usage, and streaming: the vendor-specific work is all in the Gateway, and this is where today's Gateway loses the most

This group of features is about what surrounds the text of a model reply: the finish reason (why the model stopped), usage (the token counts), how a streamed reply is delivered, and how a failure is reported. Each vendor does all four in its own way.

All the vendor-specific work is for the Gateway, and it is still to be built. The Gateway has to convert every vendor's finish reasons, errors, and token counts into one common form. Some features also change the client or the Engine, each in a way that is the same for every vendor: the client reads a standard error type that the Gateway sends (rows 86 to 88), the client retries after a rate limit (row 89), and the Engine changes how it estimates a conversation's size (row 92). A standard error type is a fixed name for one kind of failure, such as a rate limit, that is the same whichever vendor reported it.

Today's Gateway loses these things that an upstream sends (section 7): the chunk that holds only the token counts (row 91), a line that holds only an error object (row 87), keep-alive messages, which a server sends during a long silence so that the connection is not treated as dead (row 90), the text of an error and the exact status of a 5xx error (rows 86 and 88), and the retry hint, a server's statement of how long to wait before a request is sent again (row 89).

**How to read Table 12.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 12. Finish reasons, errors, usage, and streaming: 10 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 84 | **Each vendor's own words for why the model stopped.** Every vendor has its own list of values for the finish reason. | Anthropic, Google, OpenAI | On its chat completions route it converts every vendor's value into one of five standard values, OpenAI's four and one for a failure, and returns the vendor's own value in an extra field. It does not publish the full table of conversions. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert each vendor's value into one of OpenAI's four finish reasons. Returning the vendor's own value beside it is optional. | High |
| 85 | **Tool calls reported under the wrong finish reason.** Google's Gemini gives the same finish reason for a reply that ends in tool calls as for a reply that simply ended. | Google | A reply that a user posted shows it changing Gemini's value to the standard one for tool calls. Its official client library still contains corrections for replies where the value was not changed. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report the tool-call finish reason whenever a reply contains a tool call and was not cut off. | High |
| 86 | **Error formats and error types.** Every vendor reports the same kinds of failure, such as a rate limit or an overloaded server, in its own format and with its own codes. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Zhipu, MiniMax, Mistral | Its documentation says it sorts every provider error into one of 27 error types. The field that holds the type is at a different path on each of its three routes, and the vendor's own code and error body are included only when available. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to pass on the upstream's HTTP status, the vendor's error code and message, and the retry hint, and to add a standard error type, which the client reads in the same way for every vendor. | High |
| 87 | **Errors that arrive after the reply has started with HTTP 200.** A request can fail after the server has answered with the success status and begun to stream. | Anthropic, OpenAI, any vendor | Its documentation describes a chunk on its chat completions route that holds an error object and has the finish reason "error". Two recorded streams had the error object without that finish reason. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report a vendor's in-stream failure in the Gateway's own error format with a standard error type, which the client reads in the same way for every vendor. | High |
| 88 | **The signal that a prompt is too long for the model.** Each vendor has its own way of saying that a request does not fit in the model's context window. | Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Zhipu | It answers HTTP 400 with one error type for a request that is too long, whichever vendor is behind the model. One recorded rejection that OpenRouter made itself has no error type. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report every vendor's signal under one standard error type, so that the Engine is told that the request was too long and calls the prompt's compactor. | High |
| 89 | **Rate limits and hints about when to retry.** A vendor that gets too many requests answers HTTP 429 and says when to try again in a format of its own. | All | It answers HTTP 429 and adds a header that says how long to wait, but only when every provider it tried gave such a hint. The vendors' own rate-limit headers appear not to reach the caller, which is inferred. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to turn each vendor's retry hint into one standard header that gives the wait in seconds, and the client has to wait and retry, in the same way for every vendor. | High |
| 90 | **Keep-alive messages and limits on silence in a stream.** A slow model can leave a stream silent for a long time. | Anthropic | It sends a comment line from time to time while the caller waits. It does not publish how often it sends one, or any time limit on a stream as a number. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to pass an upstream's keep-alive on to the client and to limit how long it waits on a silent stream. | High |
| 91 | **Where usage arrives in a streamed reply.** OpenAI and Anthropic put the token counts in different places in the stream. | OpenAI, Anthropic | It always sends usage, whether or not the caller asks. The last chunk of the stream holds the usage, and it also repeats the finish reason. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to stop skipping the chunk that holds only usage, and to send every vendor's usage in a last chunk of OpenAI's form. | High |
| 92 | **Counts of reasoning tokens.** Each vendor reports the tokens spent on reasoning under its own field name, and Google's Gemini leaves them out of its output count. | All | On its chat completions route it reports the count in one field inside the usage object. Its output count appears to include the reasoning tokens. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report every vendor's counts in OpenAI's way, and the Engine has to stop subtracting reasoning tokens from its size estimate once they are sent back, in the same way for every vendor. | High |
| 93 | **Cancelling a request, and whether billing stops.** A caller can cancel a streamed request partway through, and whether the charges stop at that moment depends on the provider. | 24 providers that stop billing, 24 that do not | The caller cancels by aborting the connection. OpenRouter's documentation names the providers that then stop billing and the providers that keep billing. | **Gateway only.** No new code is needed if the Gateway's design-decision page is right: it says that when the client disconnects, the Gateway closes its connection to the upstream, which cancels the request. | High |

**Notes on Table 12, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 84. Each vendor's own words for why the model stopped

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (eight values), Google (23 values), and OpenAI's Responses API (`status` plus `incomplete_details.reason`).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic's values are the seven in its API reference (`end_turn`, `max_tokens`, `stop_sequence`, `tool_use`, `pause_turn`, `refusal`, `model_context_window_exceeded`) and `compaction`, which its compaction guide uses. Google's `finishReason` values include `STOP`, `MAX_TOKENS`, and `MALFORMED_FUNCTION_CALL`. OpenAI's Responses API uses two fields: a `status`, and for an incomplete reply a reason in `incomplete_details.reason`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route OpenRouter converts every vendor's value into one of five standard values of `finish_reason` (`stop`, `length`, `tool_calls`, `content_filter`, `error`) and returns the vendor's own value beside it in `native_finish_reason`. It does not publish the full table of conversions.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A dependable way to read the vendor's own value. Only the chat completions route returns `native_finish_reason`, and OpenRouter's API specification does not declare it there. In recorded traffic it is `stop` for some OpenAI models and `completed` for others, depending on which of OpenAI's two APIs, chat completions or Responses, OpenRouter used to reach the model. For Claude it held the standard values in recordings from 2025, with Anthropic's own mixed in on Amazon Bedrock, and Anthropic's own in recordings from 2026.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to convert each vendor's value into one of the four finish reasons of OpenAI's chat completions format: `stop`, `length`, `tool_calls`, or `content_filter`. It reports a failure as an error, with no finish reason (row 87). Three vendor values have no settled conversion: `pause_turn` (a turn that Anthropic paused, row 63) and `compaction` (a stop after Anthropic's own compaction, row 77), both from features that Runvil does not use, and `MALFORMED_FUNCTION_CALL` (an invalid tool call from Gemini). Returning the vendor's own value in a `native_finish_reason` field is optional. Today the Gateway forwards the finish reason unchanged (section 7).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. `native_finish_reason` would hold the vendor's own word, and nothing in Runvil would read it.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter's documentation: the five standard values and `native_finish_reason`. Inferred about OpenRouter from recorded traffic: what that field holds. LiteLLM and the Vercel AI SDK each have a conversion table in their code, and they differ on the three values above: LiteLLM, for example, reports `pause_turn` as `length`, and the Vercel AI SDK as `stop`. Neither shows which conversion OpenRouter uses.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter converts every vendor's value into a short standard list, as do LiteLLM and the Vercel AI SDK, so the Gateway can do it alone.
- **Evidence items:** O52 to O57, O77, AN71, GG61, WT16, WT44, VF4, AN50, L73

#### Row 85. Tool calls reported under the wrong finish reason

- **Vendors:** Google (`STOP` with function calls).
- **What the vendor does:** Google's Gemini reports the finish reason `STOP` for a reply that ends in function calls, Google's name for tool calls, and also for a reply that simply ended.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's documentation does not say which finish reason it reports for such a reply, and two observations of tool calls that have a thought signature disagree. In a stream chunk for `google/gemini-3-flash-preview` that a user posted in an issue report on 2025-12-18, OpenRouter converted the value: the chunk has `"finish_reason":"tool_calls"` beside `"native_finish_reason":"STOP"`. OpenRouter's official client library for the Vercel AI SDK expects the opposite: when a reply has tool calls and encrypted reasoning, its code changes `stop` to the finish reason for tool calls. A second correction there covers a reply whose finish reason is unknown or missing although the model made tool calls. Neither source shows how often OpenRouter converts the value.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A finish reason it can depend on. A caller has to look for tool calls in the reply itself.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** When a reply that the vendor reports as finished contains a tool call, the Gateway has to report the finish reason `tool_calls`. It has to leave `length` and `content_filter` alone, because the client uses those two to recognize a batch of tool calls that was cut off. The Engine already runs the requested tools whenever a reply contains tool calls, so the change only corrects the value that it records and reports to the Host.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Inferred about OpenRouter from the posted chunk and from the client library's code at a commit dated 2026-11-20.
- **Confidence:** High - the corrections were read in the client library's source code, and the Gateway's rule does not depend on which of the two observations is typical.
- **Evidence items:** O58, T17, L38, L45, L72

#### Row 86. Error formats and error types

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`error.type`, status 529), OpenAI (`error.code`), Google (status names), Zhipu (numeric codes), MiniMax (`base_resp`), and Mistral (a different format by error and by route).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic names the kind of error in `error.type` and uses its own HTTP status, 529, for an overloaded service. OpenAI names it in `error.code`, Google by a status name, and Zhipu by numeric codes. MiniMax's replies have a `base_resp` object with a status code of its own. An earlier Runvil survey (2026-10-28) says that a failed request gets an HTTP 200 with the failure in `base_resp`, which could not be re-confirmed on 2026-11-22.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's documentation says it sorts every provider error into one of 27 error types, such as `rate_limit_exceeded`, and reports the type in `error_type`. The field is at a different path on each route: `error.metadata.error_type` on the chat completions route, `error.error_type` on the Messages route, and a top-level `error_type` on the Responses route. On the chat completions route the vendor's error code is in `error.metadata.provider_code`, in OpenRouter's words "when available", and `error.metadata.raw` can hold the vendor's error body.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The vendor's HTTP status, because OpenRouter answers with its own status for the error type. When OpenRouter answers 500, it also replaces the error message with a generic one, which hides the vendor's message.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to pass on an upstream's HTTP status, the vendor's error code and message, and the retry hint as a `retry-after` header (row 89), and to add a standard error type. Today it keeps a 4xx status, reports every 5xx as 502, and replaces a remote upstream's error body with the fixed message `upstream returned {status}`. Besides the message, its error format has two fields, `type` and `code`, which today say only whether the upstream's status was a 4xx or a 5xx. Not designed: the list of standard error types, whether it follows OpenRouter's 27, and which of the two fields holds it. The client's part is the same for every vendor: it has to decide what kind of failure it has, such as `ContextOverflow` or `RateLimited`, from the standard error type first. Today it decides from the HTTP status and from phrases in the error text, which stay for an error without a type.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. The Gateway puts the standard error type into its error replies, and no request sends it.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: its error types, paths, and fields. Read in Runvil's code on 2026-11-22: what the Gateway does today (`runvil-gateway` repository, `crates/gateway/protocol/src/error.rs`) and what the client does (section 7).
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter reports every vendor's error under one standard type, so Runvil's Gateway can do the same.
- **Evidence items:** O33 to O41, AN91, OV34, OV57, OV62, L74, L75, L76, L79

#### Row 87. Errors that arrive after the reply has started with HTTP 200

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`event: error`), OpenAI (`response.failed`), and any other vendor.
- **What the vendor does:** An HTTP status is final once it is sent, so a vendor that fails after it has answered HTTP 200 reports the failure inside the stream: Anthropic with an `event: error` event, OpenAI's Responses API with a `response.failed` event.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter's documentation describes, for its chat completions route, a chunk with a top-level `error` object and `finish_reason: "error"`. A request that does not stream can also get an HTTP 200 reply whose body holds an `error`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A firm rule for how a failed stream ends. OpenRouter does not say whether `[DONE]`, the line that marks the normal end of a chat completions stream, follows the error chunk. Recorded traffic holds two streams with an error chunk, both from 2025-11. The chunk's `finish_reason` was `null` in one and `"stop"` in the other, not the documented `"error"`. `[DONE]` followed in one of them, and the other recording may have stopped before the stream did.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway already has a fixed form for a failure that it detects itself in mid-stream: one `data:` line that holds only its error format (an object with an `error` inside it) and no finish reason, followed by `[DONE]`. It has to report a vendor's in-stream failure in the same form, with a standard error type inside (row 86). Today it skips an upstream's line that holds only an error object, and the stream then ends as if it had succeeded (section 7). The client already fails the round on any line that has an `error`. Its part is the same for every vendor: it has to read the standard error type. Today it reads only the message text and, when no phrase it looks for matches, reports a transport failure, a connection that broke during the reply.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the error chunk and the HTTP 200 reply that holds an error. Inferred about OpenRouter from recorded traffic: what the two error chunks held and how those streams ended.
- **Confidence:** High - the Gateway already writes such an error line for its own failures, and the client already fails the round on it.
- **Evidence items:** O42 to O45, L85, WT34, L52, L59, L80

#### Row 88. The signal that a prompt is too long for the model

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (message text), OpenAI (`context_length_exceeded`), Google (on record only secondhand), Zhipu (code 1261).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic rejects a request that is too long with an HTTP 400 whose only sign is the message text, "prompt is too long". OpenAI sends the code `context_length_exceeded`, and Zhipu its numeric code 1261. For Google's Gemini only secondhand records exist: an HTTP 400 with the status `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, and the phrase "exceeds the maximum number of tokens allowed", which LiteLLM's code looks for.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter answers with HTTP status 400 and `error_type: "context_length_exceeded"`.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A caller cannot tell why a reply was cut off: the finish reason is `length` both when the model's context window filled up ("context full") and when the reply reached the caller's own output limit ("own cap reached"). And the error type is not always present: a recorded reply of 2026-11-18 shows OpenRouter itself rejecting a request that was too long before sending it to any provider, with an HTTP 400 whose body has no `error_type`.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to recognize each vendor's signal, by its code where the vendor has one and by phrases in the message text where it has none, and report it under one standard error type (row 86). The client then reports `ContextOverflow`, on which the Engine calls the prompt's compactor and sends the round again. Today, when a remote upstream rejects a request as too long, the Gateway replaces the upstream's error text with a fixed phrase, so the Engine is not told that the request was too long and does not call the compactor in response (section 7). The Engine's precheck can still start compaction. Whether a local model's error text is replaced too was not traced. A reply cut off by a full context window (Anthropic's `model_context_window_exceeded`) gets `length` in Runvil too, unless the Gateway returns the vendor's own value (row 84).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the status and the error type. Inferred about OpenRouter from recorded traffic: the rejection that has no error type. From earlier Runvil research: the report of 2026-09-30 on such signals (`runvil-design`, `research/2026-09-30-compaction-exhaustion-signals.md`), for Anthropic's signal, Google's status, and OpenRouter's `error_type: context_length_exceeded`, which was checked again on 2026-11-22 and still holds.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter reports every vendor's signal under one error type, so a gateway can do the recognition. Google's signal has to be confirmed when the Gemini translator is written.
- **Evidence items:** O63, O64, O76, WT39, O73, OV57, W95, K47, L81, L82

#### Row 89. Rate limits and hints about when to retry

- **Vendors:** All.
- **What the vendor does:** A vendor that is receiving too many requests answers HTTP 429, adds rate-limit headers of its own, and says when the limit resets in one of four forms on record: a duration at OpenAI, an RFC 3339 timestamp at Anthropic, a `retry-after-ms` header at Microsoft Azure, and a `RetryInfo.retryDelay` value in the reply body at Google. Anthropic also sends a plain `retry-after` header.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter answers HTTP 429. It adds a `Retry-After` header, the number of seconds to wait, only when every provider it tried for the request gave a retry hint.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The vendors' own rate-limit headers, as far as can be inferred.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to answer with one `retry-after` header in seconds: it passes on an upstream's `retry-after` header and converts each of the four forms above into seconds. Today it forwards no such header and sets `retry-after` only for its own `model_loading` error (section 7). The client's part is the same for every vendor: it has to wait that long and retry. Today it makes one attempt per round, so a 429 fails the round. Not designed: how often it retries, the longest wait it accepts, and what it does without a hint.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: the 429 and the `Retry-After` header. The loss of the vendors' rate-limit headers is inferred about OpenRouter, from its documentation's silence about them and from the replies recorded on 2026-11-18 (202 of them), which hold only OpenRouter's own headers, though the recorder may drop headers. From earlier Runvil research: the four forms, from the survey of 2026-10-28.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter turns its providers' retry hints into one standard `Retry-After` header, so Runvil's Gateway can do the same.
- **Evidence items:** O30 to O32, WT41, W101, AN93, L62, L77, L83

#### Row 90. Keep-alive messages and limits on silence in a stream

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`ping` events).
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic sends `ping` events as keep-alive messages while a stream is silent, for example before the first piece of a reply. No keep-alive is on record for the other vendors.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter sends a comment line, `: OPENROUTER PROCESSING`, in the stream. In a streamed reply a line that starts with a colon is a comment, and a client skips it. OpenRouter publishes neither how often it sends the comment nor any time limit on a request or a stream as a number.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing. A caller gets a keep-alive during a slow stream.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send the client a keep-alive whenever the upstream sends one. A comment line works: Runvil's client skips it and restarts its 120-second wait on any data it receives. The Gateway also needs a limit on how long it waits on a silent stream, which is already planned work for it. Not decided: whether the Gateway sends keep-alives of its own while an upstream is silent, how long the limit is, and what the client receives when it runs out. Today the Gateway drops an upstream's keep-alive comments and limits only the time to connect, to 10 seconds, while the client gives up after 120 seconds of silence.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: its documentation describes the comment line, and recorded traffic contains it. Read in Runvil's code: its two time limits (section 7).
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter shows that a comment line keeps a slow stream open.
- **Evidence items:** O49, O50, L60, WT35, L54, L61

#### Row 91. Where usage arrives in a streamed reply

- **Vendors:** OpenAI (a last chunk with empty `choices`, on request) and Anthropic (split across two events).
- **What the vendor does:** OpenAI sends usage in one last chunk whose `choices` list, which normally holds the next piece of the reply, is empty, and only when the caller asks for it with `stream_options.include_usage`. Anthropic splits usage across two events: the input count arrives in `message_start` and the output count in `message_delta`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** OpenRouter always sends usage, without being asked, in the last chunk of the stream. Unlike OpenAI's chunk, its `choices` list has one entry. That entry's delta, the part that holds one increment of the reply, has no content, and the entry repeats the stream's finish reason.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** OpenAI's own format for the usage chunk, in which the `choices` list is empty.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to stop skipping a chunk that holds only usage, and to send every vendor's usage in OpenAI's form: a last chunk, before `[DONE]`, whose `choices` list is empty. Runvil's client asks for that chunk in every request. Today the Gateway's stream parser treats a chunk with an empty `choices` list as malformed and skips it, so usage that an upstream sends in OpenAI's form never reaches the client. Usage on a chunk that has a choice gets through.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: its documentation says that usage is always sent and describes the last chunk, which recorded traffic also contains. Read in Runvil's code: the defect and the client's request (section 7).
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter shows that a gateway can send every vendor's usage in one fixed chunk.
- **Evidence items:** C33, C38, L57, WT36, L67, W82, L68

#### Row 92. Counts of reasoning tokens

- **Vendors:** All.
- **What the vendor does:** The arithmetic differs as well as the field name. Google's Gemini reports thinking tokens in `thoughtsTokenCount` and leaves them out of `candidatesTokenCount`, its count of the tokens in the generated reply. OpenAI's `completion_tokens` includes reasoning tokens. Anthropic's own field was not gathered. OpenRouter's Messages route, in Anthropic's format, has `output_tokens_details.thinking_tokens`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions route OpenRouter reports the count in `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, inside the reply's usage object. Its `completion_tokens` appears to include those tokens.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing. The count reaches the caller intact.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to report the counts as OpenAI's format does: `completion_tokens` includes the reasoning tokens, and `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens` gives them on their own. For Gemini that means adding `thoughtsTokenCount` to `candidatesTokenCount`. The Engine's part is the same for every vendor and follows from the opaque field (row 1). Today, when a reply's usage reaches it, the Engine's precheck subtracts that reply's reasoning tokens from its estimate of the conversation's size, because the Engine never sends reasoning back. With the opaque field reasoning does go back, so the Engine has to stop subtracting for a reply that has the field. It can see only that the field is there. If the Gateway then drops the blocks because the label does not match the next request, the estimate is too high by that reply's reasoning tokens, which can only start compaction early.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: its API specification names the field. Inferred about OpenRouter: that its output count includes the reasoning tokens. Read in Runvil's code on 2026-11-22: the Engine's subtraction (`runvil-harness` repository, `lua/src/compactors.rs`).
- **Confidence:** High - the conversion is one addition for Gemini and none for a vendor that counts as OpenAI does.
- **Evidence items:** R71, GG94, L29, GG95, L69

#### Row 93. Cancelling a request, and whether billing stops

- **Vendors:** Providers, not vendors: OpenRouter names 24 that stop billing when the caller aborts a request (among them OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI, and DeepSeek) and 24 that do not (among them Google, Mistral, MiniMax, Alibaba, and Amazon Bedrock).
- **What the vendor does:** When a caller cancels a request partway through, some providers stop the model and stop charging. Others let the model finish and charge for the complete reply.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** A caller cancels a streaming request by aborting the HTTP connection. OpenRouter's two lists describe requests sent through it and cover 48 of the 123 providers in its list of providers.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A predictable cost for a cancelled request. When Anthropic itself serves a Claude model, billing stops on a cancel. When Amazon Bedrock serves the same model, billing continues.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway's design-decision page says cancelling is built: when the client disconnects, the Gateway closes its connection to the upstream, which is how a request is cancelled. The code was not read for it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: how to cancel, and the two groups of providers. The Gateway's behavior is from its design-decision page (`runvil-design` repository, `decisions/gateway.md`).
