- **Confidence:** High - the Gateway's design-decision page describes the behavior as built, and it is the same for every upstream.
- **Evidence items:** O51, V16

### 3.9 Model discovery, routing, and pass-throughs for vendor settings: operator settings in the Gateway

OpenRouter lets each caller say, on every request, which providers may serve it. Runvil's Gateway does not need those routing controls: one operator configures a Gateway and makes that choice once, in its configuration. OpenRouter's two pass-throughs for vendor settings, the `x-anthropic-beta` header (row 96) and the `provider.options` object (row 97), are what Runvil should not copy, because both make the caller know the vendor.

Terms used in this subsection: Routing means choosing which provider or model serves a request. A pass-through for vendor settings is a header or a request field through which a caller hands a gateway a vendor's own setting names and values. An allowlist is a list of the values OpenRouter forwards, and OpenRouter drops every value that is not on it. The Gateway's decision page is the Runvil document that records its design and lists its planned work.

**How to read Table 13.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 13. Model discovery, routing, and pass-throughs for vendor settings: 7 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 94 | **Published data on what each model can do.** A caller has to know what a model accepts, such as tools or a reasoning setting, and some vendors' own model lists say little or nothing about it. | All | Its model catalog lists the request settings a model accepts at one or more of its providers and the model's reasoning settings, and a separate list says which `tool_choice` values work at each endpoint. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to fill in defaults from the model sheet's values when it handles a request, where today it only publishes them, and the tooling that builds the sheet has to read OpenRouter's catalog, because an operator can add OpenRouter as an upstream. | High |
| 95 | **Several providers for one model.** The same model is served by more than one company, and each has its own features, price, and limits. | Anthropic and the cloud platforms that serve Claude; providers of open-weight models | The caller can steer the choice with routing settings on each request. Left alone, OpenRouter picks a provider at random, weighted by price and measured performance, and then uses sticky routing. | **Gateway only.** The operator lists a model's endpoints on its model entry, the Gateway uses the first one today, and the planned pick among them by load has to keep a conversation on one endpoint while that endpoint works. | High |
| 96 | **Vendor headers for beta features and API versions.** Anthropic switches a beta feature on only when a request header names it, and a second header names the API version. | Anthropic | The caller sends Anthropic's beta names in an OpenRouter header, and OpenRouter forwards only the names on an unpublished allowlist for each provider. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send the version header and the beta name of each feature it writes into a request, so no caller has to know Anthropic's header values. | High |
| 97 | **Passing a vendor's own settings straight through.** Some request settings exist at one vendor only, under a setting name that only that vendor uses. | All | The caller writes the vendor's own setting names and values into an object keyed by provider. OpenRouter forwards the ones on an unpublished allowlist for the endpoint. | **Gateway only**, as long as the operator sets it in the Gateway's configuration. If a prompt could set it, the prompt would have to know the vendor. The Gateway has to give a model entry a place where the operator writes the vendor's settings, and add them to the requests it forwards for that model. | Medium |
| 98 | **An identifier for the end user.** A caller can send the vendor an identifier for the person using its application, which the vendor uses to deal with abuse. | Anthropic, OpenAI | It accepts a user identifier from the caller and sends the vendor a hashed identity that includes it, never the original value. | **Gateway only.** A tool loop needs no identifier, and one that the Gateway sends has to be hashed and stay the same through a conversation. | High |
| 99 | **Credentials, regions, and data retention.** Providers differ in the kind of credential they accept, in the regional servers they offer, and in their terms for keeping request data. | All | It stores a caller's own provider keys, has request settings that keep a request away from providers that store data, and has separate hostnames for regions. | **Gateway only.** The operator chooses the providers, their regional base URLs, and the credentials in the Gateway's configuration, where today an endpoint can hold only an API key. | High |
| 100 | **Falling back to another model or provider.** When the first model or provider fails or refuses, a second one answers the request. | Anthropic; any vendor | The caller lists fallback models and OpenRouter does the switching itself, even when the caller uses Anthropic's own setting name. It keeps trying other providers until the first piece of a reply reaches the caller. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to send a request that fails at one of a model's endpoints to another, and a different model is for the prompt's Lua code and the Host to choose. | High |

**Notes on Table 13, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row.

#### Row 94. Published data on what each model can do

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic's own model list gives token limits and abilities, Google's list for Gemini gives token limits and one true-or-false value for thinking, and OpenAI's and DeepSeek's give model ids only.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** In its model catalog, `supported_parameters` lists every request setting that at least one of the model's endpoints accepts (the union of the endpoints' lists). A `reasoning` object there says whether reasoning can be turned off, whether it is on by default, and which reasoning-effort values the model accepts. A separate public list of each model's endpoints has `supports_tool_choice`, which says which values of `tool_choice` work at that endpoint. OpenRouter lists abilities it can test, such as tools and `tool_choice`, only when its own tests of the endpoint pass, whatever the provider declares.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** `supported_parameters` does not say whether a setting works at the provider that will serve a request. For `deepseek/deepseek-v3.2`, only 2 of the 4 endpoints list `tools`.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to look up the model sheet's values when it handles a request (its per-model table) and fill in a default that the request leaves out, such as the default temperature. Today it only publishes them. Dropping a setting a model rejects is compatibility code, which reads the same values (row 28). An operator can add OpenRouter as an upstream. For its models, the program that builds the model sheet, which is Runvil's own tooling for the Gateway and no part of the client or the Engine, has to read `supported_parameters` and the `reasoning` object. Today it skips both, so the sheet records those models as unable to call tools or to think. How the Gateway handles a listed setting that fails at the provider OpenRouter picks is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** Optional: more requirements that a prompt can declare for a model role than today's two, `min_context` and `thinking`. Which ones to add is not decided.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation and API specification, except the union, which is inferred from its data. From earlier Runvil research: the code of the Gateway and of the `runvil-cloud-providers` repository, read on 2026-11-22.
- **Confidence:** High - the Gateway already publishes these values, so using them changes only the Gateway and the tooling that feeds it. `supported_parameters` equaled that union for 55 of 55 models compared, and for 25 of 25 in a second check.
- **Evidence items:** M12, M24, M50, T4, L91, L94, GG108, V23, M15, L95, L96

#### Row 95. Several providers for one model

- **Vendors:** Anthropic's models, served by Anthropic directly and by Amazon Bedrock, Google Vertex AI, and Microsoft Azure; providers that serve open-weight models
- **What the vendor does:** Providers differ in features, price, and limits.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** A request can include a `provider` object. Thirteen of its documented fields say which providers may serve the request and which to prefer. By default OpenRouter picks one of the model's providers at random, weighted by price and measured performance, and then uses sticky routing.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** One predictable set of features per model id, unless the caller pins a provider. The providers under one id differ in whether web search runs on the vendor's own search tool, whether Anthropic's fast mode, a faster and costlier tier, is offered (row 80), how prompt caching behaves, whether structured output works, and whether billing stops when the caller cancels a request (row 93).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The operator lists the endpoints that serve a model on its model entry, so no caller chooses a provider. Today the Gateway uses the first endpoint on the list, and its decision page plans a pick among them by load, with fallback chains and priority weights. That pick has to keep a conversation on one endpoint for as long as the endpoint works (row 100 covers a failure), because reasoning data works only at the provider account that produced it (row 5) and a prompt cache helps only at the provider that holds it (row 36). To recognize a conversation it has to use a hash of its opening messages or a conversation id from the client (row 36).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation and, for the differences between providers, in its public endpoint data. From earlier Runvil research: the Gateway's code and decision page.
- **Confidence:** High - model entries already have a list of endpoints that the operator writes, and using the first one, as today, already keeps a conversation on one endpoint.
- **Evidence items:** O1 to O12, AN94 to AN97, P12, L117, AN85

#### Row 96. Vendor headers for beta features and API versions

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`anthropic-beta`, `anthropic-version`)
- **What the vendor does:** Anthropic's `anthropic-beta` request header names the beta features a request uses, and a beta feature works only when the header names it. Its `anthropic-version` header names the version of the API.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends Anthropic's beta names in OpenRouter's request header `x-anthropic-beta`. OpenRouter checks them against an unpublished allowlist that it keeps for each provider and, in its documentation's words, "drops every other value". It attaches some beta names itself when a request uses the feature. Its documentation does not say which `anthropic-version` it sends to Anthropic.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A name off the list is dropped without an error, so the caller cannot know which beta features will work.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway's translator for Anthropic, once built, has to send `anthropic-version` with every request and add to `anthropic-beta` the name of each beta feature whose fields it writes into the request. A header set by the caller is ruled out, because the client, the Engine, or a prompt would then hold Anthropic's values.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter attaches beta names without the caller's help, so Runvil's Gateway can do the same.
- **Evidence items:** AN81 to AN83, O13, AN70

#### Row 97. Passing a vendor's own settings straight through

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** Some request settings exist at one vendor only, under a setting name that only that vendor uses, such as Mistral's `safe_prompt`.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller writes such settings into `provider.options[<provider slug>]`, an object with one entry for each provider, keyed by OpenRouter's id for that provider. OpenRouter forwards only the entry of the provider that serves the request, and of that entry only the keys on an unpublished allowlist that it keeps for each of its endpoints.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** A key off the list is dropped without an error, so the caller cannot know which settings will reach the vendor.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only**, as long as the operator sets it in the Gateway's configuration. If a prompt could set it, the prompt would have to know the vendor. The Gateway has to give a model entry a table of extra settings, in which the operator writes a vendor's own setting names and values, and add them to each request it forwards for that model. Neither exists today, and a tool loop works without them: they are for an operator who needs a vendor-only setting. Which endpoints get the settings when a model entry has endpoints at several providers is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter: `provider.options` in its API specification, the allowlist in its guide for providers.
- **Confidence:** Medium - leaving these settings to the operator is a design choice with a cost: a prompt cannot change such a setting for one request.
- **Evidence items:** O3, S46

#### Row 98. An identifier for the end user

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (`metadata.user_id`), OpenAI (`safety_identifier`)
- **What the vendor does:** The vendor uses the identifier to handle abuse. Anthropic's field is `metadata.user_id`, which Anthropic says "should be a uuid, hash value, or other opaque identifier". OpenAI's field is `safety_identifier`. Starting with OpenAI's GPT-6.2, the API removes reasoning that a request sends back if the request that created it had a different identifier, or if only one of the two had one.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** The caller sends a `user` field. OpenRouter folds it "into the hashed identity sent upstream" and never forwards it raw. When the caller sends none, OpenRouter uses one identity for the whole account.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** The raw value: the vendor never receives the identifier the caller chose.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** A tool loop with reasoning needs no code for this: OpenAI keeps the reasoning when no request has an identifier. An identifier that the Gateway does send has to be hashed, and the same on every request of a conversation. What it hashes is not designed: the Gateway has no record of end users, and one identifier for all its callers, like OpenRouter's account-wide identity, would need nothing from the caller.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its API specification, and by Anthropic and OpenAI in their documentation. From earlier Runvil research: the Gateway's code.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter hashes the identifier itself, so Runvil's Gateway can do the same.
- **Evidence items:** O69, AN87, OA77, OA27, L6, VV3

#### Row 99. Credentials, regions, and data retention

- **Vendors:** All
- **What the vendor does:** Providers differ in three matters: the kind of credential they accept (an earlier Runvil survey counted eleven kinds, from a plain bearer key to requests signed with the keys of a cloud account), the regions where they run servers, and their retention terms, which say whether and how long they keep the content of requests and replies.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** A caller can give OpenRouter its own API keys for a provider, which OpenRouter stores per workspace, its own word for the unit in which it stores an account's provider keys and settings. Two request fields limit where a request may go: `provider.data_collection` can exclude providers that collect user data, and `provider.zdr` limits the request to zero data retention endpoints, whose providers promise not to store the content of requests and replies. Regional hostnames, such as `https://eu.openrouter.ai`, keep the processing of a request inside one region.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Not applicable: the caller chooses the keys, the region, and the retention terms.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The operator settles all three in the Gateway's configuration: which providers to add as endpoints, and so whose retention terms apply, which regional base URL each endpoint uses, and which credential it holds. Today an endpoint can hold only an API key, which the Gateway sends as a bearer token, so a provider that takes one needs no new code and any other kind of credential needs new code in the Gateway.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation. From earlier Runvil research: the Gateway's code, and section 5.1 of Runvil's 2026-10-28 survey of proprietary inference APIs for the eleven kinds of credential.
- **Confidence:** High - only the operator holds the keys and chooses the providers, so no caller could set any of the three.
- **Evidence items:** O25 to O29, L12

#### Row 100. Falling back to another model or provider

- **Vendors:** Anthropic (server-side `fallbacks`), and any other vendor
- **What the vendor does:** When the first model or provider fails (an error, a rate limit, or downtime) or refuses (declines to answer), a second one answers the request. Anthropic offers this on its own servers, as a beta feature for refusals: a request can have a `fallbacks` setting, and Anthropic itself hands the request to another model.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** On its chat completions and Responses routes the caller sends a `models` array, a list of model ids in order of preference. On its Messages route the caller sends `fallbacks`, the name Anthropic uses, and OpenRouter does the switching itself, on errors as well as refusals, without using Anthropic's server-side feature. OpenRouter also fails over from one provider of a model to another, and keeps doing so after it has sent the HTTP 200 status, until the first token of the reply reaches the caller.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Anthropic's `fallback` content block, which marks each point in a reply where one model's output gives way to the next. Also `fallback_credit_token`, a request field of Anthropic's beta that the request schema of OpenRouter's Messages route leaves out. Its purpose is not documented in the evidence.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to build the pick among a model's endpoints that its decision page plans (row 95), so that a request that fails at one endpoint goes to another. Today the client sends each request once, and a failure goes back to the prompt's Lua code. A conversation that moves loses the first endpoint's prompt cache (row 36). It also loses the vendor's data in the opaque field if the label on that field records the endpoint, as row 5 recommends: the Gateway then sends the data back only to the endpoint that produced it (row 19). A different model is not the Gateway's choice: Lua starts each model request and can start another after a failure or a refusal, and the Host decides which model fills each role. So the Gateway should not send Anthropic's `fallbacks`.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, in its documentation, and by Anthropic for its own fallback. From earlier Runvil research: the client's and the Gateway's code, and the Gateway's decision page.
- **Confidence:** High - OpenRouter fails over between providers without the caller's help, and choosing the model for a role is already the Host's job.
- **Evidence items:** O15 to O18, O47, AN74, S33, L8, L83

### 3.10 Vendors with OpenAI-style APIs and local servers: small fixes, most of which matter today

These features belong to servers that already accept requests in OpenAI's chat completions format: vendors with OpenAI-style APIs (here DeepSeek, Alibaba's Qwen, Zhipu's GLM, MiniMax, Mistral, and xAI), local servers (here llama.cpp and vLLM), and OpenRouter when an operator makes it one of the Gateway's upstreams. Moonshot (Kimi) and Ollama belong to the first two groups, and their features are in other tables.

The Gateway forwards chat completions requests to these servers today, before it supports any vendor's own protocol, so their differences from OpenAI's format already reach Runvil's client. The exception is the Messages and Responses routes that local servers also offer (row 105), which the Gateway does not call today. llama.cpp's server is the local server that the Gateway starts itself, which is why two features (rows 103 and 104) are about it alone.

Terms used in this subsection: a chat template is the template shipped with an open-weight model that turns a message list into the model's own input format.

OpenRouter takes no part where the Gateway talks to a local server directly (rows 103 to 105). It reaches vLLM and Ollama software only at providers that run it [OV79].

**How to read Table 14.** Each row is one feature or behavior that differs from vendor to vendor. "What Runvil needs" begins with one of the six options listed in "Terms used in this report" and defined in section 1. "With no new client or Engine work" is about the opaque field alone: the feature reuses the field once the client and the Engine store it and send it back, which is built once for Anthropic's thinking blocks (row 1). Such a row can still list a client or Engine change that is the same for every vendor. "If adopted" marks a feature that Runvil does not use today and that its planned work, a tool loop with reasoning, does not need. Confidence is how sure the report is about the "What Runvil needs" answer. The notes under the table give the details for each row. In a note, "Where the answer comes from" names the sources for the whole note: what OpenRouter does, and what shows that the handling proposed for Runvil works. The codes on the last line of each note, such as R36, are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The start of section 3 describes the columns and the notes in full.

*Table 14. Vendors with OpenAI-style APIs and local servers: 5 features.*

| Row | Feature | Vendors | What OpenRouter does with it | What Runvil needs | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | **APIs that look like OpenAI's but behave differently.** Several servers accept requests in OpenAI's chat completions format, yet they differ from it: content that is a list, an error code inside the reply body, extra finish reasons. | Mistral, MiniMax, xAI, DeepSeek, and OpenRouter when the Gateway forwards to it | Its callers get OpenRouter's own reply format for these vendors' models, by conversions it does not publish. | **Compatibility code.** The Gateway has to repair these replies: a few lines of code each for MiniMax, DeepSeek, and Mistral, and more for OpenRouter, whose reasoning fragments it would join and keep in the opaque field (the field that the features in rows 1 to 20 need). xAI needs none. | Medium |
| 102 | **Different names for the reasoning switch on OpenAI-style APIs.** The vendors and local servers use different field names for turning reasoning on or off and for limiting how many tokens the reasoning may use. | DeepSeek, Alibaba (Qwen), Zhipu (GLM), MiniMax, llama.cpp, vLLM | Its callers set reasoning through one request object for every model, and OpenRouter converts it for the provider that serves the model. | **Gateway only.** The client should send its on/off switch for reasoning under one standard name, and the Gateway has to write it under the field name of the server a request goes to. | High |
| 103 | **Finding out what a local server's model supports.** llama.cpp reports whether the loaded model's chat template supports tools and several tool calls in one reply, and which reasoning-effort values it supports. | llama.cpp | Not applicable: the server is local. | **Gateway only.** The Gateway has to keep this data when it starts a local model. The tools part decides whether it forwards tools (row 104), and a use for the rest is not designed. | High |
| 104 | **Silent failures on a local server.** llama.cpp changes a forced choice of one tool into a free choice without an error, is reported to ignore tools that the model's chat template cannot render, and rejects a grammar sent together with tools. | llama.cpp | Not applicable: the server is local. | **Compatibility code.** In each case the Gateway would change the request itself before forwarding it and report the change to the caller. The alternative is to reject the request. | Medium |
| 105 | **Messages and Responses routes on local servers.** llama.cpp and vLLM also accept requests in Anthropic's Messages format and in OpenAI's Responses format, and the signature on the reasoning they return is not one a vendor issued: llama.cpp's is empty, and earlier Runvil research says vLLM's is random. | llama.cpp, vLLM | Not applicable: the server is local. | **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. The planned translators could be pointed at a local server's routes, and the label on the opaque field would then keep that server's blocks from being sent to Anthropic. | High |

**Notes on Table 14, row by row.** Each note gives the details for one row of the table.

#### Row 101. APIs that look like OpenAI's but behave differently

- **Vendors:** Mistral, MiniMax, xAI, DeepSeek, and OpenRouter itself when it is the Gateway's upstream
- **What the vendor does:** Mistral's documentation says its `/v1/chat/completions` route "uses the native Mistral format", in which a reply's content is a list of chunks when reasoning is on (row 15). The reply schema of MiniMax's OpenAI-compatible route has an error code in `base_resp.status_code`. A Runvil survey of proprietary inference APIs dated 2026-10-28 says MiniMax's other route, in its own format, "returns HTTP 200 with `base_resp.status_code`", so a failure can look like a success. Whether the OpenAI-compatible route does the same is not known. xAI's documentation labels its chat completions API "(Deprecated)" and "Function calling only", and nothing in its replies needs repair. DeepSeek has two extra finish reasons, `insufficient_system_resource` and `aborted`. OpenRouter, as an upstream, sends `reasoning_details` in fragments that the caller has to join (row 18), and can send an error inside a streamed reply whose HTTP status is already 200 (row 87).
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its callers get one reply format for these vendors' models: a standard finish reason (row 84), its own error format (row 86), and reasoning in its own fields (row 15). Its translator is closed source.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Little: OpenRouter also returns the vendor's own finish reason (row 84), and the vendor's error code only when available (row 86).
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** Today a reply whose content is a list fails in the client (row 15), and an extra finish reason passes through unchanged. The Gateway has to add code for each server but xAI to its `openai` upstream code, which forwards to servers that speak chat completions, and pick it by endpoint entry. For MiniMax it would read `base_resp.status_code` before it accepts a reply. For DeepSeek it would convert the extra finish reasons into standard ones. For Mistral it would turn the chunk list into plain text plus reasoning (row 15), needed because Runvil's provider file for Mistral records `/v1`, the base of the route in Mistral's own format, as Mistral's OpenAI base URL. Each of these is a few lines. OpenRouter's is larger: it would join the fragments of `reasoning_details` into one list, keep the list in the opaque field, and send it back to OpenRouter in later requests.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by the vendor: each vendor's documentation. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-10-28 survey, for MiniMax's HTTP 200, and the provider files in the `runvil-cloud-providers` repository, read on 2026-11-22, for Mistral's base URL.
- **Confidence:** Medium - each vendor documents its difference, so what the code has to repair is known, except MiniMax's error inside an HTTP 200 reply, which was not re-confirmed on 2026-11-22. None of the code is written, so the sizes are estimates.
- **Evidence items:** OV13, OV27, OV35, OV62, OV80, L71, L73

#### Row 102. Different names for the reasoning switch on OpenAI-style APIs

- **Vendors:** DeepSeek, Alibaba's Qwen, Zhipu's GLM, and MiniMax; the local servers llama.cpp and vLLM
- **What the vendor does:** For the on/off switch, DeepSeek, GLM, and MiniMax use `thinking.type` and Qwen uses `enable_thinking`. The local servers take it inside `chat_template_kwargs`, which passes values to the model's chat template. A token budget field was found on three: Qwen (`thinking_budget`), vLLM (`thinking_token_budget`), and llama.cpp (`thinking_budget_tokens`). llama.cpp also has `reasoning_format`, which tells the server how to pick the reasoning out of the model's output. pi keeps eleven named sets of such field names, its thinking formats, for servers that call themselves "OpenAI-compatible".
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Its callers set reasoning through one request object, `reasoning`, for every model, and OpenRouter converts it into the field names of the provider that serves the model.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Nothing: the caller can still switch reasoning on or off and cap its tokens.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** Today the client sends its on/off switch for reasoning, the thinking switch, as `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, and the Gateway forwards it as written. The client should send it under a standard name (row 22), the same change for every vendor, and the Gateway has to write it under the field name of the server the request goes to, from a table with one line for each endpoint entry. Runvil's design document for the Gateway plans this as a per-model setting instead, and the choice is open. The client sends no budget: for a model that takes a budget in place of an effort value, as some Qwen models do, the Gateway would derive one from the reasoning-effort value (rows 21 and 23).
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No. The setting exists already, as a prompt's `thinking` requirement and the switch in each request. Only the request field's name changes, to a standard name that is not decided yet.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Stated by OpenRouter, for the `reasoning` object. Taken from another open-source project: pi.
- **Confidence:** High - every field name is in its vendor's or server's documentation, and pi's lookup by provider URL does the same job as the Gateway's table.
- **Evidence items:** OV78, OV21, OV46, OV47, OV67, OV54, OV60, OV73, L7, L32

#### Row 103. Finding out what a local server's model supports

- **Vendors:** llama.cpp
- **What the vendor does:** llama.cpp's server reports on its `/props` route, in a field named `chat_template_caps`, what the loaded model's chat template supports: tools, parallel tool calls (several tool calls in one reply), and which reasoning-effort values.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Not applicable: the server is local.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Not applicable.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Gateway only.** When it starts a local model, the Gateway already reads `/props`, to detect the model's tool dialect, according to its design document (the code was not read). It has to keep `chat_template_caps` from the same reply and look it up when it handles a request for that model. The tools part decides whether it forwards tools (row 104). A use for the other two parts is not designed.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from another open-source project: llama.cpp's README and source code.
- **Confidence:** High - the data is in a reply the Gateway already fetches when it starts the model.
- **Evidence items:** OV68, L113

#### Row 104. Silent failures on a local server

- **Vendors:** llama.cpp
- **What the vendor does:** A `tool_choice` that names one tool is changed to `auto`, which leaves the choice to the model. Tools sent to a model whose chat template has no tool support are ignored, according to the 2026-09-20 report on per-model tool differences. For a template that takes tool calls and does not describe the tools, llama.cpp's code logs a warning and uses a fallback. In all of these the caller gets no error. A grammar (a set of rules that limits the text the model may produce) sent together with tools gets an HTTP 400 error.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Not applicable: the server is local.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Not applicable.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Compatibility code.** Before it forwards a request to llama.cpp, the Gateway would make each change itself and report it in `gateway_warning`. It would send `auto` in place of a `tool_choice` that names one tool. It would leave the tools out for a model whose chat template reports no tool support in `/props` (row 103), unless the model has a tool dialect, for which the Gateway already describes the tools itself (row 50). It would never forward a grammar together with tools. The alternative in each case is to reject the request. Not designed: which of the two it leaves out, and what it does for a template that takes tool calls without describing the tools.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from another open-source project: llama.cpp's source code. From earlier Runvil research: Appendix C of the 2026-09-20 report.
- **Confidence:** Medium - llama.cpp's source code, read again on 2026-11-22, shows the forced tool choice changed to `auto` and the grammar error, so those two changes are needed. That llama.cpp ignores tools for a template with no tool support comes from the 2026-09-20 report alone.
- **Evidence items:** OV72, OV70

#### Row 105. Messages and Responses routes on local servers

- **Vendors:** llama.cpp, vLLM
- **What the vendor does:** Besides chat completions, both local servers serve `/v1/messages`, in the format of Anthropic's Messages API, and `/v1/responses`, in the format of OpenAI's Responses API. On its Messages route llama.cpp returns reasoning with an empty signature. vLLM's documentation shows its two routes. That its signature is random comes from the 2026-11-17 report and a student report of the same date.
- **What OpenRouter does with it:** Not applicable: the server is local.
- **What a caller loses through OpenRouter:** Not applicable.
- **What Runvil needs:** **Opaque field**, with no new client or Engine work. Today the Gateway calls a local server through chat completions only. The planned translators for Anthropic's and OpenAI's protocols could be pointed at a local server's routes. That gains nothing for reasoning, because a local model's reasoning is plain text with no real signature (row 17), but it has to be safe. The reply's blocks would go into the opaque field and back to the same server in later requests. If the conversation moves to a Claude model, such a block must not go to Anthropic, which did not sign it. The label prevents that: the model differs, so the Gateway sends the message's text and tool calls without the blocks (row 19). The 2026-11-17 design already has this check, so the feature needs nothing beyond it.
- **New vendor-neutral setting needed:** No.
- **Where the answer comes from:** Taken from other open-source projects: llama.cpp's README and source code, and vLLM's documentation. From earlier Runvil research: the 2026-11-17 report and the student report.
- **Confidence:** High - the label check compares the model and never reads the signature. vLLM's random signature was not checked again on 2026-11-22.
- **Evidence items:** OV71, OV76, W19, L116

## 4. Runvil should keep the same rule as OpenRouter for sending reasoning data back, can use OpenAI's own names for three fields (`reasoning_effort`, `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens`, `annotations`), and should not copy `reasoning_details` as its opaque field

OpenRouter is a commercial gateway: a service that puts one OpenAI-style API in front of the models of many vendors, the companies that make models and define their own APIs for them. Its main route (URL path) takes chat completions, OpenAI's original request and reply format for chat models, plus fields beyond it. OpenAI itself now defines some of those names, and other gateways use some of the rest.

Which names others use matters to Runvil, a system that runs prompts (Markdown files with Lua code). Its Gateway is the server that accepts every model request and forwards it to a remote model or a local one, and the plan is to give it a translator for each vendor's own protocol. The Gateway's README has two rules for its API [L88]. An addition to the standard has to be named so that someone who knows the standard can tell what it extends. And each route follows the standard it is modeled on, here chat completions, so that any program written for the standard can call the Gateway. Runvil's own client, the HTTP library that sends its requests, is only one such program. For the same reason, the best name for a field is one that OpenAI defines, and the next best is one that other gateways already use: a program written for them then works against the Gateway too.

The names that matter most are for reasoning, the text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer. Some vendors return reasoning data that they expect the caller to send back unchanged in later requests of the same conversation: Anthropic's thinking blocks, which have a signature that Anthropic checks when a block comes back, and OpenAI's encrypted reasoning. Runvil's plan for such data is the opaque field, an optional extra field on an assistant message. The Gateway would fill it, the client and the Engine, which runs the prompt, would store it and send it back without reading it, and prompts would never see it. In the design from the 2026-11-17 report "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build", it holds the reply's whole list of vendor blocks (pieces of text, reasoning, and tool calls) in the vendor's order, plus a label that says what produced them. OpenRouter's field for the same job is `reasoning_details`, an array on the assistant message.

Codes such as [R42] are evidence items (section 12), and (row 52) points to a feature's entry in the tables of section 3. After Table 15, each confidence rates the bold statement before it, on the evidence that a practice works or that a fault is real. Section 8 rates the opaque field itself.

**How to read Table 15.** Each entry is one name, or one group of related names, from OpenRouter's chat completions route.

- **Who else uses the name** lists other parties that use it, drop it, or ignore it. A count covers the parties for which a source was read, so there may be more. The sources are the parties' own documentation and source code, read on 2026-11-22.
- **Advice for Runvil** says what Runvil should do with the name and why. "Another program" is a program other than Runvil's client that calls the Gateway. An upstream is the server a gateway forwards a request to.
- **Confidence** is how sure the report is about the advice, followed by the reason.

The parties are of these kinds. Vercel AI Gateway and Infron are commercial gateways. LLM Gateway is an open-source, self-hostable gateway that its maker also runs as a service. LiteLLM, Bifrost, agentgateway, Envoy AI Gateway (now named Agent Router), and Portkey's AI Gateway are open-source gateways. Groq, Cerebras, and Together are providers, companies that serve requests for models, here open-weight ones. Amazon Bedrock is a cloud platform that serves vendors' models. Open Responses is an open specification based on OpenAI's Responses API. A clone is a product whose public documentation reproduces OpenRouter's API description nearly word for word, and a reseller is a smaller service that sells other companies' models through an API compatible with OpenRouter's.

*Table 15. Field names in OpenRouter's chat completions route: who else uses each one, and what Runvil should do with it.*

| The field in OpenRouter's API, and what it is | Who else uses the name | Advice for Runvil | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens`. A count in a reply's usage data, the token counts the reply reports: how many prompt tokens the request wrote into the vendor's prompt cache. | OpenAI's own chat completions reference defines this field. LLM Gateway, Amazon Bedrock (for OpenAI models), agentgateway, Bifrost, and Envoy AI Gateway use it too. | The Gateway should report this count under this name (row 38). The field is part of OpenAI's own format today, so it is not an addition to the standard. | High - the field was read in OpenAI's live reference. |
| `annotations` with `url_citation`. `annotations` is a list on the assistant message, and an entry of type `url_citation` names a web page that the reply cites. | OpenAI's own reference defines it, and the schema of Open Responses has it too. | Runvil does not use citations today. If it adopts them, the Gateway should return them under this name (row 64). | High - it is a field of OpenAI's own format. |
| `reasoning_effort` and `reasoning.effort`. Both are the reasoning-effort setting. The first is OpenAI's top-level field in chat completions, which OpenRouter accepts too. The second is the same setting inside a `reasoning` object that OpenRouter added to requests. | The object form is used by Vercel AI Gateway, LLM Gateway, Infron, and Ollama. | Runvil's client should send `reasoning_effort` (row 21). The Gateway should also accept the object form, so that a program written for that form works. | High - one of the two names is OpenAI's standard. |
| A `reasoning` string on a message and on a delta. The model's reasoning text as a plain string, on the assistant message of a reply and, in a streamed reply, on each delta, one increment of the reply. | Nine parties use `reasoning`, among them vLLM (which renamed its field from `reasoning_content`), Vercel AI Gateway, Groq, Cerebras, and Together [Q37]. Eleven use the competing name `reasoning_content` for the same text, among them DeepSeek and llama.cpp [Q38]. | Today the Gateway forwards the field under the name the upstream gave it, and the client reads `reasoning`, `reasoning_content`, and Ollama's `thinking`. That should stay: one name for every reply would help only another program. A translator has to write a name itself, and each should write `reasoning_content`, which the 2026-11-17 design picked for Anthropic and under which the client stores the text. | Medium - the parties are split nine against eleven, so neither name has won. |
| The `reasoning_details` array, with the entry types `reasoning.text`, `reasoning.summary`, and `reasoning.encrypted` and a `format` value on each entry. Most entries hold one piece of reasoning: readable text, a summary, or an encrypted value. `format` names the vendor format the entry came in, such as `anthropic-claude-v1`. | Four parties. Vercel AI Gateway copies it field for field. LLM Gateway copies it and adds two fields of its own. Bifrost uses the field name and the type names, without `format`. Infron documents the field name only. A reseller's page lists the vendor MiniMax as a fifth, but MiniMax's own pages name `reasoning_content` today. | Runvil should not use `reasoning_details` as its opaque field. The Gateway can still meet the array in a request. When its upstream is OpenRouter or a gateway that copies the field, the Gateway forwards the reply's extra fields untouched today, and a program that keeps them sends the array back (Runvil's client drops it [L53]). The Gateway should pass the array back to that upstream unchanged and leave it out of a request to any other (row 19). It should not rebuild a vendor's blocks from it, because it would have to guess their order. | Medium - no other structured format for reasoning data is as widely adopted, which argues for the name. Against it are four weaknesses: it loses the order of a reply's blocks, it leaves out some of what a vendor expects back, such as the `phase` label on an OpenAI message or a compaction summary, which OpenRouter returns only on its other two routes, it arrives in fragments in a streamed reply, and code that handles it edits its entries. |
| `native_finish_reason`. The standard `finish_reason` field of a reply says why the model stopped, using one of a few standard values. OpenRouter adds `native_finish_reason` beside it, with the vendor's own untranslated value. | Two clones of OpenRouter's API emit it, and one reseller removes it from its replies [Q41]. | Runvil should take the idea: the Gateway would return the vendor's raw value beside the standard one, in an optional reply field under this same name, for a person who is debugging. The Engine should not branch on it: the standard `finish_reason` is all it has to handle. Reading the raw value would mean handling each vendor's own set of values, and Runvil's design goal is that the Engine does not have to know the vendor. | Medium - the field takes little work and does no harm, and no other name competes with it. |

One states it in US dollars and one in points [Q44]. | Runvil does not report cost today (row 39). If the Gateway adds the field, it should use this name, and its documentation has to say which unit the number is in. | Medium - six gateways adopted the name, and they do not agree on the unit. |

This is a cache marker: a field on one content part of a message that tells the vendor to cache the request up to and including that part. | Vercel AI Gateway, Infron, LiteLLM, and Alibaba accept it. | The Gateway should let the marker through when another program sends it. Runvil's own client should not need to send it, because the Gateway should decide where the markers go (row 31). | High - five harnesses surveyed in the 2026-11-17 report each place the markers in the code that builds the vendor's request, and in Runvil that code would be in the Gateway. |

In `provider` a caller tells OpenRouter which providers may serve the request and in what order. `models` lists other models to fall back to. | Three services whose APIs copy OpenRouter's use the `provider` object. Vercel AI Gateway accepts `models` and one member of `provider` [Q42, Q43]. | Runvil should leave both out of its request format. One operator sets up a Gateway, so these choices belong in the Gateway's configuration and not in each request. | High - the parties that adopted these fields sell access to many providers' models, as OpenRouter does, and Runvil's Gateway does not. |

OpenRouter itself has deprecated `transforms`, `:thinking`, and `:online`. | Runvil should leave all of them out. | High - no independent gateway adopted them, and OpenRouter has deprecated three of them itself. |

Source for Table 15: the evidence file on other aggregators and cloud gateways, items Q30 to Q48, and the file that verifies third-party pages, items VT1 to VT7. What the Gateway and the client do with reasoning fields today is in items L24 and L26.

### What to take from OpenRouter: its rule for sending data back, Gateway data stored in the same field, tested capability flags, and an option that shows the translated request

**Runvil's design has the same rule as OpenRouter for sending reasoning data back to the vendor, and should keep it.** OpenRouter's rule is that the caller sends the reasoning data from a reply back whole, unchanged, and in the original order of its entries, on the assistant message it came from [R52]. The design of the opaque field says the same, so nothing in it has to change. Confidence: high - it is what Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google each require in their own APIs.

**A gateway can keep data of its own in the field that the caller already sends back. Runvil should take that idea for the opaque field.** OpenRouter puts three things besides vendor reasoning into entries of `reasoning_details`. Two are its own. One is its record of which deferred tools a conversation has loaded, in an entry of the encrypted type. A deferred tool is one whose definition is held back from the model until the model searches for it, which is called tool search and which Anthropic and OpenAI have built in. The other is its records of calls to tools that OpenRouter runs on its own servers, in a fourth entry type, `reasoning.server_tool_call`, so that a later request can refer to an earlier call. The third is Google's: the continuation token that Gemini 4 returns when a reply reaches the output cap of one request and that the next request has to send (row 13) [R33, R40, R42, P50, GG41]. A gateway that works this way needs no storage of its own between requests, because the caller brings the data back each time. So Runvil's Gateway could provide tool search for a vendor that lacks it, with its record of loaded tools in the opaque field (row 52), and remember for later requests the name of a cache object it created at Google (row 37). Confidence: high - OpenRouter documents doing this and has named error codes, such as `deferred_tools_state_missing`, for a request that arrives without the data.

**OpenRouter publishes which settings each model accepts, and it lists support for tools and structured output only after its own test passes. Runvil's Gateway should consult a per-model table when it handles a request, and should prefer a tested value to a provider's own list where one exists.** OpenRouter's model catalog (`https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/models`) has a `reasoning` object for each model with three values: `mandatory` (reasoning cannot be turned off), `supported_efforts` (the reasoning-effort values the model accepts), and `default_effort`. For each of its endpoints (one provider's offering of one model) it also publishes `supports_tool_choice`: which values of `tool_choice` passed OpenRouter's tests there. OpenRouter says that support for tools and for structured output is "never enabled from the feed, only by the capability tests passing", where the feed is a provider's own declaration of what its endpoints support [T4, M24]. Runvil's Gateway already publishes three similar values for each model in its model list (`/v1/models`): `thinking`, `effort_levels`, and `default_effort`. Today no code applies them to a request [L91]. The Gateway should convert a requested effort into one of the model's `effort_levels` (row 21), apply `default_effort` when a request gives none, and translate the thinking switch where the `thinking` mode is `switchable` (row 22). Runvil has no capability tests of its own, and this report proposes none: the model sheet behind the Gateway's model list is built from each provider's own list of models [L95]. The tested values at hand are OpenRouter's, which hold for models the Gateway reaches through OpenRouter. The program that builds the model sheet skips them today and should read them (row 94) [L94]. Confidence: high - the Gateway already has its three values, and OpenRouter's are public data, read twice with the same result for 25 models [V17].

**OpenRouter has a debug option that returns the request body it actually sent to the provider. Runvil's Gateway should have the same option as a test aid, in development builds only.** When a request for a streamed reply includes `debug: {"echo_upstream_body": true}`, OpenRouter returns, in the first chunk of the reply, the request body it sent to its upstream after translating it into the provider's format [O65 to O67]. OpenRouter's translator is closed source, so this option is the only way to see what it builds. A Gateway that translates requests needs the same view for its golden tests, which compare a translated request with a stored, known-good copy. Confidence: medium - the echoed body can contain sensitive data from the request, which OpenRouter redacts only on a best-effort basis, so the option belongs in development builds only.

### What to avoid in OpenRouter's design: it loses the order of blocks, leaves assembly and vendor settings to the caller, splits features across three routes, and changes requests silently

**OpenRouter's chat reply does not record where each piece of reasoning was among the text and the tool calls. The design of Runvil's opaque field does, and should keep doing so.** In a vendor's own reply, reasoning can come between pieces of text and between tool calls. OpenRouter's chat reply splits the reply into three separate fields on the assistant message: `reasoning_details`, `content` (the text), and `tool_calls`. When the three come back in a later request, a gateway has to put their contents back into one list for the vendor and guess the order, and OpenRouter's documentation does not say how OpenRouter does it [R61]. A wrong guess fails, because two vendor rules depend on the order. On seven Claude models (Fable and Mythos 5.1 and 5.2, and Opus, Sonnet, and Haiku 5.5) a thinking block is valid only while everything before it in the request is unchanged, including the text before it in its own message (rows 3 and 4) [AN3, AN13]. Google's Gemini attaches its signatures to individual blocks of a reply and expects each one back with the block it came on (rows 10 and 11). LLM Gateway, known here from its documentation and not its code, copies `reasoning_details` and had to add two fields of its own, `block_index` and `google_part`, to put the position back [Q31, VT2]. Confidence: high - all five open-source gateways whose code was read split a reply into separate fields by kind of block, under names of their own (of the five, only Bifrost uses `reasoning_details`), and all five lose the order: agentgateway, Bifrost, Envoy AI Gateway, Portkey's AI Gateway, and LiteLLM, whose wrong order got an HTTP 400 from Anthropic [the comparison table in evidence file J titled "where each vendor feature is handled", K57, VF16].

**OpenRouter leaves it to the caller's code to join the streamed pieces of `reasoning_details`, and the libraries written for it disagree on how. Runvil's Gateway should join the pieces itself and send the opaque field once.** OpenRouter's official library (`@openrouter/ai-sdk-provider`) for the Vercel AI SDK, a model library, needed nine fixes in 2026 to its handling of reasoning data. One way of losing data was still unfixed on 2026-11-22: reasoning entries that arrive after the answer text has started are lost. That library joins consecutive fragments of the same type and starts a new entry when the type changes, and its source says that `index`, each entry's sequence number, "cannot be used to reassemble the array". The model libraries rig and LangChain join the fragments by `index`. So three libraries use two different rules [F1 to F3, F59, F60, VF6, VT9]. The Gateway should send the field in the final chunk of a streamed reply, so that Runvil's client has nothing to join. Confidence: high - the history of fixes is in the source of OpenRouter's own library.

**The entries of `reasoning_details` have named types and readable fields, so code that handles them edits them before sending them back. Runvil's client and Engine should never parse the opaque field.** OpenRouter's own library for the Vercel AI SDK does three things to the array before it sends it back. It removes Anthropic and Gemini text entries that have no signature. It removes entries that repeat across the messages of one request. And it drops every entry whose `format` value is outside the six values the library knows, while OpenRouter's API specification lists twelve. LangChain's code for OpenRouter deletes the `id` of every entry whose id starts with `rs_`, the prefix of OpenAI's reasoning item ids [F6 to F8, F13, H42, VF6, VF9]. The opaque field holds blocks with types and fields too, and nothing stops another program from editing them. Runvil can only keep its own client and Engine from doing so. Confidence: high - the edits were read in the code, and running the library's own schema on sample entries confirmed that it drops the entries it does not know.

**OpenRouter appears to write the name of the provider that produced a value into the value itself. Runvil should record what produced a value beside it, in the label on the opaque field, and leave the vendor's bytes untouched.** The signatures and encrypted values in OpenRouter's documentation examples and in recorded traffic end with a short suffix. Decoded from base64url, it is JSON that names the model version and the provider, for example `{"endpoint_slug":"openai/gpt-5.2-20251211|openai"}`, which suggests that OpenRouter adds it [R41, G37]. If so, the value a caller holds is no longer exactly what the vendor sent. The label holds the protocol and the model in the 2026-11-17 design, and section 8 proposes adding the endpoint, as the suffix names the provider. The Gateway should put the blocks into the field exactly as the vendor sent them. Confidence: medium - that OpenRouter adds the suffix is an inference from decoding sample values, and OpenRouter does not document it.

**OpenRouter has several pass-throughs for vendor settings: request fields and headers through which a caller asks for something only one vendor has, mostly in that vendor's own setting names and values. Runvil should not offer them to prompts.** The `x-anthropic-beta` request header lets the caller switch on Anthropic's beta features by name. `provider.options[<slug>]` is an object, keyed by OpenRouter's id for a provider, in which the caller writes that provider's own parameter names. `reasoning.mode: "pro"` selects a deeper reasoning mode that only OpenAI's GPT-5.6 and newer models support. The `x_search` object turns on search of X (Twitter) on xAI's models. And `processing: "agentic"` on the video part of a message selects a video processing mode that only Google's Gemini has [AN82, O3, R7, P15, P75]. In Runvil the Lua code in a prompt decides what a request asks for, so a prompt that set any of these would have to know which vendor runs it, and Runvil rules that out. A setting of this kind belongs in a model entry (a `[[model]]` entry in `gateway.toml`), where the operator writes it once. A tool that only one vendor has, such as X search, would need a vendor-neutral name that the Gateway translates (row 73). Confidence: high - OpenRouter documents each one as something the caller writes in one vendor's terms.

**OpenRouter serves each chat model on three routes that do not offer the same features, so a caller has to choose a route according to the vendor. Runvil's Gateway can offer the same features on its one route.** Besides the chat completions route, OpenRouter has the Responses route, in the format of OpenAI's newer Responses API, and the Messages route, in Anthropic's format. The chat completions route lacks at least the features below.

These Anthropic features exist only on the Messages route:

- `between_tools`, a value of Anthropic's `thinking.type` setting and the nearest thing to thinking off on Claude Sonnet 5.5: the model does not think before it replies, and its short notes between tool calls come back as thinking blocks (row 24) [R23].
- `drop_block`, a setting that makes Anthropic drop a thinking block when the part of the request before it has changed, instead of answering with an error (row 4) [R62].
- Context editing, in which Anthropic removes old tool results or old thinking from a request before the model sees it (row 78) [AN52].
- Document citations, which point from a reply into documents the caller supplied (row 64) [AN55].
- Tool changes, which are blocks that add or remove a tool in the middle of a conversation (row 41) [AN64].
- Compaction, in which Anthropic shortens a long conversation on its own servers by replacing older messages with a summary block (row 77) [AN52].

These OpenAI features exist only on the Responses route:

- `phase`, the label (`commentary` or `final_answer`) that OpenAI's Responses API puts on each assistant message in a reply and that the caller has to send back (row 8) [OA41].
- Async tools, which are tool calls on OpenAI's GPT-6 models whose results the caller may deliver in a later request (row 53) [OA43].
- Custom tools, whose input is free text and not JSON arguments (row 51) [T37].
- Compaction items, which hold OpenAI's own summary of a long conversation (row 77) [OA47].

Shell, a tool with which the model runs commands in a sandbox that OpenAI or OpenRouter runs, works on both of those routes, and a request for it on the chat completions route gets an HTTP 400 (row 65) [OA52, S39].

A caller that wants one of these has to leave the chat completions route, and for every one except shell it has to pick the other route by the model's vendor. That is a separate vendor path, which Runvil wants to avoid. Runvil's Gateway has one route for model requests, `POST /v1/chat/completions`, and the Gateway's decision page rules out a second (section 10). Runvil's planned work needs three of these features, and none takes a second route. Two are request settings, which the Gateway would add to the request itself: `drop_block` and `between_tools`. Sonnet 5.5 rejects the value `disabled`, so the Gateway would send `between_tools` when a request switches thinking off, and its model list has to give that model the `thinking` mode `switchable` [R22]. The third, `phase`, is a label on a message that the vendor expects back, and the opaque field would hold it. The rest are features Runvil does not use. Context editing would be an operator setting. Tool changes, async tools, citations, shell, and compaction would each take a new vendor-neutral setting, and custom tools a little vendor-specific code in the Gateway. One harness, pi, already sends every `anthropic/*` model to the Messages route, to keep Claude's signed thinking, which its own code for the chat completions route dropped [F40]. Confidence: high - OpenRouter's own guides and API specification name the route for each feature.

**OpenRouter changes or drops parts of a request, and in most cases the caller is not told. Runvil's Gateway should report every change it makes in `gateway_warning`.** OpenRouter's documentation describes five cases. In the first four the request succeeds and no notice to the caller is documented:

- It accepts a reasoning token budget (`reasoning.max_tokens` or `thinking.budget_tokens`), and for Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5 "the request goes upstream as adaptive thinking with no budget" [S49, AN21]. Adaptive thinking is Anthropic's mode in which the model decides for itself how much to think.
- It removes the `strict` flag from a Claude tool definition when the request lacks the beta header that Anthropic's strict tools need [S49]. `strict` asks the vendor to guarantee that the model's JSON matches the schema.
- It keeps its own allowlist of Anthropic beta header values and drops every value that is not on the list [AN82].
- When a request asks a model that always reasons to stop reasoning and names the model by an alias id (an id that always points at a model's newest version, such as `~anthropic/claude-opus-latest`), OpenRouter changes the request into one for the lowest reasoning effort the model supports. Under the model's ordinary id the same request gets an HTTP 400 [R15].
- It shortens a request that is too long with its `context-compression` plugin [O60], which removes or truncates messages from the middle of the conversation until the request fits [O61]. The plugin is on by default on endpoints with a context window of 8,192 tokens or less [O62]. This case is reported, but only in extra metadata that a caller has to ask for [O61].

Under this report's proposals Runvil's Gateway would drop or replace settings too: it would remove a temperature that a model rejects (row 28) and part of a tool's schema that a vendor rejects (row 48), and send `between_tools` in place of thinking off (row 24). It should report each one in `gateway_warning`. Today the Gateway writes that field only for a tool call it could not parse [L110], and the client drops it [L53]. So the client would have to pass the warning on, and how it would reach a prompt author is not designed. Confidence: medium - reporting a change takes little work, but how often a prompt author would read the warning is unknown.

## 5. Where OpenRouter's callers still have to know the vendor, Runvil's Gateway will face the same problem

This section describes six situations in which the caller, the program that sends requests to OpenRouter, still has to know which vendor made a model or which provider is serving it, or has to repair what comes back. OpenRouter is a commercial service that puts one API in front of the models of many vendors (a vendor is a company that makes models and defines its own API for them). Its main route for model requests uses OpenAI's chat completions format, and it has two more: a Messages route in Anthropic's format and a Responses route in the format of OpenAI's newer Responses API. A provider is a company that serves requests for a model. OpenRouter lists five for Anthropic's Claude models: Anthropic itself, Amazon Bedrock, Google Vertex AI, Microsoft Azure, and Claude Platform on AWS, which Anthropic's documentation lists as a platform apart from Amazon Bedrock [M44, VV7].

Runvil is a system that runs prompts (Markdown files with Lua code blocks). Its Gateway, meant to do the same job as OpenRouter, is the server that accepts every model request in chat completions format and forwards it to a provider or to a model on the machine of the operator, the person who installs and configures the Gateway. Runvil's design goal is that everything vendor-specific is handled inside the Gateway, so that the client, which sends the requests, and the Engine, which runs the prompt, never have to know the vendor.

A number such as (row 28) points to one of the 105 features in the tables of section 3, and a code such as [R63] to an evidence item in the files that section 12 lists.

**1. Some models refuse a request that uses only vendor-neutral settings.** A caller would expect a request made only of vendor-neutral settings, which mean the same thing for every vendor and name none, to work on every model. OpenRouter's stated rule is that a setting is ignored when the chosen model does not support it [S41]. But a text search of its documentation and its API specification, the machine-readable description of its API, found 22 documented cases in which OpenRouter rejects the request itself, 18 with an HTTP 400, one with a 403, and three with no status stated [S47]. Four are things the model or its provider cannot do. Five ask for something on a route that does not offer it, such as a stored reply on the Responses route (`store: true` or a `previous_response_id`) [OA1]. Ten are settings that conflict, are in the wrong part of the request, or are malformed, such as more than three `fallbacks` models, or a forced tool call while tool definitions are held back for tool search [AN74, T12]. Three are requests that the caller's OpenRouter account may not make or that the request's own choice of providers rules out. The search missed at least one more conflict: OpenRouter also rejects `reasoning.effort` together with `reasoning.max_tokens`, a cap on reasoning tokens [R3].

Runvil will meet requests that fail because of the model they go to. Five such cases are documented: OpenRouter rejects the first itself (one of those four), the provider rejects the next three and OpenRouter passes its 400 on [S48], and in the fifth the model rejects the setting or ignores it:

- Reasoning off. A request turns off reasoning, the text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer, on a model that cannot turn it off. OpenRouter's model catalog, its public list of models and their properties, marks such a model with `reasoning.mandatory: true`, and OpenRouter rejects the request with a 400 and the message `Reasoning is mandatory for this endpoint and cannot be disabled.` [R14, R15].
- A forced tool call, asked for with `tool_choice`, the request setting that says whether the model may or must call a tool. Six of Anthropic's Claude models reject every forced call: Fable 5.2, Mythos 5.2, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Fable 5.1, and Mythos 5.1. OpenRouter's documentation says the caller gets "the provider's 400" [AN67, T10].
- Prefill, which means the request ends with a partial assistant message for the model to continue. Claude 4.6 and later models reject it, and the caller again gets the provider's 400 [AN69].
- Thinking sent back after an earlier part of the request has changed. Thinking is Anthropic's name for reasoning, and a Claude reply contains it as thinking blocks (a block is one element of a message: a piece of text, a piece of reasoning, or a tool call) that the caller sends back in later requests. Seven Claude models accept a thinking block only while everything before it in the request is unchanged: the system text, the tool definitions, and every earlier message [AN3]. Otherwise the provider can answer with a 400, and OpenRouter's documentation says "OpenRouter passes the upstream error through and does not drop thinking blocks for you", where the upstream is the provider [R62].
- A temperature or another sampling setting. OpenRouter's guides say that Claude Haiku 5.5 "rejects" a `temperature` other than `1`, without saying what the caller then gets, and that Claude Opus 4.7 accepts the request and ignores the setting [AN66, M35].

*What this means for Runvil:* The Gateway has to know what a model accepts before it forwards a request. That is the per-model table, data that the Gateway would look up for each request and does not consult today. What it should do then:

- Reasoning off (row 22). A prompt asks for it only through the `thinking` requirement in its front matter. On a model whose `thinking` mode in the Gateway's model list is `always`, `thinking: false` fails the Engine's requirement check, which ends the run before the prompt's code runs. On a `switchable` model each request has a thinking switch, which the Gateway has to translate into the vendor's own form. Claude Sonnet 5.5 has to be listed as `switchable`: it rejects Anthropic's off value, so for off the Gateway has to send `between_tools`, its nearest mode, in which the model does not think before it answers and still writes short notes between its tool calls, and report the replacement (row 24).
- A forced tool call (row 44) and prefill (row 30), neither of which Runvil uses today. For a model that refuses a forced call, the Gateway would let the model choose, add an instruction to call the tool, and report the change. For a model that rejects prefill, it would refuse the request before sending it.
- Thinking blocks (row 4). The Gateway has to send `drop_block`, the setting that makes Anthropic drop a thinking block when what came before it has changed, where it could otherwise reject the request.
- Sampling settings (row 28). The Gateway has to remove a setting that the model rejects or ignores, and report the removal.

The reports would go in `gateway_warning`, as section 8 proposes. Today the Gateway adds that field to a reply only for a tool call it could not parse. Confidence: high - the vendors document, model by model, which of these requests they refuse, so the Gateway can know before it forwards one, but only if it keeps data per model.

**2. The same model behaves differently depending on which provider served the request.** OpenRouter picks one of a model's providers for each request, and each provider's offering of a model is called an endpoint. OpenRouter's public list of each model's endpoints and its documentation show these differences:

- Anthropic's own web search and web fetch tools exist only on a Claude model's endpoints named Anthropic and Claude Platform on AWS. On its Amazon Bedrock, Microsoft Azure, and Google Vertex AI endpoints, OpenRouter runs both tools itself [P12, AN40].
- Four properties differ from one endpoint to the next: which `tool_choice` values it accepts (the `supports_tool_choice` flags), whether it lists `structured_outputs`, the parameter for structured output (a reply in JSON that matches a schema the caller supplies), its context length, and its output limit. Zhipu's GLM 5.3 (`z-ai/glm-5.3`), for example, has 46 endpoints with context lengths from 262,144 to 1,048,576 tokens [M49, M50, T9].
- Anthropic's rule about thinking blocks (situation 1) is enforced by some providers and not by others. OpenRouter says only that enforcement "depends on the provider that serves the request" [AN10].
- When a caller aborts a streamed reply, one that arrives in chunks while the model writes it, billing stops on 24 providers that OpenRouter names and continues on 24 others that it names [O51, V16].
- A video given as a YouTube link works when a Gemini model is served by Google AI Studio and not when it is served by Google Vertex AI, Google's two providers for Gemini [GG76].
- Fast mode, Anthropic's option for faster replies, "is only served by the Anthropic first-party provider", which means by Anthropic itself and not by a cloud platform that resells the model [AN85].

*What this means for Runvil:* In the Gateway's configuration a model entry (`[[model]]`) lists the model's endpoints, and an endpoint there is one backend: a base URL with its API key, so one account at one provider. Today the Gateway sends every request for a model to the first endpoint in that list [L117]. Once it chooses among a model's endpoints, which is planned (row 100), the per-model table of situation 1 has to hold its values for each endpoint, at least the four properties of the second bullet, so that the Gateway applies those of the endpoint it sends a request to (row 95). Confidence: high - the differences belong to the providers and are in OpenRouter's public endpoint data and documentation, so the Gateway will meet them as soon as it serves one model from two providers.

**3. Data that a vendor expects back is accepted only by the provider account that produced it, or one linked to it.** Some vendors put data into a reply that the caller has to send back unchanged in later requests of the same conversation, usually a record of the model's reasoning that the vendor has signed or encrypted. A signature is a string a vendor attaches to a block, most often a reasoning block, so that it can confirm, when the block comes back, that the block is its own, unchanged output. An encrypted value is a string that only the vendor can read. In OpenRouter's words such data is readable only by "the provider account that produced them" [R63], so a caller of OpenRouter ends up needing to know which provider served a request:

- When a Gemini conversation moves from Google AI Studio to Google Vertex AI or the other way between a tool call and the request that returns its result, that request gets a 400. The team that maintains OpenRouter's official client libraries confirmed this on 2026-06-17 in an issue report. It advised callers to pin the provider, which means the caller names the providers to use and forbids any other, with `provider: { order: [...], allow_fallbacks: false }`. That comment is still the last one on the issue [F45, VT8].
- Google's documentation for Vertex AI says that a thought signature, Google's name for its signature, has to be returned unchanged within "the same conversation history, model, and Google Cloud project" [VV18].
- Thinking blocks from four Claude models (Sonnet 5.5, Haiku 5.5, Fable 5.2, and Mythos 5.2) work only in the account that produced them or a linked one, and in any other account Anthropic drops them without an error. On Anthropic's own API the account is the caller's organization [VV7].
- OpenAI removes reasoning data from a request when the data was produced under another organization [OA27].

OpenRouter does three things about this, as far as the evidence shows. First, it appears to mark each value with its origin: in its documentation examples and in recorded traffic (requests and replies that open-source projects saved in their test suites), signatures and encrypted values end with a base64 suffix that decodes to the model version and the provider, such as `{"endpoint_slug":"openai/gpt-5.2-20251211|openai"}`. OpenRouter's documentation never describes the suffix, so this is an inference [R41, G37]. Second, it documents a routing rule for two of those four models: a request that sends back thinking blocks from Haiku 5.5 stays on the provider that produced them, and one with blocks from Sonnet 5.5 stays among Anthropic, Claude Platform on AWS, and Microsoft Azure, which count as linked because, in OpenRouter's words, they "share an account group" [V6]. Third, when the caller's provider preferences (the request fields `provider.only` and `provider.ignore`) exclude the provider that produced the blocks, OpenRouter returns a 400. Its documentation does not say how OpenRouter knows which provider produced a block [R63].

The rule for those two models is not sticky routing, OpenRouter's practice, documented for prompt caching, of sending a conversation's later requests to the provider that served the earlier ones. Sticky routing recognizes a conversation by the caller's OpenRouter account, the model, and a hash of the opening messages, or by a `session_id` that the caller sends. It falls back to another provider when the first is unavailable, so such data can still reach a provider that cannot read it [C25 to C27].

*What this means for Runvil:* Runvil plans to keep such data in the opaque field (row 5): an optional extra field on an assistant message in the universal API, the planned request format between the client and the Gateway.
