The Gateway would fill it, and the client and the Engine would store it and send it back in every later request without reading it. Beside the vendor's data the Gateway would store a label that says what produced the data. In the design from the 2026-11-17 report "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build", the label holds the protocol and the model. The vendors' rules above mean that the label has to name the Gateway's endpoint too, and that the Gateway has to send a conversation's later requests to that endpoint. How it would route by the label is not designed, and today all of a model's requests go to one endpoint anyway (situation 2). When a request goes elsewhere, because the model was changed or that endpoint is down, the label does not match. The Gateway should then leave the vendor's data out and rebuild the message from its text and tool calls (rows 5 and 19). With Anthropic that is known to work between turns and untested in the middle of a tool loop, and Gemini then needs Google's placeholder signature (row 12). Confidence: medium - the vendors define "account" differently, so a label that names the Gateway's endpoint only approximates what each vendor checks, and nobody has measured how often a mismatch occurs.

**4. Some vendor features can be reached only on a route that uses the vendor's own format.** OpenRouter's chat completions route offers none of these ten features.

These five work on the Messages route only:

- `between_tools`, a value of Anthropic's request setting `thinking.type` that only Claude Sonnet 5.5 accepts (situation 1, row 24) [AN26].
- `drop_block`, a value of another Anthropic request setting (situation 1, row 4) [R62].
- Context editing done by the vendor, a request setting. Anthropic removes old tool results or old thinking from a request before the model sees it, while the caller keeps the full history (row 78) [AN52].
- Document citations, a switch on a document in the request plus citation objects on the text of the reply: Anthropic's references from a reply into documents that the caller supplied (row 64) [AN55].
- Tool changes, which are Anthropic blocks in a request that add or remove tools in the middle of a conversation (row 41) [S36].

These three work on the Responses route only:

- `phase`, a field on each assistant message in a reply of OpenAI's Responses API, with the value `commentary` or `final_answer`. One reply can hold several such messages, and the caller has to send the field back (row 8) [OA41].
- Async tools, a flag on a tool definition. With it, a model of OpenAI's GPT-6 family does not wait for the tool's result: it keeps working, and the result arrives in a later request (row 53) [OA43].
- Custom tools, a second kind of tool definition and of tool call: OpenAI tools whose input is plain text and not a JSON object (row 51) [T37].

These two are not limited to one route:

- Compaction done by the vendor, which involves a block: the vendor writes a summary of the older part of a long conversation and returns it in a block that the caller sends back in later requests. Anthropic's form works only on the Messages route and OpenAI's only on the Responses route (row 77) [AN52, OA47].
- Shell, a tool with which the model runs commands in a sandbox on OpenRouter's or the vendor's servers. OpenRouter offers it as `openrouter:shell` on both of those routes and answers a request for it on the chat completions route with a 400 (row 65) [OA52, S39].

A caller that wants one of them has to leave the chat completions route, and except for shell the model's vendor decides which route it needs.

*What this means for Runvil:* Runvil has one route for model requests, and its planned work needs three of the ten on it. Two are settings that the Gateway would send itself: `between_tools` and `drop_block` (situation 1). The third is `phase` (row 8). A chat completions reply is one assistant message: its text is in `content`, its tool calls are in `tool_calls`, and `reasoning_details`, OpenRouter's list on that message for data that goes back, holds mostly reasoning entries. So the format has no place for a vendor's own text blocks or for the order of a reply's blocks, and OpenRouter's documentation says it "cannot represent multiple output items with distinct phases in a single response" [OA41]. If Runvil's opaque field were limited in the same way, Runvil would need a second route too, which is the separate vendor path it wants to avoid. Runvil's design avoids it by having the opaque field hold the reply's whole list of vendor blocks, in the vendor's order. The other seven features, which Runvil does not use today, could be offered on the one route the same way: by Gateway code, with the opaque field for a block that has to come back and, for compaction, document citations, tool changes, async tools, and shell, a new vendor-neutral field (rows 77, 64, 41, 53, and 65). Confidence: high - this rates what OpenRouter shows: its own guides name the route each feature needs, so a field limited like `reasoning_details` forces a second route. Whether Runvil's opaque field can hold every such block is the separate question that section 8 rates medium.

**5. For some settings the caller has to use a vendor's own setting names and values.** OpenRouter leaves these to the caller:

- Cache markers. A cache marker is a `cache_control` field on one block of a request, which tells the vendor to cache the request up to and including that block. The open-source harness opencode (a harness runs a model and its tools for a user) decides which markers to send by the start of the model id, which names the vendor: none unless it begins with `anthropic/` or names Qwen [F19].
- The `x-anthropic-beta` request header, which names the Anthropic beta features a request wants. OpenRouter attaches it itself for the features it detects in a request and leaves two to the caller. One is strict tools, where the flag `strict` on a tool definition asks the vendor to guarantee that the model's JSON matches the tool's schema: without the header OpenRouter removes the flag for Anthropic's models. The other is interleaved thinking, in which Claude's thinking is spread through a reply and does not come as one block [T21, R25].
- `provider.options`, a request field in which the caller writes a provider's own setting names and values under that provider's name [O3].
- Cache lifetimes. The caller chooses how long a cache entry is kept, and each vendor has its own set of allowed values, such as `1h` at Anthropic and `12h` at OpenAI [C19, C22].
- The `x_search` object, a request field that turns on search of the X social network and works only on xAI's models [P15].

*What this means for Runvil:* In Runvil a caller's settings come from a prompt, and a prompt must not name a vendor, so each of these has to come from the Gateway or from its configuration. The Gateway should place cache markers itself (row 31). It has to attach the vendor's headers for beta features and API versions (row 96), and to fit tool schemas and the `strict` flag to each vendor, which treats them differently (row 48). The operator would write the remaining values into the model entry: a cache lifetime (row 33), and any other setting that exists at one vendor only, which the Gateway would add to each request for that model (row 97). Confidence: high - the open-source gateway agentgateway already places cache markers when its caller sends none [J15], and OpenRouter attaches the beta header itself for the features it detects [R25], so a gateway can supply both without its caller.

**6. Callers of OpenRouter repair its replies in their own code.** Some replies reach the caller in a form that has to be repaired before it can be used. OpenRouter's official client library for the Vercel AI SDK (an open-source TypeScript model library, which gives a program one message type across vendors) makes such repairs for its callers. It creates a new tool-call id when a provider sends an empty or repeated one [T18]. When a Gemini reply holds tool calls with a thought signature and has the finish reason `stop`, it changes that to the finish reason for tool calls, where the finish reason is the field in a reply that says why the model stopped [O58]. When a reply ends for a reason other than the model finishing, such as the output limit, it drops the tool calls whose arguments are not complete JSON that parses [T28]. It has fifteen special cases, pieces of code written for one behavior of OpenRouter's, for sending reasoning data back in a later request [F67]. A count of such cases found at least 187 in six TypeScript projects, this library among them, and at least 90 in nine Rust and Python projects, with sending reasoning data back as the largest group in both (43 and 19) [F67, and the count table in evidence file G]. Harnesses also keep their own rules for single model ids, for what OpenRouter's model catalog does not tell them [F35, F39], and search error messages for the phrase "Provider returned error" to decide whether to retry a request [F64, F41].

*What this means for Runvil:* So that Runvil's client never collects such a list of repairs, the Gateway has to put four things into one standard form itself: make every tool-call id unique (row 46), report the tool-call finish reason when a reply contains a tool call, unless the reply was cut off at its output limit (row 85), check the JSON of each tool call's arguments (row 49, where its handling of a failed check is not designed), and return errors with a named type, so that a client does not have to search the text of a message (row 86). The rest is covered elsewhere: sending reasoning data back, the largest group, by the opaque field (situation 3), and the rules for single model ids by the per-model table (situation 1). Confidence: high - each repair was read in source code at a fixed commit, which shows that these defects reach a gateway's caller unless the gateway repairs them.

## 6. OpenRouter's chat completions route leaves out three kinds of vendor feature: data kept on the vendor's servers, blocks a chat message cannot hold, and settings in a vendor's own words

OpenRouter is a commercial service that puts one API in front of the models of many vendors, the companies that make models and define their own APIs for them. It has three routes (URL paths) for model requests: a chat completions route in OpenAI's original request and reply format for chat models (with fields of OpenRouter's own added), a Messages route in Anthropic's format, and a Responses route in the format of OpenAI's newer Responses API. OpenRouter documents no way for a caller, the program that sends the requests, to get three kinds of vendor feature, or a count of tokens, through the chat completions route. The last two kinds both include a vendor's own settings. What divides them is where OpenRouter documents a feature: on its Messages or Responses route, or on no route.

These gaps matter to Runvil, a system that runs prompts written as Markdown files with Lua code blocks, because Runvil plans an API of the same kind. Runvil's client, the HTTP library that sends each model request, is to talk to its Gateway, the server that forwards each request to a model vendor or to a model on the local machine, in the universal inference API: the chat completions format plus a small number of optional extra fields. Nothing proposed below is built yet.

Row numbers in round brackets point to section 3's feature tables. Codes in square brackets, such as [OA1], are evidence items (section 12).

**OpenRouter leaves out, on every route, features in which the vendor keeps data on its own servers between requests.** The data is the conversation so far (row 74) or a job that is still running (row 75), and the caller refers to it by an id.

- In OpenAI's Responses API, `store: true` asks OpenAI to store a reply, and `previous_response_id` tells OpenAI to continue from a stored reply. On OpenRouter's Responses route, a request that sets `store: true` or a non-null `previous_response_id` gets an HTTP 400 error [OA1, OA24].
- OpenAI's API has routes that create, retrieve, cancel, and delete a reply. OpenRouter's API specification, the machine-readable description of its API, has only the one that creates a reply [OA2, S2].
- OpenAI's `conversation` field, which names a conversation that OpenAI keeps on its servers, is not a request field of OpenRouter's Responses route [OA4, OA24].
- OpenRouter does not offer Google's Interactions API, Google's newer API in which Google's servers store the conversation by default [GG79, GG82].
- OpenAI's background mode keeps a long request running on OpenAI's servers after the HTTP call has returned. The caller fetches the result later by id, after asking for it again and again (polling) or being called at a URL it supplied (a webhook) [OA57]. OpenRouter has no route for fetching it, and its documentation does not say what the `background` field of its Responses request does [OA58].

Six open-source gateways (servers that translate between a caller and several vendors' formats) forward a `previous_response_id` to OpenAI or drop it, and none makes it work for a vendor that does not store replies: agentgateway, Bifrost, claude-code-router, Portkey's AI Gateway, Envoy AI Gateway, and new-api [J22, J41, J63, the comparison table in evidence file J]. A seventh, LiteLLM, imitates the feature for any model: it rebuilds the conversation from its own database of logged requests, and imitates background mode with a Redis cache that the caller polls [CT43, CT44].

The three projects found to support Google's Interactions API each give it an entry point separate from their ordinary chat requests [GX64, GX65, GX66]. To use it, Runvil's client would need one too, beside the universal API (row 74).

*What this means for Runvil:* Runvil should use none of these features. Chat completions is stateless: each request contains the whole conversation, and the server remembers nothing in between. To offer a stored conversation for every vendor, the Gateway would need a store of its own. Runvil has no need for one, because the Engine, which runs the prompt, already holds the conversation in its own list of message records and sends all of it with every request. A background job would need a second route for fetching the result, which Runvil does not need, because the Engine sends each request and waits for the reply before it goes on. What a vendor's stored copy holds beyond the visible messages is mostly the model's reasoning, the text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer. A caller that sends the conversation itself has to send that data back unchanged, signed or encrypted as the vendor returned it (rows 1 to 20). Runvil's plan for that is the opaque field: an optional extra field on an assistant message, which the Gateway fills and which the client and the Engine store and send back without reading it. Confidence: high - OpenRouter rejects a stored reply, those six gateways do not make one work for a vendor that lacks it, and the one that imitates it needs a database of its own.

**OpenRouter documents some vendor features on its Messages route or its Responses route only: each needs a block type, a request setting, or a stop reason that the chat completions format has no field for.** A block is one element of a message in a vendor's own format: Anthropic's content block, the output item of OpenAI's Responses API, Google's part. A stop reason, which chat completions calls a finish reason, is the value in a reply that says why the model stopped. A chat completions assistant message has one text field (`content`) and one list of tool calls (`tool_calls`), and OpenRouter adds no field to that route for the settings and stop reasons below.

Anthropic's features of this kind are these [AN26, AN45, AN52, AN55, AN64, AN73, AN80, R23, R62, S36]. Some are blocks, or fields on a block or a message:

- `server_tool_use` blocks and their result blocks record a call to a vendor-run tool, one that the vendor runs on its own servers during a request, and what it returned (row 62).
- Compaction blocks hold Anthropic's summary of the older part of a long conversation (row 77).
- `tool_addition` and `tool_removal` blocks, which the caller puts in a system message in the middle of a conversation, add or remove tools. Of these two OpenRouter's documentation says: "the Chat Completions API has no representation for these blocks".
- `clear_at` is a field on such a system message, with the values `next_user_message` and `never`. Anthropic calls a message with it turn-scoped, and what the field does was not found.
- Document citations are references from the reply's text blocks into documents that the caller supplied.

Others are request settings:

- Context editing makes Anthropic remove old tool results or old thinking from a request before the model sees it. Thinking is Anthropic's word for reasoning.
- `between_tools` is a value of the `thinking` setting that only Claude Sonnet 5.5 accepts, a model that rejects the ordinary off value, `disabled`. With it the model does not think before it replies, and the short notes it writes between tool calls come back as thinking blocks.
- `drop_block` is a value of a setting for thinking blocks. On its newer Claude models Anthropic accepts a thinking block only while everything before it in the request is unchanged. With `drop_block`, Anthropic drops a block that fails that check, and the request succeeds.

Two concern how a reply stops:

- `pause_turn` is the stop reason Anthropic gives when a series of vendor-run tool calls reaches its iteration limit before the model's answer is finished. The caller sends the unfinished reply back as it is, and the model continues. OpenRouter lists it for the Messages route and does not say what a caller on the chat completions route gets.
- `stop_details` gives the category and the explanation when a model refuses a request.

OpenAI's features of this kind are these [OA41, OA43, OA47, OA54]:

- `phase` is a label (`commentary` or `final_answer`) that OpenAI's Responses API puts on each assistant message in a reply and that the caller has to send back. One reply can contain several assistant messages, and a chat completions reply holds one (row 8).
- Async tool calls are turned on with `async: true` on a tool definition: the model does not wait for the tool's result, and the result arrives in a later request.
- Custom tools are tools whose input is plain text and not a JSON object, and apply patch is a tool through which the model proposes file edits as a patch.
- Compaction items hold OpenAI's encrypted summary of the older part of a conversation (row 77).

Shell, a tool with which the model runs commands in a sandbox, is on the Messages route and the Responses route as OpenRouter's own `openrouter:shell`, and the Responses route also accepts OpenAI's form of the tool. On the chat completions route a request for it gets an HTTP 400 error [S39, OA52].

Google has two pairs of part types of the same sort, with one difference: OpenRouter's documentation mentions them on none of its three routes, so whether a caller can get them is not known [GG13, GG70]. `executableCode` and `codeExecutionResult` hold code that Gemini wrote and Google ran on its servers, and the result of running it (row 65). `toolCall` and `toolResponse` record a call to one of Google's vendor-run tools and its result (row 62). The second pair appears when a request combines those tools with the caller's own tools, and each of its parts has a signature, a string that Google attaches to the part and requires back with it.

*What this means for Runvil:* Blocks that arrive in a reply and have to go back would be stored in the opaque field: Anthropic's vendor-run tool blocks and compaction blocks, OpenAI's items with their `phase` and its compaction items, and Google's parts. For that the field has to hold the reply's whole list of vendor blocks, in the vendor's order. OpenRouter's field for that job on its chat completions route, `reasoning_details`, cannot: it is a list on the assistant message of reasoning entries and a few other records, with no place for a vendor's text, tool-call, and tool-result blocks or for the order of a reply's blocks [R33, R40, R42].

The Gateway has to send the request settings itself, so that no prompt names them: `drop_block` whenever thinking is on (row 4), `between_tools` on Claude Sonnet 5.5 when a request's thinking switch is off (row 24), and, if Runvil adopts it, context editing where the operator, the person who installs and configures a Gateway, turns it on for a model (row 78).

The Gateway should also handle a stop reason that asks the caller to continue, by sending the follow-up request itself and returning one complete reply. That covers a reply that Anthropic ended with `pause_turn` (row 63), and an answer from Google's Gemini 4 that stops at the per-request output limit and has to be continued (row 13). Four projects continue such a reply by themselves: the model libraries Pydantic AI and the Vercel AI SDK (libraries that give a program one message type over several vendors), LiteLLM, and Google's own client libraries. A refusal's text has to go into the chat completions field `refusal`, which has no place for the category in `stop_details` (row 57).

The opaque field cannot hold what the caller writes into a request. `tool_addition` and `tool_removal` would need a vendor-neutral "tools changed" record, and async tool calls a vendor-neutral marker for a tool result that arrives later. Neither is designed (rows 41 and 53). `clear_at` has no use in Runvil, because the Engine accepts system messages only at the start of a conversation. Document citations have no design (row 64). The Gateway would hand a custom tool call or an apply patch call to the client as an ordinary tool call with one text argument (row 51). Shell is not needed, because Runvil runs code in tools of its own (row 65).

The 2026-11-17 report "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build", which designed the opaque field, said that vendor-run tools and compaction done by the vendor "produce blocks and stop reasons with no home in chat completions", and that adopting either might force Runvil to add a route in Anthropic's own format. The problem is smaller than that: the blocks would have a place in the opaque field, and the Gateway can continue a reply itself. Confidence, for those two points: medium - LiteLLM keeps Anthropic's vendor-run tool blocks in an extra field on the message, `provider_specific_fields` [K45], and four projects continue a reply without the caller. But neither LiteLLM's field nor `reasoning_details` keeps the order of a reply's blocks: LiteLLM rebuilds it when the blocks come back [CT29].

**OpenRouter documents no field of its own, on any route, for settings and tools that exist only under one vendor's own setting names and values [GG65, GG69, GG75, AN34, AN88, OV26, OV42, OV43, OV52, OV56].** Some exist at one vendor only. Others are one vendor's form of something several vendors have, such as the web search of Moonshot, Alibaba, and Zhipu, which OpenRouter's own web search tool does not use [OV10].

- Google's `safetySettings` sets the thresholds of Google's safety filter in Google's own categories (row 72).
- Google's `media_resolution` setting controls how Gemini processes media such as images and video, and so how many tokens the media cost.
- Google's URL context is a vendor-run tool that reads web pages whose URLs the request names, and its Maps grounding is one that bases an answer on Google Maps data (row 73).
- Anthropic's `container.skills` names the Skills, code kept at Anthropic under an id such as `pptx`, that a request may use with Anthropic's code execution tool. OpenRouter's API specification has the field only in the reply of the Messages route (row 82).
- Anthropic's cache diagnostics: the caller sends the id of the previous reply in a `diagnostics` field, and Anthropic's reply says why the request missed the prompt cache, its stored copy of the processed beginning of an earlier request (row 40).
- Moonshot's formula tools are its own tools for its Kimi models, such as web search. The caller gets their definitions from Moonshot's separate Formula API and sends each call the model makes to that API, where Moonshot runs it (row 73).
- Alibaba's `enable_search` is a request switch that turns on vendor-run web search for its Qwen models, and Zhipu's `web_search` tool type does the same for Zhipu's (row 73).
- DeepSeek's `prefix: true` and Moonshot's `partial: true` are flags on the last message of a request that mark it as a prefill, a partial assistant message that the model is supposed to continue (row 30).

The one request field that could pass such a setting through is `provider.options` (row 97). In it the caller writes setting names and values in the words of one provider, a company that serves requests for a model. OpenRouter forwards only the names on an allowed list that it does not publish, so a caller cannot tell whether any of these settings would reach the vendor.

*What this means for Runvil:* An API built on chat completions could pass such settings through unchanged, at the price of a caller that uses each vendor's own words. Runvil should not offer that to prompts, because the author of a prompt would then have to know which vendor runs it, and Runvil rules that out. A setting that holds for every request to a model, such as Google's safety settings or media resolution, should be set by the operator on that model's entry in the Gateway's configuration (rows 72 and 97). A flag that goes with one request would be added by the Gateway, such as the prefill flag where a request ends with an assistant message (row 30). For a tool, a prompt would ask for the capability under a vendor-neutral name, which is not designed (row 73). Runvil uses none of these tools, Skills, or cache diagnostics. Confidence, for keeping these settings out of prompts: high - no open-source project examined has a vendor-neutral form of Google's safety settings, and two gateways send a default that the operator sets [GX11]. Only Pydantic AI has vendor-neutral names for tools [GX21].

**OpenRouter has no route that counts tokens, so its caller has to estimate the size of a request itself.** Anthropic has a token-counting route, which returns an estimate of the input tokens in a request (row 79). OpenRouter's API specification lists 123 routes, and none counts tokens [AN86]. After a reply arrives, the caller can read the real numbers from `usage`, the token counts that a reply reports. Runvil works this way today: the Engine's precheck estimates the size of a request before it is sent, and starts from the reported numbers once a reply has reported `usage`.

## 7. What Runvil's Gateway, client, and Engine do today, and the defects that lose token counts and error details, so that a vendor's "prompt too long" error does not start compaction

Runvil is a system that runs prompts written as Markdown files with Lua code blocks. Four layers handle a model request, and Table 16 has a column for each: the prompt's Lua code, the Engine, the gateway client, and the Gateway. The Lua code starts a model call: `models.infer` sends one request, and `models.loop` runs a tool loop, in which the model asks for a tool call, the tool's result is sent back, and the model continues. A round is one request and its reply inside the loop. The Engine, which runs the prompt and holds the conversation as a list of message records, runs the rounds and builds each request. Lua continues when the call returns. The Host, the program that embeds the Engine, passes each request to the gateway client, Runvil's HTTP library, which writes it in chat completions, OpenAI's original format for chat models, and always asks for a streamed reply. The Gateway, Runvil's server, which an operator installs and configures, forwards the request to an upstream: a remote one, reached over the network, such as a vendor's API, or a local model. A local model is a local server, a program that runs a model on the operator's own machine, that the Gateway starts itself: llama.cpp's `llama-server`.

Table 16 shows what each layer does today and names the main files, and six defects in that code follow it. The code is written in Rust. It was read on 2026-11-22 at these commits: `runvil-harness` at `22a0992f` (the gateway client and the Engine), `runvil-gateway` at `52fd92d` (the Gateway), and `runvil-cloud-providers` at `50961b2`, which publishes the model sheet, `cloud-provider-models.json`, a weekly file that lists each cloud provider's models with their settings. A cloud provider is a service that serves models over the network. The code was not built, and its tests were not run. So every statement here about what the code does is a reading of the code, to be confirmed by running it. Codes in square brackets such as [L57] are evidence items, which section 12 explains. The L items record this reading, with line numbers, in the evidence file titled "Runvil today: a code map of the Gateway, the gateway client, and the Engine". The M items are in the file titled "OpenRouter model discovery".

An earlier Runvil report read the same code at older commits: "How agent harnesses handle Anthropic's Messages API, and what Runvil should build", finished on 2026-11-17. It designed the opaque field, which is not built yet: an optional extra field on an assistant message for data that a vendor sent and expects back unchanged. The client and the Engine would store it and send it back, unread, in every later request, and Lua would never see it. That report's statements about Runvil's code (its Table 3 and Appendix D) were read again and still match today's code. Only line numbers moved, in four places, which the L items list.

**How to read Table 16.** Each row is an area of a model request or its reply, apart from the last two: the model list, and the interfaces where a translator would be added. File paths in a cell are relative to the folder in the column's header. A streamed reply arrives as chunks, each on a line that starts with `data:`. In chat completions each chunk has a list named `choices`, each choice has a `delta` that holds the next increment of the reply, and the line `data: [DONE]` ends the stream. A line that starts with a colon is a comment. Servers send comments as keep-alives: small messages during a long silence, so that the connection is not treated as dead.

*Table 16. What each layer of Runvil's code does today in each area of a model request and its reply.*

| Area | Gateway (`runvil-gateway`, `crates/gateway`) | Gateway client (`runvil-harness`, `crates/harness-gateway-client/src`) | Engine (`runvil-harness`, `crates/runvil-internal`) | What the prompt's Lua code can see or set |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Request fields.** The settings and data in the body of a model request. | The Gateway reads three request fields by name: `model`, `messages`, and `stream`. It forwards every other top-level field unchanged, and in `model` it puts the model's name at the upstream. A tool dialect is the one exception, in this row and the next (see the Tools note). (`protocol/src/wire.rs`, `protocol/src/upstream/openai.rs`) | The client's request body has at most nine keys. Four are always sent: `model`, `messages`, `stream`, and `stream_options`, which asks for usage. `tools` and `tool_choice: "auto"` are sent only when the request has tools. `temperature`, `max_tokens`, and `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, the thinking switch, are each sent only when the setting has a value. (`wire/request.rs`) | The Engine's type for the settings of one request, `ModelInvocation`, has three optional values: `temperature`, `max_tokens`, and `thinking`, the thinking switch, which tells a model whether to reason before it answers. (`model-client/src/model/options.rs`) | The prompt's Lua code can set two settings, by calling `models.use(role, {temperature, max_tokens})`. The thinking switch comes from the prompt's front matter, the YAML header at the top of the prompt file (see the Model metadata note). The model handle that `models.use` returns has a read-only `provider` field, the model's provider id from the model list, such as `xai`. |
| **Message records.** The fields that one message of the conversation has in each layer. | The Gateway handles each message as untyped JSON and forwards every field of it unchanged, in requests and in replies. | The client builds each reply from a fixed set of fields that it collects from the stream: text, reasoning text, tool calls, finish reason, model name, usage, and two timing sections for the Host, llama.cpp's `timings` and vLLM's `metrics`. Any other field in a reply is dropped at the client and never reaches the Engine. (`wire/parse.rs`, `wire/stream.rs`) | The Engine's type for one message of a request, `Message`, has four fields: `role`, `content`, `tool_call_id`, and `tool_calls`. A stored record also has four: `role`, `content`, `tool_calls`, and `meta`. The `role` of a tool result holds the id of the tool call it answers, which Lua reads as a fifth field, `tool_call_id`. The decision page `decisions/engine.md` in `runvil-harness`, a record of the Engine's design decisions, states the intent to keep data for each reply where Lua cannot see it, as the opaque field needs. That hidden place is still to be added to the record type. | The prompt's Lua code reads all five. `meta` is any JSON that the prompt attaches to a record, and it is never sent to the model. |
| **Reasoning.** The text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer. Some vendors call it thinking. | The Gateway forwards the reasoning fields `reasoning_content`, `reasoning`, and `reasoning_details` exactly as the upstream wrote them. | The client reads reasoning as one string, from the first of the fields `reasoning_content`, `reasoning`, and `thinking` that has text. A field of another name, such as `reasoning_details`, is dropped. The client hands each fragment to a Host that shows the reply as it forms, and puts the whole text on the finished reply. It never sends reasoning back to the model in a later request. | The Engine reports each round's reasoning text to the Host in one `Thinking` event (events are the Engine's progress messages to the Host) and then discards it. A Host that showed the fragments receives the text a second time. The precheck (see defect 3) leaves out the reasoning tokens that a reply reported, because reasoning is not sent again. (`lua/src/compactors.rs`) | The prompt's Lua code cannot read reasoning text, which goes to the Host. It can read the thinking switch, as `handle.thinking`. |
| **Text in the same reply as tool calls, and the order of a reply's parts.** A reply can hold text, reasoning, and tool calls together, in the order the model produced them. | The Gateway forwards each part of the reply as the upstream sent it, in the same order. | When a finished reply has tool calls, the client returns the tool calls without the text it collected beside them, which a Host that shows the reply as it forms has already received. It also keeps a reply's text, reasoning, and tool calls as three separate values, so the order in which the model produced them is lost. | For a reply with tool calls, the Engine appends an assistant record that has empty content and the tool calls. | The prompt's Lua code sees that assistant record, with its empty content. |
| **Tools.** Functions that the model can ask the caller's program to run. | The Gateway forwards `tools` and `tool_choice` unchanged, except for a model that uses the tool dialect `gemma3_tool_code` (see the note). | The client sends each tool in OpenAI's `function` format. It fails the round when a reply's tool calls were cut off, which it recognizes by a finish reason of `length` or `content_filter`. | The projection, the Engine step that converts the stored records into the messages of a request, enforces two rules. Each tool-call id must be unique across the whole message list. A reply's tool calls must be followed directly by their results. (`lua/src/projection.rs`) | The prompt's Lua code reads tool calls in the `tool_calls` field of a record. |
| **Streaming.** How a reply travels while it is still being produced. | The Gateway parses each chunk from the upstream and writes it out again. On a chunk it can parse, it keeps the fields it does not know, whether at the top of the chunk, in a choice, or in a delta. It skips a chunk that has no `model` field or no choice. It writes its own `[DONE]` at the end of the stream. Its only time limit on a stream is 10 seconds for connecting. (`protocol/src/upstream.rs`, `protocol/src/http_util.rs`, `app/src/relay.rs`) | The client requires the stream to end with `[DONE]`, and fails the round if it does not. It skips comment lines. It waits at most 120 seconds each time it waits for more data, and it accepts at most 16 MiB for one reply. | The Engine receives one finished reply for each round, as a `Completion` value. | The prompt's Lua code sees a reply only after the round has ended, as records in the message list or as the text that `models.infer` returns. |
| **Usage.** The token counts that a reply reports. | The Gateway forwards a `usage` object only when it arrives on a chunk that has at least one choice (defect 1). | The client reads five counts: `prompt_tokens`, `completion_tokens`, `total_tokens`, `cached_tokens`, and `reasoning_tokens`. | The Engine's `Usage` type has the same five counts. (`types/src/metrics.rs`) | The Engine reports usage to the Host with each reply. Lua is not shown it. |
| **Finish reasons.** The field in a reply that says why the model stopped. | The Gateway forwards the finish reason unchanged. | The client keeps the finish reason as the text it received. It compares that text with known values in one place only, where it checks for `length` and `content_filter`. | The Engine compares the finish reason with two values, `stop` and `length`. It uses `stop` to let a tool loop end normally after an empty reply, and `length` to report to the Host that a text reply was cut off. | The prompt's Lua code can read the finish reason in one place: as the `finish_reason` field of an `empty_model_reply` error that it has caught. |
| **Errors.** What each layer does when the upstream refuses or fails a request. | When a remote upstream answers with an error, the Gateway replaces the body of that answer with the fixed message `upstream returned {status}` (defect 3). A 4xx status keeps its number, and every 5xx status is reported as 502. The Gateway sets the `Retry-After` header for one error only, its own `model_loading` (a local model is still starting), and does not forward an upstream's. (`protocol/src/error.rs`) | The client turns a failure into an error with an error code, such as `ContextOverflow` or `RateLimited`, which it chooses from the HTTP status and from phrases that it looks for in the error body. It makes one HTTP attempt per round, so a failed attempt is a failed round. (`wire/classify.rs`) | The Engine handles two error codes itself. On `ContextOverflow` it calls the prompt's compactor, the Lua function that shortens a conversation that no longer fits the model's context window, and sends the round again once. On `EmptyReply`, a reply with no text and no tool calls, the tool loop ends normally if the reply finished with `stop` and the loop has answered at least one tool call. Otherwise it raises an error of kind `empty_model_reply`. Every other error code ends the model call with that error. | The prompt's Lua code can catch the error of a failed model call. It has the fields `kind`, `code`, and `message`, and an `empty_model_reply` error also has `finish_reason`. `kind` is a group such as `model_failure` or `empty_model_reply`, `code` names the error code, such as `rate_limited`, and `message` is that code's fixed phrase. |
| **Model metadata.** What the Gateway tells callers about each model. | The Gateway's model list, `GET /v1/models`, publishes for each model what its model entry (its `[[model]]` entry in the Gateway configuration file `gateway.toml`) says: `id`, `kind`, `description`, `context`, the thinking mode `thinking`, and nine settings, `provider` among them (see the note). When the Gateway handles a request it reads four things from the model entry: its kind, such as chat or embedding, which the chat route requires to be chat, its first endpoint, its tool dialect, and its name at the upstream. It does not use `thinking` or the nine settings. (`app/src/models.rs`) | From each entry of the model list the client reads five fields: `id`, `description`, `context`, `thinking`, and `provider`. | At a run's first step, before any Lua runs, the Engine checks the model that fills each model role against the requirements that the prompt declared for the role (see the note). | A prompt declares two requirements for a model role in its front matter: `min_context` and `thinking`. |
| **Where a translator would plug in.** A translator is the Gateway code that converts a request into one vendor's own protocol and converts the vendor's reply back. | The `Upstream` trait is the Gateway's interface to an upstream. Its chat methods, `Upstream::send` and `stream`, receive the request and the model's name at the upstream. The `Protocol` type has one value, and one place in the code, `Routing::from_config`, selects the code for it. The tool dialect `gemma3_tool_code` is three pure functions that run before and after the upstream call. (`protocol/src/upstream.rs`, `config/src/config/vocab.rs`, `app/src/routing.rs`, `app/src/dialect.rs`) | Among the client's public functions, two let other code reuse its request and reply handling: `build_request_body`, which builds the request body, and `read_completion_stream`, which reads a streamed reply. | Each model request leaves the Engine through one method, `InferenceBroker::chat` (see the note). | Not applicable. This is about interfaces inside Runvil's code. |

Source: evidence items L1 to L121, which give the file and lines behind every cell.

**Notes on Table 16, by row label.**

- **Tools.** A tool dialect is the Gateway's rewrite for a model that writes tool calls in its own text format. Today there is one, `gemma3_tool_code`, which a model entry names or, for a local model, the Gateway detects. For such a model the Gateway removes `tools` and `tool_choice` from the request and adds a system message that describes the tools at the front of `messages`. In the reply it converts the tool calls that the model wrote as text into ordinary tool calls. When it cannot parse one, it empties the reply's content and adds a `gateway_warning` field, which the client drops [L53, L110]. For a caller that asked for a stream, it gets the whole reply from the upstream first and sends it on as chunks of its own.
- **Model metadata.** A prompt never names a model. It names one of five model roles (`default`, `strong`, `small`, `fast`, `creative`), and the Host decides which model fills each role. Besides the thinking switch, two things are named `thinking`. The model list's `thinking` is the model's thinking mode: `never`, `always`, or `switchable`, which means that a request can turn reasoning on or off. The front matter's `thinking` is the thinking requirement of a role, true or false: `true` fails on a `never` model, and `false` fails on an `always` model. When the requirement passes, the Engine sends its true or false as the switch of every request on that role, and Lua reads it as `handle.thinking`. A role without the requirement sends no switch. The other requirement, `min_context`, is a token count that the model's context window must reach. A failed requirement ends the run with `requirements_unmet`. The nine settings are `max_output` (the largest number of output tokens in one reply), `default_temperature`, `images` (image input), `parallel_tool_calls`, `effort_levels` (the reasoning-effort values the model accepts), `default_effort` (the default among them), `adaptive_thinking` (the model decides for itself how much to think), `voices` (for a speech model), and `provider` (the model's provider id).
- **Where a translator would plug in.** The Gateway's request handler holds the model entry and passes the chat methods only the request and the model's upstream name. So a translator written as an `Upstream` cannot read settings such as `max_output` and `effort_levels` as the trait is today. `Protocol` names the protocol of an endpoint, an `[[endpoint]]` entry in the Gateway configuration: one backend with its base URL and API key. Its one value, `Openai`, means chat completions. A local model gets a second implementation of `Upstream` from the code that starts it, not from an endpoint entry. `InferenceBroker` is the interface through which the Engine hands a model request to the Host, and the standard implementation sends it with the gateway client.

**The code has six defects: in each, one piece of Runvil's code drops or spoils something that another piece asks for or checks.** In four the Gateway drops something an upstream sent: token counts, an error, the text of an error, and keep-alive messages. In one the Gateway makes up tool-call ids that repeat, which the Engine refuses, and in one the model sheet says that OpenRouter's models cannot reason. OpenRouter is a commercial gateway that puts one API in front of many vendors' models, and the model sheet lists it as a cloud provider. The other losses in Table 16 are of things that no piece of Runvil asks for or checks today: the text beside tool calls, the upstream's `Retry-After` header (nothing retries a round), and the `gateway_warning` field. Section 3 takes them up feature by feature. Confidence in each defect: medium - read from the code, not yet run.

1. **When an upstream reports usage the way OpenAI does, the Gateway drops it, so the client and the Engine get no token counts for that reply.** The client asks for usage by sending `stream_options.include_usage` in every request. OpenAI answers with one extra chunk at the end of the stream, which holds the usage and has an empty `choices` list. The Gateway's chunk parser treats such a chunk as malformed and skips it (`protocol/src/wire.rs`, `protocol/src/upstream.rs`). Usage on a chunk with a choice gets through. Every upstream's stream goes through this parser, a local model's too, and which of the two forms the other upstreams use was not checked [L57, L58, L63, L67, L68].
2. **A reply that failed partway through its stream can arrive at the client looking like a reply that ended normally.** An upstream can report a failure in the middle of a stream by sending a line that holds only an error object, `{"error": ...}`. The Gateway skips that line, because it has no `model` field and no choice. When the upstream then closes the stream, the Gateway ends it with a normal `[DONE]`. The client takes any text that had arrived as the whole reply, or reports an empty reply if none had. The upstream's error never reaches the client [L85].
3. **When a remote upstream rejects a request as too long for the model, the Gateway replaces the upstream's error text with a fixed phrase, so the Engine is not told that the request was too long and does not call the compactor in response.** The model call fails instead. The client recognizes the case by phrases such as "prompt is too long" in the error text. Through the Gateway it gets only the fixed message, so it reports the error code `Rejected` where it should report `ContextOverflow`. The upstream's own words do reach the client when the error arrives inside a stream, on a chunk that also has a `model` field and a choice. A local model's implementation of `Upstream` uses the same stream parser, and the evidence items do not show what it does with an error text [L63, L114]. The precheck, the Engine's own estimate before each round of a tool loop of whether the request fits the model's context window, still calls the compactor. But it leaves out tool definitions and images, and it starts from the upstream's token counts only when the last reply's usage arrived, which defect 1 can prevent. So a request can pass the precheck and still be rejected as too long. Two other failures get the wrong code in the same way, and with it the wrong fixed message: spent quota is reported as `RateLimited` where it should be `QuotaExhausted`, and a request refused for its content as `Rejected` where it should be `Refused`. The 2026-11-17 report found the too-long case for Anthropic, and it holds for every remote upstream [L58, L69, L74 to L81, L82, L84, L85, L105].
4. **When a stream goes silent, the Gateway waits without any deadline and the client gives up after 120 seconds, so the client can abandon a reply that the Gateway is still waiting for.** The Gateway drops the keep-alive comments that an upstream sends and sends the client nothing in their place. The client, which receives nothing during the silence, fails the round [L54, L60, L61].
5. **A conversation can fail in a later round of tool calls, because the Engine requires every tool-call id in the whole conversation to be different, while providers and the Gateway itself reuse ids.** The projection refuses a message list in which two tool calls have the same id, anywhere in the conversation and not only within one reply. The Engine stores each id exactly as the reply gave it. Yet a comment in the Engine's own metrics type says that providers reuse ids such as `call_1` from one round to the next. The Gateway reuses ids too: for a model that uses the tool dialect `gemma3_tool_code` it makes up the tool-call ids, and it starts counting at 0 again on every reply. So the ids of a second reply repeat the ids of the first, and the projection refuses to build the next request [L46].
6. **Every OpenRouter model in the model sheet is recorded as unable to call tools and unable to reason, so a model entry made from the sheet says that the model never reasons, and a prompt that requires thinking is refused on it.** The program that builds the model sheet reads ten values from each entry of OpenRouter's model catalog, its public list of models and their properties. It leaves unread the two fields that say what a model can do: `supported_parameters`, which lists the request settings a model accepts and includes `tools` for a model that can call tools, and the `reasoning` object, which describes the model's reasoning settings. So every OpenRouter model keeps the program's starting values, `tool_calling: false` and `thinking.supported: false`. An operator adds a model from the model sheet on the Cloud page of the Gateway's browser UI for editing `gateway.toml`, and the page fills in the new model entry from the sheet. For an OpenRouter model that entry gets `thinking = "never"`, which the model list publishes. A prompt whose front matter requires `thinking: true` then fails the Engine's requirement check on that model, even when the model can reason. The `tool_calling` value stays in the sheet, so it changes nothing in a run today [L10, L93, L94, M58, M59].

**The gateway client sends one field that only the local servers llama.cpp and vLLM understand: the thinking switch.** That field, `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, is an argument for a chat template, the text template shipped with an open-weight model that turns a message list into the model's own input format. The client, which every vendor's requests pass through, thus contains code that only those servers need, which section 1 calls a vendor branch in shared code. Today the Gateway forwards the field as the client wrote it, and a provider that does not know the field ignores it, so the switch has no effect there [L32]. The branch exists today, and the proposal in section 8 removes it, which is why no feature is counted under that option: the client would send one vendor-neutral on/off field, whose name is still to be chosen, and the Gateway would convert it into each vendor's own form. In three other places the client reads, and does not write, what particular servers send: three names for the reasoning field, phrases in error text, and the two timing sections [L25, L65, L79]. The Gateway's decision page (`runvil-design`, `decisions/gateway.md`, dated 2026-11-10) plans a narrower step, under the title "The thinking switch for remote providers": a setting on each model with which the Gateway translates `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking` for a provider that ignores the field. Under that plan the client keeps sending today's field.

## 8. What Runvil should change: store more in the opaque field, build the OpenAI Responses translator early, fix four Gateway defects first, and give the Gateway a table of what each model accepts

Runvil's current plan has three parts. Only the first exists in code today.

- The gateway client, Runvil's HTTP library for model requests, keeps sending requests in OpenAI's chat completions format.
- The Gateway, Runvil's server that forwards each request to a vendor or a local model, would get one translator for each vendor's own protocol: code that converts a request into that vendor's format and the reply back. Every translator would be reached through the Gateway's one existing route for model requests, `POST /v1/chat/completions`.
- One optional extra field on an assistant message, the opaque field, would hold the data a vendor sends and expects to get back unchanged, such as Anthropic's signed thinking blocks. The design, made for Anthropic in Runvil's report of 2026-11-17, stores the reply's whole list of vendor blocks (text, reasoning, and tool calls) in the vendor's order, with a label that records which protocol and model produced them. The client and the Engine, which runs the prompt and holds the conversation, would store the field and send it back in later requests without reading it.

Seven changes to that plan follow. "(row 5)" points to a feature's entry in the tables of section 3.

**1. The opaque field should record which endpoint produced its contents, be used on the plain `openai` protocol too, hold other data that has to come back besides one vendor's blocks, and be sent once at the end of a reply.** The field itself is the right mechanism. Confidence: high - Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google each want their blocks back unchanged and in order (row 3), which a stored copy of the vendor's own list gives them, and each format examined that stores less loses something a vendor checks, such as where each piece of reasoning was among the text and the tool calls. How well the four additions below would work is less sure. Confidence: medium - none is built, and the only evidence that the field can hold each kind of data is that the libraries rig and Vercel AI SDK and the gateway LiteLLM store it that way.

- **The label should name the endpoint that produced the data, not only the protocol and the model.** Here an endpoint is an endpoint entry in the Gateway's configuration: one base URL with its API key, so one account at one provider, a company that serves a model. The protocol and the model do not tell the Gateway whether a vendor will accept its data back. Anthropic drops, without an error, thinking blocks from its newest Claude models in any account but the one that produced them, which on its own API is the organization. Google's Vertex AI scopes a thought signature, Google's signature on a block of a Gemini reply, to one Google Cloud project. OpenAI drops, without an error, reasoning produced under another organization. OpenRouter, the commercial gateway in front of many vendors' models, appears to record the same: the encrypted values (strings only the vendor can read) in its examples and in recorded replies end with an undocumented, decodable suffix that names the model version and the provider (row 5). With the endpoint in the label, the Gateway would send the field's contents back only to that endpoint. For any other request it would leave the field out, text included, and rebuild the message from the vendor-neutral fields the client sends, `content` and `tool_calls`. Today the client drops the text that arrives in the same reply as tool calls, so that text would be lost unless the client keeps it and the Engine stores it (rows 3, 5, and 19).
- **The Gateway should use the opaque field on the plain `openai` protocol too.** `openai` is the one protocol an endpoint entry can have today: the Gateway forwards the chat completions request to it unchanged. DeepSeek, Moonshot's Kimi, Alibaba's Qwen, Zhipu's GLM, and MiniMax are reached this way. They return the model's reasoning as plain text in `reasoning_content` and expect it back in later requests, as do the chat templates of the open-weight models gpt-oss, Kimi, Qwen3, and GLM on a local server. Runvil's client reads the reasoning and never sends it back, and DeepSeek answers a request with tools that lacks it with HTTP 400. So a tool loop fails on DeepSeek today. The Gateway would store the reasoning in the opaque field and write it back into the vendor's or server's own field in later requests (rows 14 and 17). When it has no reasoning to write back for an assistant message, it would send `reasoning_content` holding a single space, which satisfies DeepSeek's check (row 14).
- **The same ordered list should also hold other data that has to come back.** A reply in OpenAI's Responses API, its newer format, can hold several assistant messages, each with a `phase` label that the caller sends back (row 8). The Gateway could keep data of its own in the list and so store nothing about a conversation between requests: for example which tool definitions it has given the model, if Runvil adopts tool search, in which a definition is sent only after the model searches for the tool (rows 20 and 52). For features Runvil does not use today, it can hold the blocks that record a tool the vendor ran on its own servers (row 62), the summary block a vendor returns when it compacts a conversation itself (row 77), and the container id of vendor-run code execution (row 65).
- **The Gateway should assemble the field's contents and send them once, complete, on the final chunk of a streamed reply.** The client would then copy one value. OpenRouter does the opposite: it streams `reasoning_details`, its field for such data, in fragments and leaves the joining to the caller, which puts code written for OpenRouter into the caller (row 18).

**2. The Gateway needs a translator for OpenAI's Responses API early, because OpenAI's newest models cannot run a tool loop with reasoning through chat completions, and five of them cannot call tools through it at all.** OpenAI's chat completions API limits tool calling on two groups of models (row 7). On GPT-5.4 to 5.6, GPT-6 Sol, and GPT-6 Luna it supports tool calling only with reasoning off. On five others (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6.1 Terra, GPT-6.2 Astra, and GPT-6.2 Sol) it does not support tool calling at all. What OpenAI answers to a request that breaks the limit is not documented and was not tested. Today the Gateway forwards chat completions requests to OpenAI unchanged, so a tool loop on these models has to wait for the translator. Runvil's client sends no reasoning effort, so each model uses its default: `none` on GPT-5.4, where tool calls therefore still work, and `medium` from GPT-5.5 on, where today's requests break the limit. That is an inference from OpenAI's documented defaults. A stopgap for the first group is to send `reasoning_effort: "none"`, which turns reasoning off. A translator of the same design also serves xAI and Meta: their chat completions APIs do not return encrypted reasoning or take it back, and their APIs in the Responses format do (row 6). Confidence: high - the limit is OpenAI's own documented rule, and its pages were fetched twice.

**3. Google Gemini needs a translator of the same design, which also puts a placeholder signature on tool calls from other models and can continue a reply that Gemini stopped at its output limit.** The Gateway would store the reply's list of parts (Google's name for blocks) in the opaque field and send it back. Tool calls that another vendor's model made earlier in the conversation have no thought signature, so the Gateway would put Google's documented placeholder, `skip_thought_signature_validator`, on the first tool call of each model reply (row 12). A reply longer than Gemini 4's output limit for one request stops with a continuation token, and the Gateway would send follow-up requests with that token until the reply is complete (row 13). Only a request that asks for more output than that limit needs this, so it can wait (decision 11). Confidence: medium - five open-source projects insert the placeholder the same way, and the long-reply feature is new.

**4. Today's Gateway loses a remote model's token counts, its error text, and errors that arrive in mid-stream, and it waits on a silent stream without limit. These four defects should be fixed before any vendor translator is built.** They affect every model the Gateway reaches over the network (section 7).

- An upstream, the server the Gateway forwards a request to, that follows OpenAI's format sends the token usage in a last chunk that holds nothing else. The Gateway skips that chunk, so the usage numbers are lost.
- The Gateway replaces an upstream's error text with the fixed phrase `upstream returned {status}`, so the client cannot tell what went wrong. When the upstream rejected the request because the prompt is too long, the Engine is not told that the prompt was too long and does not call the compactor, which shortens a conversation, in response. The Engine's precheck can still start compaction.
- The Gateway skips an error that arrives in the middle of a streamed reply and ends the stream normally, so a failed reply can look complete.
- The Gateway drops an upstream's keep-alive messages and has no deadline for a silent stream. So in a long silence the client, which gives up after 120 seconds without data, can abandon a reply that is still coming, while the Gateway keeps waiting.

Confidence: medium - all four were read from the code and none was reproduced by running it.

**5. The Gateway needs a table of what each model accepts, and it has to consult that table on every request.** With it the Gateway can remove a temperature the model would reject (row 28), translate the thinking switch into the form the model accepts (rows 22 and 24), relax a forced tool choice to `auto` on a model that rejects being forced (row 44), rewrite tool-call ids into the format the model wants (row 46), clean a tool's schema (row 48), and fill in `max_tokens` where it is required (row 29). The model sheet, a file Runvil publishes every week with each cloud provider's models and their settings, holds `thinking`, `effort_levels`, and `max_output`. The Gateway fills its model entries from the sheet and publishes those values in its model list, but ignores them when it handles a request. For more, the program that builds the sheet can read OpenRouter's public model catalog (row 94), which gives for each model `reasoning.mandatory`, `supported_efforts`, and `supported_parameters`. OpenRouter's list of each model's endpoints adds `supports_tool_choice`: which `tool_choice` values passed its tests. It publishes nothing on tool-call id formats or tool schemas, so those rules have to come from each vendor's documentation and code. Confidence: high - the vendors document these limits model by model, so the Gateway cannot apply one without per-model data.

**6. Runvil should start using a few vendor-neutral fields: a reasoning-effort setting and a count of the tokens written to the prompt cache, both under names that OpenAI's chat completions format already has, and a field for the thinking switch.** A vendor-neutral field means the same thing for every vendor and names none. The first two are part of chat completions, so the universal API, the format planned between the client and the Gateway, already has them, and only their use by the client and the Gateway is new.

- The reasoning-effort setting, `reasoning_effort` (row 21).
- The count of the tokens written to the vendor's prompt cache, as `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens` in a reply's token usage (row 38).
- A standard name for the thinking switch (row 22, and section 7). Today the client sends the `thinking` value that a prompt requires in its front matter as `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking`, which only local servers understand, and no switch when the prompt requires none. The client should send it under a standard name, which the Gateway would translate.
- Later, each only when Runvil wants the feature behind it: `refusal` (the model declined to answer), a way to ask for a reply that matches a schema, `annotations` (citations on a reply), a conversation id, a flag on a tool that holds its definition back for tool search, and file and audio parts in a message.

Confidence: high - the first two are fields of OpenAI's own chat completions format. Confidence: medium - the thinking field has no name yet, and each later field waits on a decision to support its feature.

**7. Runvil needs one rule for a request that asks for something the model cannot do. The suggested rule: the Engine refuses the run when the model fails a requirement the prompt declared, and in every other case the Gateway drops or replaces the setting and reports it.** OpenRouter has no single rule: sometimes it ignores the setting silently, sometimes it hands the provider's HTTP 400 error to the caller, and sometimes it rejects the request itself, with a 400 in most of the cases it documents. Its callers ended up writing per-model rules into their own client code. Runvil has two better tools. One works today: the Engine refuses the run at its first step when the model fails what the prompt's front matter requires of it (`min_context` and `thinking`). So `thinking: false` ends the run on a model whose mode in the Gateway's model list is `always`: the rule's first half. For thinking off, Claude Sonnet 5.5 rejects `disabled` but accepts `between_tools`, in which the model does not think before it replies and writes only short notes between tool calls. Its mode has to be `switchable`, so `thinking: false` passes the check and the rule's second half applies: the Gateway would send `between_tools` and report the replacement (rows 22 and 24). The other is `gateway_warning`, a field the Gateway adds to a reply today in one case, a tool call it could not parse. Under the rule it would report there every setting it drops or replaces, such as a temperature dropped on Claude 4.7 and later (row 28) or a forced tool choice relaxed to `auto` (row 44). The client drops that field today (section 7), so it would have to pass the warning on, and how the warning would reach a prompt author is not designed. Confidence: medium - the right answer depends on how much a prompt author should be told.

### What should stay as planned: one route for model requests, Anthropic first, and no vendor names in prompts

- **The Gateway should keep one route for model requests.** Beside chat completions, OpenRouter has a Messages route in Anthropic's format and a Responses route in the format of OpenAI's Responses API. Ten vendor features work only there, so its callers have to pick a route according to the vendor. In Runvil all ten would be handled on the one route. Three are request settings of Anthropic's that the Gateway would send itself: the `between_tools` mode (row 24), `drop_block`, with which Anthropic drops a thinking block that is no longer valid because something before it in the request changed, where it would otherwise reject the request (row 4), and context editing by the vendor (row 78). Two return blocks that the opaque field would hold: `phase` labels (row 8) and a vendor's summary block (row 77). The other five are document citations (row 64), mid-conversation tool changes (row 41), async tools (row 53), free-text tools (row 51), and a shell tool (row 65), and each would need code in the Gateway and, for some, a new vendor-neutral field. Runvil's planned work needs only `between_tools`, `drop_block`, and `phase` labels. Confidence: medium - the opaque field is not built, and the evidence that it can hold these blocks is that LiteLLM stores both kinds, and the Vercel AI SDK `phase` labels.
- **The Anthropic translator should still be built first.** Anthropic's rules for sending data back are the strictest that any vendor documents. It rejects a request in which a thinking block was edited or moved, and on its newest models one in which anything before the block changed (rows 1 and 4). OpenAI drops, without an error, reasoning it cannot use, and Google checks only the signatures of the current turn (rows 9 and 10). So a design that satisfies Anthropic is the design OpenAI and Google need too. Confidence: medium - OpenAI's limit blocks tool loops on its newest models today (change 2), which argues for OpenAI first. It comes second (decision 4) so that the opaque field is first built against the strictest rules.
- **Prompts should stay free of vendor names.** None of the 105 features requires a prompt to know which vendor runs it. Three kinds come closest (section 2). A vendor's own settings, such as Google's safety thresholds (row 72), would be written into the Gateway's configuration by the operator, the person who configures it. A tool that one vendor alone offers, such as xAI's X search (row 73), would be offered under a vendor-neutral id. A setting that some models refuse, such as a temperature (row 28), would be dropped and reported. Confidence: high - a prompt names only a model role, so everything written for one vendor can be in the Gateway's code or configuration.

### What the project owner needs to decide, starting with what the opaque field records and where it is used

1. **Whether the label on the opaque field should name the endpoint entry that produced its contents, in addition to the protocol and the model** (change 1). Without it, the Gateway can send data to an account that drops it or, for Gemini, rejects the request. Recommended: yes. Confidence: medium - Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI each accept such data only in the account that produced it, but each defines the account differently, so a match on the endpoint entry only approximates what the vendor checks.
2. **Whether the Gateway should use the opaque field on the plain `openai` protocol** (change 1), to store the plain-text reasoning that DeepSeek and similar vendors want sent back. Recommended: yes. Confidence: high - DeepSeek documents the rule, and by that rule a tool loop on DeepSeek fails today, so the reasoning has to be sent back. How well the opaque field does that is untried.
3. **What the opaque field is called: a name of Runvil's own, or OpenRouter's name, `reasoning_details`.** Recommended: a name of Runvil's own, because `reasoning_details` has no place for a vendor's text and tool-call blocks or for the order of a reply's blocks (section 4). When a reply came from OpenRouter, the client and the Engine would hold its `reasoning_details` array as part of the stored assistant message. The Gateway should pass it back unchanged to OpenRouter only and never rebuild a vendor's blocks from it. Confidence: medium - `reasoning_details` is the most widely copied name for structured reasoning data, yet it cannot record that order.
4. **Which translator to build second, after Anthropic's: one for OpenAI's Responses API or one for Google's Gemini API.** Recommended: OpenAI's Responses API, because tool calling with reasoning on OpenAI's models from GPT-5.4 on works only through it (change 2). Confidence: medium - it depends on which vendor's models the Runvil class wants to run first.
5. **Whether the standalone client has to send reasoning data back to OpenRouter when it talks to OpenRouter directly, without Runvil's Gateway in between.** The standalone client is a planned version of the gateway client that can talk to other servers (item 3 of the 2026-11-17 lesson plan). If yes, the client needs code that joins the fragments in which OpenRouter streams `reasoning_details` (row 18). That code, specific to OpenRouter, would be a vendor branch in the code that every vendor's requests pass through. If no, one vendor-neutral rule is enough: the client keeps every non-standard field of the final assistant message and of each tool call without reading it, records which server and model it came from, and sends it back only to the same server and model. That rule covers DeepSeek's `reasoning_content`, Google's `extra_content`, and Runvil's own opaque field, and not `reasoning_details`, so a model reached through OpenRouter would lose its signed or encrypted reasoning. `reasoning_content` arrives as pieces of text that the client joins by appending. How the client would know, without reading a field, that appending is right is not designed. Recommended: the vendor-neutral rule only. Confidence: low - the rule is an inference from how those fields work, and nobody has run it.
6. **Whether to adopt the rule in change 7 for a request that asks for something the model cannot do.** Recommended: yes: the Engine refuses the run where a declared requirement fails, and otherwise the Gateway drops or replaces the setting and reports it in `gateway_warning`. Confidence: medium - it depends on how much a prompt author should be told.
7. **Which vendor-neutral fields to start using now.** Recommended: two of the three in change 6, `reasoning_effort` and `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens`. The field for the thinking switch waits until its name is decided. Confidence: high - both are OpenAI's own field names.
8. **Whether a prompt should still be able to read which provider serves its model.** Today the model handle, the Lua object a prompt gets for a model, has a read-only `provider` field, so a prompt can behave differently for each provider. The decision is whether that field stays. This report makes no recommendation: no feature needs the field, and what existing prompts use it for was not examined. Confidence: medium - this field is the one way a prompt can depend on its provider today.
9. **Whether to keep the 26 evidence files.** They hold every quote and source behind this report, and they are in a temporary research folder whose files are deleted after 30 days. Recommended: move them to a permanent place beside this report. Confidence: high - without them, the quote, source, and read date behind a statement here cannot be looked up.
10. **Whether the Gateway should also accept requests in Anthropic's Messages format, on a second route `/v1/messages`.** Runvil's own documents disagree (section 10). The Gateway's decision page (`runvil-design`, `decisions/gateway.md`) rules it out. A research report of 2026-11-09 recommends it because the local server llama.cpp already serves that route. A student report of 2026-11-17 (not the report that designed the opaque field) recommends it and describes what the harness Claude Code, which sends Anthropic's format, expects of a gateway. Whether Runvil wants such callers was not examined. Recommended: keep the decision page's rule of one route. Confidence: medium - Runvil's own client, which sends chat completions, needs only the one route if the opaque field can hold what OpenRouter's other routes offer, which only LiteLLM's and the Vercel AI SDK's code shows.
11. **Who continues a reply that the vendor stopped partway: the Gateway or the Engine.** Gemini 4 stops a reply at its output limit for one request (row 13), and Anthropic's Claude pauses a long run of vendor-run tools (row 63). Each expects a follow-up request to continue the reply. The Gateway can send it, so that the client sees one complete reply, or hand the stopped reply to the Engine, which would then need a new step in its tool loop. Recommended: the Gateway, and only once Runvil adopts these features. Confidence: medium - four libraries continue Gemini's long replies this way and one continues Claude's paused turns, but a long request during which the client receives nothing is untested.

## 9. OpenRouter's published material says how it handles 85 of the 105 features, 7 are inferred, and other projects or vendor documentation cover the other 13

OpenRouter, a commercial gateway (a server that translates a caller's requests for many vendors), is the first reference consulted for each feature. Its translator, the code that converts a request into a vendor's protocol and the reply back, is closed source. What can be read is its published material: its documentation, its API specification, its model catalog, its changelog, and the source code of its official client libraries. Where it is silent, a vendor's documentation or an open-source project fills in, and what another project does is never presented as OpenRouter's behavior.

The projects named are gateways, model libraries (which give a program one message type for several vendors), harnesses (programs such as coding agents that run a model with tools), and local servers (programs that run a model on the operator's own machine). "(row 14)" after a statement points to that feature in section 3, and a code in square brackets such as [O58] is an evidence item (section 12). The opaque field is planned and not built: an extra field on an assistant message in which the Gateway, Runvil's server for model requests, would store data that a vendor expects back unchanged, under a label that says what produced the data.

*Table 17. The main source for each feature.*

How to read Table 17: the first column is the kind of source, under the five names that the notes in section 3 use. The second column counts the features whose main source is of that kind. The main source is the one the feature's note names first. The first row names the subjects of its 85 features, and the other rows name every feature.

| Kind of source | Features | Which features |
|---|---|---|
| **Stated by OpenRouter.** It is in OpenRouter's published material. | 85 | Every feature that the other rows do not name. By subject, the material states: the names and formats of the fields on its three routes (chat completions, Messages in Anthropic's format, and Responses in the format of OpenAI's newer API); its rule that reasoning data goes back to the vendor unchanged (row 1); its reasoning settings, such as one effort setting with seven values (row 21); its cache markers and how it converts a marker from one vendor's form to another's (row 35); the token counts it reports, such as tokens read from and written to a cache (row 38); its list of tools that run on a server, such as web search (row 61); its 27 error types (row 86); its settings for choosing which provider serves a request (row 95); the per-model flags in its model catalog, such as the request settings a model accepts (row 94); and at least 23 cases in which it rejects a request (section 5). |
| **Inferred about OpenRouter.** OpenRouter does not state it. It is worked out from the source named. | 7 | A chat completions reply does not record the order of its blocks: the API specification gives it separate fields for reasoning, text, and tool calls and none for their order (row 3). In replies, tool-call ids reach the caller as the vendor wrote them, as recorded traffic shows (row 46).
