OpenRouter does not seem to supply the reasoning text that DeepSeek requires back when a caller drops it: issue reports show DeepSeek's HTTP 400 reaching callers through OpenRouter, and no recorded request tests it (row 14). OpenRouter moves the text of a system message in the middle of a conversation to the top-level `system` field of a request to Claude. This is read from one sentence of its documentation, about a field on a system message with which a caller changes the reasoning effort partway through a conversation: on Claude that field "stays inline in `messages` while ordinary system text is still sent as the top-level `system` prompt" (row 59). The chat completions route leaves out Google's safety settings: the API specification has no field for them (row 72). OpenRouter has no route that counts the tokens of a request: the specification lists none (row 79). The finish reason that OpenRouter reports for a Gemini reply that ends in tool calls cannot be relied on, so a caller has to look for the tool calls themselves (row 85). A comment in its client library's code says that such a reply arrives with `stop` when the tool call has a thought signature, and the code changes that to the tool-call value, while a recorded reply with such a tool call, in an issue of the gptel project dated 2025-12-18, has `tool_calls` [O58, T17]. |
| **Taken from another open-source project.** OpenRouter's material is silent, or the feature has nothing to do with OpenRouter. The note says how a project handles the feature. | 6 | The gateway LiteLLM and four other projects insert the placeholder value that Google accepts in place of a thought signature on tool calls that did not come from Gemini (row 12). After a switch to another model or provider, the harnesses pi and Goose drop the earlier model's reasoning, and the model library rig and the harness opencode strip the vendor's data from it and pass its readable text on. The Gateway would do as pi and Goose do: where the label on the opaque field does not match the request's target, it would rebuild the message from its text and tool calls (row 19) [R83, H51, H61, H65, H69]. The gateway Bifrost imitates structured output for a model with no built-in way to return JSON that matches a schema, by forcing a call to a tool whose arguments have that schema (row 56). The other three concern local servers. llama.cpp's `/props` route reports what the loaded model supports (row 103). llama.cpp changes or ignores some requests with tools without an error, for example a forced call of one named tool (row 104). llama.cpp and vLLM both serve a route in Anthropic's Messages format and one in OpenAI's Responses format, and llama.cpp returns reasoning on its Messages route with an empty signature (row 105). llama.cpp was read in its source code, vLLM in its documentation. |
| **Stated by the vendor.** OpenRouter's material is silent, and the vendor's own documentation, read on 2026-11-22, gives the rule. | 7 | OpenAI's rule that from GPT-5.4 on, tool calls together with reasoning work only through its Responses API (row 7). The encrypted reasoning that Alibaba's Qwen returns beside a summary of it (row 16). Mistral's reasoning, which arrives as a list of chunks inside the reply's content (row 15). Anthropic's cache diagnostics (row 40). Anthropic's Skills, and OpenAI's feature in which the model writes a program that calls the caller's tools (row 82). A reply that Anthropic pauses (row 63). APIs that look like OpenAI's chat completions format and behave differently: four vendors' own, and OpenRouter's when the Gateway forwards to it (row 101). |

Ten are in feature notes and two are in no note. Section 10 lists them. |
| Total | 105 | |

Source: counted from the notes under Tables 5 to 14.

A feature counted under OpenRouter's material can also have an inferred part: the signatures and encrypted values that OpenRouter returns end in a base64 suffix that, decoded, names the model version and the provider that produced the value. OpenRouter does not document the suffix (row 5).

**For 19 more features, an open-source project is the evidence that what this report proposes for Runvil can be built.** Their main source, for 14 of them OpenRouter's material, says what the field or the rule is. A project's code shows one way to build the handling, and none of it was run. With the 6 taken from another project, that is 25 features.

The Gateway would keep a vendor's data in the opaque field and send it back:

- **Anthropic's signed thinking blocks (row 1).** The reply's whole list of blocks would go back unchanged in later requests. rig and two harnesses store and return the list that way.
- **The order of reasoning, text, and tool calls in a reply (row 3).** The list would go back in the vendor's order, never rebuilt. rig keeps each of the vendor's blocks whole, beside its own vendor-neutral version of it.
- **Thinking blocks that are valid only while the conversation before them is unchanged (row 4).** The Gateway would also send Anthropic's `drop_block` setting, with which Anthropic drops a block that is no longer valid and still answers. rig and four harnesses send it.
- **Reasoning data that works only in the customer account that produced it (row 5).** The label would name the protocol, the model, and the endpoint (one backend in the Gateway's configuration), and the data would go back only on a match. rig does the same with a narrower label: protocol, provider, and model. The model library Pydantic AI returns the id of data stored at a vendor only to a server that shares the data, and Bifrost removes the reasoning and retries once when a vendor refuses it.
- **OpenAI's encrypted reasoning items, which exist only in its Responses API (row 6).** A translator for that API would keep the reply's list of items in the field. LiteLLM, Bifrost, and a third gateway answer a chat completions caller through the Responses API, and rig and the model library Vercel AI SDK keep the items.
- **OpenAI's `phase` labels on the messages of a Responses reply (row 8).** The list of items in the field would keep each label. LiteLLM stores the order of the items with each one's label, and the Vercel AI SDK stores the label on each text part.
- **Google's thought signatures on tool calls (row 10).** A Gemini translator would keep the reply's parts in the field, each with its signature. The Vercel AI SDK and opencode store each signature with its part.
- **Plain-text reasoning that DeepSeek and four other vendors require back (row 14).** The Gateway would keep it in the field and write it into the vendor's `reasoning_content` field in later requests, or a single space when it has none to write. pi and opencode send the reasoning back, and LiteLLM sends the single space.
- **Blocks from vendor-run tools (row 62).** If adopted, they would go into the field and back. rig and opencode return such blocks as they received them.

The Gateway would send a follow-up request itself:

- **An answer from Gemini 4 that is longer than one request's output limit (row 13).** The Gateway would send the follow-up requests and return one reply, as LiteLLM, the Vercel AI SDK, and Google's own client libraries do.
- **A reply that Anthropic pauses (row 63).** Anthropic pauses only when a request offers a tool that Anthropic runs. If adopted, the Gateway would send the unfinished reply back itself, as Pydantic AI does up to 10 times.
- **A vendor's web search (row 61).** If adopted, the model would still call Runvil's own search tool, and the vendor's search would supply the results in one of two ways. The tool could make a second model request with the vendor's search switched on, as the search tool of Gemini CLI, Google's own harness, does. Or the Gateway could answer each search call itself and return one finished reply, as LiteLLM and Bifrost answer tool calls inside the gateway. How it would get the vendor's results that way is not designed.

The Gateway would add to a request or rewrite part of it:

- **Cache markers (row 31).** The Gateway would place them, and the client would send none. The gateway agentgateway adds markers when its caller sent none.
- **A key that keeps a conversation on the machine that holds its cache (row 36).** The Gateway would compute one from the conversation's opening messages. pi sends its session id as such a key.
- **Google's cache objects, which a caller creates and then names in later requests (row 37).** If adopted, the Gateway would create the object and find it again later, as LiteLLM does.
- **Tool-call ids, whose accepted format differs by vendor (row 46).** The Gateway would rewrite each id to fit the vendor a request goes to, as Bifrost, opencode, pi, and one more harness do.
- **Tool schemas that a vendor rejects (row 48).** The Gateway would remove what the vendor rejects. For Gemini, four projects do so for its older schema field, and the Vercel AI SDK, Bifrost, and Gemini CLI send plain JSON Schema to its newer one.
- **Asynchronous tool calls, whose result arrives in a later request (row 53).** If adopted, the Gateway would use OpenAI's own form on GPT-6 models and a placeholder result on other models. The Vercel AI SDK and Bifrost keep the mark that OpenAI puts on each such call and send it back.
- **Google's safety settings and blocked requests (row 72).** The Gateway would send the settings that the operator chose and turn a request that Google blocks into a reply with the finish reason `content_filter`. Two gateways do the first and four projects the second.

Outside the 19, a project supplies one detail in each of three features: the three field names under which the local servers llama.cpp, vLLM, and Ollama return plain-text reasoning (row 17); the rule of adding Anthropic's three input-token counts into one, from LiteLLM (row 38); and Pydantic AI's vendor-neutral tool classes for web search, web fetch, and code execution, each mapped to the vendor's own tool (row 73, on vendors' own tools).

**Where the open-source gateways fall short shows where translating formats is not enough.** The gateways are six that evidence file J compares side by side (agentgateway, Bifrost, claude-code-router, Portkey's AI Gateway, Envoy AI Gateway, and new-api) and, where named, LiteLLM.

- **The order of the blocks in an Anthropic reply (row 3).** None of the six keeps it. Those that split a reply into the same separate fields rebuild the order when the reply goes back to Anthropic, as LiteLLM does, and LiteLLM's order made Anthropic reject a request with the error that thinking blocks "cannot be modified" [K57]. This is the strongest single argument for making the opaque field an ordered list of the vendor's blocks.
- **A label on stored reasoning data (rows 5 and 19).** Of the six, only claude-code-router records with the data what produced it. The model libraries and harnesses that label disagree on what the label holds: from the provider alone to the protocol, the provider, and the model.
- **The vendor's own finish reason beside the standard one (row 84).** The four of the six that were read on this point return only the value they converted to one of OpenAI's standard values. Returning both, as OpenRouter does in `native_finish_reason`, is undecided for the Gateway.
- **Conversations stored at a vendor (row 74).** OpenAI, xAI, and Google can store a conversation, so that the caller sends only an id. None of the six offers this for a vendor that lacks it, and LiteLLM does so with a database of logged requests. Runvil sends the whole conversation in every request and does not need it.
- **Google's safety settings and Maps grounding (rows 72 and 73).** No project examined has a vendor-neutral form of either, and Pydantic AI's tool classes do not include Maps grounding, a Google tool that answers from Google Maps data. So the operator would set the safety settings in Google's own terms, and Maps grounding would work on Google's models only.

**What is still unknown about OpenRouter changes what Runvil has to build in one case only: where the Gateway forwards to OpenRouter itself.** Section 11's Table 20 lists the unknowns about what OpenRouter does inside, in 16 rows, each with what Runvil would do without the answer. Either the Gateway would do the work itself in a way that a project shows: whether OpenRouter rewrites the tool-call ids in a request is unknown, and the Gateway would rewrite them (row 46). Or Runvil never meets the case: whether OpenRouter drops Claude's reasoning when it arrives without its signature is unknown, and the Gateway would never send it that way. But where an operator makes OpenRouter one of the servers the Gateway forwards to, the Gateway is a caller of OpenRouter, 13 of the 16 rows describe what OpenRouter may then do with its requests, and the code for that case (row 101) can be settled only with live requests.

## 10. The earlier Runvil documents this report uses, and what it found wrong or out of date in them

Runvil, a system that runs prompts written as Markdown files with Lua code blocks, already had documents about model APIs before this report was written. Most are in the Runvil repositories, such as `runvil-design`, `runvil-gateway`, `runvil-harness`, and `runvil-classroom`. A decision page records what the project has decided about one part of Runvil. A design plan, a file in a `vibe` folder, plans one piece of work. Runvil is being built in a class, whose repository holds lesson plans (the work items that are left and their open questions), transcripts of the classes, and students' reports. This section lists the documents this report uses, what it took from each, whether that was read again in a live source, and what it found wrong or out of date in them. It ends with a project decision that two reports ask the project owner to change.

OpenRouter is a commercial service that puts one API in front of the models of many vendors. The Gateway is Runvil's server: it accepts model requests and forwards each one to a model vendor or to a model running on the local machine. The opaque field is a planned, optional extra field on an assistant message, in which the Gateway would store data that a vendor sent and expects to get back unchanged. The main example is reasoning, the text a model writes for itself while it works out an answer: Anthropic returns it in thinking blocks (a block is one element of a message's content) and signs each block, so that it can check the block when it comes back. Two other parts of Runvil would store the opaque field and send it back without reading it: the client (the library that sends each request to the Gateway) and the Engine (which runs the prompt).

A search of the Runvil repositories and of the finished reports not yet filed in a repository found about 60 relevant documents. It also covered the 23 provider files, the source files in `runvil-cloud-providers` that each read the model list of one provider, a company that serves requests for a model. Evidence file W groups all of these into 43 sources (one source can be several documents) and holds 250 evidence items taken from them [W]. Table 18 lists the documents this report relies on.

Codes in square brackets such as [S41] are evidence items: numbered entries in the evidence files listed in section 12. Each entry holds the exact quote, where it came from, and the date it was read. The letters name the file. A letter with no number refers to a whole evidence file: [W] indexes the earlier Runvil research, and [M] is about OpenRouter's model catalog, its public list of models. Row numbers such as "row 5" refer to the feature tables in section 3, which list 105 vendor features.

**How to read Table 18.** The last column says whether what this report took from a document was read again on 2026-11-22 in a live source: the current documentation of OpenRouter or of a vendor, or source code as it stood that day, Runvil's own included. "Not applicable" marks what a document decided, planned, recommended, or recorded from a class, which an outside source can neither confirm nor contradict.

Source: evidence file W (section 12), items W1 to W250.

**The plan for this research listed nineteen claims about OpenRouter from these documents, to be checked against OpenRouter's live documentation. Fifteen hold and four are corrected below.** Sixteen are the survey's (two of them are also in the 2026-09-30 report on overflow signals), and one each comes from that report, the 2026-10-14 report on dynamic tools, and the 2026-11-17 report on Anthropic's Messages API [W182 to W199, W201]. The four corrected claims are the survey's on unsupported settings, on reasoning that is "replayed verbatim or dropped", and on cache markers, and the 2026-11-17 report's on the `cache_write_tokens` count [S68, R84, C19, C42]. The fifteen that hold:

- **Routes and requests (3 claims):** its three routes (chat completions in OpenAI's original chat format, a Responses-style route, and an Anthropic Messages route) and a model catalog that needs no API key; the fields its requests add to chat completions; a Responses route that rejects a request for a stored conversation [S66, S67, S69].
- **Tools (3 claims):** three forms in which tool definitions reach a provider (the third is stated in one code comment only); tools that OpenRouter runs itself and tools held back with the flag `defer_loading`; a tool search by regular expression, in beta [T67, P83, P84].
- **Replies and errors (4 claims):** on the chat completions route, the vendor's own finish reason beside a standard one; HTTP 200 sent before a failure, which then arrives inside the reply; a last stream chunk with a non-empty `choices` list; HTTP 402 for no credit, 403 for a budget, and the error type `context_length_exceeded` for a request that is too long [O52, O42, C38, O37].
- **Routing, limits, and prices (4 claims):** provider order and a maximum price set by the caller; rate limits per account and none per API key; no markup on model prices; data-retention rules that also bind a provider reached with the caller's own vendor key [O11, O30, O26, O28].
- **Advice for a request that is too long (1 claim):** the `context-compression` plugin, no longer the "middle-out" transform. When that changed could not be checked [O60, O76].

**This report still uses twelve statements from these documents that were not read again, or only in part.** Statements 1 to 10 are single facts inside feature entries, and 11 and 12 are used only in Table 18.

1. The survey: providers accept eleven kinds of credential between them (row 99).
2. The survey: MiniMax's route in its own format answers a failed request with HTTP 200 and an error code in the body (rows 86 and 101). MiniMax's documentation no longer lists that route [OV62].
3. The 2026-11-17 report on Anthropic's Messages API: four harnesses (Goose, Zed, pi, and opencode) send Anthropic's `drop_block` setting, which makes Anthropic drop a thinking block when the conversation before it has changed (row 4). A fifth project it names was read again: rig, a model library, which gives a program one message type for several vendors [H53].
4. The same report: the harness Agno stores a reply's whole list of blocks (row 1).
5. The same report: Anthropic requires a tool-call id to match `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+$` (row 46) [T50].
6. The same report: Anthropic rejects a request that has an empty text block (row 60).
7. The same report: Anthropic combines consecutive messages from the same role into one (row 60).
8. The same report and the student report of 2026-11-17: vLLM signs its reasoning with a random value (row 105) [W19].
9. The 2026-09-20 report on tool differences: llama-server ignores tools that the model's chat template, the text template that turns a message list into the model's input, cannot show to the model. llama.cpp's code logs a warning in that case, and what it then does with the tools was not read (row 104) [OV72].
10. The 2026-09-30 report on overflow signals: Anthropic signals a request that is too long only by the words "prompt is too long" in its error message (row 88) [O73].
11. The 2026-11-16 report on LiteLLM: 5 percent of LiteLLM's code for translating chat requests has moved from Python to Rust.
12. The same report: LiteLLM publishes a release every week.

**The 2026-10-28 survey is wrong or out of date on how OpenRouter treats unsupported settings, reasoning data, and cache markers, and on Google's OpenAI-compatible route.**

- **OpenRouter ignores an unsupported setting only by default, and it rejects requests in many documented cases.** The survey said: "Unsupported parameters are silently ignored". That is the default, which a caller can switch off with `provider.require_parameters`. OpenRouter's documentation also lists 22 cases in which OpenRouter itself rejects a request, such as one that turns reasoning off on a model that requires it, and several more in which it passes a provider's HTTP 400 error through to the caller. Section 5 lists the cases that matter for Runvil. The survey named one rejection: a request on the Responses route that sets `previous_response_id`, the field with which a caller continues a conversation that is stored on OpenAI's servers [S41 to S48, V22].
- **OpenRouter's documentation says that reasoning has to be sent back unchanged. It does not say that reasoning the next provider cannot read is dropped.** OpenRouter returns reasoning in `reasoning_details`, a list of entries on the assistant message. The survey's claim was: "Reasoning is replayed verbatim or dropped". The documentation supports "replayed verbatim": the caller sends the entries back exactly as it received them. "Dropped" is the survey's inference about an entry that the next provider cannot read, because a different provider or account produced it. OpenRouter documents such a case only for thinking blocks from two of Anthropic's Claude models on its chat completions route: it keeps the request on a provider that can read them, or it answers with an HTTP 400 error. For any other unreadable entry its documentation is silent. The survey also said that each entry has "a vendor `format` tag such as `anthropic-claude-v1`". OpenRouter's API specification, the machine-readable description of its API, defines four types of entry and twelve values for `format`, and its reasoning guide still says three types [R32 to R34, R63, R64, R84, V2].
- **OpenRouter converts a cache marker according to the model that serves the request. The route the caller used plays no part.** Prompt caching means that a vendor stores the processed start of a request, so that a later request with the same start is cheaper and faster. A cache marker is a field on one block of a request that tells the vendor to cache the request up to and including that block. Anthropic's is named `cache_control`, and OpenAI has one of its own, `prompt_cache_breakpoint`. The survey said that OpenRouter turns one into the other "on the OpenAI route", and OpenRouter's documentation says that the serving model decides. Cache lifetimes are dropped in both directions: a lifetime set on one vendor's marker is not converted into the other vendor's [C19 to C22, V9].
- **Google's OpenAI-compatible route keeps thought signatures and cache handles.** A thought signature is Google's signature on a tool call or a piece of reasoning. A cache handle is the name of a cache that a caller has created at Google. The survey said that Google's OpenAI-compatible route "loses thought signatures" and cache handles. A signature arrives in `tool_calls[].extra_content.google.thought_signature`, and a caller names a cache with `cached_content`, inside a top-level `extra_body` object. The survey also gave `missing_thought_signature` as the code of the HTTP 400 error for a missing signature. That name is a finish reason (the value in a reply that says why the model stopped) in Google's Gemini API, and an error code for a reply without a signature in the Interactions API, Google's second API for model requests. The HTTP 400 for a missing signature has `status: "INVALID_ARGUMENT"` [GG6, GG25, VV13, VV16].
- **Smaller statements in the survey that are out of date or need a caution.**
  - The survey treated Hugging Face Inference Providers as a second service like OpenRouter. Since 2026-11-16 it has routed requests for language models through OpenRouter, so it is no longer an independent example [VT4].
  - The survey listed vLLM among the servers that return reasoning text in a field named `reasoning_content`. vLLM has renamed that field to `reasoning` [VV23].
  - The survey remarked that the string `in_flight_budget_exhausted` is gone from OpenRouter's documentation. The documentation shows the string today, as the `reason` in the body of an HTTP 402 error [O37].
  - The survey's description of the Responses API, "the system prompt is top-level `instructions`", still holds, and since OpenAI's GPT-6.2 it has a cost: OpenAI appends top-level `instructions` after the rest of the input and bills them at the uncached rate, so system text sent that way loses prompt caching [OA62].
  - The survey treated Mistral's main chat route as OpenAI-compatible. Mistral now says that its `/v1` chat completions route "uses the native Mistral format", which has the layout of chat completions and differs in details (row 101) [VV20].

Confidence: high - each correction was read in a live source on 2026-11-22, and most were fetched twice.

**In the 2026-11-17 report on Anthropic's Messages API, the label on the opaque field has to name the endpoint, a second route is less likely to be needed than the report judged, its statements about agentgateway and Bifrost need correcting, Anthropic has an eighth stop reason, and the `cache_write_tokens` count is no longer an extension.** The report's design of the opaque field is kept.

- **The label on the opaque field has to name the endpoint as well as the protocol and the model.** The label is a few values that the Gateway stores inside the opaque field, beside the vendor's blocks, to say what produced them, and it sends the blocks back only when the label matches the place the next request is going. In the report's design the label holds the protocol and the model. That is too little, because vendors tie reasoning data to the account that produced it. Outside the organization (the customer account) that produced them or one linked to it, Anthropic drops the thinking blocks of four of its newest Claude models with no error, and OpenAI drops reasoning from another organization. Google scopes a signature to one Google Cloud project, and a Gemini request that continues a tool call with a signature from the other of Google's two services (AI Studio and Vertex AI) fails with an HTTP 400 error. So when the Gateway reaches one model at two endpoints, a label of protocol and model lets the blocks go to an account that drops them or rejects the request. With the endpoint in the label (in the Gateway's configuration, one backend with its base URL and API key), the Gateway would know when it has to leave the blocks out, and would send the message without them. The model loses that reasoning either way, so the gain is that the Gemini request does not fail and that the Gateway knows what it left out (rows 5, 12, and 19) [VV7, VV18, OA27, VT8].
- **A second route for Anthropic-style requests is less likely to be needed than that report judged.** The report gave a roughly even chance that Runvil would later want a route in Anthropic's own format, `/v1/messages`, beside its chat completions route. Its reason was that three things had "no home in chat completions": the blocks that record the call and result of a vendor-run tool (a tool such as web search that the vendor runs on its own servers), `pause_turn` (a stop reason, Anthropic's name for a finish reason, which means that Anthropic paused a long reply and expects the caller to send it back to continue), and compaction blocks (Anthropic's summary of a conversation that it shortened itself). The Gateway would store the blocks of vendor-run tools and the compaction blocks in the opaque field (rows 62 and 77). And where a vendor stops a reply and expects another request to continue it, the Gateway would send that request itself: for a reply that Anthropic paused (row 63), and for an answer from Google's Gemini 4 that is longer than the output limit of one request (row 13). The model libraries Pydantic AI and the Vercel AI SDK, LiteLLM, and Google's own client libraries already send such requests themselves.
- **agentgateway does not rewrite tool-call ids.** The report credited agentgateway, an open-source gateway written in Rust, with rewriting tool-call ids that Anthropic would reject. At the commit read on 2026-11-22 agentgateway copies them unchanged, and the project that rewrites them is Bifrost, an open-source gateway written in Go. The older commit that the report read was not available, so whether it rewrote ids then is not known [J34, VF14].
- **A client that reads `reasoning_details` cannot treat Bifrost's entries like OpenRouter's.** The report said that such a client "also works against OpenRouter and Bifrost". It can read both, because Bifrost uses the same field name and three of the same type names. But Bifrost's entries have no `format`, and Bifrost discards the `format` of an entry sent through it, so the entry no longer says which vendor format it is in [J27, VF15].
- **Anthropic has an eighth stop reason.** The report listed seven. Anthropic's reference for the beta version of the Messages API lists an eighth, `compaction` [VV8].
- **The token count `prompt_tokens_details.cache_write_tokens` is part of OpenAI's own format today.** It counts the prompt tokens written to a cache. The report's Table A3 calls it "an extension name that agentgateway and OpenRouter both use". OpenAI's own Chat Completions reference defines it today [W198, Q30, VV4].

Confidence: high for the label, the statements about agentgateway and Bifrost, the eighth stop reason, and the token count - each fact behind them was read in live documentation or in source code at a pinned commit, which is one exact version of a project's code. Confidence: medium for the judgment about a second route - the evidence is what other open-source projects do, and Runvil has not built any of it.

**The 2026-09-20 report on tool differences is out of date on Kimi K3's tool-call ids, on what OpenAI does with reasoning from another organization, and on Gemini's tool-call ids.**

- **Moonshot's Kimi K3 no longer requires a fixed format for tool-call ids.** The report said that tool-call ids for Kimi, K3 included, must have the form `functions.name:idx`. Moonshot's code for Kimi K3 says that K3 "drops the ``func:index`` format requirement". For the older Kimi K2, Moonshot's guide still gives the format, and K2's chat templates were not read again (row 46) [T57, VV21, OV41].
- **OpenAI's documentation says it removes reasoning that came from another organization and reports the removal.** The report said that OpenAI answers an encrypted value from another organization, here encrypted reasoning that only OpenAI can read, with an `invalid_encrypted_content` error. OpenAI's pages today say that the reasoning item is removed and that the reply's `input_transformations` list reports the removal with the reason `organization_mismatch`. The string `invalid_encrypted_content` is in none of the 30 OpenAI pages that were searched for it [VV5].
- **Google's Gemini 3 and later return an id on every tool call, and the caller has to send it back.** About these ids the report said "ids optional". The `id` is on every function call, Google's term for a tool call, and Google tells the caller to send it back with the tool's result [GG85].

Confidence: high - each was read in the vendor's live documentation or code on 2026-11-22.

**Smaller notes on other earlier documents and on a comment in Runvil's code.**

- **OpenRouter signals a used-up quota with HTTP 402 or 403, not with OpenAI's 429 codes.** In its table of exhaustion signals, the 2026-09-30 report on overflow signals puts OpenRouter in one group with OpenAI, whose signal for a used-up quota is an HTTP 429 with a quota code. OpenRouter's own signals are an HTTP 402 with `error_type: "payment_required"`, and an HTTP 403 when a spending budget rejects the request [O76].
- **OpenRouter now documents what it does with `tool_reference` blocks.** In Anthropic's tool search, where a caller holds tool definitions back, Anthropic's API expands a `tool_reference` block into the full definition of the tool it names. The 2026-10-14 report on dynamic tools says that most proxies, servers that pass a caller's requests on to the vendor, do not forward these blocks. OpenRouter passes the block through where the provider supports tool search itself. Elsewhere it adds the named tool's definition to the request, and the block "reaches the provider as text" [P46].
- **A code comment about OpenRouter's model list is out of date.** A comment in `runvil-cloud-providers`, in the file `openrouter.rs`, says that the list OpenRouter returns by default is its full model catalog. Today the default list holds only the models whose output is text, which is 476 of the catalog's 739 models [M].
- **xAI's own pages disagree on the largest number of tools in one request.** Its function calling guide says 350, and its two API reference pages say 1,000. That explains why two earlier Runvil documents disagree: the 2026-10-14 report gives 350 and the survey gives 1,000. Which number xAI's API enforces was not tested [VV24].

**The Gateway's decision page rules out a second route for Anthropic-style requests, and two reports ask for one, to serve other callers.** Today the Gateway has one route for model requests, `POST /v1/chat/completions`.

- **Against a second route: the Gateway's decision page and the Gateway plan of 2026-08-23.** The decision page says that "an Anthropic-shaped request is outside the API", and the plan lists "Anthropic inbound shim (rejected)".
- **For a second route: the 2026-11-09 report on porting Unsloth Studio and the student report of 2026-11-17.** Both want it for other callers that send Anthropic's format, above all Claude Code, Anthropic's coding agent. The first would pass requests on `/v1/messages` and `/v1/responses` through to llama-server. The second would have the Gateway serve `/v1/messages`, and asks the project owner to amend or reaffirm the decision page first.
- **For one route: the 2026-11-17 report on Anthropic's Messages API.** It recommends that the Gateway keep its existing route and translate requests for Anthropic's models into Anthropic's format behind it.

For Runvil's own client, and only for it, the evidence gathered for this report supports the decision page: keep one route. The Gateway would handle on that route every feature that OpenRouter serves only on its Messages route or its Responses route (section 8), and OpenRouter shows what further routes lead to: once a feature exists on only one of its routes, its callers have to choose a route according to the vendor (section 4). Whether the Gateway should serve other callers that send Anthropic's format, which is what the two reports ask for, is a separate question. This report did not examine it, and the project owner has to decide it. Confidence: medium - the opaque field exists only as a design, and other projects' code is the only evidence that it can hold what a second route would offer.

## 11. Limits: nothing was run, every source was read on one day, and some of OpenRouter's behavior is still unknown

This report comes from reading, with one exception described below. The reading was done by AI research agents, which wrote the evidence files, and the report's author is the lead agent that directed them.

All of the evidence was gathered on one day, 2026-11-22, in three rounds. The first round read OpenRouter's published material, vendor documentation, open-source code, issue reports and other third-party pages, recorded traffic (requests and replies to OpenRouter that open-source projects saved in their test suites), and Runvil's own code. This report looked to OpenRouter first for how to map vendor features onto Runvil's universal API. Then the verification round read the sources of selected first-round statements a second time, and the gap-filling round took 36 questions about OpenRouter's behavior that the first round had left open, and looked for answers in recorded traffic, open-source code, and the documentation of vendors and of other gateways.

The findings are numbered evidence items in the 26 evidence files that section 12 lists: 1,787 from the first round and 162 from the gap-filling round, 1,949 together. The verification round's four files hold its verdicts. Letters alone in square brackets, such as [O], cite a whole file.

**No model request was sent anywhere, and no Runvil code was built or tested.** A statement that a vendor rejects a request, or that a translation works, means that a document, a piece of code, or a recording says so. Nobody working on this report saw it happen. One piece of code was run. Each entry of `reasoning_details`, OpenRouter's list of reasoning entries, has a `format` value that names the vendor format it came in, such as `anthropic-claude-v1`. The verification round wrote six entries by hand and ran them through the code that checks such entries in `OpenRouterTeam/ai-sdk-provider`, one of OpenRouter's official client libraries. The library drops an entry whose `format` it does not know, keeps the others, and reports no error [VF6].

**Everything was read without a key or other credential, so two routes of OpenRouter's API that need a key were left unread.** One is `GET /api/v1/tools`. According to the API specification it returns, for each of OpenRouter's server tools (tools that OpenRouter or a vendor runs on a server during a request, such as web search), the names the tool can be requested under, how a call to it shows up in a reply on each route, and its prices. That route would settle one open question in Table 20: which server tools leave a record of their calls in a reply. The other is `GET /api/v1/models/user`, which lists the models that one caller's key can use.

**OpenRouter's translator is closed source, and for 20 of the 105 features its published material does not state what OpenRouter does.** It does for the other 85. For 7 of the 20, what OpenRouter does is inferred from its API specification or documentation, from recorded traffic, or from issue reports: the order of a reply's blocks (row 3), plain-text reasoning that a vendor requires back (row 14), tool-call id formats (row 46), system messages in mid-conversation (row 59), Google's safety settings (row 72), token counting (row 79), and the finish reason of a Gemini reply with tool calls (row 85). An inference settles less than a statement by OpenRouter would, and for rows 3, 14, 46, and 72 Table 20 lists what it leaves open. For 6, section 3 describes how an open-source project handles the feature: Gemini's placeholder signature for other models' tool calls (row 12), a switch of model or provider in mid-conversation (row 19), structured output where the schema cannot be enforced (row 56), and three features of local servers (rows 103 to 105). For 7, it describes what the vendor's documentation requires: tool calls with reasoning on OpenAI's newest models (row 7), Mistral's reasoning chunks (row 15), Alibaba's encrypted reasoning (row 16), Anthropic's cache diagnostics (row 40), a reply that Anthropic pauses (row 63), Skills and code that a model writes to call tools (row 82), and APIs that look like OpenAI's but behave differently (row 101). An open-source project shows one way to handle a feature. OpenRouter may do something else.

**The subject changes from week to week, so what was read may already be out of date.** OpenRouter's changelog shows changes on most days of November, and the list of `format` values in its API specification gained new values five times since July. Three of the facts used here were at most five weeks old: Anthropic's API version of 2026-10-21 (row 28), OpenAI's rules for GPT-6.2 of 2026-11-17 (rows 9 and 58), and Hugging Face's inference service sending its language-model requests through OpenRouter since 2026-11-16.

**What OpenRouter documents is not necessarily what its service does, and OpenRouter's own sources contradict each other in at least seven places.** A statement about what OpenRouter does means what its published material says, unless the sentence names a recording or an inference. The contradictions:

- **How long sticky routing lasts.** One documentation page says a conversation stays with its provider until 60 minutes pass without a request, and another says 10 minutes [V10].
- **Whether Anthropic's Claude Haiku 5.5 accepts a forced tool choice.** OpenRouter's migration guide for the Claude 5.5 models says it does, and OpenRouter's model catalog says it does not [V17].
- **The default reasoning effort of Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5.** The same guide says `medium`, and the model catalog says `high` [AN25].
- **Which routes accept the server tool `openrouter:bash`.** The API specification lists the tool for the chat completions and Responses routes. The tool's documentation page says that both answer it with HTTP 400 and that only the Messages route accepts it [V19].
- **How many types of reasoning entry exist.** The documentation says each entry of `reasoning_details` is of one of "three possible types", and the API specification defines four [V2].
- **What happens when a caller asks for structured output from a model that does not support it.** One page says the request "will fail", and another says the setting "is ignored" [T34].
- **Which HTTP status reports an overloaded provider.** OpenRouter's table of error types gives 503. Its API specification lists 503 and a separate 529 response for the same condition, and no page says when a caller gets which [O].

Where OpenRouter's sources disagree, the feature tables in section 3 say so and take neither side.

**The recorded traffic shows what OpenRouter answered on the day of each recording, which may differ from what it answers today.** The recordings are about 1,600 exchanges, dated 2025-05-20 to 2026-11-18. They come from seven projects in eight sets, because one project's deleted recordings were recovered from its git history [WT1].

**Which option each feature needs is the judgment of the report's author.** Section 1 defines six options for supporting a vendor feature, and the author chose one for each of the 105 features from the evidence. Where the author's confidence in a choice is medium or low, the feature's note in section 3 says why. Every feature that needs the opaque field depends on a field that is designed and not built. The line between the options "Gateway only" and "Compatibility code" is also a matter of judgment: under the first the Gateway translates a request without changing what it asks for, and under the second it changes or repairs a request or a reply so that one vendor accepts it, mostly in a way that a prompt author could notice. A reader who draws that line elsewhere can reclassify a feature: the "What Runvil needs" line of its note says what the Gateway would do.

**The verification round read a small, hand-picked part of the first round's evidence a second time.** It picked the claims in that evidence (single statements, each in one evidence item or a few) that the conclusions depend on most and the most surprising ones. They are in about 200 of the first round's 1,787 evidence items, and they got 128 verdicts. Web pages were fetched again, and code was read in the copies of the repositories that the first round had downloaded. Nothing from the gap-filling round was read a second time. A verdict says whether the statement that was checked holds:

- **Confirmed (97).** The source says what the statement says.
- **Confirmed with a correction (22).** The statement holds once a detail is changed, for example a count: 24 providers keep billing a request that the caller cancelled, where the first round wrote 30 [V16].
- **Not confirmed (4).** The statement does not hold: its source does not say it. One of the four is the first round's own, that MiniMax's API returns `reasoning_details` itself. MiniMax's documentation names only its own field for reasoning text, `reasoning_content`, which the feature tables use (row 14) [VT3]. The other three are claims from earlier Runvil documents that the first round had already found wrong or unsupported: an OpenAI error code `invalid_encrypted_content`, `missing_thought_signature` as the code of Google's HTTP 400, and a rule by Google on whether its two services for Gemini accept each other's signatures [VV5, VV13, VV18].
- **Conflict between sources (3).** Two sources of one publisher disagree on what the statement is about, so the fact stays open: the lifetime of sticky routing and forced tool choice on Claude Haiku 5.5, both listed above, and xAI's limit on the tools in one request [VV24]. Of the other five contradictions listed above, two were noted beside confirmed statements (`openrouter:bash` and the types of entry), and three were not read a second time.
- **Overstated (2).** The statement holds for part of what it covers. `ai-sdk-provider` removes Gemini's reasoning text only when the entry has no signature, and the code of the open-source gateway LiteLLM counts cached tokens in `prompt_tokens` for three vendors. The first round wrote "always" and "every vendor" [VF6, VF13].

The corrections are applied in the feature tables and in section 10.

**How to read Table 19.** The rows are the four verification evidence files, with the key letters V, VV, VF, and VT in that order. A pinned commit is one exact version of a repository, named by its commit id.

*Table 19. Results of the verification round: 128 verdicts.*

| What was re-checked | Confirmed | Confirmed with a correction | Not confirmed | Conflict between sources | Overstated |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenRouter's documentation, API specification, and model catalog (27 verdicts) | 21 | 4 | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| Vendor documentation (27 verdicts) | 21 | 2 | 3 | 1 | 0 |
| Open-source code at pinned commits (48 verdicts) | 39 | 7 | 0 | 0 | 2 |
| Third-party pages and issue reports (26 verdicts) | 16 | 9 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| Total (128 verdicts) | 97 | 22 | 4 | 3 | 2 |

**In the first round, some web pages were read only as the excerpts that a search tool returned.** That is so for 31 pages in the evidence file on other gateways (key letter Q) and for about 15 issue and pull request pages in the two files on how open-source projects treat OpenRouter in their own code (key letters F and G). The verification round opened in full the ones the conclusions depend on most. The rest are marked in their evidence files, and a statement that cites one is based on the excerpt alone.

**The six defects that section 7 describes in Runvil's own code were found by reading the code.** None has been reproduced by running it.

### What is still unknown about OpenRouter, and why none of it changes what Runvil has to build

None of it changes what Runvil has to build, with one exception: where an operator makes OpenRouter one of the servers the Gateway forwards to (row 101), 13 of the 16 rows of Table 20 describe what OpenRouter may do with the Gateway's requests. The Gateway is then a caller of OpenRouter's chat completions route, and the other three rows (OpenRouter's server tools, its Messages and Responses routes, and a reply that Anthropic pauses) cannot arise, because it would use none of these. For that upstream the Gateway would keep each reply's `reasoning_details` in the opaque field and send it back as it came, which is what OpenRouter asks of every caller, for as long as the conversation stays on one model. Whether its code for that upstream has to do more, for example rewrite tool-call ids before a request to a Mistral model, depends on those 13 rows and can be learned only from live requests. To every other upstream the Gateway would send requests in the vendor's own format, so what it has to satisfy is the vendor's rule, and OpenRouter's way of satisfying it is one example among others.

The 20 above are features. The 36 are questions about how OpenRouter behaves: a question can have several parts and can concern any of the 105 features, because the material often names a field without saying what the translator does behind it. Recorded traffic answered 2 of the 36: for a request that Anthropic serves, OpenRouter's `prompt_tokens` includes the cached tokens and the tokens written to the cache [WT23], and when the output limit cuts off a tool call from one of OpenAI's models, the finish reason is still `tool_calls` and the arguments stop in mid-string [WT29]. For 24 of the other 34, an open-source project or the documentation of a vendor or of another gateway shows one way to handle the feature. For 10 nothing does: they concern things that exist in OpenRouter alone, such as its records of server tool calls. Table 20 holds 18 of the 34 in its 16 rows, with three questions that came up during the gap-filling round: what a cache marker does on Gemini 4, what OpenRouter does when Anthropic pauses a reply, and which safety thresholds it sends to Gemini by default. It leaves out 13 that have such an example, among them how OpenRouter turns a reasoning budget into an effort value and its full table of stop reasons, and 3 that no feature depends on: how OpenRouter knows which account produced a thinking block, the internals of its model catalog, and what it does with request fields outside its API specification.

**How to read Table 20.** Each row holds one question or a few related ones, and where it holds several, the columns take them in the same order. In the third column, "would", "should", and "has to" mark what this report proposes and is not built, and "today" marks what the code does now.

*Table 20. What is still unknown about OpenRouter, what was tried to find out, and what Runvil would do without the answer.*

| What is still unknown about OpenRouter | What was tried | What Runvil would do without the answer |
|---|---|---|
| What OpenRouter does with a reasoning entry in another vendor's format after a conversation switches model or provider, for example an entry that Claude produced in a request to a Gemini model. | OpenRouter's documentation, API specification, and official client libraries, and all of the recorded traffic. No recorded request contains such an entry: before the request that follows a switch, the model libraries rig and Pydantic AI turn the reasoning into plain text (rig) or move it into a user message (Pydantic AI) [WT9, WT11]. | The Gateway would never send one model's reasoning data to another. Where the label on the opaque field does not match the request, the Gateway would leave the blocks out and build the message from its text and tool calls (row 19). The harnesses pi and Goose also drop another model's reasoning [H61, H69]. rig and the harness opencode pass on its readable text without the signature or the encrypted value [H51, H65]. The gateway Bifrost removes the reasoning and retries once after a vendor's rejection. |
| Whether and how OpenRouter restores the order of Claude's thinking, text, and tool calls when it sends a conversation back to Anthropic. Its chat completions reply has three separate fields, `reasoning_details`, `content` (the text), and `tool_calls`, and so does not record the order. | The documentation, and the only two published examples of OpenRouter's debug output, which is the request body it sent to the provider, returned when the caller sets `debug: {"echo_upstream_body": true}`. One is in its documentation, one in a third-party pull request, and neither contains an earlier reply. In recorded traffic, each of the 67 Claude replies with thinking has a single thinking block, which comes first. | The Gateway would not have to restore the order: it would send back the reply's whole list of blocks, in the vendor's order, from the opaque field (row 3). rig and the harnesses Hermes Agent and Agno store the whole list in this way. The five open-source gateways examined that split a reply into separate fields lose the order. |
| The rule by which OpenRouter matches a Gemini thought signature to the part of the reply it belongs to, such as a tool call. OpenRouter returns the signature as a separate entry in `reasoning_details`. | Recorded traffic shows five layouts for these entries. A signature for a tool call has an `id` equal to the tool call's id. A signature for a text part has only its position in the list, and what OpenRouter does with it when it comes back is not visible. | The Gateway would have nothing to match: its translator for Gemini would keep each signature on its part in the opaque field and send the parts back that way (rows 10 and 11). Open-source projects keep a signature with its part in six different ways (row 10). |
| What happens when a signature or an encrypted value that names one of OpenRouter's endpoints (a provider's offering of a model) arrives at another. In OpenRouter's documentation examples and in recorded traffic, such a value ends with a short suffix that, decoded from base64, names the model version and the provider. OpenRouter does not explain it. That OpenRouter adds it is an inference, and what it then sends to the vendor is not visible. Also unknown is whether OpenRouter still moves a Gemini conversation between Google AI Studio and Vertex AI, Google's two services for Gemini, after a tool call, which its staff confirmed on 2026-06-17 fails with HTTP 400. | In 34 recorded requests that sent such a value back unchanged, the request succeeded and the reply came from the endpoint the value names. No recording shows a value arriving at another endpoint. The staff's advice was to pin the provider, with `provider: { order: [...], allow_fallbacks: false }`. | Runvil has to satisfy each vendor's rule on where such a value is accepted (row 5), whatever OpenRouter does. The label of the 2026-11-17 design, the protocol and the model, cannot tell two accounts at one vendor apart, so section 8 recommends that it also record the endpoint (in Runvil, one backend in the Gateway's configuration). The Gateway would then send such a value back only to the backend that produced it. LiteLLM records which upstream account produced each piece of encrypted reasoning and sends the next request there, or removes the reasoning. |
| Whether OpenRouter removes Claude reasoning that arrives without its signature, or forwards it and lets Anthropic reject it. | Two accounts point opposite ways. A code comment of March 2026 in `ai-sdk-provider` says that a plain `reasoning` string sent without `reasoning_details` makes OpenRouter build thinking blocks without valid signatures, which Anthropic rejects [F7]. A user's comment of October 2026 on a pi issue report says OpenRouter drops unsigned reasoning without an error. That is an inference from a prompt token count that did not change, and nobody at OpenRouter has confirmed it [F46, VT10]. The inputs may differ, a plain string in one and an entry without its signature in the other, so both can be true. No recording sends unsigned reasoning to Claude. | The Gateway would never send Claude's thinking without its signature: it would send the thinking blocks back from the opaque field as they arrived, or leave them out (row 19). |
| The rule OpenRouter's server uses to remove reasoning entries that appear more than once in a request. | The only source is `ai-sdk-provider`. It removes repeated entries itself before it sends a request, and a comment in its code says that its test for sameness copies the server's. | Only OpenRouter's server applies the rule, so it matters only where the Gateway forwards to OpenRouter. |
| Whether OpenRouter supplies a vendor's required `reasoning_content` field when the caller left the reasoning out, and what it writes to the provider. Several vendors whose own API copies the chat completions format return reasoning as plain text in `reasoning_content` and require it back on later requests. DeepSeek answers a conversation with tools that lacks it with HTTP 400. | Issue reports from April and May 2026 show DeepSeek's HTTP 400 arriving at callers through OpenRouter. No recording sends reasoning back to these models. | The Gateway has to meet the vendor's rule itself (row 14): write the reasoning stored in the opaque field back into `reasoning_content`, or a single space for an assistant message that has no opaque field. pi and opencode send the reasoning back themselves, and LiteLLM writes a single space when the caller left it out. |
| How OpenRouter passes the system message of a chat completions request on to OpenAI, and which values it sends for `store` and `include`, two settings of OpenAI's Responses API. `store` says whether OpenAI keeps the response on its servers, and `include` asks for extra data in the reply, such as encrypted reasoning. | A search of the documentation, issue reports, and the web found examples of OpenRouter's debug output for Claude only. Recorded replies suggest which of OpenAI's two APIs OpenRouter calls: the Responses API for every recorded OpenAI model except `gpt-4o` and `gpt-4o-mini`. | The Gateway would build its own Responses API request from OpenAI's documentation. It should send the system message as the first message of the input, with the role `developer`, and not in the separate `instructions` field (row 58). LiteLLM's code that turns a chat completions request into a Responses API request also keeps it in the input. For the two settings, Codex, OpenAI's own harness, sends `store: false` with `include: ["reasoning.encrypted_content"]`. That fits Runvil: today the Engine sends the whole conversation with every request (row 74), and the opaque field would need the encrypted reasoning (row 6). |
| Four details of prompt caching. (1) Whether a caller's `prompt_cache_key`, a string that steers the requests of one conversation to the server that holds its cache, is passed on to OpenAI. (2) Whether OpenRouter sets `x-grok-conv-id`, the header that xAI uses for the same purpose. (3) What a cache marker does on Google's Gemini 4. (4) Which lifetime of sticky routing is real, 60 minutes or 10. | The documentation is silent on (1) to (3) and gives both lifetimes for (4). No recording sets `prompt_cache_key` on the chat completions route, and the programs that made the recordings strip the cache markers that would test (3). | (1) and (2): the Gateway would derive a key from a hash of the conversation's opening messages and send it to the vendor itself, as OpenAI's `prompt_cache_key` or xAI's `x-grok-conv-id` (row 36). pi sends the `prompt_cache_key` itself. (3): Google documents that Gemini 4 caches automatically and offers no cache that a caller creates, so the Gateway would have nothing to turn a cache marker into. LiteLLM's caching code has no check for Gemini 4. (4): the lifetime belongs to OpenRouter's own router. Today the Gateway sends every request for a model to the first backend listed for it. |
| Whether OpenRouter still imitates tool calling for a model that has no tool support, by writing the tool definitions into the model's input as a text template. | The only source is one comment in a code sample of OpenRouter's API reference. In recorded traffic, a request with tools to a model that had no endpoint supporting tools was answered with HTTP 404. | Runvil's Gateway does this itself today, with its tool dialect `gemma3_tool_code`. Another such model would get a tool dialect of its own (row 50). |
| Whether OpenRouter rewrites the tool-call ids in a request it receives, so that an id issued by one vendor fits the format another vendor requires. | Recorded traffic shows ids issued by one vendor accepted by another. Third-party issue reports show Mistral's rejection of an id arriving at the caller through OpenRouter. An OpenRouter employee wrote on 2026-03-31 that a fix was coming, and no page found says that it shipped. | The Gateway has to rewrite the ids itself to fit the vendor the request is going to (row 46). Bifrost, opencode, pi, and the harness Roo Code do the same. |
| Which of OpenRouter's server tools leave an entry of type `reasoning.server_tool_call` in `reasoning_details` on the chat completions route, and whether the caller has to send that entry back. | The API specification, the documentation page for every server tool, two of OpenRouter's official client libraries, and recorded traffic. The entry type is defined in the API specification and appears on none of those pages, and no recording contains one. | This entry type exists only in OpenRouter, and Runvil does not use OpenRouter's server tools. Today its own tools run inside the Host. |
| Whether three Anthropic features work through OpenRouter's Messages route (`/messages`): code execution on Anthropic's servers, the memory tool, and the MCP connector, through which Anthropic's servers call tools on a separate MCP server. Also unknown is what two request fields do on OpenRouter's Responses route (`/responses`): `background`, which in OpenAI's API starts a run that the caller checks on later, and `item_reference`, which points to an item of an earlier response by its id. | The API specification and the documentation. Settling it needs a live request. | These are questions about OpenRouter's own routes, and Runvil uses none of these features today. It runs code and MCP tools itself, inside the Host (rows 65 and 67), a memory tool of its own would be an ordinary tool (row 66), and the Engine sends the whole conversation with every request and waits for each reply (rows 74 and 75). LiteLLM imitates both: background runs with a cache that the caller polls, and a stored conversation that it rebuilds from its own database of logged requests. |
| What OpenRouter does when Anthropic pauses a reply. Anthropic ends a reply with the stop reason `pause_turn` when its loop of tool calls that run on Anthropic's servers stops before the answer is finished, and the answer continues when the reply is sent back unchanged. It is unknown whether OpenRouter resends the reply itself or hands the pause to the caller, and which finish reason its chat completions route reports. | The documentation, the API specification, the changelog, and two of OpenRouter's official client libraries. The value `pause_turn` is in the API specification's list of stop reasons for the Messages route and on no documentation page. | Anthropic pauses a reply only when the model was offered a tool that Anthropic runs, and Runvil offers none today. If it did, the Gateway would send the paused content back itself while it handles the client's one request (row 63). Pydantic AI and Anthropic's own client libraries resend a paused reply automatically, and LiteLLM hands the pause to the caller. |
| Three details of OpenRouter's streamed replies: whether the closing line `[DONE]` always follows a chunk that reports an error, how often OpenRouter sends keep-alive messages (sent during a long silence so that the connection is not treated as dead), and whether it applies any timeout. | Two recordings from 2025-11 contain an error chunk. `[DONE]` follows it in one, and the other may have been cut short by the program that recorded it. The recordings store the bytes that arrived and not the times, so they cannot show an interval or a timeout. | The client should treat any chunk that contains `error` as a failure, whether or not `[DONE]` follows (row 87). The Gateway should pass keep-alive messages on and set its own limit on how long a stream may stay silent (row 90). Today it drops them and sets no such limit (section 7). |
| Two lists that OpenRouter does not publish, and one of its defaults: (1) the values it lets through in the `x-anthropic-beta` header, which turns on Anthropic's beta features, (2) the settings it forwards from `provider.options`, the field in which a caller hands OpenRouter a provider's own setting names and values, and (3) the safety thresholds, one for each harm category, that it sends to Google's Gemini by default. | The documentation and the API specification. | OpenRouter needs the lists because it lets a caller send a vendor's own values. Runvil rules out a prompt that must know its vendor, so no caller of the Gateway would send them. The Gateway would attach Anthropic's beta header itself (row 96), and the operator would write a vendor's own settings, and Gemini's default safety thresholds, into the Gateway's configuration (rows 97 and 72). The gateways new-api and one-api also send a safety default that their operator sets. Where a gateway forwards a caller's beta values, Anthropic's advice is to forward them as written. |

**Questions about vendors' own rules also stayed open, and only live requests can settle them.**

- **Google has published no rule on whether Google AI Studio and Vertex AI accept each other's Gemini thought signatures.** Most reports say they do not: OpenRouter's staff confirmed the HTTP 400 described in Table 20, and three projects report that each service rejects the other's signatures, two of them after live requests. A fourth project's code says the opposite: it treats the two as sharing their signature formats [GX30 to GX35]. With the endpoint in the label, the Gateway would never send one service a signature that the other produced (row 5), whichever is right.
- **Which of two values an API enforces, where a vendor's pages disagree with each other or with OpenRouter's model catalog.** xAI's pages give 350 and 1,000 as the limit on tools in one request, and the lower one is the safe one for a gateway to enforce [VV24]. Meta says its API accepts only `auto` for `tool_choice`, while OpenRouter's model catalog says that two of three Meta models accept all four values (`none`, `auto`, `required`, and a named function) [VV22]. The feature tables follow Meta (row 44).

## 12. Sources and evidence files

This section lists the 26 evidence files and then a selection of the documents, code, and web pages they were written from. Each evidence file ends with a "Sources" table of every source it used.

**How to read an evidence key.** An evidence item is one numbered entry in an evidence file: the exact quote, where it came from (a URL, or a repository with its commit, file, and line), and the date it was read. A key such as `R36` is on the "Evidence items" line of a feature's note or in square brackets in running text. Its letters (`R`) name the file and its number names the item, and letters alone, such as `[O]`, mean the whole file. Table 21 gives the file for each set of letters: its name is `2026-11-22-conduct-research-openrouter-<subject>.md`, with the `<subject>` from the table.

**The evidence files come from three rounds of evidence gathering, all on 2026-11-22, and hold 2,035 evidence items.**

- **The first round** produced 19 files (the first 19 in Table 21) with 1,787 evidence items, from the sources in the six lists that end this section.
- **The verification round**, the second, produced four files (V, VV, VF, and VT) with 86 evidence items. It read again, in their sources, the first-round statements that the conclusions depend on most and the most surprising ones. Each item covers one or several of those statements, and each statement got one verdict: 128 in all (27 in V, 27 in VV, 48 in VF, 26 in VT). 97 held as recorded, 22 held with a correction, 4 did not hold, 3 were conflicts in which two sources of one publisher disagree, and 2 were overstated (true for only part of what they say). Three of the 4 were statements that the first round had itself recorded as wrong or unsupported, and the fourth, that MiniMax's API returns `reasoning_details`, is not used: row 14 has MiniMax's own field [VT3]. Section 11 names the rest.
- **The gap-filling round**, the third, produced three files (WT, GX, and CT) with 162 evidence items. With the first round's they make 1,949 findings, a count that leaves out the 86 items of verdicts. It took the 36 questions about OpenRouter, a commercial gateway, that the first round could not answer because OpenRouter's translator is closed source. Recorded traffic answered 2. For 24, a project's code or a vendor's or gateway's documentation shows one way to handle the feature, and what OpenRouter does is still unknown or known in part. For 10 there is no answer and no such example (section 11).

The 26 files are in the research staging folder of the Runvil workspace, outside the project repositories, and Table 21 names them. Files there are deleted 30 days after they were written, and section 8 recommends moving them to a permanent place beside this report, a decision still open.

The last column is each file's own count of its sources, which are web pages, files of code, or repositories (the VF file counts repositories).

The titles are the evidence files' own main headings, most of them shortened. The files use working labels that the report replaces with plain wording. Inside them:

- "First wave" means the first round, "verification pass" the verification round, and "gap-fill" in a title the gap-filling round.
- "Filled", or "a fill", marks what a named open-source project does where OpenRouter's material is silent. "OR-documented" marks a statement in OpenRouter's published material, "OR-inferred" one worked out about OpenRouter from client code, recorded traffic, or an issue report, and "Vendor-documented" one in a vendor's documentation.
- "Workspace" means the folder that holds the Runvil repositories, so that label marks earlier Runvil research or Runvil's code.
- In titles, "field evidence" is what open-source projects do in their code when they call OpenRouter, and "server tools" are tools that OpenRouter or a vendor runs on its servers.

**What each kind of source can and cannot show.** No model request was sent. Public pages, OpenRouter's model catalog (its public list of models), and its endpoint lists were fetched, and code was read. So documentation, OpenRouter's or a vendor's, shows what a company says its service does, code shows what a program is written to do, and neither shows what the service does when it is called.

- OpenRouter's documentation, its API specification (the machine-readable description of its API), its model catalog, and its official client libraries state what its API accepts and returns, not what the translator does with a request.
- Source code at a pinned commit (one exact version of a repository, named by its commit id) shows what a project does at that version.
- Recorded traffic shows what OpenRouter sent on the day of the recording.
- An issue report is a user's or a maintainer's account, which OpenRouter or the vendor has not necessarily confirmed. A statement in this report that comes from one says so.

### OpenRouter's own material (all public, read without credentials)

- **API specification:** `https://openrouter.ai/openapi.json` (OpenAPI 3.1, 123 paths, 1,071 schemas; three downloads on 2026-11-22 were identical byte for byte).
- **Model catalog:** `https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/models` (476 entries), and the list of endpoints for one model at `https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/models/{author}/{slug}/endpoints`, fetched for 59 models [M]. An endpoint here is one provider's offering of one model.
- **Documentation.** Each page is at `https://openrouter.ai/docs/` followed by the path shown. A local copy of 559 pages was made on 2026-11-22. It is not complete: `guides/features/server-tools/files` was missing and was read on the live site. A selection:
  - `guides/best-practices/reasoning-tokens`, `api_reference/responses/reasoning`
  - `guides/best-practices/prompt-caching`, `cookbook/administration/usage-accounting`
  - `guides/features/tool-calling`, `guides/features/structured-outputs`, `guides/features/plugins/response-healing`
  - `guides/features/server-tools` and its thirteen tool pages (twelve in the local copy), `guides/features/plugins`, `guides/overview/multimodal/*`
  - `guides/routing/provider-selection`, `guides/routing/model-fallbacks`, `guides/providers/traffic-routing`, `guides/providers/integration`
  - `api_reference/overview`, `api_reference/parameters`, `api_reference/streaming`, `api_reference/errors-and-debugging`, `api_reference/limits`, `api_reference/versioning`, `api_reference/models`
  - the generated API reference pages: `api/api-reference/chat/create-a-chat-completion`, `api/api-reference/responses/create-a-response`, `api/api-reference/anthropic-messages/create-a-message`
  - the guides for moving to a newer model, under `cookbook/evaluate-and-optimize/model-migrations/`: `claude-4-6`, `claude-4-7`, `sonnet-5`, `opus-5`, `fable-5-1`, `claude-5-5`, `gpt-5-4`, `gpt-5-6`, `gpt-6`, `gemini-4`
  - `changelog`
- **Source code of OpenRouter's official client libraries**, each at the commit shown: `OpenRouterTeam/ai-sdk-provider` at `7a235a4`, `OpenRouterTeam/typescript-sdk` at `7ad8458`, `OpenRouterTeam/python-sdk` at `b48773e`, `OpenRouterTeam/go-sdk` at `654d9d4`, `OpenRouterTeam/typescript-agent` at `8a2d96e`.

### Vendor documentation (read live on 2026-11-22)

A path after "plus" is added to the end of the address before it. A path that starts with `/` replaces the path of the full address before it (in the Alibaba entry, only its last part). `...` is short for `https://developers.openai.com/api/reference`.

- **Anthropic:** `https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/` plus `build-with-claude/preserved-thinking`, `build-with-claude/preserved-thinking/prefix-checks`, `build-with-claude/thinking`, `build-with-claude/effort`, `build-with-claude/prompt-caching`, `build-with-claude/handling-stop-reasons`, `build-with-claude/structured-outputs`, `build-with-claude/files`, `api/errors`, `api/versioning`, `agents-and-tools/tool-use/server-tools`, `agents-and-tools/tool-use/web-search-tool`, `agents-and-tools/tool-use/tool-search-tool`, `agents-and-tools/mcp-connector`. Anthropic's guidance for gateways: `https://code.claude.com/docs/en/llm-gateway-protocol`.
- **OpenAI:** `https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/` plus `reasoning`, `migrate-to-responses`, `conversation-state`, `function-calling`, `prompt-caching`, `structured-outputs`, `tools-web-search`, `tools-tool-search`, `latest-model/gpt-6-astra`. API references: `https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/resources/responses/methods/create` and `.../resources/chat/subresources/completions/methods/create`.
- **Google:** `https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/` plus `thought-signatures`, `thinking`, `caching`, `function-calling`, `structured-output`, `openai`, `google-search`. Google redirects these to `https://aistudio.google.com/docs/`, where they were read along with `long-decode` and `safety-settings`. API reference: `https://ai.google.dev/api/generate-content`. Vertex AI, Google's cloud platform: `https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/thought-signatures`.
- **xAI:** `https://docs.x.ai/developers/` plus `model-capabilities/text/reasoning`, `model-capabilities/text/comparison`, `rest-api-reference/inference/responses`, `rest-api-reference/inference/chat-completions`, `advanced-api-usage/prompt-caching`.
- **DeepSeek:** `https://api-docs.deepseek.com/guides/thinking_mode`, `/guides/tool_calls`, `/guides/kv_cache`, `/api/create-chat-completion`.
- **Mistral:** `https://docs.mistral.ai/capabilities/function_calling/`, `https://docs.mistral.ai/openapi.yaml`, and the `mistral-common` validator (Mistral's open-source code that validates a request, at commit `fc079a0`).
- **Moonshot:** `https://platform.kimi.ai/docs/guide/use-thinking-models`, `/docs/api/chat`, `/docs/guide/use-official-tools`, and `https://huggingface.co/moonshotai/Kimi-K3/blob/main/encoding_k3.py` (Moonshot's code for the model, at revision `1f5d44f`).
- **Alibaba:** `https://www.alibabacloud.com/help/en/model-studio/deep-thinking`, `/context-cache`, `/qwen-api-via-openai-chat-completions`.
- **Zhipu:** `https://docs.z.ai/guides/capabilities/thinking-mode`, `https://docs.z.ai/api-reference/llm/chat-completion`.
- **MiniMax:** `https://platform.minimax.io/docs/api-reference/text-openai-api`.
- **Meta:** `https://dev.meta.ai/docs/tool-calling`, `https://dev.meta.ai/docs/reasoning`.

### Issue reports and pull requests (third-party accounts)

Each item says in round brackets what the page reports and ends with the evidence item of the verification round that quotes it. The first three are in OpenRouter's own repository.

- `OpenRouterTeam/ai-sdk-provider` issue 491 (a Gemini thought signature from Google AI Studio or Vertex AI gets HTTP 400 when OpenRouter sends the next request to the other; OpenRouter staff confirmed this in a comment dated 2026-06-17) [VT8].
- `OpenRouterTeam/ai-sdk-provider` issue 574 (on streamed requests with tools, OpenRouter forces on Anthropic's eager streaming of tool arguments, which sends them as the model writes them without checking that they are valid JSON, and the reporter got valid JSON in 18 of 36 calls) [VT11].
- `OpenRouterTeam/ai-sdk-provider` pull request 608 (the library loses reasoning entries that arrive after it has closed the reasoning part of a streamed reply; the pull request is open) [VT9].
- `earendil-works/pi` issue 9534 (pi lost the signatures on Claude's reasoning from OpenRouter's chat completions route; a commenter inferred that OpenRouter then drops the unsigned reasoning) [VT10].
- `pydantic/pydantic-ai` issue 5869 (Pydantic AI sent reasoning that was unsigned or came from another model to Claude as text, and Claude then printed its own reasoning to users) [VT12], and issue 4370 (OpenRouter sent encrypted reasoning entries without the `format` and `index` fields that Pydantic AI's parser required) [VT16].
- `BerriAI/litellm` issue 23047 (Anthropic rejected a request in which LiteLLM had reordered the thinking and tool blocks of an earlier reply), issue 32188 (LiteLLM's own field `thinking_blocks` reached a server that rejects unknown fields), and issues 37849 and 52001 (clients that shorten a tool call's id break the Gemini thought signature that LiteLLM stores in it) [VT13].
- `maximhq/bifrost` issue 7729 (after Azure rejects encrypted reasoning with HTTP 400, Bifrost retries once without it, and the request succeeds with the reasoning gone) [VT14].
- `anomalyco/opencode` issue 41469 (a rate-limit error from OpenRouter whose `limit_source` field has the undocumented value `upstream_provider_shared_pool`) [VT17].
